=== pyp-01 | Expense tracker 1: planning and parsing commands
--- teach
The expert course ended with a calculator. A tokenizer cut `2 + 3 * 4` into pieces, and a recursive-descent parser worked out what the pieces meant. You now know every idea you need to build real programs. This projects course is where you build them.

Everything here uses what the three courses before it taught: the idioms of the intermediate course (comprehensions, sorting with `key=`, f-string formatting), the classes and algorithms of the advanced one (dataclasses, special methods), and the design habits of the expert one (a pure core, a thin shell, injected dependencies, tests that catch bugs).

The course has two parts:

- **Three projects**, each a real program built over four lessons. **Each lesson starts from where the last one finished**: the code in the editor is your finished program from the lesson before, and you add the next piece.
- **Four capstones**, where you get only a [[specification|specification]] and design everything yourself.

### The expense tracker

Picture a small notebook where you write down every bit of money you spend: how much, on what, and a word about it. The first project is that notebook as a program. You drive it by typing commands:

```
add 12.50 food lunch with Sam
add 3.20 transport bus
total food
report
```

Line by line: spend 12.50 on food (the note says "lunch with Sam"); spend 3.20 on transport; ask how much went on food in total; ask for a summary of everything.

### Plan before you type

A program this size has four jobs. Each one gets its own lesson and its own [[layer of code|layers]]:

1. **Parse**: turn a line of text into a structured command, or reject it with a clear reason.
2. **Model**: store expenses and answer questions about them.
3. **Report**: turn the stored data into readable output.
4. **Run**: the loop that reads input, calls the layers above, and prints results.

Keeping these apart means each one can be tested on its own. It also means a change to the report format never breaks parsing. This lesson writes layer 1.

### Money is kept in whole cents

You met the trap in the floating-point lesson: `0.1 + 0.2` is not `0.3` in floating point. Money must add up exactly. So this program stores every amount as a whole number of **cents**, an `int`: `12.50` becomes `1250`. It turns cents back into a decimal string only for display, in the next lesson. Storing money as [[whole cents|integer-money]] is what real payment systems do.

### Turning text into cents, step by step

The user types an amount as text, like `"12.5"`. You met `partition` in the intermediate course. It cuts a string at the first `.` and gives back [[three pieces|partition-pieces]]: the part before, the dot itself, and the part after. If there is no dot, the last two pieces are empty strings.

```python
"12.5".partition(".")     # ('12', '.', '5')
"12".partition(".")       # ('12', '', '')
```

The part after the dot needs one more step. `"5"` after the dot means 5 tenths, which is 50 cents, not 5 cents. The string method **`ljust`** ("left-justify") fixes that. `s.ljust(2, "0")` puts `s` on the left and pads it on the right with `"0"` until it is 2 characters long. A string that is already long enough is left alone.

```python
"5".ljust(2, "0")         # '50'  pad on the right: 5 tenths is 50 cents
"50".ljust(2, "0")        # '50'  already 2 long, unchanged
"".ljust(2, "0")          # '00'  no decimals at all: 0 cents
```

Now put it together. For `"12.5"`: the whole part is `"12"`, the padded fraction is `"50"`, so the cents are `12 * 100 + 50 = 1250`. Check it makes sense: twelve and a half dollars is 1250 cents.

### Which amounts to reject

An amount must be digits, with an optional `.` and at most two decimal places. Each bad example below breaks one rule. (Remember that `isdigit()` is `False` for an empty string.)

- `"abc"`: the whole part is not digits.
- `"-5"`: the minus sign is not a digit.
- `"12.345"`: three decimal places.
- `"1."`: there is a dot, but nothing after it, so the fraction is `""`.
- `".5"`: nothing before the dot, so the whole part is `""`.
- `"0"` and `"0.00"`: these read fine, but they come to zero cents. An expense of nothing is a mistake, so reject it too.

### The error policy

A command-line tool must never crash on a typo. So the parser **raises** `ValueError` with a message the user can act on, like `usage: add <amount> <category> [note]`. A message that shows the right way to type a command is called a [[usage message|usage-line]]. The parser does not print anything. The run loop (lesson 4) will catch the error and print it. `ValueError` is the [[right exception to raise here|why-valueerror]].

### Reading one command line

`line.split()` with no argument splits on any run of spaces and throws away spaces at both ends. So extra spaces never matter:

```python
"add 12.50 Food lunch   with Sam".split()
# ['add', '12.50', 'Food', 'lunch', 'with', 'Sam']
```

The first word is the command. The rest are its **arguments**: the values that go with it. `add` needs at least two (an amount and a category), and everything after those is the note. `" ".join(words)` glues the note words back together with single spaces, and joining an empty list gives `""`.

Commands are **case-insensitive**: `ADD`, `Add` and `add` all mean the same. Lowercase the word with `.lower()` before you compare it. Categories are lowercased too, so `Food` and `food` land in the same pile.

### The shape of a parsed command

A parsed command is a small dict. Its shape is your [[contract|contract]] between the parser and the rest of the program: the other layers will rely on exactly these keys.

```python
{"cmd": "add", "amount": 1250, "category": "food", "note": "lunch with Sam"}
{"cmd": "total", "category": None}
{"cmd": "report"}
```

`total` with no category means "the total of everything", so its category is `None`.

**Watch out:** trying to handle every command in one long function that parses, stores and prints. It works for three commands and becomes impossible to follow at ten. Parse here, and only parse.

::: context specification What a specification is
A **specification**, or "spec", is a written description of what a program must do, without saying how. It lists the inputs, the outputs, and what should happen in every case, including the awkward ones. Engineers write specs before they write code, so that everyone agrees on the goal. Flight software for spacecraft starts from requirements documents that can run to hundreds of pages, and every piece of code must trace back to a requirement. In the capstones you get a short spec and nothing else.
:::

::: context layers Four layers, each with one job
The run loop is the only layer that meets the user. It hands each line to the parser, passes the parsed command to the model, and asks the report layer for text to show. None of the lower layers print or read anything.

:::

::: context integer-money Why money is counted in cents
An `int` in Python is always exact, however big it gets. A float is only the nearest value it can store, so adding thousands of float prices lets tiny errors pile up until a total is off by a cent. That is why many payment systems store amounts as whole numbers of the smallest unit: cents, or pence, or yen. The expert course showed the other safe choice, `Decimal`. Cents are simpler here, because every amount in this program has at most two decimal places.
:::

::: context partition-pieces partition always gives three pieces
`partition` never fails and never changes how many pieces it gives back: always three. That is why `whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")` is safe for any text at all. The middle piece tells you whether the dot was found: it is `"."` if it was, and `""` if it was not.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="32" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">"12.5"</text>
  <text x="10" y="82" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">"12"</text>
  <g fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2">
    <rect x="90" y="12" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="180" y="12" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="270" y="12" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/>
    <rect x="90" y="62" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="180" y="62" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="270" y="62" width="80" height="30" rx="4"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="13" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="130" y="32">'12'</text><text x="220" y="32">'.'</text><text x="310" y="32">'5'</text>
    <text x="130" y="82">'12'</text><text x="220" y="82">''</text><text x="310" y="82">''</text>
  </g>
  <g font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="130" y="106">whole</text><text x="220" y="106">dot</text><text x="310" y="106">frac</text>
  </g>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context usage-line How to read a usage line
Usage lines follow a convention that Unix tools have used for decades. Words in angle brackets, like `<amount>`, are placeholders: type a real value there, not the word "amount". Anything in square brackets, like `[note]`, is optional. Plain words, like `add`, are typed exactly as shown. So `usage: add <amount> <category> [note]` means "type add, then an amount, then a category, and a note if you like". Many command-line tools print a line of the same shape when you give them the wrong arguments.
:::

::: context why-valueerror The right type, the wrong value
Python has many built-in exceptions, and picking the matching one tells the reader what went wrong. `ValueError` means "this is the right kind of thing, but its value is not acceptable". The amount is a string, as expected, but `"abc"` is not a valid amount. A `TypeError` would mean the wrong kind of thing altogether, like a list where a string belongs. Because every layer raises `ValueError` for bad input, the run loop can catch that one kind and let real bugs through.
:::

::: context contract A contract between two parts of a program
In everyday life a contract is an agreement: I do this, you do that. In code it is an agreement about shapes. The parser promises that an `add` command always comes back as a dict with the keys `cmd`, `amount`, `category` and `note`, with the amount already in cents. The rest of the program promises to rely on nothing else. As long as both sides keep their promise, either side can be rewritten without breaking the other.
:::
--- task
Write the parsing layer: two functions.

`to_cents(text)` turns a string amount into whole cents: `"12.50"` → `1250`, `"3"` → `300`, `"0.5"` → `50`. It raises `ValueError` for anything that is not digits with an optional `.` and at most two decimal places (such as `"abc"`, `"12.345"`, `"-5"`, `"1."`), and for zero.

`parse_command(line)` returns one of the three dicts shown in the lesson:

- Command words are case-insensitive, and categories are lowercased.
- The note is the rest of the words joined by single spaces, or `""` if there are none.

It raises `ValueError` with exactly these messages:

- an empty line: `"empty command"`
- `add` with fewer than two arguments: `"usage: add <amount> <category> [note]"`
- `total` with more than one argument: `"usage: total [category]"`
- `report` with any argument: `"usage: report"`
- an unknown command: `"unknown command: <word>"`, with the word as it was typed.
--- hint
Two functions, two jobs. In `to_cents`, cut the text with `partition`, check the rules from the lesson one by one, and build the cents. In `parse_command`, split the line into words; the first word (lowercased) is the command and the rest are its arguments.
--- hint
`whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")`. The text is bad if the whole part is not digits, or there was a dot and the fraction is not digits, or the fraction is longer than 2. Otherwise the cents are `int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))`; raise if that is not more than zero.
--- hint
In `parse_command`: `words = line.split()`; if the list is empty, raise `"empty command"`. Then `cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]`. Give each command its own `if` that checks `len(args)` before returning its dict. If no `if` matched, raise `f"unknown command: {words[0]}"`.
--- starter
# Expense tracker, step 1: understand one command.
# Amounts are kept in whole cents (ints), never floats.


def to_cents(text):
    pass


def parse_command(line):
    pass
--- solution
# Expense tracker, step 1: understand one command.
# Amounts are kept in whole cents (ints), never floats.


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
--- check test | to_cents converts valid amounts
[to_cents(t) for t in ("12.50", "3", "0.5", "0.05", "1200")] == [1250, 300, 50, 5, 120000]
--- check test | to_cents rejects bad amounts
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: to_cents(t)) for t in ("abc", "12.345", "-5", "1.", ".5", "0", "0.00", "", "1,000"))
--- check case | parse an add command
parse_command("add 12.50 Food lunch   with Sam")
=> {"cmd": "add", "amount": 1250, "category": "food", "note": "lunch with Sam"}
--- check case | The note is optional
parse_command("ADD 7 rent")
=> {"cmd": "add", "amount": 700, "category": "rent", "note": ""}
--- check test | total and report
parse_command("total") == {"cmd": "total", "category": None} and parse_command("Total FOOD") == {"cmd": "total", "category": "food"} and parse_command("report") == {"cmd": "report"}
--- check test | Bad commands raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda l=l: parse_command(l)) for l in ("", "   ", "add 5", "add", "total a b", "report now", "spend 5 fun", "add x food"))
--- check test | The error messages say what is wrong
(lambda msg: msg("") == "empty command" and msg("add 5") == "usage: add <amount> <category> [note]" and msg("fly") == "unknown command: fly")(lambda l: (lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_command(line)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)\n", g), g.get("msg"))[1])({**globals(), "line": l}))

+++ practice | Kilograms into grams
--- task
A kitchen scale app stores weights as whole grams, an `int`, for the same reason the tracker stores cents. Write `to_grams(text)`, which turns a weight in kilograms, written as text, into grams:

- `"2.5"` → `2500`, `"0.125"` → `125`, `"3"` → `3000`, `"0.001"` → `1`.
- A weight is digits, then optionally a `.` and one to three more digits.
- Raise `ValueError` for anything else, such as `"abc"`, `"1.2345"`, `"-1"`, `"1."`, `".5"` or `""`.
- Raise `ValueError` for a weight of zero too, such as `"0"` or `"0.000"`.
--- starter
def to_grams(text):
    return int(float(text) * 1000)
--- solution
def to_grams(text):
    """Turn "2.5" (kilograms) into 2500 (grams)."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 3:
        raise ValueError(f"bad weight: {text}")
    grams = int(whole) * 1000 + int(frac.ljust(3, "0"))
    if grams == 0:
        raise ValueError(f"weight must be more than zero: {text}")
    return grams
--- hint
This is the lesson's cents idea with a different unit: a kilogram is 1000 grams, so the part after the dot stands for thousandths, not hundredths.
--- hint
Cut the text with `partition(".")` and check the three rules. Then pad the fraction on the right to 3 characters with `ljust(3, "0")`, so `"5"` after the dot counts as 500 grams.
--- check case | Weights with 0 to 3 decimals
[to_grams(t) for t in ("2.5", "0.125", "3", "0.001", "10.05")]
=> [2500, 125, 3000, 1, 10050]
--- check test | Bad weights raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: to_grams(t)) for t in ("abc", "1.2345", "-1", "1.", ".5", "", "2,5", "1e3", "1 .5"))
--- check test | Zero is refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: to_grams("0")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: to_grams("0.000"))
--- check test | The answer is an int
type(to_grams("1.5")) is int and to_grams("1.5") == 1500

+++ practice | Race times
--- task
Race times are typed as minutes, a colon, and two digits of seconds: `"3:07"` is 3 minutes and 7 seconds. Write `to_seconds(text)`, which returns the time as a whole number of seconds:

- `"3:07"` → `187`, `"0:45"` → `45`, `"125:00"` → `7500`, `"0:00"` → `0`.
- The minutes are one or more digits. The seconds are exactly two digits, from `00` to `59`.
- Anything else raises `ValueError` with the message `"bad time: <text>"`, for example `"bad time: 3:60"`. That covers `"3:7"`, `"3:60"`, `":30"`, `"3"`, `"3:07:00"`, `"a:bc"` and `"-1:00"`.
--- starter
def to_seconds(text):
    minutes, seconds = text.split(":")
    return int(minutes) * 60 + int(seconds)
--- solution
def to_seconds(text):
    """Turn "3:07" into 187 seconds."""
    minutes, colon, seconds = text.partition(":")
    valid = minutes.isdigit() and colon and len(seconds) == 2 and seconds.isdigit()
    if not valid or int(seconds) >= 60:
        raise ValueError(f"bad time: {text}")
    return int(minutes) * 60 + int(seconds)
--- hint
`partition(":")` always gives three pieces, even when there is no colon, so it never crashes the way `split` with two names does.
--- hint
Check the pieces in order: the minutes are digits, the colon was found, the seconds are exactly 2 characters and all digits. Only then is it safe to call `int(seconds)` and check that it is below 60.
--- check case | Valid times
[to_seconds(t) for t in ("3:07", "0:45", "125:00", "0:00", "10:59")]
=> [187, 45, 7500, 0, 659]
--- check test | Bad times raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: to_seconds(t)) for t in ("3:7", "3:60", ":30", "3", "3:07:00", "a:bc", "-1:00", "", "3:", "3:+5"))
--- check case | The message shows the bad text
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    to_seconds(t)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), t=t)) for t in ("3:60", "3")]
=> ["bad time: 3:60", "bad time: 3"]

+++ practice | A thermostat's commands
--- task
Write the parsing layer for a thermostat. `parse_thermostat(line)` returns one of these dicts:

```python
{"cmd": "set", "tenths": 215}       # from "set 21.5"
{"cmd": "mode", "mode": "heat"}     # from "mode Heat"
{"cmd": "status"}                   # from "status"
```

- A temperature is digits, then optionally a `.` and exactly one more digit. It is kept as whole tenths of a degree: `"21.5"` → `215`, `"18"` → `180`.
- Command words and mode names are case-insensitive. The modes are `heat`, `cool` and `off`.

It raises `ValueError` with exactly these messages:

- an empty line: `"empty command"`
- `set` without exactly one argument: `"usage: set <temperature>"`
- a temperature that is not in that format: `"bad temperature: <text>"`
- a temperature below 5.0 or above 30.0: `"out of range: <text>"` (5 and 30 themselves are fine)
- `mode` without exactly one argument: `"usage: mode <heat|cool|off>"`
- a mode that is not one of the three: `"unknown mode: <name>"`, with the name lowercased
- `status` with any argument: `"usage: status"`
- any other command: `"unknown command: <word>"`, with the word as it was typed.
--- starter
def parse_thermostat(line):
    words = line.split()
    return {"cmd": words[0]}
--- solution
MODES = {"heat", "cool", "off"}


def to_tenths(text):
    """Turn "21.5" into 215 tenths of a degree."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and (len(frac) != 1 or not frac.isdigit())):
        raise ValueError(f"bad temperature: {text}")
    tenths = int(whole) * 10 + int(frac or "0")
    if not 50 <= tenths <= 300:
        raise ValueError(f"out of range: {text}")
    return tenths


def parse_thermostat(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "set":
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: set <temperature>")
        return {"cmd": "set", "tenths": to_tenths(args[0])}
    if cmd == "mode":
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: mode <heat|cool|off>")
        mode = args[0].lower()
        if mode not in MODES:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown mode: {mode}")
        return {"cmd": "mode", "mode": mode}
    if cmd == "status":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: status")
        return {"cmd": "status"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
--- hint
Give the temperature its own small function, the way the tracker has `to_cents`: it checks the format, builds the tenths, then checks the range. The command function only splits words and calls it.
--- hint
With one decimal, the fraction after the dot is already tenths: `int(whole) * 10 + int(frac or "0")`. The range check is `50 <= tenths <= 300`, because 5.0 degrees is 50 tenths.
--- hint
In `parse_thermostat`, lowercase the first word, give each command its own `if` that checks `len(args)` first, and keep the three modes in a set so the check is `mode not in MODES`.
--- check case | set keeps tenths
[parse_thermostat(l) for l in ("set 21.5", "SET 18", "set 5", "set 30.0")]
=> [{"cmd": "set", "tenths": 215}, {"cmd": "set", "tenths": 180}, {"cmd": "set", "tenths": 50}, {"cmd": "set", "tenths": 300}]
--- check case | mode and status
[parse_thermostat(l) for l in ("mode Heat", "Mode OFF", "  status  ")]
=> [{"cmd": "mode", "mode": "heat"}, {"cmd": "mode", "mode": "off"}, {"cmd": "status"}]
--- check case | Every error message
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_thermostat(line)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), line=l)) for l in ("", "set", "set 21.55", "set 4.9", "set 30.1", "mode", "mode Fan", "status now", "Fly 3", "set .5", "set 21.")]
=> ["empty command", "usage: set <temperature>", "bad temperature: 21.55", "out of range: 4.9", "out of range: 30.1", "usage: mode <heat|cool|off>", "unknown mode: fan", "usage: status", "unknown command: Fly", "bad temperature: .5", "bad temperature: 21."]

+++ practice | Amounts with thousands separators
--- task
People often type big amounts with commas, like `"1,234.50"`. Write `to_cents(text)`, which accepts them:

- The part before the dot is either plain digits (`"1234"`), or groups joined by commas where the first group has 1 to 3 digits and every later group has exactly 3 (`"1,234"`, `"12,345,678"`).
- After that, optionally a `.` and one or two digits, as in the lesson.
- Return whole cents: `"1,234.50"` → `123450`, `"12"` → `1200`, `"1,000"` → `100000`.
- Raise `ValueError` for anything else, such as `"1,23"`, `",123"`, `"1234,567"`, `"1,,234"`, `"1,234,"` and `"12.345"`, and for zero, such as `"0"` or `"0,000.00"`.
--- starter
def to_cents(text):
    pass
--- solution
def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "1,234.50" into 123450. Commas must split the digits into groups of three."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    groups = whole.split(",")
    first, rest = groups[0], groups[1:]
    groups_ok = first.isdigit() and all(len(g) == 3 and g.isdigit() for g in rest)
    if not groups_ok or (rest and len(first) > 3):
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    if (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(first + "".join(rest)) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents
--- hint
Deleting every comma with `replace` is the obvious answer, and it is wrong: it lets `"1,23"` through. Split the whole part on `","` and check each group instead.
--- hint
`"1,234".split(",")` is `['1', '234']` and `"1234".split(",")` is `['1234']`. The first group must be digits; if there are more groups, the first may have at most 3 digits and each of the others exactly 3. An empty group, from `",123"` or `"1,,234"`, is not digits, so it fails.
--- check case | Amounts with and without commas
[to_cents(t) for t in ("1,234.50", "12", "1,000", "999,999,999.99", "1234.5", "0.05", "12,345,678")]
=> [123450, 1200, 100000, 99999999999, 123450, 5, 1234567800]
--- check test | Badly placed commas are refused
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: to_cents(t)) for t in ("1,23", ",123", "1234,567", "1,,234", "1,234,", "12,34.5", "1,2345", ","))
--- check test | The old rules still hold
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: to_cents(t)) for t in ("12.345", "abc", "-5", "1.", ".5", "", "0", "0,000.00"))

+++ practice | Debug: extra spaces break the rover
--- task
`parse_move(line)` reads commands for a rover and returns a dict:

- `move <metres> <direction>` gives `{"cmd": "move", "metres": 5, "direction": "north"}`. The metres are a whole number above zero; the direction is `north`, `south`, `east` or `west`, in any case.
- `stop` gives `{"cmd": "stop"}`.
- Errors raise `ValueError`: `"empty command"`, `"usage: move <metres> <direction>"`, `"bad distance: <text>"`, `"bad direction: <text>"`, `"usage: stop"` and `"unknown command: <word>"`.

It works for neatly typed commands. But `"move  5 north"`, with two spaces, is refused with the usage message, `" stop"` with a space in front is an unknown command, and an empty line says `"unknown command: "` instead of `"empty command"`. Fix the bug, so that extra spaces anywhere never matter.
--- starter
DIRECTIONS = {"north", "south", "east", "west"}


def parse_move(line):
    words = line.split(" ")
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "move":
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: move <metres> <direction>")
        metres, direction = args[0], args[1].lower()
        if not metres.isdigit() or int(metres) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad distance: {metres}")
        if direction not in DIRECTIONS:
            raise ValueError(f"bad direction: {args[1]}")
        return {"cmd": "move", "metres": int(metres), "direction": direction}
    if cmd == "stop":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: stop")
        return {"cmd": "stop"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
--- solution
DIRECTIONS = {"north", "south", "east", "west"}


def parse_move(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "move":
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: move <metres> <direction>")
        metres, direction = args[0], args[1].lower()
        if not metres.isdigit() or int(metres) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad distance: {metres}")
        if direction not in DIRECTIONS:
            raise ValueError(f"bad direction: {args[1]}")
        return {"cmd": "move", "metres": int(metres), "direction": direction}
    if cmd == "stop":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: stop")
        return {"cmd": "stop"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
--- hint
Try `"move  5 north".split(" ")` in your head, or in a scratch line: what is the second item of the list?
--- hint
Splitting on one space keeps an empty string for every extra space, and `"".split(" ")` is `['']`, a list that is not empty. `split()` with no argument splits on any run of spaces and drops them at both ends.
--- check case | Extra spaces do not matter
[parse_move(l) for l in ("move  5 north", " stop", "MOVE 12   West  ")]
=> [{"cmd": "move", "metres": 5, "direction": "north"}, {"cmd": "stop"}, {"cmd": "move", "metres": 12, "direction": "west"}]
--- check case | Blank lines are empty commands
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_move(line)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), line=l)) for l in ("", "   ")]
=> ["empty command", "empty command"]
--- check case | The other errors still work
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_move(line)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), line=l)) for l in ("move 5", "move 0 north", "move 5 up", "stop now", "jump")]
=> ["usage: move <metres> <direction>", "bad distance: 0", "bad direction: up", "usage: stop", "unknown command: jump"]

+++ practice | Settings for an engine burn
--- task
A flight controller types engine burns with named settings, in any order: `"burn dv=12.5 axis=x seconds=30"`. Write `parse_burn(line)`, which returns

```python
{"cmd": "burn", "dv": 1250, "axis": "x", "seconds": 30}
```

- The first word must be `burn`, in any case.
- Every other word is a setting written `key=value`. Keys are case-insensitive.
- `dv` is required: a speed change with at most two decimals, like an amount of money, kept as whole hundredths (`"12.5"` → `1250`). It must be more than zero.
- `axis` is required: `x`, `y` or `z`, in any case, stored lowercased.
- `seconds` is optional: a whole number above zero. If it is missing, it is `0`.

Raise `ValueError` with exactly these messages, checked in this order:

- an empty line: `"empty command"`; a first word that is not `burn`: `"unknown command: <word>"`
- a word with no `=`, or with nothing before or after it: `"bad setting: <word>"`
- a key that is not `dv`, `axis` or `seconds`: `"unknown setting: <key>"`; a key given twice: `"duplicate setting: <key>"`
- `dv` missing: `"missing setting: dv"`; then `axis` missing: `"missing setting: axis"`
- a bad value: `"bad dv: <value>"`, `"bad axis: <value>"` or `"bad seconds: <value>"`.

Keys in messages are lowercased; words and values appear as typed.
--- starter
def parse_burn(line):
    words = line.split()
    settings = dict(word.split("=") for word in words[1:])
    return {"cmd": "burn", **settings}
--- solution
KEYS = ("dv", "axis", "seconds")


def to_hundredths(text):
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad dv: {text}")
    value = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if value <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"bad dv: {text}")
    return value


def parse_burn(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    if words[0].lower() != "burn":
        raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
    settings = {}
    for word in words[1:]:
        key, eq, value = word.partition("=")
        if not key or not eq or not value:
            raise ValueError(f"bad setting: {word}")
        key = key.lower()
        if key not in KEYS:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown setting: {key}")
        if key in settings:
            raise ValueError(f"duplicate setting: {key}")
        settings[key] = value
    for key in ("dv", "axis"):
        if key not in settings:
            raise ValueError(f"missing setting: {key}")
    dv = to_hundredths(settings["dv"])
    axis = settings["axis"].lower()
    if axis not in ("x", "y", "z"):
        raise ValueError(f"bad axis: {settings['axis']}")
    seconds = settings.get("seconds", "0")
    if "seconds" in settings and (not seconds.isdigit() or int(seconds) == 0):
        raise ValueError(f"bad seconds: {seconds}")
    return {"cmd": "burn", "dv": dv, "axis": axis, "seconds": int(seconds)}
--- hint
Work in two passes. First collect the settings into a dict of key to raw text, checking each word's shape, its key, and duplicates. Then, with every setting collected, check what is missing and convert each value.
--- hint
`word.partition("=")` gives the key, the `=` (or `""` if there was none) and the value. The word is bad if any of the three pieces is empty.
--- hint
`dv` converts exactly like `to_cents`. For `seconds`, use `settings.get("seconds", "0")`, and only check it when the key was really given.
--- check case | All three settings, in any order and case
parse_burn("BURN Seconds=30 axis=X dv=12.5")
=> {"cmd": "burn", "dv": 1250, "axis": "x", "seconds": 30}
--- check case | seconds is optional
parse_burn("burn dv=0.05 axis=z")
=> {"cmd": "burn", "dv": 5, "axis": "z", "seconds": 0}
--- check case | Every error message, in order
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_burn(line)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), line=l)) for l in ("", "fire dv=1", "burn dv", "burn =3", "burn dv=", "burn Mass=4", "burn dv=1 DV=2", "burn axis=x", "burn dv=1", "burn dv=1.234 axis=x", "burn dv=0 axis=x", "burn dv=1 axis=Q", "burn dv=1 axis=x seconds=0", "burn dv=1 axis=x seconds=2.5")]
=> ["empty command", "unknown command: fire", "bad setting: dv", "bad setting: =3", "bad setting: dv=", "unknown setting: mass", "duplicate setting: dv", "missing setting: dv", "missing setting: axis", "bad dv: 1.234", "bad dv: 0", "bad axis: Q", "bad seconds: 0", "bad seconds: 2.5"]

=== pyp-02 | Expense tracker 2: the ledger
--- teach
Last lesson you wrote the parser: a line of text goes in, a tidy dict comes out. Now comes layer 2, the model: somewhere to keep expenses, and a set of questions you can ask about them.

Picture a club's cash book, kept by one treasurer. Anyone who spends club money tells the treasurer, and anyone who wants to know the balance asks the treasurer. Nobody else writes in the book. That way, if the club makes a new rule ("receipts over 50 need a signature"), only the treasurer has to learn it.

### One record: a dataclass

Each expense is a small, fixed bundle of fields: an amount, a category and a note. That is exactly what a [[dataclass|dataclass-recall]] is for:

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int        # cents
    category: str
    note: str = ""
```

`note: str = ""` gives the note a **default**: if you leave it out, it is `""`. So `Expense(300, "bus")` works, and its note is empty. The field with a default [[must come last|defaults-last]].

### One owner: the Ledger

The collection of expenses belongs to a class called `Ledger` (a ledger is an account book). It keeps a list, and *every* change and every question goes through its methods:

```python
class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []
```

That gives the rules [[one home|encapsulation]]. If you later need to reject duplicate entries or keep an audit log, there is exactly one place to change.

### Answer questions from the data, do not store answers

It is tempting to keep a running `self.total` and update it on every `add`. But [[stored answers go stale|derived-data]] the moment someone adds a `remove` method and forgets to update them. Working out `total()` from the list whenever you are asked is always right. For a personal expense list, it is plenty fast.

```python
def total(self, category=None):
    return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)
```

Read the `if` part aloud: "count this expense if no category was asked for, or if its category is the one asked for". So `total()` adds up everything, and `total("food")` adds up only food.

### Categories: sorted, no duplicates

A set keeps only one copy of each value, and `sorted` turns it into a list in alphabetical order. A set comprehension does both jobs in one line:

```python
sorted({c for c in ["rent", "food", "rent"]})    # ['food', 'rent']
```

### Formatting cents for people

People want to read `1,234.56`, not `123456`. Two operators you know split the cents into whole units and the leftover cents:

```python
cents = 123456
cents // 100      # 1234   whole units
cents % 100       # 56     cents left over
```

(That is the same pair `divmod(cents, 100)` gives.) Then an f-string formats each part:

```python
f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"    # '1,234.56'
```

- `:,` puts a comma between every group of three digits, the [[thousands separator|thousands-separator]].
- `:02d` means: `d`, a whole number; `2`, at least 2 characters wide; `0`, fill any gap with zeros. This is **zero padding**. It makes 5 cents show as `.05`, [[not .5|zero-padding]].

### len() for your own class

You met special methods in the advanced course. Defining `__len__` lets callers write `len(ledger)` to ask how many expenses there are. That reads naturally, and it is what Python programmers expect from anything that [[holds a collection|dunder-len]].

**Watch out:** letting other code reach in and edit `ledger.expenses` directly, like `ledger.expenses.append(...)`. It works until the day the ledger needs to enforce a rule, and then that code slips past it. Go through the methods.

::: context dataclass-recall What @dataclass writes for you
You met dataclasses in the advanced course. The `@dataclass` line reads the annotated fields (`amount: int` and so on) and writes three methods for you: `__init__`, so `Expense(1250, "food", "lunch")` works; `__repr__`, so printing one shows `Expense(amount=1250, category='food', note='lunch')`; and `__eq__`, so two expenses with the same fields count as equal. That last one is what lets a check compare `ledger.add(...)` with `Expense(1250, "food", "lunch")`.
:::

::: context defaults-last Why the default goes on the last field
The fields become the parameters of `__init__`, in the order you wrote them. A call like `Expense(300, "bus")` fills them from the left. If a field with a default came before one without, Python could not tell which values you meant to skip. So a dataclass refuses to be created: you get a `TypeError` saying a non-default argument follows a default argument. Ordinary functions have the same rule, which you met with default arguments in the intermediate course.
:::

::: context encapsulation One home for the rules
Programmers call this **encapsulation**: the data lives inside an object, and the outside world may use it only through that object's methods. It is the treasurer and the cash book. The payoff comes later. In the library project you will add rules like "a member may not borrow more than three books", and each rule goes in one method instead of being copied to every place that touches the data.
:::

::: context derived-data A total that forgot to change
A number you can work out from other data is called **derived data**. If you store it as well, you now have two copies of the truth, and they can disagree. Here, one expense was removed from the list, but the stored total was never updated.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 140" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">before remove</text>
  <text x="270" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">after remove</text>
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="140" height="44" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="57" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">list: 1250, 320</text>
  <rect x="200" y="30" width="140" height="44" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="57" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">list: 1250</text>
  <rect x="20" y="86" width="140" height="40" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="111" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">self.total = 1570</text>
  <rect x="200" y="86" width="140" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="111" font-size="13" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">self.total = 1570</text>
</svg>
```

Computing the total from the list each time gives 1250, the right answer, with nothing to forget.
:::

::: context thousands-separator Commas, dots and countries
A **thousands separator** splits a long number into groups of three digits, so `1234567` reads at a glance as `1,234,567`. The `:,` format always uses a comma and a dot. Many countries swap them: in Germany the same amount is written `1.234,56`. Programs that serve people around the world use locale settings to pick the right style. This tracker keeps it simple, with one style everywhere.
:::

::: context zero-padding Why 5 cents needs a zero in front
Without padding, 5 cents would be written `0.5`, and anyone reading that sees fifty cents. The `02` makes the cents part always two characters, filling the gap with a zero.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="36" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">123456 cents</text>
  <text x="10" y="86" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">5 cents</text>
  <g fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2">
    <rect x="130" y="16" width="90" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="250" y="16" width="30" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="280" y="16" width="30" height="30" rx="4"/>
    <rect x="130" y="66" width="90" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="250" y="66" width="30" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="280" y="66" width="30" height="30" rx="4"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="175" y="36">1,234</text><text x="235" y="36">.</text><text x="265" y="36">5</text><text x="295" y="36">6</text>
    <text x="175" y="86">0</text><text x="235" y="86">.</text><text x="295" y="86">5</text>
  </g>
  <text x="265" y="86" font-size="14" font-weight="700" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="330" y="86" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">pad</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context dunder-len What len() really calls
When you write `len(something)`, Python calls `something.__len__()` and hands back what it returns. Lists, strings, dicts and sets all have that method, which is why `len` works on all of them. Giving `Ledger` its own `__len__` makes it join that family. One bonus you get for free: an object with `__len__` and no `__bool__` counts as false in an `if` when its length is 0, so `if ledger:` means "if there are any expenses".
:::
--- task
Add the model to your parser. Keep `to_cents` and `parse_command` as they are, and add:

- A dataclass `Expense` with `amount` (int cents), `category` (str) and `note` (str, default `""`).
- `format_cents(cents)`, returning a string with a thousands separator and two decimals: `1250` → `"12.50"`, `5` → `"0.05"`, `123456` → `"1,234.56"`.
- A class `Ledger` with these methods:
  - `add(amount, category, note="")` stores a new `Expense` and returns it;
  - `total(category=None)` returns the cents for one category, or for all of them;
  - `categories()` returns the categories sorted, without duplicates;
  - `__len__` returns how many expenses are stored.
--- hint
Import `dataclass` at the top and declare the three annotated fields, with the default on the last one. The ledger keeps a list, `self.expenses`, that starts empty in `__init__`.
--- hint
`format_cents` is one f-string: `cents // 100` with `:,`, then a `.`, then `cents % 100` with `:02d`.
--- hint
`add` builds an `Expense`, appends it and returns it. `total` is the `sum` over a generator with an `if` from the lesson; `categories` is `sorted` of a set comprehension; `__len__` returns `len(self.expenses)`.
--- starter
# Expense tracker, step 1: understand one command.
# Amounts are kept in whole cents (ints), never floats.


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
--- solution
# Expense tracker, step 2: a ledger that stores expenses.
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


def format_cents(cents):
    """1250 -> "12.50", 123456 -> "1,234.56"."""
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def categories(self):
        return sorted({e.category for e in self.expenses})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.expenses)
--- check test | format_cents
[format_cents(c) for c in (1250, 5, 0, 100, 123456, 100000000)] == ["12.50", "0.05", "0.00", "1.00", "1,234.56", "1,000,000.00"]
--- check test | add stores and returns an Expense
(lambda l: (l.add(1250, "food", "lunch"), len(l))[0] == Expense(1250, "food", "lunch") and len(l) == 1)(Ledger())
--- check test | The note defaults to empty
Expense(300, "bus").note == "" and Ledger().add(5, "x").note == ""
--- check case | total, overall and by category
(lambda l: (l.add(1250, "food"), l.add(320, "transport"), l.add(4500, "food"), l.total(), l.total("food"), l.total("fun"))[3:])(Ledger())
=> (6070, 5750, 0)
--- check case | categories are sorted without duplicates
(lambda l: (l.add(1, "rent"), l.add(2, "food"), l.add(3, "rent"), l.categories())[-1])(Ledger())
=> ["food", "rent"]
--- check test | An empty ledger
len(Ledger()) == 0 and Ledger().total() == 0 and Ledger().categories() == []
--- check test | The parser still works
parse_command("add 1 food") == {"cmd": "add", "amount": 100, "category": "food", "note": ""}

+++ practice | Durations for people
--- task
A timer stores durations as whole seconds. Write `format_duration(seconds)`, which returns them as hours, minutes and seconds, separated by colons:

- `3725` → `"1:02:05"`, `59` → `"0:00:59"`, `0` → `"0:00:00"`.
- Minutes and seconds always have two digits. Hours have as many digits as they need, with a thousands separator: `90061` → `"25:01:01"`, `3600000` → `"1,000:00:00"`.
--- starter
def format_duration(seconds):
    return f"{seconds // 3600}:{seconds // 60}:{seconds % 60}"
--- solution
def format_duration(seconds):
    """3725 -> "1:02:05"."""
    hours, rest = divmod(seconds, 3600)
    minutes, secs = divmod(rest, 60)
    return f"{hours:,}:{minutes:02d}:{secs:02d}"
--- hint
First split off the whole hours (3600 seconds each). What is left over is less than an hour; split that into whole minutes and leftover seconds the same way.
--- hint
`divmod(a, b)` gives `a // b` and `a % b` together. Then one f-string: `:,` on the hours and `:02d` on the minutes and seconds.
--- check case | Hours, minutes and seconds
[format_duration(s) for s in (3725, 59, 60, 3600, 86399)]
=> ["1:02:05", "0:00:59", "0:01:00", "1:00:00", "23:59:59"]
--- check case | Zero
format_duration(0)
=> "0:00:00"
--- check case | Many hours
[format_duration(s) for s in (90061, 3600000)]
=> ["25:01:01", "1,000:00:00"]

+++ practice | A shopping cart
--- task
Write the model for a shopping cart.

- A dataclass `Item` with `name` (str), `price` (int cents, for one of them) and `qty` (int, default `1`).
- A class `Cart` with these methods:
  - `add(name, price, qty=1)` stores a new `Item` and returns it. A `qty` below 1 raises `ValueError`, and nothing is stored.
  - `count()` returns how many things are in the cart: the sum of every item's `qty`.
  - `total()` returns the cost in cents: each item's price times its quantity, all added up.
  - `names()` returns the item names sorted, without duplicates.
  - `__len__` returns how many items (lines on the receipt) are stored.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


class Cart:
    pass
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass
class Item:
    name: str
    price: int
    qty: int = 1


class Cart:
    def __init__(self):
        self.items = []

    def add(self, name, price, qty=1):
        if qty < 1:
            raise ValueError("quantity must be at least 1")
        item = Item(name, price, qty)
        self.items.append(item)
        return item

    def count(self):
        return sum(item.qty for item in self.items)

    def total(self):
        return sum(item.price * item.qty for item in self.items)

    def names(self):
        return sorted({item.name for item in self.items})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.items)
--- hint
The cart owns one list of `Item`s, which starts empty in `__init__`. Every question is worked out from that list when it is asked, not stored.
--- hint
Check `qty` before you build the item, so a refused item is never appended. `count` and `total` are each a `sum` over a generator; `names` is `sorted` of a set comprehension.
--- check test | add stores and returns an Item, with qty defaulting to 1
(lambda c: (c.add("tea", 250), len(c))[0] == Item("tea", 250, 1) and len(c) == 1)(Cart())
--- check case | count, total and names
(lambda c: (c.add("tea", 250, 2), c.add("cake", 375), c.add("tea", 250), c.count(), c.total(), c.names(), len(c))[3:])(Cart())
=> (4, 1125, ["cake", "tea"], 3)
--- check test | An empty cart
len(Cart()) == 0 and Cart().count() == 0 and Cart().total() == 0 and Cart().names() == []
--- check test | A quantity of 0 is refused and not stored
(lambda c: (raises(ValueError, lambda: c.add("tea", 250, 0)), len(c)))(Cart()) == (True, 0)

+++ practice | One line into the ledger
--- task
The starter has `to_cents`, `Expense` and a `Ledger` from the lesson. Add two methods to `Ledger`:

- `add_line(text)` reads a line like `"12.50 Food lunch with Sam"`: an amount, a category (lowercased) and an optional note (the rest of the words joined by single spaces, or `""`). It stores the expense with `add` and returns it. With fewer than two words it raises `ValueError("usage: <amount> <category> [note]")`; a bad amount raises the `ValueError` from `to_cents`, and nothing is stored.
- `biggest(category=None)` returns the stored `Expense` with the largest amount, of one category or of all of them. If several share the largest amount, return the one added first. If there is nothing to choose from, return `None`.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def add_line(self, text):
        words = text.split()
        if len(words) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: <amount> <category> [note]")
        return self.add(to_cents(words[0]), words[1].lower(), " ".join(words[2:]))

    def biggest(self, category=None):
        chosen = [e for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category]
        if not chosen:
            return None
        return max(chosen, key=lambda e: e.amount)
--- hint
`add_line` is a small parser that hands its result to `add`, the one method that stores. Convert the amount before calling `add`, so a bad amount raises before anything is stored.
--- hint
For `biggest`, first keep the expenses of the category (or all of them), and return `None` if that list is empty. `max(items, key=...)` picks the largest by the key, and on a tie it keeps the first one it saw.
--- check case | add_line parses and stores
(lambda l: (l.add_line("12.50 Food lunch   with Sam"), l.add_line("3 bus"), l.total())[::2])(Ledger())
=> (Expense(1250, "food", "lunch with Sam"), 1550)
--- check test | Bad lines raise ValueError and store nothing
(lambda l: (raises(ValueError, lambda: l.add_line("12.50")), raises(ValueError, lambda: l.add_line("abc food")), raises(ValueError, lambda: l.add_line("")), l.total()))(Ledger()) == (True, True, True, 0)
--- check case | biggest, of all and of one category
(lambda l: (l.add(500, "food", "a"), l.add(900, "rent"), l.add(700, "food", "b"), l.biggest(), l.biggest("food"))[-2:])(Ledger())
=> (Expense(900, "rent", ""), Expense(700, "food", "b"))
--- check case | Ties go to the first one added, and nothing gives None
(lambda l: (l.add(500, "food", "first"), l.add(500, "food", "second"), l.biggest("food").note, l.biggest("fun"), Ledger().biggest())[-3:])(Ledger())
=> ("first", None, None)

+++ practice | Negative amounts
--- task
A bank statement shows refunds as negative amounts. Write `format_signed(cents)`, which formats any whole number of cents, negative ones included:

- `1250` → `"12.50"`, `123456` → `"1,234.56"`, `0` → `"0.00"`.
- `-5` → `"-0.05"`, `-123456` → `"-1,234.56"`, `-100` → `"-1.00"`.

The lesson's format gets negative numbers wrong: try `-5 // 100` and `-5 % 100` to see why.
--- starter
def format_signed(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"
--- solution
def format_signed(cents):
    """-123456 -> "-1,234.56". The sign is shown once, in front."""
    sign = "-" if cents < 0 else ""
    units, rest = divmod(abs(cents), 100)
    return f"{sign}{units:,}.{rest:02d}"
--- hint
`//` rounds down, towards minus infinity: `-5 // 100` is `-1`, and then `-5 % 100` is `95`. So the lesson's format shows `-5` as `"-1.95"`.
--- hint
Split the problem in two: remember the sign, then format the size of the number, `abs(cents)`, exactly as before, and put the sign in front.
--- check case | Positive amounts and zero
[format_signed(c) for c in (1250, 123456, 0, 5, 100000000)]
=> ["12.50", "1,234.56", "0.00", "0.05", "1,000,000.00"]
--- check case | Negative amounts
[format_signed(c) for c in (-5, -123456, -100, -1, -99)]
=> ["-0.05", "-1,234.56", "-1.00", "-0.01", "-0.99"]
--- check test | Never two minus signs, never a minus on zero
format_signed(-100000) == "-1,000.00" and format_signed(0) == "0.00"

+++ practice | Debug: the tank that forgot
--- task
`Tank` records how much fuel went in at each fill, in whole litres. `fill(litres, station="")` stores a fill, `undo_last()` takes the most recent fill away (and does nothing if there are none), `total()` returns the litres of all the fills, and `stations()` returns the named stations, sorted, without duplicates.

The bug: after `undo_last()`, `total()` still counts the fill that was taken away. Fix it so that `total()` always matches the fills that are stored.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass
class Fill:
    litres: int
    station: str = ""


class Tank:
    def __init__(self):
        self.fills = []
        self._total = 0

    def fill(self, litres, station=""):
        self.fills.append(Fill(litres, station))
        self._total += litres

    def undo_last(self):
        if self.fills:
            self.fills.pop()

    def total(self):
        return self._total

    def stations(self):
        return sorted({f.station for f in self.fills if f.station})
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass
class Fill:
    litres: int
    station: str = ""


class Tank:
    def __init__(self):
        self.fills = []

    def fill(self, litres, station=""):
        self.fills.append(Fill(litres, station))

    def undo_last(self):
        if self.fills:
            self.fills.pop()

    def total(self):
        return sum(f.litres for f in self.fills)

    def stations(self):
        return sorted({f.station for f in self.fills if f.station})
--- hint
The tank keeps the same fact in two places: the list of fills, and a running total. `undo_last` updates one of them and forgets the other.
--- hint
The lesson's advice: do not store an answer you can work out. Delete `_total` and let `total()` add up the litres in the list each time it is asked.
--- check case | Undo takes the fill out of the total
(lambda t: (t.fill(40, "Shell"), t.fill(15), t.fill(30, "BP"), t.undo_last(), t.total())[-1])(Tank())
=> 55
--- check case | Undo on an empty tank, then fill again
(lambda t: (t.undo_last(), t.total(), t.fill(10), t.undo_last(), t.undo_last(), t.total(), t.fill(7, "Esso"), t.total())[1::2])(Tank())
=> (0, None, 0, 7)
--- check case | stations after an undo
(lambda t: (t.fill(20, "Shell"), t.fill(10, "BP"), t.fill(5, "Shell"), t.fill(8, "Aral"), t.undo_last(), t.stations())[-1])(Tank())
=> ["BP", "Shell"]

+++ practice | A monthly budget
--- task
Write a `Budget` class that keeps a spending limit for each category and checks spending against it. Amounts are whole cents. The starter has `format_cents` from the lesson.

- `set_limit(category, cents)` sets (or replaces) a category's limit. A limit that is not more than zero raises `ValueError`.
- `spend(cents, category)` records spending. An amount that is not more than zero raises `ValueError`; a category with no limit raises `KeyError`. Nothing is recorded when it raises.
- `spent(category)` returns the cents spent in a category (0 if nothing).
- `remaining(category)` returns the limit minus the spending, which is negative when over budget. A category with no limit raises `KeyError`.
- `over()` returns the categories whose spending is above their limit, sorted.
- `summary(category)` returns `"food: 45.00 of 100.00 (55.00 left)"`, or, when over the limit, `"food: 120.00 of 100.00 (20.00 over)"`. Spending exactly the limit is `"(0.00 left)"`.
--- starter
def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Budget:
    pass
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass


def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


@dataclass
class Spending:
    cents: int
    category: str


class Budget:
    def __init__(self):
        self.limits = {}
        self.spending = []

    def set_limit(self, category, cents):
        if cents <= 0:
            raise ValueError("a limit must be more than zero")
        self.limits[category] = cents

    def spend(self, cents, category):
        if cents <= 0:
            raise ValueError("an amount must be more than zero")
        if category not in self.limits:
            raise KeyError(category)
        self.spending.append(Spending(cents, category))

    def spent(self, category):
        return sum(s.cents for s in self.spending if s.category == category)

    def remaining(self, category):
        return self.limits[category] - self.spent(category)

    def over(self):
        return sorted(c for c in self.limits if self.remaining(c) < 0)

    def summary(self, category):
        left = self.remaining(category)
        state = f"{format_cents(left)} left" if left >= 0 else f"{format_cents(-left)} over"
        return f"{category}: {format_cents(self.spent(category))} of {format_cents(self.limits[category])} ({state})"
--- hint
Store two things: a dict from category to its limit, and a list of what was spent. Everything else (spent, remaining, over) is worked out from those two when it is asked.
--- hint
`remaining` can read `self.limits[category]` directly: a missing key raises `KeyError` by itself. `over` loops over the categories that have limits and keeps those with a negative remaining.
--- hint
`format_cents` only handles amounts of zero or more, so in `summary` format `-left` when the budget is over, and say `over` instead of `left`.
--- check case | Spending against limits
(lambda b: (b.set_limit("food", 10000), b.set_limit("fun", 2000), b.spend(4500, "food"), b.spend(2500, "fun"), b.spent("food"), b.remaining("fun"), b.over())[-3:])(Budget())
=> (4500, -500, ["fun"])
--- check case | Summaries: under, over and exactly on the limit
(lambda b: (b.set_limit("food", 10000), b.set_limit("rent", 50000), b.set_limit("fun", 2000), b.spend(12000, "food"), b.spend(50000, "rent"), [b.summary(c) for c in ("food", "rent", "fun")])[-1])(Budget())
=> ["food: 120.00 of 100.00 (20.00 over)", "rent: 500.00 of 500.00 (0.00 left)", "fun: 0.00 of 20.00 (20.00 left)"]
--- check test | Bad amounts and unknown categories are refused, and nothing is recorded
(lambda b: (b.set_limit("food", 100), raises(ValueError, lambda: b.spend(0, "food")), raises(KeyError, lambda: b.spend(50, "fun")), raises(ValueError, lambda: b.set_limit("fun", 0)), raises(KeyError, lambda: b.remaining("fun")), b.spent("food"), b.over()))(Budget()) == (None, True, True, True, True, 0, [])
--- check test | A new limit replaces the old one
(lambda b: (b.set_limit("food", 100), b.spend(150, "food"), b.over(), b.set_limit("food", 200), b.over(), b.remaining("food"))[2:])(Budget()) == (["food"], None, [], 50)

=== pyp-03 | Expense tracker 3: reports
--- teach
Last lesson the ledger learned to store expenses and total them. Now comes layer 3: a report that answers "where did the money go?" at a glance. Here is what the finished report looks like for four expenses:

```
rent          1,200.00
food             57.50
transport         3.20
----------------------
TOTAL         1,260.70
```

Making it takes three steps, and each is a standard technique.

### Step 1: aggregate

To **aggregate** means to gather things into groups and combine each group into one number. Here: group the expenses by category, and [[sum each group|aggregate]]. A plain dict with `get` does it in one pass through the list:

```python
totals = {}
for e in expenses:
    totals[e.category] = totals.get(e.category, 0) + e.amount
```

`totals.get(e.category, 0)` means "the total so far for this category, or 0 if this is the first time we have seen it". For expenses of 1250 food, 320 bus and 4500 food, `totals` ends up as `{'food': 5750, 'bus': 320}`.

### Step 2: order

Biggest spending first is the most useful order. For equal totals, order by name, so the report comes out [[the same every time|deterministic]]. A report whose rows shuffle around is hard to compare from month to month.

One sort with a **tuple key** does both. `totals.items()` gives `(category, total)` pairs; call each pair `kv`, for "key and value":

```python
rows = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
```

The key for each pair is a tuple of two things, and Python [[compares tuples|tuple-key]] one item at a time:

- `-kv[1]` is minus the total. `sorted` puts small keys first, and flipping the sign turns the biggest total into the smallest key. So the biggest spending comes first.
- `kv[0]` is the category name. It only matters when two totals are equal, and then names go in alphabetical order.

```python
totals = {"food": 5750, "art": 500, "zoo": 500, "rent": 120000}
sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
# [('rent', 120000), ('food', 5750), ('art', 500), ('zoo', 500)]
```

### Step 3: lay out

**Fixed-width columns** make numbers [[line up|fixed-width]], so the eye can scan them. You met the format codes in the intermediate course. `:<12` means "left-align in 12 characters"; `:>10` means "right-align in 10 characters". Names go on the left; amounts go on the right, so their decimal points line up.

```python
f"{'food':<12}{'57.50':>10}"     # 'food             57.50'
```

That row is 12 + 10 = 22 characters wide. So the dividing rule under the rows is `"-" * 22`: a string times a number repeats it, giving 22 dashes.

### Return lines, do not print them

`report()` returns a list of strings. Printing is the run loop's job. A method that returns data can be tested by comparing lists, reused in a different interface (a web page, an email), and combined with other output. A method that prints [[can only be looked at|return-not-print]].

### Handle the empty case on purpose

A report on no data should say so, instead of printing a header, a rule and a total of zero, or crashing on `max()` of an empty list. Deciding what happens with no data at all is one of the [[edge cases|edge-cases]] every program needs.

**Watch out:** forgetting ties. Sorting only by amount makes the order of equal totals depend on the order they were added, and your tests (and your users) see it change.

::: context aggregate Aggregating is everywhere
"Aggregate" comes from Latin for "gathering into a flock". Every dashboard you have seen does it: steps per day, sales per month, fuel used per engine burn. Databases have it built in; in the SQL course you meet `GROUP BY`, which does exactly what this loop does. The loop needs only one pass through the list, however many categories there are, because the dict finds each running total straight away.
:::

::: context deterministic Same input, same output
A program is **deterministic** when the same input always gives exactly the same output. It is what makes tests possible: a check that compares the report with an expected list only works if the report cannot come out in a different order next time. Flight software is held to this very strictly, because engineers must be able to replay a recorded flight in simulation and see the computer make exactly the same decisions it made in the air.
:::

::: context tuple-key How the tuple keys sort
Tuples are compared item by item, like words in a dictionary. First the numbers are compared. Only when those are equal does Python look at the names. Here are the keys for the example, smallest first, which is the order `sorted` returns.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2">
    <rect x="20" y="10" width="220" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="20" y="50" width="220" height="30" rx="4"/>
    <rect x="20" y="90" width="220" height="30" rx="4"/><rect x="20" y="130" width="220" height="30" rx="4"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="32" y="30">(-120000, 'rent')</text><text x="32" y="70">(-5750, 'food')</text>
    <text x="32" y="110">(-500, 'art')</text><text x="32" y="150">(-500, 'zoo')</text>
  </g>
  <line x1="262" y1="14" x2="262" y2="150" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="262,158 257,148 267,148" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="274" y="60" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">smallest key</text>
  <text x="274" y="76" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">first</text>
  <text x="274" y="128" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">tie: art</text>
  <text x="274" y="143" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">before zoo</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context fixed-width Columns need a fixed-width font
Fixed-width columns work because terminals use a **monospace** font, where every character, from `i` to `W`, takes the same width. Twelve characters are then always the same distance across the screen, whatever they are. The first column is padded to 12, the second to 10.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="40" width="192" height="34" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="212" y="40" width="128" height="34" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="28" y="62" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44">food</text>
  <text x="332" y="62" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">57.50</text>
  <text x="116" y="28" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">:&lt;12 left-aligned</text>
  <text x="276" y="28" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">:&gt;10 right-aligned</text>
  <text x="116" y="94" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">12 characters</text>
  <text x="276" y="94" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">10 characters</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context return-not-print The pure core, again
This is the design lesson from the expert course at work. `report()` is part of the pure core: data in, data out, no side effects. The list of lines it returns could be printed in a terminal, joined into an email, or turned into rows of a web page, and a test can compare it with an expected list in one line. If it printed instead, a test would have to capture the screen to see what happened.
:::

::: context edge-cases Edge cases
An **edge case** is an input at the very edge of what is allowed: no data at all, exactly one item, the largest possible number, two things that are exactly equal. Programs are usually written with the ordinary middle case in mind, so the edges are where bugs hide. Good testers list the edge cases first. The empty ledger and the tie between equal totals are the two edge cases of this report, and each has its own check.
:::
--- task
Add two methods to `Ledger`:

- `by_category()` returns a dict from each category to its total in cents.
- `report()` returns a list of lines:
  - one line per category, highest total first and ties in alphabetical order, as the category left-aligned in 12 characters then `format_cents(total)` right-aligned in 10;
  - then a line of 22 `-` characters;
  - then `TOTAL` laid out the same way, with the total of everything.

An empty ledger's report is `["no expenses"]`.
--- hint
`by_category` is the `totals.get(category, 0) + amount` loop from step 1, over `self.expenses`.
--- hint
In `report`, sort `self.by_category().items()` with `key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0])`, and format each row with `f"{category:<12}{format_cents(total):>10}"`.
--- hint
Check for an empty ledger first and return `["no expenses"]`. After the rows, append `"-" * 22` and the TOTAL row, `f"{'TOTAL':<12}{format_cents(self.total()):>10}"`.
--- starter
# Expense tracker, step 2: a ledger that stores expenses.
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


def format_cents(cents):
    """1250 -> "12.50", 123456 -> "1,234.56"."""
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def categories(self):
        return sorted({e.category for e in self.expenses})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.expenses)
--- solution
# Expense tracker, step 3: reports.
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


def format_cents(cents):
    """1250 -> "12.50", 123456 -> "1,234.56"."""
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def categories(self):
        return sorted({e.category for e in self.expenses})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.expenses)

    def by_category(self):
        totals = {}
        for e in self.expenses:
            totals[e.category] = totals.get(e.category, 0) + e.amount
        return totals

    def report(self):
        if not self.expenses:
            return ["no expenses"]
        rows = sorted(self.by_category().items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        lines = [f"{category:<12}{format_cents(total):>10}" for category, total in rows]
        lines.append("-" * 22)
        lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<12}{format_cents(self.total()):>10}")
        return lines
--- check case | by_category sums each category
(lambda l: (l.add(1250, "food"), l.add(320, "bus"), l.add(4500, "food"), l.by_category())[-1])(Ledger())
=> {"food": 5750, "bus": 320}
--- check case | The report, biggest first
(lambda l: (l.add(1250, "food"), l.add(320, "transport"), l.add(4500, "food"), l.add(120000, "rent"), l.report())[-1])(Ledger())
=> ["rent          1,200.00", "food             57.50", "transport         3.20", "----------------------", "TOTAL         1,260.70"]
--- check case | Equal totals are in alphabetical order
(lambda l: (l.add(500, "zoo"), l.add(500, "art"), l.add(500, "music"), l.report())[-1][:3])(Ledger())
=> ["art               5.00", "music             5.00", "zoo               5.00"]
--- check case | An empty report
Ledger().report()
=> ["no expenses"]

+++ practice | Who won the most games
--- task
A games club writes down the winner of every game, in a list of names. Write `leaderboard(winners)`, which returns the lines of a table:

- one line per player: the name left-aligned in 10 characters, then the number of wins right-aligned in 3;
- most wins first, and players with the same number of wins in alphabetical order.

For `["sam", "ada", "sam", "lin", "ada", "sam"]` the lines are `"sam         3"`, `"ada         2"` and `"lin         1"`. With no games at all, return `["no games"]`.
--- starter
def leaderboard(winners):
    return [f"{name:<10}{winners.count(name):>3}" for name in winners]
--- solution
def leaderboard(winners):
    if not winners:
        return ["no games"]
    wins = {}
    for name in winners:
        wins[name] = wins.get(name, 0) + 1
    ranked = sorted(wins.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return [f"{name:<10}{count:>3}" for name, count in ranked]
--- hint
Three steps, as in the lesson: count the wins of each player in a dict, sort the pairs, then lay out each pair as a line.
--- hint
Counting is the `get` pattern with 1 instead of an amount. Sort on the tuple `(-wins, name)` so the most wins come first and ties go A to Z.
--- check case | Most wins first
