leaderboard(["sam", "ada", "sam", "lin", "ada", "sam"])
=> ["sam         3", "ada         2", "lin         1"]
--- check case | Ties are in alphabetical order
leaderboard(["zoe", "bo", "max", "bo", "zoe", "max"])
=> ["bo          2", "max         2", "zoe         2"]
--- check case | No games
leaderboard([])
=> ["no games"]

+++ practice | The top few categories
--- task
Write `top_categories(expenses, n)`. `expenses` is a list of `(category, cents)` pairs, and a category can appear many times. Return a list of the `n` categories with the biggest totals, as `(category, total)` pairs:

- biggest total first, and equal totals in alphabetical order;
- if there are fewer than `n` categories, return all of them;
- if `n` is 0, or there are no expenses, return `[]`.

For example, `top_categories([("food", 1250), ("bus", 320), ("food", 4500), ("rent", 90000)], 2)` is `[("rent", 90000), ("food", 5750)]`.
--- starter
def top_categories(expenses, n):
    return sorted(expenses, key=lambda pair: -pair[1])[:n]
--- solution
def top_categories(expenses, n):
    totals = {}
    for category, cents in expenses:
        totals[category] = totals.get(category, 0) + cents
    ranked = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return ranked[:n]
--- hint
Sorting the raw pairs is not enough: `food` appears twice and must be added up first. Aggregate into a dict, then sort its items.
--- hint
A slice never fails for being too long: `ranked[:5]` of a 3-item list is the whole list, and `ranked[:0]` is `[]`.
--- check case | The top two
top_categories([("food", 1250), ("bus", 320), ("food", 4500), ("rent", 90000)], 2)
=> [("rent", 90000), ("food", 5750)]
--- check case | Ties, and n bigger than the number of categories
top_categories([("zoo", 500), ("art", 500), ("bus", 100)], 10)
=> [("art", 500), ("zoo", 500), ("bus", 100)]
--- check test | n of 0, and no expenses
top_categories([("food", 100)], 0) == [] and top_categories([], 3) == []

+++ practice | A report with each category's share
--- task
Write `share_report(expenses)`, where `expenses` is a list of `(category, cents)` pairs. It returns the lines of a report that also shows what share of all the spending each category is. The starter has `format_cents` from the last lesson.

- One line per category, biggest total first and ties in alphabetical order: the category left-aligned in 12 characters, `format_cents(total)` right-aligned in 10, then the share as a percentage with one decimal, right-aligned in 7, then `%`.
- Then a line of 30 `-` characters.
- Then `TOTAL` laid out the same way, with the total of everything and `100.0%`.

For `[("rent", 120000), ("food", 5750), ("bus", 320)]` the first line is `"rent          1,200.00   95.2%"` (the share is 120000 × 100 ÷ 126070, about 95.18). With no expenses, return `["no expenses"]`.
--- starter
def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


def share_report(expenses):
    return []
--- solution
def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


def share_report(expenses):
    if not expenses:
        return ["no expenses"]
    totals = {}
    for category, cents in expenses:
        totals[category] = totals.get(category, 0) + cents
    grand = sum(totals.values())
    lines = []
    for category, cents in sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0])):
        share = cents * 100 / grand
        lines.append(f"{category:<12}{format_cents(cents):>10}{share:>7.1f}%")
    lines.append("-" * 30)
    lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<12}{format_cents(grand):>10}{100:>7.1f}%")
    return lines
--- hint
Aggregate first, then work out the grand total once with `sum(totals.values())`. Each share is `cents * 100 / grand`.
--- hint
The format code `:>7.1f` right-aligns a number in 7 characters with one digit after the dot. The `%` goes straight after it, as plain text in the f-string.
--- check case | The full report
share_report([("rent", 120000), ("food", 1250), ("bus", 320), ("food", 4500)])
=> ["rent          1,200.00   95.2%", "food             57.50    4.6%", "bus               3.20    0.3%", "------------------------------", "TOTAL         1,260.70  100.0%"]
--- check case | One category is all of it
share_report([("fun", 999)])
=> ["fun               9.99  100.0%", "------------------------------", "TOTAL             9.99  100.0%"]
--- check case | Equal shares are in alphabetical order
share_report([("zoo", 100), ("art", 100)])[:2]
=> ["art               1.00   50.0%", "zoo               1.00   50.0%"]
--- check case | No expenses
share_report([])
=> ["no expenses"]

+++ practice | Ranks with ties
--- task
Write `ranking(points)`, where `points` is a dict from each player to their score. It returns lines like `"1. ada 30"`: the rank, a dot and a space, the name, a space and the score.

- Highest score first; equal scores in alphabetical order.
- Players with equal scores share a rank, and the next rank skips the places they used. For `{"ada": 30, "bo": 20, "cy": 20, "di": 5}` the lines are `"1. ada 30"`, `"2. bo 20"`, `"2. cy 20"` and `"4. di 5"`.
- Scores can be zero or negative. No players gives `[]`.
--- starter
def ranking(points):
    ranked = sorted(points.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return [f"{i + 1}. {name} {score}" for i, (name, score) in enumerate(ranked)]
--- solution
def ranking(points):
    ranked = sorted(points.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    lines = []
    for i, (name, score) in enumerate(ranked):
        if i == 0 or score != ranked[i - 1][1]:
            rank = i + 1
        lines.append(f"{rank}. {name} {score}")
    return lines
--- hint
Sort first. Then walk the sorted list with `enumerate`: a player's rank is their position plus one, unless their score equals the score just before theirs.
--- hint
Keep a `rank` variable. Only change it (to `i + 1`) when the score differs from the previous player's score; otherwise keep the rank you already have.
--- check case | A tie in the middle
ranking({"ada": 30, "bo": 20, "cy": 20, "di": 5})
=> ["1. ada 30", "2. bo 20", "2. cy 20", "4. di 5"]
--- check case | Everyone tied
ranking({"zed": 7, "amy": 7, "kim": 7})
=> ["1. amy 7", "1. kim 7", "1. zed 7"]
--- check case | A tie at the top, zero and negative scores
ranking({"a": 0, "b": -3, "c": 9, "d": 9, "e": 0})
=> ["1. c 9", "1. d 9", "3. a 0", "3. e 0", "5. b -3"]
--- check test | One player, and nobody
ranking({"solo": 1}) == ["1. solo 1"] and ranking({}) == []

+++ practice | Debug: ties back to front
--- task
`report(totals)` takes a dict from each category to its total in cents, and should return one line per category: the category left-aligned in 12 characters, then the cents right-aligned in 10. Biggest totals come first, and equal totals should be in alphabetical order.

The biggest totals do come first. But equal totals come out in reverse alphabetical order: `zoo` before `art`. Fix it.
--- starter
def report(totals):
    rows = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (kv[1], kv[0]), reverse=True)
    return [f"{name:<12}{cents:>10}" for name, cents in rows]
--- solution
def report(totals):
    rows = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
    return [f"{name:<12}{cents:>10}" for name, cents in rows]
--- hint
`reverse=True` turns the *whole* key around, the name included. It cannot reverse only the first part of the tuple.
--- hint
Reverse just the number, by sorting on minus the total, and leave the name as it is, without `reverse`.
--- check case | Equal totals A to Z
report({"zoo": 500, "art": 500, "music": 500})
=> ["art                500", "music              500", "zoo                500"]
--- check case | Biggest first, ties A to Z
report({"food": 5750, "zoo": 320, "bus": 320, "rent": 120000})
=> ["rent            120000", "food              5750", "bus                320", "zoo                320"]
--- check case | Nothing to report
report({})
=> []

+++ practice | A sensor summary table
--- task
A probe logs readings as `(sensor, value)` pairs, where each value is a whole number and may be negative. Write `summary(readings)`, which returns the lines of a table:

- A header line: `sensor` left-aligned in 10 characters, then `n` right-aligned in 4, `min` and `max` each right-aligned in 7, and `avg` right-aligned in 8.
- One line per sensor, in alphabetical order: the name (left, 10), how many readings it has (right, 4), its smallest and largest value (right, 7 each) and the average with one decimal (right, 8).

For `[("temp", 20), ("temp", 25), ("pressure", 101), ("temp", 22), ("pressure", 99)]` the lines are:

```
sensor       n    min    max     avg
pressure     2     99    101   100.0
temp         3     20     25    22.3
```

With no readings, return `["no readings"]`.
--- starter
def summary(readings):
    return ["no readings"]
--- solution
def summary(readings):
    if not readings:
        return ["no readings"]
    by_sensor = {}
    for sensor, value in readings:
        by_sensor.setdefault(sensor, []).append(value)
    lines = [f"{'sensor':<10}{'n':>4}{'min':>7}{'max':>7}{'avg':>8}"]
    for sensor in sorted(by_sensor):
        values = by_sensor[sensor]
        average = sum(values) / len(values)
        lines.append(f"{sensor:<10}{len(values):>4}{min(values):>7}{max(values):>7}{average:>8.1f}")
    return lines
--- hint
This time each group needs more than a sum: keep every value, so you can take the count, the smallest, the largest and the average. A dict from sensor to a list of its values does that.
--- hint
`by_sensor.setdefault(sensor, []).append(value)` adds a value to the sensor's list, making the list the first time. Then loop over `sorted(by_sensor)` and use `len`, `min`, `max` and `sum(values) / len(values)`.
--- check case | Two sensors
summary([("temp", 20), ("temp", 25), ("pressure", 101), ("temp", 22), ("pressure", 99)])
=> ["sensor       n    min    max     avg", "pressure     2     99    101   100.0", "temp         3     20     25    22.3"]
--- check case | One reading, and negative values
summary([("cabin", -3), ("airlock", -40), ("airlock", -12)])
=> ["sensor       n    min    max     avg", "airlock      2    -40    -12   -26.0", "cabin        1     -3     -3    -3.0"]
--- check case | No readings
summary([])
=> ["no readings"]

=== pyp-04 | Expense tracker 4: the program
--- teach
Last lesson the ledger learned to write its own report. Every layer exists now, but nothing joins them together yet. This lesson writes layer 4, the loop that makes them a program: read commands, run them, and collect what happened.

```python
def run(lines):
    ledger = Ledger()
    out = []
    for line in lines:
        ...
    return out
```

`run` gets a list of command lines and gives back a list of output lines. The `...` is what you fill in, one step at a time.

### Step 1: skip blank lines and comments

First, `line.strip()` removes spaces from both ends. Then a blank line, or a line starting with `#`, is skipped with `continue` before it ever reaches the parser. So an input file can be laid out with blank lines and [[annotated for humans|comment-lines]]:

```
# September
add 12.50 food lunch with Sam

add 3.20 transport bus
```

### Step 2: the error boundary

Picture a shop where every worker at the back can hand a problem up to the front desk, but only the front desk talks to customers. The layers below the loop work that way. Each one raises `ValueError` when something is wrong, and knows nothing about screens or users.

The run loop is the **error boundary**: the [[one place that reports problems|error-boundary]]. It catches the error, turns it into an `error: ...` line, and carries on with the next command. One typo must never lose the rest of someone's input.

```python fragment
try:
    command = parse_command(line)
except ValueError as err:
    out.append(f"error: {err}")
    continue
```

`f"error: {err}"` puts the exception's message into the line, so `add abc food` becomes `error: bad amount: abc`.

Catch only what you expect: `ValueError` from parsing. An unexpected `TypeError` is a bug in *your* code, and [[hiding it|narrow-except]] would make it much harder to find.

### Step 3: do each command

Now `command` is one of the three dicts from lesson 1. Branch on `command["cmd"]`:

- `add`: store it in the ledger, and produce `added 12.50 to food`.
- `total`: the label is `total` when the category is `None`, or `total food` when there is one, then a colon and the amount.
- `report`: the report is already a list of lines, so add them all to `out` with `out.extend(...)`. That [[adds each line separately|extend-vs-append]], where `append` would add the whole list as one item.

### Step 4: the thin shell

`run(lines)` returns output instead of printing, for the same reason as `report()`: it can be tested with a list of lines in and a list of lines out, with no keyboard involved. The real program is then a thin shell around it:

```python
if __name__ == "__main__":
    for line in run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines()):
        print(line)
```

You met both parts in the expert course. `sys.stdin.read()` reads everything the user typed or piped in (it needs `import sys` at the top), and `.splitlines()` cuts it into a list of lines. The [[name guard|name-guard]] means the loop runs when the file is executed as a program, but not when another file imports its functions. In a real terminal you could [[feed it a whole file|redirect]] of commands.

### Look back

That is the whole program: four layers, each small, each testable, each with one job. Look back at how little each lesson needed to change the ones before it. That is [[what a good structure buys you|structure-pays]].

**Watch out:** forgetting the `continue` after appending the error line. The loop then goes on to use `command`, which still holds the *previous* line's command. A typo after `add 5 food` would quietly add another 5 to food. On the very first line there is no previous command, and the program crashes.

::: context comment-lines Why input files allow comments
Many files that programs read allow comment lines that start with `#`: Python code itself, Bash scripts, `.gitignore` files and lots of settings files. The program skips them, but people reading the file see the notes. A month of expenses kept in a text file with `# September` and `# October` headings is easier to check by eye, and the program does not care.
:::

::: context error-boundary Errors travel up to one place
When `to_cents` raises, Python leaves `to_cents`, then leaves `parse_command`, looking for a matching `except`. The first one it finds is in the run loop. That is the only layer that turns an error into output.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 190" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="14" width="150" height="40" rx="8" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="95" y="39" font-size="13" font-weight="700" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">run loop</text>
  <rect x="20" y="78" width="150" height="40" rx="8" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="95" y="103" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">parse_command</text>
  <rect x="20" y="142" width="150" height="40" rx="8" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="95" y="167" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">to_cents</text>
  <line x1="95" y1="142" x2="95" y2="126" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="95,119 90,128 100,128" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <line x1="95" y1="78" x2="95" y2="62" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="95,55 90,64 100,64" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="104" y="136" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">ValueError</text>
  <text x="104" y="72" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">ValueError</text>
  <line x1="170" y1="34" x2="200" y2="34" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="207,34 199,30 199,38" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="212" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">error: bad</text>
  <text x="212" y="46" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">amount: abc</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context narrow-except Why not catch everything
It is tempting to write `except Exception:` so that nothing can ever crash the loop. But then a typo in your own code, like a misspelled variable, turns into a polite `error:` line and the program carries on with wrong results. Nobody finds out until the totals are wrong. A crash with a traceback is annoying, but it points straight at the bug. Catch the errors you planned for, and let the rest be loud.
:::

::: context extend-vs-append append adds one item, extend adds many
`out.append(x)` adds `x` as one new item, even when `x` is a list. `out.extend(x)` goes through `x` and adds each of its items separately.

```python
out = ["x"]; out.append(["a", "b"])    # ['x', ['a', 'b']]
out = ["x"]; out.extend(["a", "b"])    # ['x', 'a', 'b']
```

With `append`, the whole report would sit inside `out` as one list, and printing it would show brackets and quotes instead of five neat lines.
:::

::: context name-guard The guard, once more
When you run the file, Python sets `__name__` to `"__main__"`, so the guarded block runs and reads standard input. When a test file imports this one to call `run` or `parse_command`, `__name__` is the module's own name instead, and the block is skipped. Without the guard, importing the file would sit waiting for input that never comes.
:::

::: context redirect Feeding the program a file
In the Terminal course you met redirection. If this program were saved as `tracker.py` and your commands as `september.txt`, the command `python3 tracker.py < september.txt` sends the file into standard input, as if you had typed every line. The `<` means "take input from this file". A pipe works too: `cat september.txt | python3 tracker.py`. In this editor, the Input box under your code plays the part of the file.
:::

::: context structure-pays Small changes are the sign of a good design
Count what changed from lesson to lesson. Lesson 2 added a class without touching the parser. Lesson 3 added two methods without touching anything else. Lesson 4 added one function and four lines of shell. When adding a feature means changing only new code, a design is working. When every new feature means editing old code all over the program, bugs creep in. The next two projects follow the same shape, and in the capstones you will design it yourself.
:::
--- task
Write `run(lines)`, returning the list of output lines:

- Skip blank lines and lines starting with `#`.
- A line that fails to parse produces `"error: <message>"`, and processing continues with the next line.
- `add` stores the expense and produces `"added 12.50 to food"`.
- `total` produces `"total: <amount>"`; `total food` produces `"total food: <amount>"`, with the amount formatted by `format_cents`.
- `report` produces the report lines.

Then add the entry point under `if __name__ == "__main__":` that runs the lines of standard input and prints each output line. (The Input box already holds a September of commands.)
--- hint
Inside the loop: strip the line, skip blanks and comments, then `try: command = parse_command(line)` / `except ValueError as err:` append the error line and `continue`.
--- hint
Then branch on `command["cmd"]`. For `add`, call `ledger.add(...)` with the three values from the dict. For `total`, the label is `"total"` or `f"total {category}"`. For `report`, `out.extend(ledger.report())`.
--- hint
You need `import sys` at the top for `sys.stdin.read().splitlines()`, and the entry point prints each line that `run` returns.
--- starter
# Expense tracker, step 3: reports.
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


def format_cents(cents):
    """1250 -> "12.50", 123456 -> "1,234.56"."""
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def categories(self):
        return sorted({e.category for e in self.expenses})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.expenses)

    def by_category(self):
        totals = {}
        for e in self.expenses:
            totals[e.category] = totals.get(e.category, 0) + e.amount
        return totals

    def report(self):
        if not self.expenses:
            return ["no expenses"]
        rows = sorted(self.by_category().items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        lines = [f"{category:<12}{format_cents(total):>10}" for category, total in rows]
        lines.append("-" * 22)
        lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<12}{format_cents(self.total()):>10}")
        return lines
--- solution
# Expense tracker, step 4: the program.
import sys
from dataclasses import dataclass


def to_cents(text):
    """Turn "12.50" into 1250. Only positive amounts with at most 2 decimals."""
    whole, dot, frac = text.partition(".")
    if not whole.isdigit() or (dot and not frac.isdigit()) or len(frac) > 2:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount: {text}")
    cents = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if cents <= 0:
        raise ValueError(f"amount must be positive: {text}")
    return cents


def parse_command(line):
    """Turn one command line into a dict, or raise ValueError saying what is wrong."""
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "add":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: add <amount> <category> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "add", "amount": to_cents(args[0]), "category": args[1].lower(), "note": " ".join(args[2:])}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [category]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "category": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


@dataclass
class Expense:
    amount: int
    category: str
    note: str = ""


def format_cents(cents):
    """1250 -> "12.50", 123456 -> "1,234.56"."""
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Ledger:
    def __init__(self):
        self.expenses = []

    def add(self, amount, category, note=""):
        expense = Expense(amount, category, note)
        self.expenses.append(expense)
        return expense

    def total(self, category=None):
        return sum(e.amount for e in self.expenses if category is None or e.category == category)

    def categories(self):
        return sorted({e.category for e in self.expenses})

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.expenses)

    def by_category(self):
        totals = {}
        for e in self.expenses:
            totals[e.category] = totals.get(e.category, 0) + e.amount
        return totals

    def report(self):
        if not self.expenses:
            return ["no expenses"]
        rows = sorted(self.by_category().items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        lines = [f"{category:<12}{format_cents(total):>10}" for category, total in rows]
        lines.append("-" * 22)
        lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<12}{format_cents(self.total()):>10}")
        return lines


def run(lines):
    """Run every command line; return the output lines. Never crashes on bad input."""
    ledger = Ledger()
    out = []
    for line in lines:
        line = line.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            command = parse_command(line)
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "add":
            ledger.add(command["amount"], command["category"], command["note"])
            out.append(f"added {format_cents(command['amount'])} to {command['category']}")
        elif command["cmd"] == "total":
            category = command["category"]
            label = "total" if category is None else f"total {category}"
            out.append(f"{label}: {format_cents(ledger.total(category))}")
        else:
            out.extend(ledger.report())
    return out


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for output_line in run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines()):
        print(output_line)
--- stdin
# September
add 12.50 food lunch with Sam
add 3.20 transport bus
add 45 food groceries
add 1200 rent

add abc food
total
total food
spend 5 fun
report
--- check output | The program runs a whole session
added 12.50 to food
added 3.20 to transport
added 45.00 to food
added 1,200.00 to rent
error: bad amount: abc
total: 1,260.70
total food: 57.50
error: unknown command: spend
rent          1,200.00
food             57.50
transport         3.20
----------------------
TOTAL         1,260.70
--- check case | run returns the output lines
run(["add 2 fun", "total fun", "total"])
=> ["added 2.00 to fun", "total fun: 2.00", "total: 2.00"]
--- check case | Errors do not stop the run
run(["add", "add 1.234 x", "report"])
=> ["error: usage: add <amount> <category> [note]", "error: bad amount: 1.234", "no expenses"]
--- check case | Nothing in, nothing out
run(["", "# just a comment"])
=> []

+++ practice | A counter you can talk to
--- task
The starter has `parse(line)`, a finished parser for three commands: `add <number>`, `sub <number>` and `show`. It returns `{"cmd": "add", "n": 5}`, `{"cmd": "sub", "n": 2}` or `{"cmd": "show"}`, and raises `ValueError` for anything else.

Write `run(lines)`, which returns the list of output lines. A counter starts at 0.

- Skip blank lines and lines starting with `#` (after stripping).
- A line that fails to parse produces `"error: <message>"`, and the run carries on with the next line.
- `add` and `sub` change the counter and produce no output.
- `show` produces `"value: <counter>"`, for example `"value: 7"`. The counter may go below zero.
--- starter
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd in ("add", "sub"):
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError(f"usage: {cmd} <number>")
        if not args[0].isdigit():
            raise ValueError(f"bad number: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": cmd, "n": int(args[0])}
    if cmd == "show":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: show")
        return {"cmd": "show"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


def run(lines):
    return [f"value: {parse(line)}" for line in lines]
--- solution
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd in ("add", "sub"):
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError(f"usage: {cmd} <number>")
        if not args[0].isdigit():
            raise ValueError(f"bad number: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": cmd, "n": int(args[0])}
    if cmd == "show":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: show")
        return {"cmd": "show"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


def run(lines):
    counter = 0
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            command = parse(line)
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "add":
            counter += command["n"]
        elif command["cmd"] == "sub":
            counter -= command["n"]
        else:
            out.append(f"value: {counter}")
    return out
--- hint
The shape is the lesson's `run`: a counter and an output list before the loop, then for each line strip, skip, parse inside `try`, and act.
--- hint
Put `continue` right after appending the error line, so a bad line never reaches the part that acts on `command`. Then branch on `command["cmd"]`.
--- check case | A short session
run(["add 5", "show", "sub 7", "show"])
=> ["value: 5", "value: -2"]
--- check case | Errors are reported and the run carries on
run(["add x", "add 3", "jump", "show now", "show"])
=> ["error: bad number: x", "error: unknown command: jump", "error: usage: show", "value: 3"]
--- check case | Blank lines and comments produce nothing
run(["", "   ", "# notes", "  # indented note"])
=> []

+++ practice | A vote from standard input
--- task
Write a program that counts votes for the next planet to visit. Each line of input is one vote: a single word, the planet's name, in any case.

Write `tally(lines)`, which returns the output lines:

- Blank lines are skipped.
- A line with more than one word is not a vote: it produces `"skipped line <n>: <the line, stripped>"`, where `n` counts every line from 1, blank ones included. Skipped lines come first, in the order they appear.
- Then one line per planet, `"<planet> <votes>"`, lowercased, most votes first and ties in alphabetical order.
- Then `"votes: <number of valid votes>"`.
- With no valid votes, the results part is just `"no votes"` (skipped lines still come before it).

Then add the entry point under `if __name__ == "__main__":` that runs `tally` on the lines of standard input and prints each output line. (The Input box holds some votes.)
--- starter
import sys


def tally(lines):
    return lines
--- solution
import sys


def tally(lines):
    out = []
    votes = {}
    for number, raw in enumerate(lines, start=1):
        words = raw.split()
        if not words:
            continue
        if len(words) > 1:
            out.append(f"skipped line {number}: {raw.strip()}")
            continue
        planet = words[0].lower()
        votes[planet] = votes.get(planet, 0) + 1
    if not votes:
        return out + ["no votes"]
    for planet, count in sorted(votes.items(), key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0])):
        out.append(f"{planet} {count}")
    out.append(f"votes: {sum(votes.values())}")
    return out


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for output_line in tally(sys.stdin.read().splitlines()):
        print(output_line)
--- stdin
Mars
venus

mars
Jupiter moons
MARS
venus
titan
--- hint
`tally` is a pure function: a list of lines in, a list of lines out. The entry point is the thin shell around it: read all of standard input, split it into lines, print what comes back.
--- hint
Use `enumerate(lines, start=1)` so the line numbers count from 1 and include the blank lines. Count votes in a dict, then sort its items on `(-votes, planet)`.
--- check output | The whole program, from standard input
skipped line 5: Jupiter moons
mars 3
venus 2
titan 1
votes: 6
--- check case | tally returns the lines
tally(["Io", "io", "  europa  ", "big red spot", ""])
=> ["skipped line 4: big red spot", "io 2", "europa 1", "votes: 3"]
--- check case | No valid votes
tally(["", "not a vote"])
=> ["skipped line 2: not a vote", "no votes"]

+++ practice | The storeroom program
--- task
The starter has the first three layers of a storeroom program:

- `parse(line)` understands `in <qty> <item>`, `out <qty> <item>`, `count <item>` and `report`, and raises `ValueError` with a message for anything else.
- `Stock` stores counts: `add(item, qty)`, `remove(item, qty)` (which raises `ValueError("only 3 bolts left")` when there are not enough), `count(item)` and `report()`, which returns a list of lines.

Write layer 4, `run(lines)`, returning the output lines:

- Skip blank lines and lines starting with `#`.
- `in` adds to the stock and produces `"stocked 5 bolts"`; `out` removes and produces `"took 2 bolts"`.
- `count` produces `"bolts: 3"`; `report` produces the report's lines.
- Any `ValueError`, from the parser **or** from the stock, produces `"error: <message>"`, and the run carries on.
--- starter
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd in ("in", "out"):
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError(f"usage: {cmd} <qty> <item>")
        if not args[0].isdigit() or int(args[0]) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad quantity: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": cmd, "qty": int(args[0]), "item": args[1].lower()}
    if cmd == "count":
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: count <item>")
        return {"cmd": "count", "item": args[0].lower()}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


class Stock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}

    def add(self, item, qty):
        self.counts[item] = self.counts.get(item, 0) + qty

    def remove(self, item, qty):
        have = self.counts.get(item, 0)
        if qty > have:
            raise ValueError(f"only {have} {item} left")
        self.counts[item] = have - qty

    def count(self, item):
        return self.counts.get(item, 0)

    def report(self):
        rows = [(item, n) for item, n in sorted(self.counts.items()) if n > 0]
        if not rows:
            return ["storeroom is empty"]
        return [f"{item:<10}{n:>5}" for item, n in rows]


def run(lines):
    pass
--- solution
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd in ("in", "out"):
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError(f"usage: {cmd} <qty> <item>")
        if not args[0].isdigit() or int(args[0]) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad quantity: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": cmd, "qty": int(args[0]), "item": args[1].lower()}
    if cmd == "count":
        if len(args) != 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: count <item>")
        return {"cmd": "count", "item": args[0].lower()}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


class Stock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}

    def add(self, item, qty):
        self.counts[item] = self.counts.get(item, 0) + qty

    def remove(self, item, qty):
        have = self.counts.get(item, 0)
        if qty > have:
            raise ValueError(f"only {have} {item} left")
        self.counts[item] = have - qty

    def count(self, item):
        return self.counts.get(item, 0)

    def report(self):
        rows = [(item, n) for item, n in sorted(self.counts.items()) if n > 0]
        if not rows:
            return ["storeroom is empty"]
        return [f"{item:<10}{n:>5}" for item, n in rows]


def execute(stock, command):
    """Do one parsed command; return its output lines. May raise ValueError."""
    if command["cmd"] == "in":
        stock.add(command["item"], command["qty"])
        return [f"stocked {command['qty']} {command['item']}"]
    if command["cmd"] == "out":
        stock.remove(command["item"], command["qty"])
        return [f"took {command['qty']} {command['item']}"]
    if command["cmd"] == "count":
        return [f"{command['item']}: {stock.count(command['item'])}"]
    return stock.report()


def run(lines):
    stock = Stock()
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            out.extend(execute(stock, parse(line)))
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
    return out
--- hint
This time a `ValueError` can come from two places: `parse`, and `stock.remove`. The error boundary has to cover both, so the whole "parse, then act" step goes inside the `try`.
--- hint
A small helper that takes the stock and one parsed command, does it, and returns a list of output lines keeps `run` short: `out.extend(execute(stock, parse(line)))` inside the `try`.
--- check case | A day in the storeroom
run(["in 10 bolts", "in 4 nuts", "out 3 bolts", "count bolts", "report"])
=> ["stocked 10 bolts", "stocked 4 nuts", "took 3 bolts", "bolts: 7", "bolts         7", "nuts          4"]
--- check case | Errors from the stock are caught too
run(["in 2 gears", "out 5 gears", "count gears", "out 0 gears", "fly"])
=> ["stocked 2 gears", "error: only 2 gears left", "gears: 2", "error: bad quantity: 0", "error: unknown command: fly"]
--- check case | Comments, and an empty storeroom
run(["# start of shift", "", "report", "count wire"])
=> ["storeroom is empty", "wire: 0"]

+++ practice | Line numbers and comments at the end of a line
--- task
A timesheet file has lines like `log 30 website` and `total`. The starter has `parse(text)`, which returns `{"cmd": "log", "minutes": 30, "project": "website"}` or `{"cmd": "total"}` and raises `ValueError` for anything else.

Write `run(lines)`, returning the output lines:

- Everything from a `#` to the end of a line is a comment and is ignored, so `"log 30 web # standup"` means `log 30 web`, and a line that is only a comment, or only spaces, is skipped.
- `log` adds minutes and produces nothing. `total` produces `"total: <hours>:<minutes>"`, with the minutes always two digits, for example `"total: 2:05"`.
- A line that fails to parse produces `"line <n>: <message>"`, where `n` counts every line of the file from 1, blank lines and comment lines included.
--- starter
def parse(text):
    words = text.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "log":
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: log <minutes> <project>")
        if not args[0].isdigit() or int(args[0]) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad minutes: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": "log", "minutes": int(args[0]), "project": args[1]}
    if cmd == "total":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: total")
        return {"cmd": "total"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


def run(lines):
    out = []
    minutes = 0
    for line in lines:
        if line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        command = parse(line)
        if command["cmd"] == "log":
            minutes += command["minutes"]
        else:
            out.append(f"total: {minutes // 60}:{minutes % 60}")
    return out
--- solution
def parse(text):
    words = text.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty command")
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "log":
        if len(args) != 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: log <minutes> <project>")
        if not args[0].isdigit() or int(args[0]) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"bad minutes: {args[0]}")
        return {"cmd": "log", "minutes": int(args[0]), "project": args[1]}
    if cmd == "total":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: total")
        return {"cmd": "total"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


def run(lines):
    out = []
    minutes = 0
    for number, raw in enumerate(lines, start=1):
        line = raw.partition("#")[0].strip()
        if not line:
            continue
        try:
            command = parse(line)
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"line {number}: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "log":
            minutes += command["minutes"]
        else:
            out.append(f"total: {minutes // 60}:{minutes % 60:02d}")
    return out
--- hint
Number the lines before you skip any of them: `enumerate(lines, start=1)` gives each line its real number, and skipping a line later does not change the numbers of the others.
--- hint
`raw.partition("#")[0]` is everything before the first `#` (or the whole line if there is none). Strip that, and skip it if nothing is left.
--- hint
Wrap `parse` in `try`/`except ValueError`, append `f"line {number}: {err}"`, and `continue`. In the total, `:02d` keeps the minutes at two digits.
--- check case | Comments anywhere, and the total
run(["# Monday", "log 30 web # standup", "   # just a note", "log 95 api", "total"])
=> ["total: 2:05"]
--- check case | Errors carry the real line number
run(["", "# header", "log 20", "log x web", "", "logg 5 web", "log 40 web", "total  # end of day"])
=> ["line 3: usage: log <minutes> <project>", "line 4: bad minutes: x", "line 6: unknown command: logg", "total: 0:40"]
--- check case | Nothing but blanks and comments
run(["", "   ", "#", "  # a"])
=> []
--- check case | Short totals keep two digits
run(["log 5 a", "total", "log 60 b", "total"])
=> ["total: 0:05", "total: 1:05"]

+++ practice | Debug: the report inside a list
--- task
A scoreboard program understands `score <points> <team>` and `board`. `run(lines)` should return a flat list of strings, one per output line: `"ok"` for every score, and for `board` each line of the board.

It runs, but after `board` the output holds one strange item: a whole list inside the list, like `["ok", ["red  5", "blue 3"]]`. Fix it so each board line is its own item.
--- starter
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if len(words) == 3 and words[0] == "score" and words[1].isdigit():
        return {"cmd": "score", "points": int(words[1]), "team": words[2]}
    if words == ["board"]:
        return {"cmd": "board"}
    raise ValueError(f"cannot read: {line}")


class Board:
    def __init__(self):
        self.points = {}

    def score(self, team, points):
        self.points[team] = self.points.get(team, 0) + points

    def lines(self):
        ranked = sorted(self.points.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        return [f"{team:<5}{points}" for team, points in ranked] or ["no scores"]


def run(lines):
    board = Board()
    out = []
    for line in lines:
        try:
            command = parse(line.strip())
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "score":
            board.score(command["team"], command["points"])
            out.append("ok")
        else:
            out.append(board.lines())
    return out
--- solution
def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    if len(words) == 3 and words[0] == "score" and words[1].isdigit():
        return {"cmd": "score", "points": int(words[1]), "team": words[2]}
    if words == ["board"]:
        return {"cmd": "board"}
    raise ValueError(f"cannot read: {line}")


class Board:
    def __init__(self):
        self.points = {}

    def score(self, team, points):
        self.points[team] = self.points.get(team, 0) + points

    def lines(self):
        ranked = sorted(self.points.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        return [f"{team:<5}{points}" for team, points in ranked] or ["no scores"]


def run(lines):
    board = Board()
    out = []
    for line in lines:
        try:
            command = parse(line.strip())
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "score":
            board.score(command["team"], command["points"])
            out.append("ok")
        else:
            out.extend(board.lines())
    return out
--- hint
`board.lines()` already returns a list. What does `append` do when you give it a list?
--- hint
`append` adds its argument as one item, even a whole list. `extend` adds each item of the list separately.
--- check case | Every board line is its own item
run(["score 3 blue", "score 5 red", "board"])
=> ["ok", "ok", "red  5", "blue 3"]
--- check case | An empty board, and a bad line
run(["board", "score x red"])
=> ["no scores", "error: cannot read: score x red"]
--- check test | Every item is a string
all(isinstance(item, str) for item in run(["score 1 a", "board", "board"]))

+++ practice | A help-desk queue
--- task
Write a program for a help desk, where people wait in line to be served. Design it in layers, as the tracker did: a parser, a queue that raises `ValueError` when an action is not allowed, and `run(lines)`, which returns the output lines. Command words are case-insensitive; names keep the case they were typed in.

- `join <name>` puts the person at the end of the line: `"Ada joined (position 2)"`, where the position counts from 1. Someone already waiting gives `"error: Ada is already waiting"`.
- `serve` takes the person at the front: `"serving Ada"`. With nobody waiting: `"error: nobody is waiting"`.
- `leave <name>` takes that person out of the line: `"Ada left"`. Someone not waiting gives `"error: Ada is not waiting"`.
- `status` gives `"waiting: Ada, Bo"`, in line order, or `"waiting: nobody"`.
- The wrong number of words gives `"error: usage: join <name>"`, `"error: usage: serve"`, `"error: usage: leave <name>"` or `"error: usage: status"`. Any other command gives `"error: unknown command: <word>"`.
- Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped.
--- starter
def run(lines):
    return []
--- solution
USAGE = {"join": "join <name>", "serve": "serve", "leave": "leave <name>", "status": "status"}
NEEDS_NAME = {"join", "leave"}


def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd not in USAGE:
        raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")
    if len(args) != (1 if cmd in NEEDS_NAME else 0):
        raise ValueError(f"usage: {USAGE[cmd]}")
    return {"cmd": cmd, "name": args[0] if args else None}


class Queue:
    def __init__(self):
        self.people = []

    def join(self, name):
        if name in self.people:
            raise ValueError(f"{name} is already waiting")
        self.people.append(name)
        return len(self.people)

    def serve(self):
        if not self.people:
            raise ValueError("nobody is waiting")
        return self.people.pop(0)

    def leave(self, name):
        if name not in self.people:
            raise ValueError(f"{name} is not waiting")
        self.people.remove(name)

    def status(self):
        return ", ".join(self.people) or "nobody"


def run(lines):
    queue = Queue()
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            command = parse(line)
            name = command["name"]
            if command["cmd"] == "join":
                out.append(f"{name} joined (position {queue.join(name)})")
            elif command["cmd"] == "serve":
                out.append(f"serving {queue.serve()}")
            elif command["cmd"] == "leave":
                queue.leave(name)
                out.append(f"{name} left")
            else:
                out.append(f"waiting: {queue.status()}")
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
    return out
--- hint
Keep the line of people in a list inside a small class. Each method checks its own rule and raises `ValueError` with the exact message when the action is not allowed; it never prints.
--- hint
The front of the line is the start of the list: `append` adds at the end, `pop(0)` takes the first item out and returns it, and `remove(name)` takes out the item equal to `name`. `", ".join(people) or "nobody"` gives the status text.
--- hint
The parser can check the number of words for every command in one place: a dict from each command to its usage text, and a set of the commands that need a name. Put both parsing and acting inside one `try`, so every `ValueError` becomes an `error:` line.
--- check case | Joining, serving and the status
run(["join Ada", "join Bo", "status", "serve", "status", "join Cy", "status"])
=> ["Ada joined (position 1)", "Bo joined (position 2)", "waiting: Ada, Bo", "serving Ada", "waiting: Bo", "Cy joined (position 2)", "waiting: Bo, Cy"]
--- check case | Leaving from the middle
run(["join Ada", "join Bo", "join Cy", "leave Bo", "status", "serve", "serve", "serve", "status"])
=> ["Ada joined (position 1)", "Bo joined (position 2)", "Cy joined (position 3)", "Bo left", "waiting: Ada, Cy", "serving Ada", "serving Cy", "error: nobody is waiting", "waiting: nobody"]
--- check case | Rules and usage errors
run(["JOIN Ada", "join Ada", "leave Bo", "join", "serve now", "leave", "status all", "dance", "# comment", ""])
=> ["Ada joined (position 1)", "error: Ada is already waiting", "error: Bo is not waiting", "error: usage: join <name>", "error: usage: serve", "error: usage: leave <name>", "error: usage: status", "error: unknown command: dance"]
--- check case | Names keep their case
run(["join ada", "join Ada", "status"])
=> ["ada joined (position 1)", "Ada joined (position 2)", "waiting: ada, Ada"]

=== pyp-05 | Markdown converter 1: inline formatting with regular expressions
--- teach
You finished the expense tracker: four small layers, each with one job and each tested on its own. The second project uses the same habits on a new kind of problem: turning text written in Markdown into a web page.

**Markdown** is a plain-text way of marking up writing. You have probably typed it without knowing its name: put stars around a word in a chat message and it comes out bold. [[READMEs on code sites|markdown-origin]], chat apps and note apps all use it.

A web browser does not read Markdown, though. It reads **HTML**, the language web pages are written in. HTML wraps text in **[[tags|html-tags]]**, words inside angle brackets: `<strong>` means "bold starts here" and `</strong>`, with a slash, means "bold ends here".

So the converter rewrites one into the other. Markdown on the left, HTML on the right:

```
**bold**        <strong>bold</strong>
*italic*        <em>italic</em>
`code`          <code>code</code>
[text](url)     <a href="url">text</a>
```

The third line is a **code span**: text between two backticks (the ` key, top left on most keyboards), shown in a code font.

Markdown has two kinds of formatting. **Inline** formatting happens *inside* a line, like making one word bold. **Block** structure is how lines group into headings, paragraphs, lists and code. This lesson does the inline kind. The next three lessons do the blocks.

### Step 1: escape the text first

Some characters mean something special in HTML. `<` starts a tag. So if your text says `a < b`, the browser tries to read `< b` as the start of a tag, and the text comes out broken.

**[[Escaping|why-escape]]** means swapping each special character for a safe code that the browser shows as the plain character. These codes are called **entities**: a short name between `&` and `;`.

| Character | Entity | Read it as |
|---|---|---|
| `&` | `&amp;` | ampersand |
| `<` | `&lt;` | less than |
| `>` | `&gt;` | greater than |
| `"` | `&quot;` | quote |

`str.replace` does each swap. The order matters: replace `&` **first**. Every entity you make contains an `&`, so if the `&` swap came later, it would turn each `&lt;` you had just made into `&amp;lt;`:

```python
"a < b".replace("<", "&lt;").replace("&", "&amp;")    # 'a &amp;lt; b'   wrong order
"a < b".replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;")    # 'a &lt; b'       right
```

### Step 2: describe a shape, not exact text

`replace` needs the exact text to look for. But `**bold**` could have any words between its stars. You need to describe a *shape*: "two stars, some text, two stars".

A **[[regular expression|regex-history]]** (regex for short) is a small language for describing shapes of text. Python runs them with the `re` module, which you import at the top of the file:

```python
import re

re.findall(r"\d+", "3 cats and 12 dogs")    # ['3', '12']
```

Read the pattern `\d+` piece by piece. `\d` means "one digit, 0 to 9". `+` means "one or more of the thing just before". So `\d+` is a run of digits, and `re.findall` returns every run it finds, in a list.

The `r` in front of the quote makes a **[[raw string|raw-strings]]**: Python leaves every backslash alone and hands it to `re` untouched. Write every pattern as a raw string.

### Step 3: find and replace, keeping a group

`re.sub(pattern, replacement, text)` finds every match of the pattern in the text and replaces it. ("sub" is short for substitute.)

```python
re.sub(r"\*\*(.+?)\*\*", r"<b>\1</b>", "a **b** c")    # 'a <b>b</b> c'
```

That pattern is a first try at bold. Piece by piece:

- `\*` is a real star. A bare `*` in a pattern means "repeat", so a star you want to *find* needs a backslash in front. `\*\*` is two real stars.
- `.` is any one character.
- `+?` is "one or more, but as few as possible". Step 4 explains the `?`.
- `( )` round brackets make a **group**: they capture the part of the match inside them. In the replacement, `\1` means "whatever group 1 captured".

So it finds two stars, some text, two stars, and the replacement keeps only the text, wrapped in tags.

Here are all the pieces this project uses:

| Pattern | Matches |
|---|---|
| `\*` | a real `*` (a bare `*` means "repeat") |
| `.` | any one character |
| `\S` | any one character that is not a space |
| `+` | one or more of the thing before |
| `*` | zero or more of the thing before |
| `?` | the thing before is optional (zero or one) |
| `+?`, `*?` | like `+` and `*`, but as **few** as possible |
| `( )` | a group; the replacement calls it `\1`, `\2` and so on |
| `[^\]]` | any one character except `]` |

### Step 4: greedy and lazy

Plain `+` is **greedy**: it grabs as much text as it can. In `"**a** and **b**"`, a greedy pattern runs from the first `**` all the way to the *last* one, and swallows ` and ` into the bold:

```python
re.sub(r"\*\*(.+)\*\*", r"<b>\1</b>", "**a** and **b**")     # '<b>a** and **b</b>'
re.sub(r"\*\*(.+?)\*\*", r"<b>\1</b>", "**a** and **b**")    # '<b>a</b> and <b>b</b>'
```

Adding `?` after `+` makes it **[[lazy|greedy-lazy]]**: it takes as little as it can, so it stops at the first closing `**`.

### Step 5: say exactly what counts as emphasis

`5 * 3 * 2` is arithmetic, not italic. Markdown agrees: emphasis needs a non-space character just inside each star. A loose pattern gets this wrong:

```python
re.sub(r"\*(.+?)\*", r"<em>\1</em>", "5 * 3 * 2")    # '5 <em> 3 </em> 2'   wrong
```

This pattern states the rule exactly:

```
\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*
```

Read it as: a star; then *either* a single non-space character, *or* a non-space, as little as possible of anything, and another non-space; then a star. The `|` bar means "or". The regex tries the left side first, which is why `*a* or *b*` gives two short matches instead of one long one.

`re.compile(pattern)` turns a pattern into a **pattern object** you can keep in a variable and reuse. Its `.sub` works like `re.sub`, minus the pattern argument:

```python
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", "5 * 3 * 2")     # '5 * 3 * 2'   left alone
EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", "*a* or *b*")    # '<em>a</em> or <em>b</em>'
```

A name in capitals, like `EM`, is Python's way of saying [[this is a constant|constants]]: set once at the top of the file and never changed.

Bold uses the same idea with two stars on each side: `\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*`. Run bold **before** italic. Otherwise the italic pattern takes the first star of `**bold**` as an opening star and makes a mess: `<em>*bold</em>*`.

### Step 6: links

A link is written `[text](url)`. Its pattern is

```
\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)
```

In pieces:

- `\[` is a real `[`. Square brackets mean something in a pattern too, so a real one needs a backslash.
- `([^\]]+)` is group 1, the link text: one or more characters that are not `]`. Square brackets with `^` just inside mean "any one character *except* these".
- `\]\(` is a real `]` and then a real `(`.
- `([^)\s]+)` is group 2, the URL: one or more characters that are not `)` and not a space (`\s` is any space character).
- `\)` is a real `)`.

The replacement puts group 2 in the tag and group 1 between the tags:

```python
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")
LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', "see [the docs](https://example.com)")
# 'see <a href="https://example.com">the docs</a>'
```

### Step 7: code spans are left alone

A code span is never formatted: backticks around `a*b*c` must give `a*b*c`, stars and all, not italics. It is still escaped, though: a `<` inside code must still become `&lt;`, so the browser shows it as a plain `<`. So cut the code spans out *before* formatting anything.

`re.split(pattern, text)` cuts the text wherever the pattern matches. Usually the matched parts are thrown away. But when the pattern is wrapped in a group, `re.split` **keeps** them in the list:

```python
re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", "use `a*b*c` here")    # ['use ', '`a*b*c`', ' here']
```

Inside the group, the pattern is a backtick, then one or more characters that are not backticks, then a backtick: one code span. The list always takes turns: ordinary text, code, ordinary text, code, and so on. So the code spans land at the **[[odd positions|odd-positions]]** of the list: 1, 3, 5.

That gives `inline` its shape. Loop over the pieces with `enumerate`. At an odd position (`i % 2 == 1`), cut off the two backticks with `part[1:-1]`, escape what is left and wrap it in `<code>` tags. At an even position, escape and then format: links, then bold, then italic. Finally join all the pieces with `"".join(...)`.

**Watch out:** formatting first and escaping after. Then the `<strong>` tags you just added get escaped too, and the page shows the text `<strong>` instead of bold. Always escape first, then add tags.

::: context markdown-origin Where Markdown came from
John Gruber created Markdown in 2004, working with Aaron Swartz. The goal was writing that reads well as plain text *and* converts cleanly to HTML. It spread everywhere: the `README.md` at the top of almost every project on GitHub is Markdown, and so are posts on many forums and chat apps. Because the first description left gaps, different converters disagreed on odd cases, so in 2014 a group wrote **CommonMark**, a precise specification with hundreds of examples. Your converter handles the common core of it.
:::

::: context html-tags Opening tag, text, closing tag
An HTML element is usually an opening tag, some content, and a closing tag with a slash. Tags can also carry **attributes**, extra settings written as `name="value"` inside the opening tag. A link's `href` attribute is the address it points to ("href" is short for hypertext reference).

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="96" height="34" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="68" y="52" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;strong&gt;</text>
  <rect x="122" y="30" width="96" height="34" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="170" y="52" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">bold</text>
  <rect x="224" y="30" width="110" height="34" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="279" y="52" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;/strong&gt;</text>
  <text x="68" y="88" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">opening tag</text>
  <text x="170" y="88" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">content</text>
  <text x="279" y="88" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">closing tag</text>
  <text x="279" y="106" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">note the slash</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context why-escape Escaping keeps a page safe
Escaping is not only about looks. Picture a website that shows visitors' comments without escaping them. Someone types a comment containing a `<script>` tag, and every browser that shows the page runs that person's program. This attack is called **cross-site scripting**, and it is one of the most common security bugs on the web. Escaping every piece of text before it goes into HTML shuts the door: the script shows up as harmless text.
:::

::: context regex-history An old idea in every toolbox
The mathematician Stephen Kleene described "regular expressions" in the 1950s, as a way of writing down which strings a simple machine accepts. In 1968 Ken Thompson built them into a text editor, and later tools such as `grep` (a Unix command that finds lines matching a pattern) made them part of every programmer's toolbox. Today nearly every language has them. The details differ a little between languages, but `\d`, `+`, `*`, `?`, groups and square brackets mean the same thing almost everywhere.
:::

::: context raw-strings Why the r matters
In an ordinary Python string, a backslash starts an escape: `"\n"` is a newline, and `"\b"` is a backspace character. In a regex, `\b` means something else (the edge of a word), so an ordinary string would quietly hand `re` the wrong thing. It is worse in a replacement: in an ordinary string `"\1"` becomes an invisible character with code 1, not "group 1". A raw string, `r"\1"`, keeps the backslash and the 1 exactly as typed. Newer versions of Python even warn you about backslashes they do not recognize in ordinary strings.
:::

::: context greedy-lazy Greedy grabs, lazy stops early
Both patterns start at the first `**`. The greedy one keeps going to the last `**` it can find. The lazy one stops at the first `**` that ends a match, then starts looking again after it.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 130" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="62" font-size="16" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44" font-family="monospace">**a** and **b**</text>
  <text x="20" y="22" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">greedy: one match</text>
  <line x1="112" y1="38" x2="248" y2="38" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="3"/>
  <line x1="112" y1="32" x2="112" y2="44" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="248" y1="32" x2="248" y2="44" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="112" y1="80" x2="156" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3"/>
  <line x1="112" y1="74" x2="112" y2="86" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="156" y1="74" x2="156" y2="86" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="204" y1="80" x2="248" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3"/>
  <line x1="204" y1="74" x2="204" y2="86" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="248" y1="74" x2="248" y2="86" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="20" y="110" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">lazy: two matches</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context constants Constants by agreement
Python has no rule that stops you changing `EM` later. Capital letters are a promise between programmers: "this is set once; do not change it". The official Python style guide, PEP 8, asks for this naming. Compiling patterns once at the top also gives each pattern a name, so `STRONG.sub(...)` reads better than a long pattern in the middle of a function. (The `re` module also remembers recently used patterns, so compiling is mostly about clarity, not speed.)
:::

::: context odd-positions Why code always lands on odd positions
`re.split` always puts the text *before* the first match at position 0, even when there is none. If the line starts with code, position 0 is an empty string:

```python
re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", "`x` and `y`")
# ['', '`x`', ' and ', '`y`', '']
```

So the pattern never shifts: even positions (0, 2, 4) are ordinary text, possibly empty, and odd positions (1, 3) are code. Formatting an empty string gives an empty string, so the empty pieces do no harm.
:::
--- task
Write two functions.

- `escape(text)`: replace `&`, `<`, `>` and `"` with `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;` and `&quot;`. Replace `&` first.
- `inline(text)`: escape the text and convert `**strong**` to `<strong>`, `*em*` to `<em>` (only when the characters just inside the stars are not spaces), a code span between backticks to `<code>`, and `[text](url)` to `<a href="url">text</a>`. Text inside backticks is escaped but not otherwise formatted.
--- starter
# Markdown to HTML, step 1: inline formatting.
import re


def escape(text):
    return text


def inline(text):
    return text
--- solution
# Markdown to HTML, step 1: inline formatting.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)
--- hint
`escape` is four `replace` calls in a chain, with `&` first. For `inline`, cut out the code spans first with `re.split` and the grouped pattern from Step 7. In the list you get back, the odd positions are code spans.
--- hint
A small helper can handle an ordinary piece of text: escape it, then run the link pattern, then strong, then em, each with `.sub`. The strong pattern is `r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*"`, and em is the same with single stars.
--- hint
In `inline`, loop `for i, part in enumerate(parts):`. When `i % 2 == 1`, append `f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>"`; otherwise append the helper's result. Return `"".join(out)`.
--- check case | escape, with & first
escape('a < b && "c" > d')
=> "a &lt; b &amp;&amp; &quot;c&quot; &gt; d"
--- check case | Bold and italic
inline("**bold** and *soft*")
=> "<strong>bold</strong> and <em>soft</em>"
--- check case | Two bold phrases stay separate
inline("**a** or **b**")
=> "<strong>a</strong> or <strong>b</strong>"
--- check case | Stars with spaces around them are not emphasis
inline("5 * 3 * 2")
=> "5 * 3 * 2"
--- check case | Code spans are escaped but not formatted
inline("run `a*b*c < d` now")
=> "run <code>a*b*c &lt; d</code> now"
--- check case | Links
inline("see [the docs](https://example.com/a?x=1&y=2)")
=> 'see <a href="https://example.com/a?x=1&amp;y=2">the docs</a>'
--- check case | Plain text is only escaped
inline("fish & chips")
=> "fish &amp; chips"

+++ practice | Crossing words out
--- task
Many Markdown writers cross words out with two tildes on each side: `~~old~~` becomes `<del>old</del>` in HTML. (The tilde is the `~` key.)

Write `escape(text)`, which replaces `&`, `<`, `>` and `"` with `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;` and `&quot;` (the `&` first), and `strike(text)`, which escapes the text and then turns every `~~text~~` into `<del>text</del>`:

- The characters just inside the tildes must not be spaces, so `"a ~~ b ~~ c"` is left alone.
- Two crossed-out parts on one line stay separate: `"~~a~~ and ~~b~~"` gives `"<del>a</del> and <del>b</del>"`.
--- starter
import re


def strike(text):
    return text.replace("~~", "<del>")
--- solution
import re

STRIKE = re.compile(r"~~(\S|\S.*?\S)~~")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def strike(text):
    return STRIKE.sub(r"<del>\1</del>", escape(text))
--- hint
It is the lesson's emphasis pattern with `~~` in place of the stars. A tilde has no special meaning in a pattern, so it needs no backslash.
--- hint
`~~(\S|\S.*?\S)~~` is two tildes, a group that starts and ends with a non-space (or is one non-space), and two tildes. Escape first, then `sub` with `\1` inside the tags.
--- check case | One crossed-out word
strike("~~old~~ new")
=> "<del>old</del> new"
--- check case | Two on a line stay separate
strike("~~a~~ and ~~b c~~")
=> "<del>a</del> and <del>b c</del>"
--- check case | Tildes with spaces inside are not crossing out
strike("a ~~ b ~~ c")
=> "a ~~ b ~~ c"
--- check case | The text is escaped first
strike('~~x < y~~ & "z"')
=> "<del>x &lt; y</del> &amp; &quot;z&quot;"

+++ practice | Markdown to forum code
--- task
Old web forums use a different markup, called BBCode, with square-bracket tags. Write `to_bbcode(text)`, which turns Markdown into it:

- `**text**` → `[b]text[/b]`
- `*text*` → `[i]text[/i]`
- `[text](url)` → `[url=url]text[/url]`

The rules for what counts are the lesson's: emphasis needs a non-space just inside each star, and a link's URL has no spaces or `)`. Forums do their own escaping, so do **not** escape anything: `a < b` stays `a < b`.
--- starter
import re


def to_bbcode(text):
    return text.replace("**", "[b]").replace("*", "[i]")
--- solution
import re

LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")
BOLD = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
ITALIC = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")


def to_bbcode(text):
    text = LINK.sub(r"[url=\2]\1[/url]", text)
    text = BOLD.sub(r"[b]\1[/b]", text)
    return ITALIC.sub(r"[i]\1[/i]", text)
--- hint
The patterns are the lesson's. Only the replacements change: the groups `\1` and `\2` go into BBCode tags instead of HTML tags.
--- hint
Order still matters: bold before italic, or the italic pattern eats the first star of `**`. A link's replacement puts group 2 (the URL) after `url=` and group 1 (the text) between the tags.
--- check case | Bold and italic
to_bbcode("**bold** and *soft*")
=> "[b]bold[/b] and [i]soft[/i]"
--- check case | A link inside bold
to_bbcode("read **[the docs](http://x.io/a)** now")
=> "read [b][url=http://x.io/a]the docs[/url][/b] now"
--- check case | Arithmetic and angle brackets are left alone
to_bbcode("5 * 3 * 2 < 40")
=> "5 * 3 * 2 < 40"
--- check case | Two of each stay separate
to_bbcode("*a* *b* **c** **d**")
=> "[i]a[/i] [i]b[/i] [b]c[/b] [b]d[/b]"

+++ practice | Add up the prices in a note
--- task
Write `total_prices(text)`, which finds every price in a piece of text, adds them up, and returns the total formatted like the expense tracker: a thousands separator and two decimals.

- A price is one or more digits, a dot and exactly two digits, like `2.50` or `1200.00`. Whole numbers like `3` and one-decimal numbers like `4.5` are not prices.
- Add up in whole cents, never in floats.
- No prices gives `"0.00"`.

For example, `total_prices("Tea 2.50, cake 3.75 and a 10.00 tip")` is `"16.25"`.
--- starter
import re


def total_prices(text):
    return "0.00"
--- solution
import re

PRICE = re.compile(r"\d+\.\d\d")


def total_prices(text):
    cents = 0
    for price in PRICE.findall(text):
        whole, _, frac = price.partition(".")
        cents += int(whole) * 100 + int(frac)
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"
--- hint
`re.findall` returns every match of a pattern, as a list of strings. The pattern for a price is digits, a real dot, and two single digits: `\d` twice.
--- hint
Turn each price into cents with `partition(".")`, as in the first lesson, add them up, and format the total with `:,` and `:02d`.
--- check case | Three prices
total_prices("Tea 2.50, cake 3.75 and a 10.00 tip")
=> "16.25"
--- check case | Big totals get a thousands separator
total_prices("rent 1200.00 and power 99.99")
=> "1,299.99"
--- check case | Numbers that are not prices
total_prices("3 apples and 4.5 kg of flour")
=> "0.00"
--- check case | Exact to the cent
total_prices(" ".join(["0.10"] * 3 + ["0.07"] * 100))
=> "7.30"

+++ practice | Shout, but not the code
--- task
Write `shout(text)`, which turns the text into capital letters, except inside code spans: a code span (text between two backticks) stays exactly as it was, backticks included.

- `"run `ls -la` now"` gives `"RUN `ls -la` NOW"`.
- The text may start or end with a code span, contain several, or have two side by side.
- A lone backtick with no partner does not start a code span, so the text around it is shouted as usual.
- An empty string gives `""`.
--- starter
import re


def shout(text):
    return text.upper()
--- solution
import re

CODE_SPAN = re.compile(r"(`[^`]+`)")


def shout(text):
    parts = CODE_SPAN.split(text)
    return "".join(part if i % 2 == 1 else part.upper() for i, part in enumerate(parts))
--- hint
Cut the code spans out with `re.split` and a pattern wrapped in a group, as the lesson did, so they are kept in the list.
--- hint
The code spans are always at the odd positions of that list, even when the text starts with one (then the first item is an empty string). Upper-case the even positions, keep the odd ones, and join.
--- check case | One code span in the middle
shout("run `ls -la` now")
=> "RUN `ls -la` NOW"
--- check case | Starting and ending with code, and two side by side
[shout("`cd` then `pwd`"), shout("`a``b` c")]
=> ["`cd` THEN `pwd`", "`a``b` C"]
--- check case | A lone backtick, and nothing at all
[shout("it's a ` mark"), shout("")]
=> ["IT'S A ` MARK", ""]

+++ practice | Debug: two links become one
--- task
`links(text)` escapes the text and turns every Markdown link `[text](url)` into `<a href="url">text</a>`.

It works for one link. With two links on one line, `"see [a](x.html) and [b](y.html)"`, it makes a single broken link whose text is `a](x.html) and [b`. Fix the pattern so each link is found on its own.
--- starter
import re

LINK = re.compile(r"\[(.+)\]\((.+)\)")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def links(text):
    return LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', escape(text))
--- solution
import re

LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def links(text):
    return LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', escape(text))
--- hint
`.+` is greedy: it grabs as much as it can, so the first group runs on to the *last* `](` on the line.
--- hint
Say exactly what may be inside each part: the text is characters that are not `]`, and the URL is characters that are not `)` and not spaces. The lesson's link pattern does that with `[^\]]+` and `[^)\s]+`.
--- check case | Two links on one line
links("see [a](x.html) and [b](y.html)")
=> 'see <a href="x.html">a</a> and <a href="y.html">b</a>'
--- check case | A link inside brackets
links("(see [the map](map.html))")
=> '(see <a href="map.html">the map</a>)'
--- check case | One link, escaped
links("[Q&A](faq.html)")
=> '<a href="faq.html">Q&amp;A</a>'

+++ practice | From HTML back to Markdown
--- task
Write `html_to_md(html)`, the converter in reverse. It takes HTML made by the lesson's `inline` and gives back the Markdown:

- `<strong>text</strong>` → `**text**`, `<em>text</em>` → `*text*`, `<code>text</code>` → a code span (the text between backticks).
- `<a href="url">text</a>` → `[text](url)`.
- Then the entities go back to plain characters everywhere, the URL included: `&lt;` → `<`, `&gt;` → `>`, `&quot;` → `"` and `&amp;` → `&`.

Careful with the order: the text `&amp;lt;` stands for the five characters `&lt;`, so it must come back as `&lt;`, not as `<`. Two tags of the same kind on one line stay separate.
--- starter
import re


def html_to_md(html):
    return html
--- solution
import re

TAGS = [
    (re.compile(r'<a href="(.+?)">(.+?)</a>'), r"[\2](\1)"),
    (re.compile(r"<strong>(.+?)</strong>"), r"**\1**"),
    (re.compile(r"<em>(.+?)</em>"), r"*\1*"),
    (re.compile(r"<code>(.+?)</code>"), r"`\1`"),
]


def html_to_md(html):
    for pattern, replacement in TAGS:
        html = pattern.sub(replacement, html)
    return html.replace("&lt;", "<").replace("&gt;", ">").replace("&quot;", '"').replace("&amp;", "&")
--- hint
Each tag is a pattern with a lazy group, `(.+?)`, so it stops at the first closing tag. A list of (pattern, replacement) pairs and a loop keeps the code short.
--- hint
Escaping replaced `&` first so that no entity got mangled. Going back, the order flips: replace `&amp;` last, after the other three, or `&amp;lt;` turns into `&lt;` and then into `<`.
--- check case | Strong and em
html_to_md("<strong>bold</strong> and <em>soft</em>, <strong>again</strong>")
=> "**bold** and *soft*, **again**"
--- check case | A link, with its & put back
html_to_md('<a href="https://example.com/a?x=1&amp;y=2">the docs</a>')
=> "[the docs](https://example.com/a?x=1&y=2)"
--- check case | Code, and entities everywhere
html_to_md("<code>a &lt; b</code> &amp;&amp; &quot;c&quot; &gt; d")
=> '`a < b` && "c" > d'
--- check case | &amp; goes back last
html_to_md("write &amp;lt; to show &lt;")
=> "write &lt; to show <"

=== pyp-06 | Markdown converter 2: headings and paragraphs
--- teach
Last lesson, `inline` turned the formatting *inside* one line into HTML. Now the converter learns the document's **blocks**: the big pieces a page is built from. This lesson does two of them, headings and paragraphs.

This Markdown

```
# Title

This is one paragraph
that spans two lines.

## Section
Another paragraph.
```

becomes this HTML:

```
<h1>Title</h1>
<p>This is one paragraph that spans two lines.</p>
<h2>Section</h2>
<p>Another paragraph.</p>
```

`<h1>` is a heading of [[level 1|heading-levels]], the biggest; `<h2>` is level 2, a size smaller; and so on down to `<h6>`. `<p>` is a paragraph.

The rules:

- A line that starts with one to six `#` and then a space is a heading. The number of `#` is its level.
- Ordinary lines that come one after another form one paragraph. Their text is joined with single spaces.
- A blank line ends a paragraph. A heading also ends any paragraph before it.

### Step 1: recognize a heading

A regular expression can state the heading rule exactly:

```
^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$
```

In pieces:

- `^` means "the start of the line". It matches no character; it pins the pattern to the beginning.
- `(#{1,6})` is group 1: between one and six `#`. Curly braces give a count: "at least 1, at most 6 of the thing before".
- `\s+` is one or more spaces.
- `(.*)` is group 2: the rest of the text.
- `$` means "the end of the line".

`pattern.match(line)` tries the pattern at the start of the line. It returns a **[[match object|match-objects]]** if the line fits, or `None` if it does not. A match object hands back the groups:

```python
HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")

m = HEADING.match("## Part two")
m.group(1)          # '##'
len(m.group(1))     # 2, the level
m.group(2)          # 'Part two'

HEADING.match("#hashtag")          # None: no space after the #
HEADING.match("####### seven")     # None: seven is too many
```
