`None` counts as false in an `if`, so `if m:` reads "if the line is a heading".

**Watch out:** matching headings with `line.startswith("#")`. Then `#hashtag` and `####### seven` become headings too. The regex says the rule exactly.

### Step 2: a paragraph needs a buffer

When you see the first line of a paragraph, you cannot write its `<p>` yet. You do not know where it ends. It is like a friend reading your texts aloud: they wait until you have finished the message, not after each word.

So collect the paragraph's lines in a list, called a **[[buffer|buffer-word]]**: a holding place for data that is not ready to go out yet. When something ends the paragraph (a blank line, a heading, or the end of the input) you **flush** the buffer: write it out and empty it.

```python
paragraph = []

def flush():
    if paragraph:
        html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
        paragraph.clear()
```

`' '.join(paragraph)` glues the lines together with single spaces, and `inline` formats the result. The `if paragraph:` means an empty buffer writes nothing, so calling `flush()` is always safe.

### Step 3: flush lives inside convert

`flush` is defined *inside* `convert`, so it can see `convert`'s lists `html` and `paragraph`. It is a closure, from the first lesson of the expert course.

It empties the list with `paragraph.clear()`, not `paragraph = []`. That is [[the closure rule|closure-rule]] again: assigning to a name inside `flush` would make a brand-new local variable inside `flush`, and the shared list outside would never be emptied. `.clear()` changes the shared list itself, so no `nonlocal` is needed.

### Step 4: the loop, and the end

The main loop looks at each line once and decides what it is:

```python
for raw in md.splitlines():
    line = raw.strip()
    # blank line  -> flush()
    # heading     -> flush(), then add the <h1> ... <h6> line
    # anything else -> paragraph.append(line)
flush()        # the last paragraph is still waiting
```

`raw.strip()` removes spaces at both ends, so an indented line and a line of only spaces are treated like the others.

**Don't forget the end.** When the loop finishes, the [[last paragraph|last-block]] is still sitting in the buffer, because nothing came after it to end it. One more `flush()` after the loop writes it out. Losing the final block is the most common bug in this kind of code.

At the very end, `"\n".join(html)` puts one block on each line of the result.

::: context heading-levels Six levels, like an outline
HTML has exactly six heading levels, `<h1>` to `<h6>`, and Markdown's one to six `#` map onto them. They work like an outline: `<h1>` is the title of the page, `<h2>` a section, `<h3>` a part of that section. Headings are not only for looks. Screen readers, the programs that read a page aloud for blind users, let people jump from heading to heading, and search engines use them to understand what a page is about.
:::

::: context match-objects match, search, and why ^ is still worth writing
`pattern.match(text)` only looks for the pattern at the very start of the text. Its cousin `pattern.search(text)` looks anywhere. So with `match`, the `^` is not strictly needed, but writing it makes the pattern say "start of line" out loud to whoever reads it next. A match object's `group(0)` is the whole matched text; `group(1)`, `group(2)` are the round-bracket groups, counted by their opening bracket from the left.
:::

::: context buffer-word Buffers are everywhere
A buffer is any holding area between something that produces data and something that uses it. A video app fills a buffer a few seconds ahead so the picture does not stutter. The text you type in a terminal sits in a buffer until you press Enter.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 140" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">input lines</text>
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="110" height="24" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="75" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">One paragraph</text>
  <rect x="20" y="60" width="110" height="24" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="75" y="76" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">over two lines.</text>
  <rect x="20" y="90" width="110" height="24" rx="4" fill="#fff" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="75" y="106" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">(blank)</text>
  <line x1="130" y1="57" x2="165" y2="57" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="165,52 175,57 165,62" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="178" y="34" width="64" height="46" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="210" y="61" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">buffer</text>
  <line x1="242" y1="57" x2="270" y2="57" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="270,52 280,57 270,62" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="261" y="100" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">blank line: flush</text>
  <rect x="284" y="44" width="66" height="26" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="317" y="61" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">&lt;p&gt;...&lt;/p&gt;</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context closure-rule Changing a list versus replacing it
The rule from the closures lesson: if an inner function *assigns* to a name (`=` or `+=`), that name becomes local to the inner function, unless you write `nonlocal`. Calling a method like `.clear()` or `.append()` is not an assignment. It reaches the outer list through the name and changes the list itself, so it works without `nonlocal`. Next lesson you will meet a helper that really does assign, and then `nonlocal` is needed.
:::

::: context last-block Test the edges
Losing the last item is a cousin of the "off by one" bug: the loop handles every line, but the work that happens *between* blocks never happens after the final one. The habit that catches it is to test the edges every time: empty input, a single block, and input that ends without a blank line. The checks for this lesson do exactly that, and so will the tests you write yourself in the capstones.
:::
--- task
Write `convert(md)`. It returns the HTML as a string, one block per line (joined with `"\n"`):

- A line of `#` to `######` followed by a space becomes `<h1>` to `<h6>`. Its text is trimmed and run through `inline`.
- Other non-blank lines that come one after another (each stripped) become one `<p>`. Their text is joined with single spaces and run through `inline`.
- Blank lines separate blocks and produce nothing themselves.

Keep `escape`, `inline` and the patterns from last lesson.
--- starter
# Markdown to HTML, step 1: inline formatting.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)
--- solution
# Markdown to HTML, step 2: headings and paragraphs.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)


HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif heading:
            flush()
            level = len(heading.group(1))
            html.append(f"<h{level}>{inline(heading.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>")
        else:
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
Loop over `md.splitlines()`, strip each line, and decide: blank, heading (use a compiled pattern like `HEADING` above), or paragraph text.
--- hint
Keep a `paragraph` list and a nested `flush()` that writes it as a `<p>` and clears it. Call `flush()` on a blank line, before a heading, and once more after the loop.
--- hint
For a heading, the level is `len(match.group(1))`, the number of `#`. Append `f"<h{level}>{inline(match.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>"`, and return `"\n".join(html)` at the end.
--- check case | A heading and a two-line paragraph
convert("# Title\n\nOne paragraph\nover two lines.")
=> "<h1>Title</h1>\n<p>One paragraph over two lines.</p>"
--- check case | A heading ends a paragraph even without a blank line
convert("text\n## Part *two*\nmore")
=> "<p>text</p>\n<h2>Part <em>two</em></h2>\n<p>more</p>"
--- check case | Not headings: no space, or seven #
convert("#hashtag\n\n####### seven")
=> "<p>#hashtag</p>\n<p>####### seven</p>"
--- check case | Extra blank lines and indentation
convert("\n\n   first  \n\n\n  second\n\n")
=> "<p>first</p>\n<p>second</p>"
--- check case | Every heading level
convert("###### small\n### mid")
=> "<h6>small</h6>\n<h3>mid</h3>"
--- check case | Empty input
convert("")
=> ""

+++ practice | List the headings
--- task
Write `headings(md)`, which returns a list of `(level, text)` pairs, one for each heading line in a Markdown document, in order.

- A heading is a line that, after stripping, is 1 to 6 `#`, then at least one space, then the text. The level is the number of `#`.
- The text is stripped, and kept exactly as written otherwise (no HTML).
- No headings, or an empty document, gives `[]`.

For example, `headings("# Title\ntext\n## Part two")` is `[(1, "Title"), (2, "Part two")]`.
--- starter
import re


def headings(md):
    return [(line.count("#"), line.strip("# ")) for line in md.splitlines() if line.startswith("#")]
--- solution
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def headings(md):
    found = []
    for raw in md.splitlines():
        m = HEADING.match(raw.strip())
        if m:
            found.append((len(m.group(1)), m.group(2).strip()))
    return found
--- hint
Use the lesson's heading pattern on each stripped line. `match` gives a match object for a heading and `None` for anything else.
--- hint
The level is `len(m.group(1))`, the number of `#` that group 1 captured, and the text is `m.group(2).strip()`.
--- check case | Headings among other lines
headings("# Title\ntext\n## Part *two*\nmore text")
=> [(1, "Title"), (2, "Part *two*")]
--- check case | Not headings: no space, or seven #
headings("#hashtag\n####### seven\n###### six")
=> [(6, "six")]
--- check case | Indented headings, and a # inside the text
headings("   ### Deep   \n# C# for beginners")
=> [(3, "Deep"), (1, "C# for beginners")]
--- check case | No headings at all
[headings(""), headings("just text\n\nmore")]
=> [[], []]

+++ practice | Paragraphs of plain text
--- task
Write `paragraphs(text)`, which splits plain text into paragraphs and returns them as a list of strings:

- Non-blank lines that come one after another form one paragraph. Each line is stripped, and the lines are joined with single spaces.
- Blank lines (empty, or only spaces) separate paragraphs. Several blank lines in a row are the same as one.
- No text gives `[]`.

For example, `paragraphs("One line\nand another.\n\nA second one.")` is `["One line and another.", "A second one."]`.
--- starter
def paragraphs(text):
    return text.split("\n\n")
--- solution
def paragraphs(text):
    found = []
    lines = []

    def flush():
        if lines:
            found.append(" ".join(lines))
            lines.clear()

    for raw in text.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        if line:
            lines.append(line)
        else:
            flush()
    flush()
    return found
--- hint
Keep a buffer of the lines of the paragraph you are in. A blank line means the paragraph is finished: write out the buffer and empty it.
--- hint
A nested `flush()` that does nothing when the buffer is empty can be called on every blank line, and once more after the loop for the last paragraph. Empty the buffer with `.clear()`, not by assigning a new list.
--- check case | Two paragraphs
paragraphs("One line\nand another.\n\nA second one.")
=> ["One line and another.", "A second one."]
--- check case | Many blank lines, spaces and indentation
paragraphs("\n\n   first   line\n  second\n\n  \n\nthird\n\n")
=> ["first   line second", "third"]
--- check test | No text, and only blank lines
paragraphs("") == [] and paragraphs("\n   \n\n") == []

+++ practice | Quotations
--- task
Markdown marks a quotation with `>` at the start of a line. Write `convert(md)`, which turns a document of quotations and paragraphs into HTML, one block per line (joined with `"\n"`). The starter has `escape` from the last lesson.

- A line whose stripped text starts with `>` is a quote line. Its text is what comes after the `>`, stripped.
- Quote lines that come one after another form one `<blockquote>...</blockquote>`, their texts joined with single spaces. A quote line with no text adds nothing, but does not end the quotation.
- Other non-blank lines form paragraphs, `<p>...</p>`, as in the lesson.
- A blank line ends a block. A quote line also ends a paragraph, and an ordinary line ends a quotation.
- All text is escaped. There is no other formatting.
--- starter
def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def convert(md):
    return ""
--- solution
def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []
    quote = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{escape(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()
        if quote:
            html.append(f"<blockquote>{escape(' '.join(quote))}</blockquote>")
            quote.clear()

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif line.startswith(">"):
            if paragraph:
                flush()
            text = line[1:].strip()
            if text:
                quote.append(text)
        else:
            if quote:
                flush()
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
Two buffers this time: one for the paragraph you are in, one for the quotation. A single `flush()` can write whichever of them has lines, and empty it.
--- hint
Before adding a quote line, flush if a paragraph is waiting; before adding an ordinary line, flush if a quotation is waiting. Then only one buffer ever holds lines at a time.
--- hint
The quote's text is `line[1:].strip()`: everything after the `>`. Escape each block's joined text when you write it out, not before (the `>` must be seen first).
--- check case | A quotation, then a paragraph
convert("> To be\n> or not to be\n\nsaid Hamlet.")
=> "<blockquote>To be or not to be</blockquote>\n<p>said Hamlet.</p>"
--- check case | Quote lines end a paragraph, and text ends a quote
convert("Intro\n>quoted\nafter")
=> "<p>Intro</p>\n<blockquote>quoted</blockquote>\n<p>after</p>"
--- check case | An empty quote line keeps the quotation going
convert("> one\n>\n>   two")
=> "<blockquote>one two</blockquote>"
--- check case | Everything is escaped
convert("> a < b & c\n\nx > y")
=> "<blockquote>a &lt; b &amp; c</blockquote>\n<p>x &gt; y</p>"
--- check case | Empty input
convert("")
=> ""

+++ practice | Closing hashes on a heading
--- task
Markdown allows a heading to end with `#` characters too, just for looks: `## Title ##` is a level-2 heading whose text is `Title`. Write `heading(line)`, which returns `(level, text)` for a heading line, or `None` for any other line:

- After stripping, a heading is 1 to 6 `#`, then at least one space, then the text. The text is stripped.
- If the text ends with a run of `#` that has a space just before it, that run is removed, and the text is stripped again: `"# Title #####"` → `(1, "Title")`.
- A `#` with no space before it is part of the text: `"## Learn C#"` → `(2, "Learn C#")`.
- If the text is only `#` characters, the heading is empty: `"### ###"` → `(3, "")`.
- `"#hashtag"`, `"####### seven"`, `"##"` and `""` are not headings: `None`.

(`text.rstrip("#")` removes every `#` from the right end of a string.)
--- starter
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def heading(line):
    m = HEADING.match(line.strip())
    return (len(m.group(1)), m.group(2).strip("# "))
--- solution
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def heading(line):
    m = HEADING.match(line.strip())
    if not m:
        return None
    text = m.group(2).strip()
    body = text.rstrip("#")
    if body == "" or body.endswith(" "):
        text = body.strip()
    return (len(m.group(1)), text)
--- hint
Start with the lesson's pattern, and return `None` straight away when it does not match. Then deal with the closing hashes as a separate step on the text.
--- hint
`text.rstrip("#")` removes every `#` from the right end. Keep that shorter text only if it is empty (the text was all hashes) or ends with a space (the hashes had a space before them); otherwise the `#` belonged to the text.
--- check case | Closing hashes are removed
[heading("## Title ##"), heading("# Title #####"), heading("  ### Deep ###  ")]
=> [(2, "Title"), (1, "Title"), (3, "Deep")]
--- check case | A # with no space before it is text
[heading("## Learn C#"), heading("# a#b #")]
=> [(2, "Learn C#"), (1, "a#b")]
--- check case | Only hashes: an empty heading
[heading("### ###"), heading("# #")]
=> [(3, ""), (1, "")]
--- check case | Not headings
[heading("#hashtag"), heading("####### seven"), heading("##"), heading(""), heading("text # not")]
=> [None, None, None, None, None]

+++ practice | Debug: paragraphs that repeat
--- task
`convert(md)` should turn plain text into paragraphs: lines that come one after another become one `<p>...</p>`, joined with single spaces, and blank lines separate paragraphs. The result has one paragraph per line.

It gets the first paragraph right. But every later paragraph also repeats all the text before it: `"a\n\nb"` gives `"<p>a</p>\n<p>a b</p>"`. And a blank line at the end writes the last paragraph twice. Fix it.
--- starter
def convert(md):
    out = []
    pending = []

    def flush():
        if pending:
            out.append(f"<p>{' '.join(pending)}</p>")

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        if line:
            pending.append(line)
        else:
            flush()
    flush()
    return "\n".join(out)
--- solution
def convert(md):
    out = []
    pending = []

    def flush():
        if pending:
            out.append(f"<p>{' '.join(pending)}</p>")
            pending.clear()

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        if line:
            pending.append(line)
        else:
            flush()
    flush()
    return "\n".join(out)
--- hint
After a paragraph has been written out, what is still in `pending`?
--- hint
Flushing means writing the buffer *and* emptying it. Empty the shared list with `pending.clear()`: assigning `pending = []` inside `flush` would make a new local name instead.
--- check case | Each paragraph only once
convert("a\n\nb")
=> "<p>a</p>\n<p>b</p>"
--- check case | Blank lines at the end
convert("one\ntwo\n\n\nthree\n\n")
=> "<p>one two</p>\n<p>three</p>"
--- check case | One paragraph, and nothing
[convert("only\nthis"), convert("")]
=> ["<p>only this</p>", ""]

+++ practice | Words in each section
--- task
Write `section_words(md)`, which counts the words in each section of a Markdown document, for a table of contents. It returns a list of `(heading text, word count)` pairs, in order:

- A heading is found by the lesson's rule (1 to 6 `#`, a space, the text, on a stripped line). Its text is stripped.
- A section's words are all the words (split on spaces) of the non-heading lines after its heading, up to the next heading of any level.
- Words before the first heading are counted under `"(intro)"`, which appears first, and only if there are some.
- A heading with nothing under it has 0 words. No text at all gives `[]`.

For example, `section_words("Hello there\n# A\none two\n\nthree\n## B\n# C\nfour")` is `[("(intro)", 2), ("A", 3), ("B", 0), ("C", 1)]`.
--- starter
import re


def section_words(md):
    return [(line, 0) for line in md.splitlines() if line.startswith("#")]
--- solution
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def section_words(md):
    sections = []
    title, count = "(intro)", 0

    def finish():
        if title != "(intro)" or count > 0:
            sections.append((title, count))

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        m = HEADING.match(raw.strip())
        if m:
            finish()
            title, count = m.group(2).strip(), 0
        else:
            count += len(raw.split())
    finish()
    return sections
--- hint
This is a buffer again, but what you collect is a count. Keep the current section's title and word count; when a heading arrives, finish the section you were in and start a new one at 0.
--- hint
Start with the title `"(intro)"` and a count of 0. When you finish a section, keep it unless it is the intro with no words. Remember to finish the last section after the loop.
--- check case | Intro, sections and an empty section
section_words("Hello there\n# A\none two\n\nthree\n## B\n# C\nfour")
=> [("(intro)", 2), ("A", 3), ("B", 0), ("C", 1)]
--- check case | No intro, and a heading with nothing under it
section_words("# Only\n\n   ")
=> [("Only", 0)]
--- check case | No headings: everything is intro
section_words("just some  words\n#tag counts too")
=> [("(intro)", 6)]
--- check test | Nothing at all
section_words("") == [] and section_words("\n\n") == []

=== pyp-07 | Markdown converter 3: lists
--- teach
Your converter now handles headings and paragraphs. Next comes a third kind of block that spans several lines: the list.

This Markdown

```
- milk
- eggs

1. mix
2. bake
```

becomes this HTML:

```
<ul>
<li>milk</li>
<li>eggs</li>
</ul>
<ol>
<li>mix</li>
<li>bake</li>
</ol>
```

`<ul>` is a [[bullet list|list-tags]] ("unordered list"), `<ol>` is a numbered list ("ordered list"), and each `<li>` is one list item.

The rules:

- A line starting with `- ` or `* ` (a dash or star, then a space) is a bullet item.
- A line starting with a number, a dot and a space is a numbered item.
- Items of the same kind that come one after another form one list.
- Anything else ends the list: a blank line, a heading, ordinary text, or an item of the *other* kind.

### Step 1: two new patterns

```
^[-*]\s+(.*)$         a bullet item
^\d+\.\s+(.*)$        a numbered item
```

`[-*]` is one character that is either `-` or `*`. Inside square brackets a star is only a star, so it needs no backslash. `\d+` is one or more digits, and `\.` is a real dot (a bare `.` would mean "any character"). In both, `\s+` insists on at least one space, and group 1 is the item's text.

That space matters, because stars also mean italics. `* item`, star then space, is a bullet. `*word* starts here` is a paragraph that begins with an italic word:

```python
BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
BULLET.match("* eggs").group(1)        # 'eggs'
BULLET.match("*word* starts here")     # None: no space after the star
```

### Step 2: the converter remembers something

Until now, each line decided its own fate. A list item is different: what to do with it depends on what came before. If no list is open, the item must *start* one (`<ul>`, then `<li>`). If a list of the same kind is already open, it only adds an `<li>`.

A program where the same input means different things depending on what it remembers is a **[[state machine|state-machine]]**. What it remembers is its **state**. A phone's power button is an everyday one: the same press wakes a sleeping phone and locks an awake one.

Here the state is one variable:

```python
list_kind = None      # None (no list open), "ul" or "ol"
```

### Step 3: a helper that changes the state

```python fragment
def list_item(kind, text):
    nonlocal list_kind
    if list_kind != kind:
        flush()                    # close whatever was open
        html.append(f"<{kind}>")
        list_kind = kind
    html.append(f"<li>{inline(text)}</li>")
```

If the open list is not this kind (no list, or the other kind), it flushes, opens a new list and records the new state. Then it adds the item.

This helper *assigns* to `list_kind`, so it needs **[[nonlocal|nonlocal-again]]**, unlike `flush` last lesson, which only changed a list. Without it, `list_kind = kind` would make a new variable inside `list_item`.

### Step 4: flush closes lists too

Extend `flush()` so that after writing any paragraph it also closes an open list:

```python
if list_kind:
    html.append(f"</{list_kind}>")
    list_kind = None
```

`flush` now assigns to `list_kind` too, so it also needs `nonlocal list_kind` as its first line. The payoff: every place that already calls `flush()` (a blank line, a heading, the end of the input) now closes lists as well, without changing any of them.

### Step 5: ordinary text right after a list

One case is left. An ordinary line straight after a list goes into the paragraph buffer, and nothing has closed the list. When `flush()` runs later, it writes the paragraph first and closes the list second, so the paragraph lands *inside* the list:

```
<ul>
<li>a</li>
<p>then text</p>
</ul>
```

The fix: in the "ordinary line" branch, if a list is open, call `flush()` first, then add the line to the paragraph.

**Watch out:** the two ways lists go wrong are ordinary text being swallowed into the list, and a list that is never closed. Test both: text right after a list, and a list at the very end of the input.

One variable and a few rules about when it changes: that is a whole state machine. The same shape, with more states, is how [[flight software|state-machines-later]] is organized.

::: context list-tags The browser does the numbering
In `<ol>` the browser numbers the items itself: 1, 2, 3. That is why your converter throws the typed numbers away. `22. two` becomes plain `<li>two</li>`, shown as number 2 if it is the second item. `<ul>`, `<ol>` and `<li>` are three of the oldest tags in HTML. Real Markdown also allows `+` as a bullet and lists inside lists (by indenting), which this converter leaves out.
:::

::: context state-machine Drawing the states
A state machine is often drawn as circles for the states and arrows for what makes it change. Here, a bullet item moves the converter into the "ul" state, a numbered item into "ol", and a blank line, heading or ordinary text back to "none". An arrow from "ul" to "ol" closes one list and opens the other.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <ellipse cx="180" cy="36" rx="46" ry="22" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="41" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">none</text>
  <ellipse cx="70" cy="132" rx="42" ry="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="137" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ul</text>
  <ellipse cx="290" cy="132" rx="42" ry="22" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="290" y="137" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ol</text>
  <line x1="145" y1="52" x2="92" y2="108" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="88,113 92,101 99,108" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="84" y="76" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">bullet</text>
  <line x1="215" y1="52" x2="268" y2="108" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="272,113 261,108 268,101" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="250" y="76" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">number</text>
  <line x1="112" y1="132" x2="242" y2="132" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="248,132 238,127 238,137" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="180" y="124" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">number</text>
  <text x="180" y="160" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">blank, heading or text: back to none</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context nonlocal-again When nonlocal is needed
Only for *assigning* to a name that belongs to the outer function. `flush` last lesson did `paragraph.clear()`, which changes a list and needs nothing. `list_kind = kind` gives the name a new value, so without `nonlocal list_kind` Python would treat `list_kind` as a fresh local variable inside the helper, and reading it on the line before would crash with `UnboundLocalError`, the same bug as the counter in the closures lesson.
:::

::: context state-machines-later State machines fly
State machines are one of the most common shapes in real software, especially where safety matters. Flight software is usually organized into modes such as "on the pad", "ascent" or "coast", and a command or a sensor reading that is fine in one mode may be ignored or refused in another. Writing the modes down as explicit states makes it possible to check every case. You will build a bigger one yourself in the text adventure capstone at the end of this course.
:::
--- task
Extend `convert` with lists:

- `- item` or `* item` makes a `<ul>`; `1. item` (any number) makes an `<ol>`.
- Output `<ul>` or `<ol>`, each `<li>...</li>`, and the closing tag, each on its own line. Item text is run through `inline`.
- A blank line, a heading, an ordinary line, or an item of the other kind closes the open list. Headings and paragraphs work as before.
--- starter
# Markdown to HTML, step 2: headings and paragraphs.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)


HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif heading:
            flush()
            level = len(heading.group(1))
            html.append(f"<h{level}>{inline(heading.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>")
        else:
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- solution
# Markdown to HTML, step 3: lists.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)


HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")
BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []
    list_kind = None

    def flush():
        nonlocal list_kind
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()
        if list_kind:
            html.append(f"</{list_kind}>")
            list_kind = None

    def list_item(kind, text):
        nonlocal list_kind
        if list_kind != kind:
            flush()
            html.append(f"<{kind}>")
            list_kind = kind
        html.append(f"<li>{inline(text)}</li>")

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        bullet = BULLET.match(line)
        numbered = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif heading:
            flush()
            level = len(heading.group(1))
            html.append(f"<h{level}>{inline(heading.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>")
        elif bullet:
            list_item("ul", bullet.group(1))
        elif numbered:
            list_item("ol", numbered.group(1))
        else:
            if list_kind:
                flush()
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
Add two patterns, `r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$"` and `r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$"`, and a `list_kind` variable that starts as `None`.
--- hint
Write a nested `list_item(kind, text)` that opens a new list when `list_kind != kind` (flushing first), then appends the `<li>`. It needs `nonlocal list_kind`.
--- hint
Make `flush()` also close an open list (append `</ul>` or `</ol>` and set `list_kind` back to `None`, with `nonlocal list_kind`). In the ordinary-line branch, call `flush()` first if a list is open.
--- check case | A bullet list
convert("- milk\n* eggs & *ham*")
=> "<ul>\n<li>milk</li>\n<li>eggs &amp; <em>ham</em></li>\n</ul>"
--- check case | A numbered list after a bullet list
convert("- a\n- b\n1. one\n22. two")
=> "<ul>\n<li>a</li>\n<li>b</li>\n</ul>\n<ol>\n<li>one</li>\n<li>two</li>\n</ol>"
--- check case | Text after a list starts a paragraph
convert("- a\nthen text\nmore text")
=> "<ul>\n<li>a</li>\n</ul>\n<p>then text more text</p>"
--- check case | A paragraph, a list, a heading
convert("Intro\n- x\n# End")
=> "<p>Intro</p>\n<ul>\n<li>x</li>\n</ul>\n<h1>End</h1>"
--- check case | Italic text at the start of a line is not a list
convert("*word* starts here")
=> "<p><em>word</em> starts here</p>"
--- check case | Headings and paragraphs still work
convert("# T\n\np1\np2")
=> "<h1>T</h1>\n<p>p1 p2</p>"

+++ practice | Runs of bullet items
--- task
Write `group_items(text)`, which finds the bullet lists in a text and returns them as a list of lists of item texts.

- A bullet item is a line that, after stripping, starts with `-` or `*`, then at least one space, then its text.
- Items that come one after another form one list. Any other line, blank or not, ends the list.
- Lines that are not items are otherwise ignored. No items gives `[]`.

For example, `group_items("- a\n- b\nx\n* c")` is `[["a", "b"], ["c"]]`.
--- starter
def group_items(text):
    return [[line[2:] for line in text.splitlines() if line.startswith("- ")]]
--- solution
import re

ITEM = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")


def group_items(text):
    groups = []
    current = None
    for raw in text.splitlines():
        m = ITEM.match(raw.strip())
        if m:
            if current is None:
                current = []
                groups.append(current)
            current.append(m.group(1))
        else:
            current = None
    return groups
--- hint
The state to remember is whether a list is open right now. A variable that is `None` when no list is open, and the open list itself otherwise, does the job.
--- hint
On an item: if no list is open, start a new one and add it to the result; then append the item to the open list. On any other line, set the state back to `None`.
--- check case | Two lists
group_items("- a\n- b\nx\n* c")
=> [["a", "b"], ["c"]]
--- check case | A blank line splits a list, and dashes and stars mix
group_items("- milk\n* eggs\n\n  - bread  ")
=> [["milk", "eggs"], ["bread"]]
--- check case | Not items: no space after the mark
group_items("*word* here\n-dash\n- real")
=> [["real"]]
--- check case | No items
group_items("")
=> []

+++ practice | Number the bullet lists
--- task
Write `renumber(md)`, which turns every bullet list into a numbered list and leaves everything else alone:

- A bullet item is a line that, after stripping, is `-` or `*`, at least one space, and its text. It becomes `"<n>. <text>"`, where `n` counts the items of that list from 1.
- Any other line (blank lines included) is kept exactly as it was, and ends the list: the next list starts again at 1.
- The result is the lines joined with `"\n"`.

For example, `renumber("- milk\n- eggs\n\n* tea")` is `"1. milk\n2. eggs\n\n1. tea"`.
--- starter
def renumber(md):
    return md.replace("- ", "1. ")
--- solution
import re

BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")


def renumber(md):
    out = []
    number = 0
    for raw in md.splitlines():
        m = BULLET.match(raw.strip())
        if m:
            number += 1
            out.append(f"{number}. {m.group(1)}")
        else:
            number = 0
            out.append(raw)
    return "\n".join(out)
--- hint
The state is a counter: 0 when no list is open, and the number of the last item otherwise.
--- hint
On an item, add 1 to the counter and write the numbered line. On any other line, set the counter back to 0 and keep the line as it is.
--- check case | Two lists
renumber("- milk\n- eggs\n\n* tea")
=> "1. milk\n2. eggs\n\n1. tea"
--- check case | Text between lists, kept exactly
renumber("Buy:\n- a\n- b\n- c\n  Then:\n* d")
=> "Buy:\n1. a\n2. b\n3. c\n  Then:\n1. d"
--- check case | Italics are not items, and nothing is nothing
[renumber("*not* an item"), renumber("")]
=> ["*not* an item", ""]

+++ practice | Lists under headings
--- task
A packing list is written in Markdown, with a heading for each bag and a bullet list under it. Write `sections(md)`, which returns a dict from each heading's text to the list of bullet items under it:

- Headings follow the lesson rule (1 to 6 `#`, a space, the text); bullet items start with `-` or `*` and a space. Lines are stripped first, and texts are stripped.
- An item belongs to the most recent heading above it. Items before any heading go under the key `""`.
- A heading with no items maps to `[]`. If the same heading appears twice, its items are all in one list.
- Every other line is ignored.

For example, `sections("# Bag\n- socks\n- hat\ntext\n# Box\n# Car\n- map")` is `{"Bag": ["socks", "hat"], "Box": [], "Car": ["map"]}`.
--- starter
import re


def sections(md):
    return {}
--- solution
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")
BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")


def sections(md):
    found = {}
    current = ""
    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        item = BULLET.match(line)
        if heading:
            current = heading.group(2).strip()
            found.setdefault(current, [])
        elif item:
            found.setdefault(current, []).append(item.group(1).strip())
    return found
--- hint
The state is the current heading's text. It starts as `""`, and every heading line changes it.
--- hint
`found.setdefault(key, [])` returns the list stored under `key`, making an empty one first if there is none. Call it on every heading (so empty sections appear) and on every item (to append to it).
--- check case | Three bags
sections("# Bag\n- socks\n- hat\ntext\n# Box\n# Car\n- map")
=> {"Bag": ["socks", "hat"], "Box": [], "Car": ["map"]}
--- check case | Items before any heading, and a heading used twice
sections("* passport\n## Bag\n- socks\n## Car\n- map\n## Bag\n- towel")
=> {"": ["passport"], "Bag": ["socks", "towel"], "Car": ["map"]}
--- check case | Nothing to pack
[sections(""), sections("just text")]
=> [{}, {}]

+++ practice | Numbered lists that start late
--- task
A numbered list can start at any number, and HTML records that with a `start` attribute. Write `numbered_lists(md)`, which returns HTML, one entry per line (joined with `"\n"`). The starter has `escape`.

- A numbered item is a line that, after stripping, is digits, a `.`, at least one space, and the text.
- Items that come one after another form one list. The list's first number decides how it opens: `<ol>` when it is 1, otherwise `<ol start="N">` with the number as a plain integer (`007.` gives `start="7"`, and `0.` gives `start="0"`). Numbers after the first do not matter.
- Output `<ol ...>`, each `<li>text</li>`, and `</ol>`, each on its own line.
- Any other non-blank line ends the list and becomes its own `<p>text</p>`. A blank line ends the list and produces nothing.
- All text is escaped.
--- starter
def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def numbered_lists(md):
    return md
--- solution
import re

NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^(\d+)\.\s+(.*)$")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def numbered_lists(md):
    html = []
    list_open = False
    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        m = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if m:
            if not list_open:
                start = int(m.group(1))
                html.append("<ol>" if start == 1 else f'<ol start="{start}">')
                list_open = True
            html.append(f"<li>{escape(m.group(2))}</li>")
            continue
        if list_open:
            html.append("</ol>")
            list_open = False
        if line:
            html.append(f"<p>{escape(line)}</p>")
    if list_open:
        html.append("</ol>")
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
Put the number in a group of its own, `^(\d+)\.\s+(.*)$`, so you can read it when a list opens. `int("007")` is `7`.
--- hint
Only the item that opens a list looks at its number. Every non-item line closes an open list first. And a list still open when the input ends must be closed after the loop.
--- check case | A list that starts at 3
numbered_lists("3. c\n4. d")
=> '<ol start="3">\n<li>c</li>\n<li>d</li>\n</ol>'
--- check case | Starting at 1, and later numbers do not matter
numbered_lists("1. a\n5. b & c")
=> "<ol>\n<li>a</li>\n<li>b &amp; c</li>\n</ol>"
--- check case | Zero and leading zeros
[numbered_lists("0. zero"), numbered_lists("007. bond")]
=> ['<ol start="0">\n<li>zero</li>\n</ol>', '<ol start="7">\n<li>bond</li>\n</ol>']
--- check case | Text, a blank line and a new list
numbered_lists("Steps:\n2. mix\n\n1. bake\n1.no space")
=> '<p>Steps:</p>\n<ol start="2">\n<li>mix</li>\n</ol>\n<ol>\n<li>bake</li>\n</ol>\n<p>1.no space</p>'
--- check case | Empty input
numbered_lists("")
=> ""

+++ practice | Debug: a paragraph inside the list
--- task
`convert(md)` turns bullet lists, numbered lists and paragraphs into HTML, one entry per line. Lists and paragraphs work on their own. But ordinary text straight after a list ends up *inside* it: `"- a\nthen text"` gives

```
<ul>
<li>a</li>
<p>then text</p>
</ul>
```

where the list should close before the paragraph. Fix it.
--- starter
import re

BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    text = []
    open_kind = None

    def flush():
        nonlocal open_kind
        if text:
            html.append(f"<p>{' '.join(text)}</p>")
            text.clear()
        if open_kind:
            html.append(f"</{open_kind}>")
            open_kind = None

    def item(kind, words):
        nonlocal open_kind
        if open_kind != kind:
            flush()
            html.append(f"<{kind}>")
            open_kind = kind
        html.append(f"<li>{words}</li>")

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        bullet = BULLET.match(line)
        numbered = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif bullet:
            item("ul", bullet.group(1))
        elif numbered:
            item("ol", numbered.group(1))
        else:
            text.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- solution
import re

BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    text = []
    open_kind = None

    def flush():
        nonlocal open_kind
        if text:
            html.append(f"<p>{' '.join(text)}</p>")
            text.clear()
        if open_kind:
            html.append(f"</{open_kind}>")
            open_kind = None

    def item(kind, words):
        nonlocal open_kind
        if open_kind != kind:
            flush()
            html.append(f"<{kind}>")
            open_kind = kind
        html.append(f"<li>{words}</li>")

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        bullet = BULLET.match(line)
        numbered = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif bullet:
            item("ul", bullet.group(1))
        elif numbered:
            item("ol", numbered.group(1))
        else:
            if open_kind:
                flush()
            text.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
Follow `"- a\nthen text"` line by line. When `then text` arrives, the list is still open. What closes it, and when does that happen?
--- hint
`flush()` writes the paragraph first and closes the list second. So the list must be closed *before* the text goes into the paragraph buffer: in the ordinary-line branch, flush first if a list is open.
--- check case | Text after a list starts a paragraph after it
convert("- a\nthen text\nmore")
=> "<ul>\n<li>a</li>\n</ul>\n<p>then text more</p>"
--- check case | After a numbered list too
convert("1. one\n2. two\nend")
=> "<ol>\n<li>one</li>\n<li>two</li>\n</ol>\n<p>end</p>"
--- check case | Lists and paragraphs still work
convert("intro\n- x\n* y\n\n1. z")
=> "<p>intro</p>\n<ul>\n<li>x</li>\n<li>y</li>\n</ul>\n<ol>\n<li>z</li>\n</ol>"

+++ practice | Blocks in a log file
--- task
A test log groups its lines into named blocks:

```
BEGIN setup
connect
login
END
BEGIN run
step 1
END
```

Write `parse_blocks(lines)`, which returns a dict from each block's name to the list of its lines, here `{"setup": ["connect", "login"], "run": ["step 1"]}`.

- Every line is stripped, and blank lines are ignored everywhere.
- A line whose first word is `BEGIN` opens a block; the line `END` closes it. Every other line belongs to the open block.

Anything wrong raises `ValueError`. Line numbers count every line from 1, blank lines included. For a `BEGIN` line, check the rules in this order:

- a block is already open: `"line N: BEGIN inside block <open block's name>"`
- not exactly one name after `BEGIN`: `"line N: BEGIN needs one name"`
- the name was used before: `"line N: duplicate block <name>"`

and for the other lines:

- `END` with no block open: `"line N: END without BEGIN"`
- any other line with no block open: `"line N: text outside a block"`
- a block still open when the lines run out: `"unclosed block <name>"`.
--- starter
def parse_blocks(lines):
    blocks = {}
    for line in lines:
        if line.startswith("BEGIN"):
            name = line.split()[1]
            blocks[name] = []
        elif line != "END":
            blocks[name].append(line)
    return blocks
--- solution
def parse_blocks(lines):
    blocks = {}
    current = None
    for number, raw in enumerate(lines, start=1):
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line:
            continue
        words = line.split()
        if words[0] == "BEGIN":
            if current is not None:
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: BEGIN inside block {current}")
            if len(words) != 2:
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: BEGIN needs one name")
            if words[1] in blocks:
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: duplicate block {words[1]}")
            current = words[1]
            blocks[current] = []
        elif line == "END":
            if current is None:
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: END without BEGIN")
            current = None
        elif current is None:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: text outside a block")
        else:
            blocks[current].append(line)
    if current is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unclosed block {current}")
    return blocks
--- hint
This is a state machine with two states: outside any block (`None`), or inside the block with a given name. Each kind of line is allowed in one state and is an error in the other.
--- hint
Use `enumerate(lines, start=1)` for the line numbers, and skip blank lines after numbering. The unclosed-block check goes after the loop, when you know the lines have run out.
--- check case | Two blocks
parse_blocks(["BEGIN setup", "  connect  ", "", "login", "END", "BEGIN run", "step 1", "END"])
=> {"setup": ["connect", "login"], "run": ["step 1"]}
--- check test | An empty block, and no blocks at all
parse_blocks(["BEGIN empty", "END"]) == {"empty": []} and parse_blocks(["", "  "]) == {}
--- check case | Every error message
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_blocks(ls)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), ls=ls)) for ls in (["BEGIN a", "BEGIN b"], ["", "BEGIN"], ["BEGIN a b"], ["BEGIN a", "END", "BEGIN a"], ["END"], ["hello"], ["BEGIN a", "END", "", "stray"], ["BEGIN last", "x"])]
=> ["line 2: BEGIN inside block a", "line 2: BEGIN needs one name", "line 1: BEGIN needs one name", "line 3: duplicate block a", "line 1: END without BEGIN", "line 1: text outside a block", "line 4: text outside a block", "unclosed block last"]

=== pyp-08 | Markdown converter 4: code blocks and the command line
--- teach
Your converter handles inline formatting, headings, paragraphs and lists. The last feature is the **fenced code block**: lines of code between two [[fences|fence-name]], each fence a line of three backticks. A language name may follow the opening fence. Here is a Markdown document with one code block; the line numbers on the left are only there to talk about, they are not part of the text:

```
line 1:  ```python
line 2:  if x < 3:
line 3:      print("*not italic*")
line 4:  ```
```

Line 1 is the opening fence, with the language `python`. Line 4 is the closing fence.

Inside a fence, *nothing* is Markdown. No headings, no lists, no emphasis, and every space of indentation must survive, because in Python the indentation is part of the code. The only job is to escape it. The output is

```
<pre><code class="language-python">if x &lt; 3:
    print(&quot;*not italic*&quot;)</code></pre>
```

`<pre>` means "[[preformatted|pre-tag]]": show the text exactly as it is, spaces and line breaks included. The **[[class|language-class]]** attribute records the language. Without a language name, the tag is plain `<code>`.

### Step 1: recognize a fence

The opening fence is three backticks, maybe some spaces, maybe a language name. Its pattern:

```
^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$
```

In pieces:

- `^` and `$` pin it to the whole line.
- Three backticks are three real backticks. A backtick means nothing special in a regex, so it needs no backslash.
- `\s*` is zero or more spaces.
- `([\w+-]*)` is group 1, the language: zero or more characters that are `\w` (a "word character": a letter, a digit or `_`), `+` or `-`. The `+` and `-` let `c++` and `objective-c` through. The `*` means it may be empty, when there is no language.

```python
FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")
FENCE.match("```python").group(1)    # 'python'
FENCE.match("```").group(1)          # ''   no language
FENCE.match("``` c++").group(1)      # 'c++'
```

### Step 2: a loop that can jump ahead

Inside a code block, lines must not be stripped or read as Markdown. So the one-line-at-a-time `for` loop no longer fits. When you see an opening fence, you want to **[[consume|consume-lines]]** every line up to the closing fence in one go, and a `for` loop always hands you the very next line.

The fix is the index loop from the tokenizer lesson: keep a number `i` pointing at the current line, like a finger on the page. A branch can then move the finger as far as it needs. Here is its shape (the `if` line is written in words):

```python
lines = md.splitlines()
i = 0
while i < len(lines):
    if opening fence:
        i += 1                                 # step past the opening fence
        while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
            code.append(escape(lines[i]))      # the raw line, not stripped
            i += 1
        ...
    i += 1          # the next line; after a code block, this steps past the closing fence
```

The rest of the branches (blank, heading, bullet, numbered, text) stay as they were. Only the loop around them changes, and every line of them still works on the stripped `line = lines[i].strip()`.

### Step 3: a fence that is never closed

If the closing fence never comes, the code block runs to the end of the document. You get that for free: the inner loop's first test, `i < len(lines)`, becomes false at the end and the loop stops. That test must come [[first|short-circuit]], before the word `and`, so that `lines[i]` is never read past the last line.

### Step 4: build the block

A code block closes any open paragraph or list first, so call `flush()` before collecting. When the inner loop ends, put the whole block into *one* entry of `html`: the opening tags, the code lines joined with `"\n"`, and the closing tags.

```python
attr = f' class="language-{language}"' if language else ""
html.append(f"<pre><code{attr}>" + "\n".join(code) + "</code></pre>")
```

`attr` is either the class attribute (with a space in front) or nothing at all.

**Watch out:** stripping the code lines, or running them through `inline`. Both are easy to do by accident, because every other branch uses the stripped `line`. Only the fence *test* strips (`lines[i].strip()`); what you store is `escape(lines[i])`, the raw line.

### Step 5: the command line

Like the expense tracker, the converter becomes a program by adding a thin shell. Read all of standard input, convert it, print it:

```python
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(convert(sys.stdin.read()))
```

You need `import sys` at the top. Because `convert` takes a string and returns a string, the shell is one line, and everything that matters can be tested without it. On a real computer you would [[pipe a file through it|pipes]].

With this lesson you have built a real tool: a document converter with inline formatting, block structure, state, a mode for code, and a command-line interface. It is about a hundred lines, blank lines included, and each part can be tested on its own.

::: context fence-name Why "fence"
The three backticks stand like a fence around the code, marking where Markdown stops and starts again. Fenced code blocks were popularized by GitHub's version of Markdown and later written into CommonMark. The first Markdown had only one way to show code: indent every line by four spaces. Fences are easier to type and leave room for a language name. CommonMark also allows fences of three tildes (`~~~`), which this converter does not handle.
:::

::: context pre-tag Normally HTML ignores your spaces
In ordinary HTML, the browser squashes any run of spaces and line breaks into a single space, which is why joining paragraph lines with spaces was fine. That would ruin code. Inside `<pre>`, spaces and line breaks are kept exactly, and the text is usually shown in a fixed-width font, where every character takes the same room so columns line up. That is also why the code lines are joined with `"\n"` inside a single `<pre>` element.
:::

::: context language-class What the class is for
`class="language-python"` does not change anything by itself. It is a label that other programs read. Syntax highlighters, small programs that color keywords, strings and comments, look for a class starting with `language-` to know which rules to use. CommonMark recommends exactly this form, so your converter's output works with the common highlighters.
:::

::: context consume-lines One index, jumping ahead
The outer loop normally moves the index by one. The code branch moves it many lines at once, so the lines inside the fence are never seen by the other branches.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 180" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="16" y="24">0</text><rect x="34" y="10" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="25">text</text>
    <text x="16" y="52">1</text><rect x="34" y="38" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="53" font-family="monospace">fence python</text>
    <text x="16" y="80">2</text><rect x="34" y="66" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="81" font-family="monospace">if x &lt; 3:</text>
    <text x="16" y="108">3</text><rect x="34" y="94" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="109" font-family="monospace">    print(x)</text>
    <text x="16" y="136">4</text><rect x="34" y="122" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="137" font-family="monospace">fence</text>
    <text x="16" y="164">5</text><rect x="34" y="150" width="150" height="22" rx="3" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="42" y="165">after</text>
  </g>
  <path d="M190 49 C 250 49, 250 161, 190 161" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="190,161 200,156 200,166" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="250" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">one branch:</text>
  <text x="250" y="116" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">i goes 1 to 5</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context short-circuit Why the order inside the and matters
Python works out `a and b` from left to right, and stops as soon as the answer is known. If `a` is false, the whole thing is false, so `b` is never looked at. This is called **short-circuiting**. In the inner loop's test, `i < len(lines)` comes first. Once `i` reaches the end, that part is false, and `lines[i]` is never read. Swap the two parts and an unclosed fence crashes with `IndexError`.
:::

::: context pipes Using it for real
Save the program as `md.py`. In a terminal, `python3 md.py < notes.md > notes.html` feeds the file `notes.md` into standard input with `<`, and sends everything printed into `notes.html` with `>`. With `|`, the "pipe", one program's output becomes the next one's input: `cat notes.md | python3 md.py`. The program never knows or cares where its text came from, which is the whole point of reading standard input.
:::
--- task
Extend `convert`:

- A line that is three backticks, optionally followed by a language name (after stripping), opens a code block. The next line that strips to exactly three backticks closes it, or the end of the input does.
- Output the block as one entry: `<pre><code>`, then the raw lines, escaped but not stripped or formatted, joined with `"\n"`, then `</code></pre>`. With a language, the opening tag is `<code class="language-NAME">`.
- A code block closes any open paragraph or list first.

Then add the entry point under `if __name__ == "__main__":` that converts all of standard input and prints the result.
--- starter
# Markdown to HTML, step 3: lists.
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)


HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")
BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []
    list_kind = None

    def flush():
        nonlocal list_kind
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()
        if list_kind:
            html.append(f"</{list_kind}>")
            list_kind = None

    def list_item(kind, text):
        nonlocal list_kind
        if list_kind != kind:
            flush()
            html.append(f"<{kind}>")
            list_kind = kind
        html.append(f"<li>{inline(text)}</li>")

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        bullet = BULLET.match(line)
        numbered = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif heading:
            flush()
            level = len(heading.group(1))
            html.append(f"<h{level}>{inline(heading.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>")
        elif bullet:
            list_item("ul", bullet.group(1))
        elif numbered:
            list_item("ol", numbered.group(1))
        else:
            if list_kind:
                flush()
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- solution
# Markdown to HTML, step 4: code blocks and the command line.
import sys
import re

STRONG = re.compile(r"\*\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*\*")
EM = re.compile(r"\*(\S|\S.*?\S)\*")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[([^\]]+)\]\(([^)\s]+)\)")


def escape(text):
    """Make text safe to put inside HTML. The & must be replaced first."""
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = escape(text)
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\2">\1</a>', text)
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def inline(text):
    """Code spans first, so nothing inside backticks is formatted."""
    parts = re.split(r"(`[^`]+`)", text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[1:-1])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)


HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")
BULLET = re.compile(r"^[-*]\s+(.*)$")
NUMBERED = re.compile(r"^\d+\.\s+(.*)$")
FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []
    list_kind = None

    def flush():
        nonlocal list_kind
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{inline(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()
        if list_kind:
            html.append(f"</{list_kind}>")
            list_kind = None

    def list_item(kind, text):
        nonlocal list_kind
        if list_kind != kind:
            flush()
            html.append(f"<{kind}>")
            list_kind = kind
        html.append(f"<li>{inline(text)}</li>")

    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        line = lines[i].strip()
        fence = FENCE.match(line)
        heading = HEADING.match(line)
        bullet = BULLET.match(line)
        numbered = NUMBERED.match(line)
        if fence:
            flush()
            language = fence.group(1)
            code = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                code.append(escape(lines[i]))
                i += 1
            attr = f' class="language-{language}"' if language else ""
            html.append(f"<pre><code{attr}>" + "\n".join(code) + "</code></pre>")
        elif not line:
            flush()
        elif heading:
            flush()
            level = len(heading.group(1))
            html.append(f"<h{level}>{inline(heading.group(2).strip())}</h{level}>")
        elif bullet:
            list_item("ul", bullet.group(1))
        elif numbered:
            list_item("ol", numbered.group(1))
        else:
            if list_kind:
                flush()
            paragraph.append(line)
        i += 1
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(convert(sys.stdin.read()))
--- stdin

if total < 10:

Match the opening fence with the `FENCE` pattern from Step 1 on the stripped line; group 1 is the language, possibly empty. Flush, then collect `escape(lines[i])` until a line strips to three backticks.
--- hint
Build the block with `attr = f' class="language-{language}"' if language else ""` and append `f"<pre><code{attr}>" + "\n".join(code) + "</code></pre>"`. For the entry point: `import sys`, then `print(convert(sys.stdin.read()))` under the `__name__` guard.

=> "<pre><code>a\n\nb</code></pre>"

convert("```\n```")
=> "<pre><code></code></pre>"

+++ practice | Pull out the code blocks
--- task
Write `code_blocks(md)`, which returns every fenced code block in a Markdown document as a `(language, code)` pair, in order:

- An opening fence is a line that, after stripping, is three backticks, optionally followed by a language name (letters, digits, `_`, `+` or `-`). The language is `""` if there is none.
- The code is the raw lines after it (not stripped), joined with `"\n"`, up to the next line that strips to exactly three backticks, or to the end of the document if no fence closes it.
- No code blocks gives `[]`.
--- starter
def code_blocks(md):
    return [("", part) for part in md.split("```")[1::2]]
--- solution
import re

FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def code_blocks(md):
    blocks = []
    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        m = FENCE.match(lines[i].strip())
        if m:
            code = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                code.append(lines[i])
                i += 1
            blocks.append((m.group(1), "\n".join(code)))
        i += 1
    return blocks
--- hint
Use the index loop from the lesson, so that finding an opening fence can move `i` past the whole block in one go.
--- hint
After an opening fence, collect `lines[i]` (the raw line) while `i < len(lines)` and the stripped line is not three backticks. Test `i < len(lines)` first, so an unclosed block stops at the end instead of reading past it.
--- check case | Two blocks with languages, indentation kept
code_blocks("Intro\n```python\ndef f():\n    return 1\n```\ntext\n``` c++\nint x;\n```")
=> [("python", "def f():\n    return 1"), ("c++", "int x;")]
--- check case | No language, and an unclosed block
code_blocks("```\na\n\nb")
=> [("", "a\n\nb")]
--- check case | An empty block, and a document with none
[code_blocks("```\n```"), code_blocks("no code here")]
=> [[("", "")], []]

+++ practice | Code lines per language, from standard input
--- task
Write a program that reads a Markdown document from standard input and prints how many lines of code it has in each language.

- `code_lines(md)` returns a dict from each language to its number of code lines, over all fenced blocks (fences as in the lesson; an unclosed block runs to the end). A block with no language counts as `"text"`.
- `summary(md)` returns the output lines: `"<language>: <lines>"`, sorted by language, or `["no code"]` if there are no blocks.
- The entry point, under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, prints the summary of all of standard input, one line at a time. (The Input box holds a document.)
--- starter
import sys


def code_lines(md):
    return {}


def summary(md):
    return ["no code"]
--- solution
import re
import sys

FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def code_lines(md):
    counts = {}
    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        m = FENCE.match(lines[i].strip())
        if m:
            language = m.group(1) or "text"
            counts.setdefault(language, 0)
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                counts[language] += 1
                i += 1
        i += 1
    return counts


def summary(md):
    counts = code_lines(md)
    if not counts:
        return ["no code"]
    return [f"{language}: {n}" for language, n in sorted(counts.items())]


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for output_line in summary(sys.stdin.read()):
        print(output_line)
--- stdin

pip install orbit
```

Then in Python:

```python

ship = orbit.Ship()
ship.launch()
```

```
plain output
more output
```

```python
print("done")
```
--- hint
The counting is the lesson's index loop, adding 1 for each code line instead of storing it. `m.group(1) or "text"` gives the language, with `"text"` when the name is empty.
--- hint
Keep the three parts apart: `code_lines` counts, `summary` turns the counts into sorted lines, and the entry point only reads, calls and prints.
--- check output | Counts the document from standard input

text: 2
