+++ practice | Headings, but not inside code
--- task
Write `headings(md)`, which returns the `(level, text)` pairs of a document's headings, as in the headings lesson (1 to 6 `#`, a space, the text, on a stripped line; the text stripped).

The twist: a line inside a fenced code block is code, never a heading. A Python comment like `# set up the ship` inside a code block must not appear in the result. Fences work as in this lesson, and an unclosed block runs to the end of the document.
--- starter
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")


def headings(md):
    found = []
    for raw in md.splitlines():
        m = HEADING.match(raw.strip())
        if m:
            found.append((len(m.group(1)), m.group(2).strip()))
    return found
--- solution
import re

HEADING = re.compile(r"^(#{1,6})\s+(.*)$")
FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def headings(md):
    found = []
    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        line = lines[i].strip()
        if FENCE.match(line):
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                i += 1
        else:
            m = HEADING.match(line)
            if m:
                found.append((len(m.group(1)), m.group(2).strip()))
        i += 1
    return found
--- hint
The starter finds headings everywhere. Switch it to the index loop, so that an opening fence can skip straight past its block.
--- hint
On an opening fence, move `i` forward past every code line (you do not need to keep them). The final `i += 1` then steps past the closing fence.
--- check case | A comment in code is not a heading
headings("# Guide\n```python\n# set up the ship\nship = 1\n```\n## Launch")
=> [(1, "Guide"), (2, "Launch")]
--- check case | An unclosed block hides everything after it
headings("# Top\n```\n# not a heading\n## nor this")
=> [(1, "Top")]
--- check case | No code: the starter's cases still work
headings("#tag\n   ### Deep  \nplain")
=> [(3, "Deep")]

+++ practice | Longer fences
--- task
To show a line of three backticks inside a code block, Markdown lets a fence be *longer*. Write `code_blocks(md)`, returning `(language, code)` pairs as before, with these fence rules:

- An opening fence is a line that, after stripping, is three **or more** backticks, optionally followed by a language name (letters, digits, `_`, `+` or `-`).
- A block closes at a line that, after stripping, is only backticks, and at least as many as its opening fence had. A shorter run of backticks inside is just code.
- Code lines are kept raw. An unclosed block runs to the end.

For example, a block opened with four backticks can contain a line of three backticks as code, and closes at the next line of four (or more).
--- starter
import re

FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def code_blocks(md):
    blocks = []
    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        m = FENCE.match(lines[i].strip())
        if m:
            code = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                code.append(lines[i])
                i += 1
            blocks.append((m.group(1), "\n".join(code)))
        i += 1
    return blocks
--- solution
import re

OPENING = re.compile(r"^(```+)\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def closes(line, size):
    """A closing fence: only backticks, at least `size` of them."""
    text = line.strip()
    return len(text) >= size and text == "`" * len(text)


def code_blocks(md):
    blocks = []
    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        m = OPENING.match(lines[i].strip())
        if m:
            size = len(m.group(1))
            code = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and not closes(lines[i], size):
                code.append(lines[i])
                i += 1
            blocks.append((m.group(2), "\n".join(code)))
        i += 1
    return blocks
--- hint
Capture the backticks of the opening fence in a group, so you know how long it was. A backtick followed by `+` in a pattern means one or more backticks, so two backticks and then a third with `+` after it means three or more.
--- hint
A small helper `closes(line, size)` keeps the inner loop readable: strip the line, and check it is made only of backticks (`text == "`" * len(text)`) and has at least `size` of them.
--- check case | A four-backtick fence holds a three-backtick line
code_blocks("````md\n```\ninside\n```\n````\nafter")
=> [("md", "```\ninside\n```")]
--- check case | A longer closing fence is fine
code_blocks("```\nx\n`````\n```py\ny\n```")
=> [("", "x"), ("py", "y")]
--- check case | Two backticks are not a fence, and backticks with text do not close
[code_blocks("``\nnot code"), code_blocks("```\n``` not closed\nz")]
=> [[], [("", "``` not closed\nz")]]
--- check case | Unclosed long fence, and an empty block
[code_blocks("`````\na\n````"), code_blocks("````\n````")]
=> [[("", "a\n````")], [("", "")]]

+++ practice | Debug: the unclosed fence crashes
--- task
`convert(md)` turns paragraphs and fenced code blocks into HTML. Code blocks come out as `<pre><code>...</code></pre>` with their lines escaped and kept raw, and a code block with no closing fence should run to the end of the document.

Closed code blocks work. But a document whose last code block is never closed crashes with `IndexError: list index out of range`. Fix it.
--- starter
import re

FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{escape(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()

    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        line = lines[i].strip()
        if FENCE.match(line):
            flush()
            code = []
            i += 1
            while lines[i].strip() != "```" and i < len(lines):
                code.append(escape(lines[i]))
                i += 1
            html.append("<pre><code>" + "\n".join(code) + "</code></pre>")
        elif not line:
            flush()
        else:
            paragraph.append(line)
        i += 1
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- solution
import re

FENCE = re.compile(r"^```\s*([\w+-]*)\s*$")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{escape(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()

    lines = md.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        line = lines[i].strip()
        if FENCE.match(line):
            flush()
            code = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != "```":
                code.append(escape(lines[i]))
                i += 1
            html.append("<pre><code>" + "\n".join(code) + "</code></pre>")
        elif not line:
            flush()
        else:
            paragraph.append(line)
        i += 1
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- hint
When the last line has been read, `i` becomes `len(lines)`. Which part of the inner loop's test runs first at that moment?
--- hint
`and` checks its left side first and stops if that is false. So put `i < len(lines)` on the left, and `lines[i]` is only read when `i` is a real position.
--- check case | An unclosed block runs to the end
convert("text\n```\na < b\n  indented")
=> "<p>text</p>\n<pre><code>a &lt; b\n  indented</code></pre>"
--- check case | A fence on the very last line
convert("```")
=> "<pre><code></code></pre>"
--- check case | Closed blocks still work
convert("```py\nx = 1\n```\nafter")
=> "<pre><code>x = 1</code></pre>\n<p>after</p>"

+++ practice | A config file with multi-line values
--- task
A settings file has one setting per line, `key = value`. A long value can span several lines, written between a marker of your choice, like this:

```
name = orbit
motd <<END
Welcome aboard!
  # this line is part of the message
END
```

Write `parse_config(text)`, which returns a dict from each key to its value (a string), here `{"name": "orbit", "motd": "Welcome aboard!\n  # this line is part of the message"}`.

- A **setting** line, after stripping, is a key (one or more letters, digits or `_`), optional spaces, `=`, and the value, which is stripped and may be empty.
- A line that **starts a multi-line value**, after stripping, is a key, optional spaces, `<<`, optional spaces, and a marker (letters, digits or `_`). The value is the raw lines after it, joined with `"\n"`, up to a line that strips to exactly the marker.
- Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped, but only outside a multi-line value; inside one they are kept.

Raise `ValueError`, where `N` is the line number counting from 1:

- a key that was already set: `"line N: duplicate key <key>"`, where `N` is the line of the second key;
- a multi-line value whose marker never comes: `"line N: unclosed <<MARKER"`, where `N` is the line of its key;
- any other line: `"line N: bad line"`.
--- starter
def parse_config(text):
    config = {}
    for line in text.splitlines():
        key, _, value = line.partition("=")
        config[key.strip()] = value.strip()
    return config
--- solution
import re

SETTING = re.compile(r"^(\w+)\s*=(.*)$")
MULTI = re.compile(r"^(\w+)\s*<<\s*(\w+)$")


def parse_config(text):
    config = {}
    lines = text.splitlines()
    i = 0
    while i < len(lines):
        number = i + 1
        line = lines[i].strip()
        multi = MULTI.match(line)
        setting = SETTING.match(line)
        found = multi or setting
        key = found.group(1) if found else None
        if key in config:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: duplicate key {key}")
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            pass
        elif multi:
            marker = multi.group(2)
            body = []
            i += 1
            while i < len(lines) and lines[i].strip() != marker:
                body.append(lines[i])
                i += 1
            if i == len(lines):
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: unclosed <<{marker}")
            config[key] = "\n".join(body)
        elif setting:
            config[key] = setting.group(2).strip()
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: bad line")
        i += 1
    return config
--- hint
Two patterns, one per kind of line: `^(\w+)\s*=(.*)$` for a setting and `^(\w+)\s*<<\s*(\w+)$` for the start of a multi-line value. A line that matches neither (and is not blank or a comment) is a bad line.
--- hint
A multi-line value needs the index loop: move `i` forward, collecting raw lines, until a line strips to the marker or the lines run out. If `i` reached `len(lines)`, the marker never came.
--- hint
Check for a duplicate key as soon as you know a line's key, before reading its value. Save `number = i + 1` at the top of the loop body, because `i` moves on inside a multi-line value.
--- check case | Settings and a multi-line value
parse_config("# server\nname = orbit\nport=8080\n\nmotd <<END\nWelcome aboard!\n\n  # kept\nEND\nempty =")
=> {"name": "orbit", "port": "8080", "motd": "Welcome aboard!\n\n  # kept", "empty": ""}
--- check test | An empty multi-line value, and an empty file
parse_config("note << E\nE") == {"note": ""} and parse_config("") == {}
--- check case | Every error, with its line number
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_config(t)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), t=t)) for t in ("a = 1\na = 2", "\nx <<EOF\ntext", "just words", "ok = 1\n\nbad key = 3", "m <<END\nEND\nm = again")]
=> ["line 2: duplicate key a", "line 2: unclosed <<EOF", "line 1: bad line", "line 3: bad line", "line 3: duplicate key m"]

=== pyp-09 | Library system 1: the data model
--- teach
Last lesson finished the Markdown converter: a pure `convert` function that turns text into HTML, with a one-line shell around it. The third project is bigger in a different way. It is a lending library: books, members, loans, rules, and saving everything to disk. You will build it over four lessons, one layer at a time. This first lesson builds the layer everything else stands on: the data.

Picture a small library before computers. There is a card for every book and a card for every member. Behind the desk sits a clerk. You never walk round the desk and scribble on the cards yourself; you ask the clerk, and the clerk follows the library's rules. Your program will work the same way.

The classes and rules that describe the real-world thing a program is about (its **[[domain|domain-word]]**) are called a **domain model**. Most business software is exactly this: a domain model with rules, plus **persistence**, which means saving the data so it is still there after the program stops. Persistence is lesson 3.

**Step 1: find the nouns.** Read a description of the problem and underline the nouns: *books*, *members*, *loans*, *the library*. Each noun is a candidate for a class. Loans are next lesson. This one builds books, members and the library.

**Step 2: decide what identifies each one.** An **identifier** (an **id** for short) is a value that picks out exactly one record and never changes. A book is identified by its **[[ISBN|isbn]]**. A member is identified by a **member id**, such as `"m1"`. Ids are what you use as [[dict keys|ids-as-keys]], and what other records store when they need to point at something.

A book is only data, so it is a dataclass. Remember from the advanced course: `@dataclass` reads the annotated fields and writes `__init__`, `__repr__` and `__eq__` for you.

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1

print(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert"))
# Book(isbn='111', title='Dune', author='Frank Herbert', copies=1)
```

`copies` is how many physical copies of the book the library owns. It has a default of `1`, so it must come last: fields with defaults go after fields without. `Member` has the same shape with two fields, `member_id` and `name`.

**Step 3: one object owns the collections.** The `Library` class is the clerk. It holds two dicts: `books` maps each ISBN to its `Book`, and `members` maps each member id to its `Member`. Every change goes [[through its methods|one-door]]. That is where the rules live, such as "a member id cannot be used twice" and "adding a book that already exists adds copies". A second delivery of *Dune* does not make a second card; it raises the count on the first one:

```python
lib = Library()
lib.add_book(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 2))
lib.add_book(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 3))
print(lib.books["111"].copies)     # 5
```

**Step 4: an exception of your own.** When the library refuses something, the code that called it needs to tell "the library said no" apart from "there is a bug". You built exception classes in the intermediate course. Here one line of body is enough (`pass` means "nothing to add"):

```python
class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass
```

Now the code that talks to the user can [[catch just this kind of failure|catching-one-kind]] and show its message:

```python
try:
    lib.add_book(Book("222", "Emma", "Jane Austen", 0))
except LibraryError as err:
    print("refused:", err)     # refused: a book needs at least one copy
```

A real bug, like a `TypeError` from a typo, is not a `LibraryError`, so it is not caught. It still stops the program, and you get to see it.

**Step 5: keep your own copy.** The advanced course's debugging lesson showed **aliasing**: two names pointing at one object, so a change through one shows through the other. If `add_book` stored the caller's own `Book` object, the library and the caller would [[share one object|aliasing-picture]]:

```python
store = {}
mine = Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")
store["111"] = mine              # the SAME object, not a copy
store["111"].copies += 4
print(mine.copies)               # 5: your Book changed too
```

It works the other way too: the caller could change the library's record without asking the clerk. The fix is `replace` from the `dataclasses` module. `replace(book)` makes a new object with the same fields. You can also change some fields as you copy, by naming them:

```python
from dataclasses import replace

mine = Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")
stored = replace(mine)           # a new Book with the same fields
stored.copies += 4
print(mine.copies, stored.copies)     # 1 5
print(replace(mine, copies=4))
# Book(isbn='111', title='Dune', author='Frank Herbert', copies=4)
```

So the library stores `replace(book)`, and owns its [[own record|shallow-replace]].

**Step 6: lookups that fail loudly.** `book(isbn)` returns the record, or raises `LibraryError("no such book: ...")` if there is none. `member(member_id)` does the same for members. Every later method can call these and trust the answer, instead of repeating `if isbn not in self.books` everywhere. Stopping with a clear message at the first sign of trouble is called [[failing fast|fail-fast]].

```python
lib.book("999")
# LibraryError: no such book: 999
```

**Step 7: a search.** `find_by_author(text)` finds books whose author *contains* some text, ignoring upper and lower case. The trick is to lowercase both sides before using `in`:

```python
"austen" in "JANE AUSTEN".lower()     # True
sorted(["Persuasion", "Emma"])        # ['Emma', 'Persuasion']
```

**Watch out:** keying things by name. Two members can both be called Sam, and titles get corrected. Ids do not change. With names as keys, the second Sam silently replaces the first, because assigning to an existing dict key overwrites it:

```python
members = {}
members["Sam"] = Member("m1", "Sam")
members["Sam"] = Member("m2", "Sam")    # the first Sam is gone
print(len(members))                     # 1
```

::: context domain-word What "domain" means here
A **domain** is the part of the real world a program is about. For this project it is lending books. For a bank it is accounts and payments. For a spacecraft's ground software it is commands, telemetry and passes over ground stations.

Programmers who study a domain talk to the people who work in it, learn their words, and use the same words as class and method names. That is why the library's classes are called `Book`, `Member` and `Loan`, and not `Item`, `Person` and `Record`. When the code speaks the domain's language, the librarian and the programmer can read the rules together and spot a wrong one.
:::

::: context isbn The number above the barcode
**ISBN** stands for International Standard Book Number. Look at the back of almost any book: the number above the barcode is its ISBN. Since 2007 it has 13 digits; older books have a 10-digit one.

Each edition of a book gets its own ISBN, so the hardback and the paperback of the same novel have different numbers. That makes it a good identifier for this program: one ISBN, one kind of book on the shelf. Libraries and shops all over the world use it for exactly this. In the lessons the ISBNs are short, like `"111"`, to keep the examples easy to read.
:::

::: context ids-as-keys A dict is a card index
A dict keyed by id works like a drawer of index cards sorted by number. Give it the key and it goes straight to the one card, without reading all the others.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="22" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" font-weight="700">books</text>
  <rect x="20" y="34" width="70" height="30" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <rect x="20" y="90" width="70" height="30" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="55" y="110" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">"222"</text>
  <line x1="90" y1="49" x2="170" y2="49" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>

  <rect x="178" y="30" width="162" height="38" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="188" y="54" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Book: Dune, 5 copies</text>

  <text x="259" y="142" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">values: the records</text>
</svg>
```

Next lesson a loan will store just the ISBN and the member id, and use them as keys to find the full records.
:::

::: context one-door One door in
Making every change go through an object's methods is called **encapsulation**: the data is wrapped up, and the methods are the only door in. Python does not lock the door. Any code *could* write `lib.books["111"].copies = 0`. The convention is that it does not, and a good design gives it no reason to.

The payoff is that each rule is written in one place. If the library later decides that no book may have more than 50 copies, you change `add_book`, and every part of the program obeys the new rule at once.
:::

::: context catching-one-kind Why one exception class helps
`except LibraryError` catches `LibraryError` and any subclass of it, and nothing else. So the caller can write one `except` that handles every "the library refused" case, whether the book was missing, the member was unknown or a rule was broken.

The alternative is worse in both directions. `except Exception` would also swallow real bugs and hide them. Catching `ValueError` would mix the library's refusals with errors from `int("abc")` and other unrelated code. Your own class draws a clear line around "failures that belong to this domain".
:::

::: context aliasing-picture Shared object or own copy
On the left, two names point at one object, so a change through either name shows through both. On the right, `replace` made a second object, and each name has its own.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="85" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="700">aliasing</text>
  <text x="270" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="700">replace(mine)</text>
  <text x="14" y="48" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">mine</text>
  <text x="14" y="108" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">store["111"]</text>
  <line x1="50" y1="44" x2="102" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="92" y1="104" x2="102" y2="92" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="104" y="62" width="70" height="36" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="139" y="78" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Book</text>
  <text x="139" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">copies 5</text>
  <line x1="190" y1="10" x2="190" y2="160" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1"/>
  <text x="204" y="54" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">mine</text>
  <text x="204" y="124" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">stored</text>
  <line x1="240" y1="50" x2="264" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="250" y1="120" x2="264" y2="120" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="266" y="32" width="80" height="36" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="306" y="48" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Book</text>
  <text x="306" y="62" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">copies 1</text>
  <rect x="266" y="102" width="80" height="36" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="306" y="118" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Book</text>
  <text x="306" y="132" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">copies 5</text>
</svg>
```

You can test which case you are in with `is`: `stored is mine` is `False` after `replace`.
:::

::: context shallow-replace replace copies one level deep
`replace` makes a **shallow** copy, like `list(x)` in the aliasing lesson: a new `Book`, but each field still points at the same value as before. For a `Book` that is fine. Its fields are strings and an int, and those can never be changed in place, only replaced. So nothing is left to share by accident.

If a dataclass had a list field, say `tags`, the copy and the original would share that one list. Then you would need `copy.deepcopy`, which copies all the way down. Knowing which of your fields can change in place tells you which copy you need.
:::

::: context fail-fast Fail fast
**Failing fast** means stopping with a clear message at the first moment something is wrong, instead of carrying on with bad data. A missing book found in `book()` gives "no such book: 999" right there. A missing book discovered three functions later gives a confusing `KeyError` or `None` somewhere else, and you have to work backwards to find the cause.

Flight software follows the same idea through fault detection: a sensor reading that makes no sense is flagged at once and the system moves to a safe response, rather than letting the bad number travel on into the guidance calculations.
:::
--- task
Write:

- `class LibraryError(Exception)`.
- Dataclasses `Book(isbn, title, author, copies=1)` and `Member(member_id, name)`.
- `Library` with `books` and `members` dicts, and these methods:
  - `add_book(book)`: if the ISBN is new, store a **copy** of the book (use `replace`); if the ISBN is already there, add `book.copies` to the stored book's copies. A book with fewer than 1 copy raises `LibraryError`. Return the stored book.
  - `add_member(member)`: store the member under its id. A member id that is already used raises `LibraryError`.
  - `book(isbn)` and `member(member_id)`: return the record, or raise `LibraryError` with the message `"no such book: <isbn>"` or `"no such member: <id>"`.
  - `find_by_author(text)`: the titles of the books whose author contains `text`, ignoring upper and lower case, sorted A to Z.
--- starter
# Library system, step 1: the data model.
from dataclasses import dataclass


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.members = {}
--- solution
# Library system, step 1: the data model.
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.members = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {member.member_id} already exists")
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def book(self, isbn):
        if isbn not in self.books:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such book: {isbn}")
        return self.books[isbn]

    def member(self, member_id):
        if member_id not in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such member: {member_id}")
        return self.members[member_id]

    def find_by_author(self, text):
        text = text.lower()
        return sorted(b.title for b in self.books.values() if text in b.author.lower())
--- hint
Start with the two dataclasses, like `Book` in the explanation. Each is `@dataclass` over a class with annotated fields, and `copies: int = 1` goes last because it has a default.
--- hint
In `add_book`, check the copies first and raise if there are fewer than 1. Then, if `book.isbn in self.books`, add to `self.books[book.isbn].copies`; otherwise store `replace(book)` (add `replace` to the `from dataclasses import` line). `add_member` is the same shape: check `member.member_id in self.members` before storing.
--- hint
`book(isbn)` checks `if isbn not in self.books:` and raises `LibraryError(f"no such book: {isbn}")`, then returns `self.books[isbn]`; `member` is the same with members. `find_by_author` lowercases `text` once, then returns `sorted(b.title for b in self.books.values() if text in b.author.lower())`.
--- check test | add_book stores books by ISBN
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")), lib.book("111").title)[1] == "Dune" and lib.book("111").copies == 1)(Library())
--- check case | Adding the same ISBN again adds copies
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 2)), lib.add_book(Book("111", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 3)), lib.book("111").copies)[-1])(Library())
=> 5
--- check test | The library keeps its own copy of the book
(lambda lib, b: (lib.add_book(b), lib.add_book(Book("1", "T", "A", 4)), b.copies)[-1] == 1)(Library(), Book("1", "T", "A"))
?? Store replace(book), not the caller's object.
--- check test | Zero copies is rejected
raises(LibraryError, lambda: Library().add_book(Book("1", "T", "A", 0)))
--- check test | Duplicate members are rejected
(lambda lib: (lib.add_member(Member("m1", "Ada")), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.add_member(Member("m1", "Sam"))))[1])(Library())
--- check test | Unknown ids raise LibraryError, which is an Exception
raises(LibraryError, lambda: Library().book("nope")) and raises(LibraryError, lambda: Library().member("nope")) and issubclass(LibraryError, Exception)
--- check case | find_by_author ignores case and sorts titles
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Emma", "Jane Austen")), lib.add_book(Book("2", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")), lib.add_book(Book("3", "Persuasion", "JANE AUSTEN")), lib.find_by_author("austen"))[-1])(Library())
=> ["Emma", "Persuasion"]

+++ practice | A fleet of ships
--- task
Write the data model for a space agency's fleet:

- `class FleetError(Exception)`.
- A dataclass `Ship` with `code` (str), `name` (str) and `crew` (int, default `0`).
- A class `Fleet` with a dict `ships` from each code to its `Ship`, and these methods:
  - `add(ship)` stores a **copy** of the ship and returns the stored copy. A code that is already used raises `FleetError(f"ship {code} already exists")`; a negative crew raises `FleetError` too.
  - `ship(code)` returns the stored ship, or raises `FleetError(f"no such ship: {code}")`.
  - `names()` returns the ships' names sorted A to Z (two ships may share a name).
  - `crew_total()` returns the crew of all the ships added up.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class Fleet:
    def __init__(self):
        self.ships = []

    def add(self, ship):
        self.ships.append(ship)
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class FleetError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Ship:
    code: str
    name: str
    crew: int = 0


class Fleet:
    def __init__(self):
        self.ships = {}

    def add(self, ship):
        if ship.code in self.ships:
            raise FleetError(f"ship {ship.code} already exists")
        if ship.crew < 0:
            raise FleetError("crew cannot be negative")
        self.ships[ship.code] = replace(ship)
        return self.ships[ship.code]

    def ship(self, code):
        if code not in self.ships:
            raise FleetError(f"no such ship: {code}")
        return self.ships[code]

    def names(self):
        return sorted(s.name for s in self.ships.values())

    def crew_total(self):
        return sum(s.crew for s in self.ships.values())
--- hint
The code is the ship's id, so it is the dict key. Every rule lives in a method: `add` refuses a used code or a negative crew before storing anything.
--- hint
Store `replace(ship)`, a new `Ship` with the same fields, so the caller's object and the fleet's record are two separate objects. `ship(code)` raises when the code is not in the dict, and returns the record otherwise.
--- check case | Ships are stored by code
(lambda f: (f.add(Ship("S1", "Vega", 4)), f.add(Ship("S2", "Altair")), f.ship("S2"), f.names(), f.crew_total())[2:])(Fleet())
=> (Ship("S2", "Altair", 0), ["Altair", "Vega"], 4)
--- check test | The fleet keeps its own copy
(lambda f, s: (f.add(s), setattr(s, "crew", 99), f.ship("S1").crew, f.add(Ship("S2", "B")) is not None)[2:] == (3, True))(Fleet(), Ship("S1", "A", 3))
--- check test | Used codes and negative crews are refused
(lambda f: (f.add(Ship("S1", "Vega")), raises(FleetError, lambda: f.add(Ship("S1", "Other"))), raises(FleetError, lambda: f.add(Ship("S3", "Bad", -1))), len(f.ships))[1:])(Fleet()) == (True, True, 1)
--- check case | An unknown code, with its message
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    Fleet().ship('S9')\nexcept FleetError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "no such ship: S9"
--- check test | Two ships may share a name, and an empty fleet
(lambda f: (f.add(Ship("B", "Vega")), f.add(Ship("A", "Vega")), f.names())[-1] == ["Vega", "Vega"] and Fleet().names() == [] and Fleet().crew_total() == 0)(Fleet())

+++ practice | A parts store that hands out copies
--- task
Write the model for a store of spare parts:

- `class StoreError(Exception)`, and a dataclass `Part` with `sku` (str, the part's id), `name` (str) and `qty` (int, default `1`).
- A class `Store` with a dict `parts` from each SKU to its `Part`, and these methods:
  - `receive(part)`: a quantity below 1 raises `StoreError`. A new SKU stores a copy of the part; a known SKU adds `part.qty` to the stored quantity.
  - `get(sku)` returns a **copy** of the stored part, so that changing what it returns never changes the store. An unknown SKU raises `StoreError(f"no such part: {sku}")`.
  - `take(sku, qty)` removes `qty` of a part and returns how many are left. An unknown SKU, a `qty` below 1, or more than the store holds raises `StoreError`, and nothing changes. A part taken down to 0 stays in the store with a quantity of 0.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class StoreError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Part:
    sku: str
    name: str
    qty: int = 1


class Store:
    def __init__(self):
        self.parts = {}
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class StoreError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Part:
    sku: str
    name: str
    qty: int = 1


class Store:
    def __init__(self):
        self.parts = {}

    def _part(self, sku):
        if sku not in self.parts:
            raise StoreError(f"no such part: {sku}")
        return self.parts[sku]

    def receive(self, part):
        if part.qty < 1:
            raise StoreError("a delivery needs at least one part")
        if part.sku in self.parts:
            self.parts[part.sku].qty += part.qty
        else:
            self.parts[part.sku] = replace(part)

    def get(self, sku):
        return replace(self._part(sku))

    def take(self, sku, qty):
        part = self._part(sku)
        if qty < 1 or qty > part.qty:
            raise StoreError(f"cannot take {qty} of {sku}")
        part.qty -= qty
        return part.qty
--- hint
Aliasing works in both directions. Storing a copy protects the store from the caller's object; handing out a copy protects it from what the caller does with the answer.
--- hint
A private helper `_part(sku)` that raises for an unknown SKU and returns the stored record gives both `get` and `take` their lookup. `get` returns `replace(...)` of that record; `take` changes the record itself.
--- check case | Deliveries add up
(lambda s: (s.receive(Part("P1", "bolt", 10)), s.receive(Part("P1", "bolt", 5)), s.receive(Part("P2", "nut")), s.get("P1"), s.get("P2"))[-2:])(Store())
=> (Part("P1", "bolt", 15), Part("P2", "nut", 1))
--- check test | Changing what get returns does not change the store
(lambda s: (s.receive(Part("P1", "bolt", 10)), setattr(s.get("P1"), "qty", 0), s.get("P1").qty)[-1] == 10)(Store())
--- check test | take, down to zero
(lambda s: (s.receive(Part("P1", "bolt", 3)), s.take("P1", 2), s.take("P1", 1), s.get("P1").qty)[1:])(Store()) == (1, 0, 0)
--- check test | Bad requests raise StoreError and change nothing
(lambda s: (s.receive(Part("P1", "bolt", 3)), raises(StoreError, lambda: s.take("P1", 4)), raises(StoreError, lambda: s.take("P1", 0)), raises(StoreError, lambda: s.take("P9", 1)), raises(StoreError, lambda: s.get("P9")), raises(StoreError, lambda: s.receive(Part("P2", "nut", 0))), s.get("P1").qty, "P2" in s.parts)[1:])(Store()) == (True, True, True, True, True, 3, False)

+++ practice | The catalogue, sorted by author
--- task
The starter has `Book` and a `Library` with `add_book` from the lesson. Add a method `catalogue()` that returns the lines of a printed catalogue:

- one line per book: the author left-aligned in 16 characters, the title left-aligned in 20, then the number of copies right-aligned in 3;
- sorted by author, and books by the same author by title;
- an empty library gives `["no books"]`.

For example, a library with *Emma* and *Persuasion* by Jane Austen (1 and 2 copies) and *Dune* by Frank Herbert (3 copies) gives:

```
Frank Herbert   Dune                  3
Jane Austen     Emma                  1
Jane Austen     Persuasion            2
```
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def catalogue(self):
        if not self.books:
            return ["no books"]
        ordered = sorted(self.books.values(), key=lambda b: (b.author, b.title))
        return [f"{b.author:<16}{b.title:<20}{b.copies:>3}" for b in ordered]
--- hint
The books are the values of `self.books`. Sort them with a tuple key, the author first and the title second, as the expense report sorted its rows.
--- hint
Each line is one f-string: `:<16` for the author, `:<20` for the title and `:>3` for the copies. Check for an empty library first.
--- check case | Sorted by author, then title
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_book(b) for b in (Book("3", "Persuasion", "Jane Austen", 2), Book("1", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 3), Book("2", "Emma", "Jane Austen"))], lib.catalogue())[-1])(Library())
=> ["Frank Herbert   Dune                  3", "Jane Austen     Emma                  1", "Jane Austen     Persuasion            2"]
--- check case | Extra copies show up
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")), lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 9)), lib.catalogue())[-1])(Library())
=> ["Frank Herbert   Dune                 10"]
--- check case | An empty library
Library().catalogue()
=> ["no books"]

+++ practice | A member changes their id
--- task
The starter has `Member` and a `Library` with `add_member` and `member` from the lesson. Add `rename_member(old_id, new_id)`, which moves a member to a new id and returns the stored member:

- An unknown `old_id` raises `LibraryError(f"no such member: {old_id}")`.
- A `new_id` that is empty or only spaces raises `LibraryError`.
- If `new_id` is the same as `old_id`, nothing changes.
- A `new_id` used by another member raises `LibraryError(f"member {new_id} already exists")`.
- Otherwise the member is stored under the new id only, and its `member_id` field says the new id too.

The `Member` object the caller first passed to `add_member` must not change: build the renamed record with `replace`. When `rename_member` raises, nothing changes.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.members = {}

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {member.member_id} already exists")
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def member(self, member_id):
        if member_id not in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such member: {member_id}")
        return self.members[member_id]

    def rename_member(self, old_id, new_id):
        member = self.members.pop(old_id)
        member.member_id = new_id
        self.members[new_id] = member
        return member
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.members = {}

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {member.member_id} already exists")
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def member(self, member_id):
        if member_id not in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such member: {member_id}")
        return self.members[member_id]

    def rename_member(self, old_id, new_id):
        current = self.member(old_id)
        if not new_id.strip():
            raise LibraryError("a member id cannot be empty")
        if new_id == old_id:
            return current
        if new_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {new_id} already exists")
        renamed = replace(current, member_id=new_id)
        del self.members[old_id]
        self.members[new_id] = renamed
        return renamed
--- hint
Check every rule before changing anything, so a refused rename leaves the dict exactly as it was. The lookup `self.member(old_id)` already raises for an unknown id.
--- hint
`add_member` stored the caller's own object, so changing its field would change the caller's object too. `replace(current, member_id=new_id)` builds a new record with one field changed; then delete the old key and store the new record under the new key.
--- check test | The member moves to the new id
(lambda lib: (lib.add_member(Member("m1", "Ada")), lib.rename_member("m1", "m9"), sorted(lib.members), lib.member("m9"))[1:])(Library()) == (Member("m9", "Ada"), ["m9"], Member("m9", "Ada"))
--- check test | The caller's own Member is not changed
(lambda lib, m: (lib.add_member(m), lib.rename_member("m1", "m2"), m.member_id)[-1] == "m1")(Library(), Member("m1", "Ada"))
--- check test | The same id changes nothing
(lambda lib: (lib.add_member(Member("m1", "Ada")), lib.rename_member("m1", "m1"), sorted(lib.members))[1:])(Library()) == (Member("m1", "Ada"), ["m1"])
--- check test | Refused renames change nothing
(lambda lib: (lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), lib.add_member(Member("b", "Bo")), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.rename_member("a", "b")), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.rename_member("zz", "c")), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.rename_member("a", "  ")), sorted(lib.members), lib.member("a"))[2:])(Library()) == (True, True, True, ["a", "b"], Member("a", "Ada"))

+++ practice | Debug: the shop that shares its records
--- task
A record shop keeps albums in a `Catalogue`. `add(album)` stores a new album by its code, or, for a code it already has, adds the delivery's `stock` to the stored album. `stock(code)` returns how many copies the shop has.

The bug: the shop's records are the caller's own objects. So after `mine = Album("A1", "Blue", "Joni Mitchell", 2)`, adding `mine`, and then adding a second delivery of 3, `mine.stock` has changed to 5 as well. And changing `mine.stock` after adding it changes the shop's count. Fix it so the shop owns its own records.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


@dataclass
class Album:
    code: str
    title: str
    artist: str
    stock: int = 1


class Catalogue:
    def __init__(self):
        self.albums = {}

    def add(self, album):
        if album.code in self.albums:
            self.albums[album.code].stock += album.stock
        else:
            self.albums[album.code] = album

    def stock(self, code):
        return self.albums[code].stock if code in self.albums else 0
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


@dataclass
class Album:
    code: str
    title: str
    artist: str
    stock: int = 1


class Catalogue:
    def __init__(self):
        self.albums = {}

    def add(self, album):
        if album.code in self.albums:
            self.albums[album.code].stock += album.stock
        else:
            self.albums[album.code] = replace(album)

    def stock(self, code):
        return self.albums[code].stock if code in self.albums else 0
--- hint
After `self.albums[album.code] = album`, how many `Album` objects are there: one or two?
--- hint
Assigning an object to a dict key stores a reference to the *same* object. Store `replace(album)`, a new album with the same fields, instead.
--- check test | A later delivery does not change the caller's album
(lambda c, mine: (c.add(mine), c.add(Album("A1", "Blue", "Joni Mitchell", 3)), mine.stock, c.stock("A1"))[2:] == (2, 5))(Catalogue(), Album("A1", "Blue", "Joni Mitchell", 2))
--- check test | Changing the caller's album does not change the shop
(lambda c, mine: (c.add(mine), setattr(mine, "stock", 100), c.stock("A1"))[-1] == 2)(Catalogue(), Album("A1", "Blue", "Joni Mitchell", 2))
--- check test | Stock still adds up, and unknown codes have none
(lambda c: (c.add(Album("A1", "Blue", "J")), c.add(Album("A1", "Blue", "J", 4)), c.stock("A1"), c.stock("Z9"))[2:] == (5, 0))(Catalogue())

+++ practice | Search by several words
--- task
The starter has `Book` and a `Library` with `add_book`. Add `search(query)`, a search box for the library's website:

- The query is split into words, and case is ignored everywhere.
- A book matches if **every** word appears somewhere in its title or in its author (as part of a word is fine: `"aust"` matches `Austen`).
- Return the ISBNs of the matching books, sorted by title, and books with the same title by ISBN.
- A query with no words (empty, or only spaces) raises `LibraryError("empty search")`.

For example, with *Emma* by Jane Austen and *Dune* by Frank Herbert, `search("jane emma")` is the ISBN of *Emma*, and `search("e")` is both, *Dune* first.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def search(self, query):
        return [b.isbn for b in self.books.values() if query in b.title]
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def search(self, query):
        words = query.lower().split()
        if not words:
            raise LibraryError("empty search")

        def matches(book):
            title, author = book.title.lower(), book.author.lower()
            return all(w in title or w in author for w in words)

        found = sorted((b for b in self.books.values() if matches(b)), key=lambda b: (b.title, b.isbn))
        return [b.isbn for b in found]
--- hint
Lowercase the query once and split it into words. A small helper that answers "does this book match?" keeps the method readable.
--- hint
`all(w in title or w in author for w in words)` is true only when every word is found in one of the two (both lowercased). Sort the matching books by `(title, isbn)`, then keep only their ISBNs.
--- check case | Every word must match, in title or author
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_book(b) for b in (Book("1", "Emma", "Jane Austen"), Book("2", "Dune", "Frank Herbert"), Book("3", "Persuasion", "Jane Austen"))], lib.search("JANE emma"), lib.search("aust"), lib.search("e"))[1:])(Library())
=> (["1"], ["1", "3"], ["2", "1", "3"])
--- check case | Same titles are ordered by ISBN, and no match gives nothing
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_book(b) for b in (Book("9", "Dune", "A"), Book("4", "Dune", "B"))], lib.search("dune"), lib.search("dune zebra"))[1:])(Library())
=> (["4", "9"], [])
--- check test | An empty query is refused
raises(LibraryError, lambda: Library().search("")) and raises(LibraryError, lambda: Library().search("   "))

=== pyp-10 | Library system 2: loans and rules
--- teach
Last lesson built the library's data: `Book`, `Member`, and a `Library` that owns them and refuses bad changes with a `LibraryError`. But a library exists to *lend* books, and that is this lesson.

Lending comes with **[[rules|business-rules]]**. A member can borrow at most three books. A member cannot borrow the same book twice at once. Nobody can borrow a book when every copy is already out. Rules like these are the heart of most real software, and they deserve care. This lesson has five small ideas, one step each.

**Step 1: a loan is a record too.** A **loan** links one member to one book, with a **due date**: the day the book must come back.

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date

@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date
```

Notice what it stores: the member's **id** and the book's **ISBN**, not the `Member` and `Book` objects themselves. Ids never change. They are easy to save to a file, which is next lesson. And the book's title and copies live in exactly one place, the `Book` in `library.books`, so there is only one copy of them to keep up to date. When you need the full record, you [[look it up by its id|lookup-by-id]].

All the library's loans go in one list, `self.loans`, which starts empty.

**Step 2: derive, do not duplicate.** How many copies of a book are available to borrow right now? You could store a number, `available`, on each book, and remember to change it on every checkout and every return. Or you could **derive** it, which means work it out from data you already have:

```
available = copies - (number of loans of that ISBN)
```

In Python, counting the matching loans is a `sum` of one `1` for each match:

```python
sum(1 for loan in self.loans if loan.isbn == isbn)
```

The worked-out answer can never disagree with the loans, because it *is* the loans. A stored count can [[drift away from the truth|drift]] the first time someone forgets to update it. Keep a **single source of truth** wherever you can: each fact stored in one place only.

**Step 3: check every rule before changing anything.** `checkout(member_id, isbn, today)` has five things to check: the member exists, the book exists, the member does not already have this book, the member has fewer than three loans, and a copy is available. Only after all five pass does it add the loan.

The order matters. If the method added the loan first and checked afterwards, a failed check would leave a loan that should never have existed. When a method either does everything or [[changes nothing at all|all-or-nothing]], the data can never be left half-updated.

`any(...)` is handy for the "already has this book" check. It is `True` if at least one item is true:

```python
any(loan.isbn == isbn for loan in current)    # does any current loan have this ISBN?
```

**Step 4: time is an input.** The due date is 14 days after today. You could call `date.today()` inside `checkout`. But then a test like "does a loan made on 1 March fall due on 15 March?" could only be run on 1 March. So `checkout` takes `today` as an argument. That is the dependency injection idea from the expert course's design lesson: the method is *given* the date instead of reaching for the clock.

To add days to a date you need a **timedelta**: a length of time, such as "14 days". It lives in the same `datetime` module as `date`. A date plus a timedelta is a new date, and it [[knows about the calendar|calendar-aware]]:

```python
from datetime import date, timedelta

print(date(2026, 3, 1) + timedelta(days=14))     # 2026-03-15
print(date(2026, 12, 25) + timedelta(days=14))   # 2027-01-08
```

**Step 5: name your numbers.** `14` and `3` in the middle of a method say nothing about what they mean. **Named constants** at the top of the file do:

```python
LOAN_DAYS = 14
MAX_LOANS = 3
```

The capital letters are a convention that says "this never changes while the program runs". Each rule now has [[one place to change it|magic-numbers]]: if the library moves to three-week loans, you edit one line.

**Returning a book** is the checkout in reverse: find the loan with that member id and ISBN, take it out of the list, and give it back. `enumerate` gives you the position of each loan, and `self.loans.pop(i)` removes the item at position `i` and returns it. If no loan matches, that is a `LibraryError` too.

**Watch out:** returning `True` or `False` from `checkout` to mean "it worked" or "it was refused". The caller has to check the answer, and one day will forget, and then the program carries on as if a refused loan went through. A raised `LibraryError` [[cannot be ignored silently|silent-failure]], and its message says *why* it was refused.

::: context business-rules Where the rules come from
Programmers call rules like "at most three loans" **business rules**: they come from the people who run the library, not from Python. A different library might allow five loans, or none at all for members who owe fines.

That is why the rules deserve care. They change more often than anything else in the program, and getting one wrong costs real people something. Keeping them in named constants and a few clearly written methods means a librarian could read them with you and say "yes, that is our rule".
:::

::: context lookup-by-id Following an id to its record
A loan says `isbn="111"`. To show the title, the program follows that id: `library.books["111"].title`. Databases do the same thing on a large scale. A table of loans stores a book's id in each row, and that stored id is called a **foreign key**, because it is the key of a row in a *different* table.

The big advantage shows up when a record changes. If a title is corrected, it is corrected in one `Book`, and every loan that points at it sees the new title at once. Nothing has to be updated in the loans.
:::

::: context drift How a stored count goes wrong
Here is a stored count drifting away from the truth. The checkout remembered to update it; the return forgot.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" font-weight="700">event</text>
  <text x="170" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">loans</text>
  <text x="245" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">derived</text>
  <text x="315" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">stored</text>
  <line x1="10" y1="28" x2="350" y2="28" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="10" y="52" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">2 copies, start</text>
  <text x="170" y="52" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="245" y="52" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="315" y="52" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="10" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Ada borrows</text>
  <text x="170" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="245" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="315" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="10" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Ada returns</text>
  <text x="170" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="245" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <rect x="298" y="98" width="34" height="20" rx="3" fill="#f2b880"/>
  <text x="315" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="140" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">one forgotten update, and the stored count is wrong forever</text>
</svg>
```

The derived count is worked out from the loans every time, so it cannot be left behind.
:::

::: context all-or-nothing Go or no-go before you commit
Before a rocket launch, the launch director polls every team: range, weather, propulsion, guidance. Each answers "go" or "no go". Only when every answer is "go" does the countdown continue. Nobody fuels half the rocket and then asks the weather team.

`checkout` works the same way: every check first, then one change. Databases have a name for an operation that either happens completely or not at all: a **transaction**. You will meet the word again whenever data is saved somewhere that must never be left half-written.
:::

::: context calendar-aware Dates handle the calendar for you
Adding 14 to the day number by hand would give "32 March", which does not exist. A `date` plus a `timedelta` rolls over into the next month, and the next year, and gets February right in leap years:

```python
from datetime import date, timedelta
print(date(2028, 2, 20) + timedelta(days=14))    # 2028-03-05
```

2028 is a leap year, so February has 29 days, and 14 days after 20 February is 5 March. Calendar arithmetic is full of traps like this, which is why engineers use the library instead of writing their own.
:::

::: context magic-numbers Magic numbers
A bare number in the middle of code, like `if len(current) >= 3`, is nicknamed a **magic number**: it works, but nobody can tell why it is 3, or whether the 3 in some other method is the same rule or a different one.

A named constant answers both questions. `MAX_LOANS` says what the number means, and every place that uses the rule uses the same name. Change it once, and the whole program follows.
:::

::: context silent-failure Refusals that cannot be missed
Some older languages, such as C, often report failure by returning a special value, like `-1`. Nothing forces the caller to look at it, and forgotten checks of this kind are a well-known source of bugs: the program carries on with a result that never happened.

An exception turns that around. If nobody catches a `LibraryError`, the program stops and prints it. The only way to ignore it is to write an `except` on purpose, and then you have at least decided to.
:::
--- task
Add loans to the library:

- The constants `LOAN_DAYS = 14` and `MAX_LOANS = 3` at the top of the file, and a dataclass `Loan(member_id, isbn, due)`.
- `Library.loans`: a list, starting empty in `__init__`.
- `available(isbn)`: the book's copies minus the number of current loans of that book. An unknown ISBN raises `LibraryError`.
- `loans_of(member_id)`: a list of that member's loans. An unknown member raises `LibraryError`.
- `checkout(member_id, isbn, today)`: checks, in this order, that the member and the book exist, that the member does not already have this book, that they have fewer than `MAX_LOANS` loans, and that a copy is available. Any failed check raises `LibraryError`. Then it adds a `Loan` due `LOAN_DAYS` after `today` to `self.loans`, and returns it.
- `return_book(member_id, isbn)`: removes that loan from `self.loans` and returns it, or raises `LibraryError` if that member has no loan of that book.
--- starter
# Library system, step 1: the data model.
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.members = {}

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {member.member_id} already exists")
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def book(self, isbn):
        if isbn not in self.books:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such book: {isbn}")
        return self.books[isbn]

    def member(self, member_id):
        if member_id not in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such member: {member_id}")
        return self.members[member_id]

    def find_by_author(self, text):
        text = text.lower()
        return sorted(b.title for b in self.books.values() if text in b.author.lower())
--- solution
# Library system, step 2: loans and rules.
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace
from datetime import date, timedelta

LOAN_DAYS = 14
MAX_LOANS = 3


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.members = {}
        self.loans = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"member {member.member_id} already exists")
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def book(self, isbn):
        if isbn not in self.books:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such book: {isbn}")
        return self.books[isbn]

    def member(self, member_id):
        if member_id not in self.members:
            raise LibraryError(f"no such member: {member_id}")
        return self.members[member_id]

    def find_by_author(self, text):
        text = text.lower()
        return sorted(b.title for b in self.books.values() if text in b.author.lower())

    def available(self, isbn):
        book = self.book(isbn)
        return book.copies - sum(1 for loan in self.loans if loan.isbn == isbn)

    def loans_of(self, member_id):
        self.member(member_id)
        return [loan for loan in self.loans if loan.member_id == member_id]

    def checkout(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        current = self.loans_of(member_id)
        self.book(isbn)
        if any(loan.isbn == isbn for loan in current):
            raise LibraryError(f"{member_id} already has {isbn}")
        if len(current) >= MAX_LOANS:
            raise LibraryError("loan limit reached")
        if self.available(isbn) < 1:
            raise LibraryError(f"no copies of {isbn} available")
        loan = Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=LOAN_DAYS))
        self.loans.append(loan)
        return loan

    def return_book(self, member_id, isbn):
        for i, loan in enumerate(self.loans):
            if loan.member_id == member_id and loan.isbn == isbn:
                return self.loans.pop(i)
        raise LibraryError(f"{member_id} has not borrowed {isbn}")
--- hint
Start with the new pieces from the explanation: `from datetime import date, timedelta`, the two constants, the `Loan` dataclass, and `self.loans = []` in `__init__`.
--- hint
Reuse the lookups from last lesson so unknown ids raise for free. `available` is `self.book(isbn).copies` minus `sum(1 for loan in self.loans if loan.isbn == isbn)`. `loans_of` calls `self.member(member_id)` first, then returns the loans whose `member_id` matches.
--- hint
In `checkout`, do every check before changing anything: `current = self.loans_of(member_id)`, then `self.book(isbn)`, then `any(...)` for a loan of this ISBN in `current`, then `len(current) >= MAX_LOANS`, then `self.available(isbn) < 1`. Only then make `Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=LOAN_DAYS))`, append it and return it. For `return_book`, loop with `for i, loan in enumerate(self.loans):` and `return self.loans.pop(i)` on a match; after the loop, raise.
--- check case | A checkout is due 14 days later
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "Frank Herbert")), lib.add_member(Member("m1", "Ada")), lib.checkout("m1", "1", date(2026, 3, 1)))[-1])(Library())
=> Loan("m1", "1", date(2026, 3, 15))
--- check case | available counts loans
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "F", 2)), lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), lib.add_member(Member("b", "Bo")), lib.available("1"), lib.checkout("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), lib.available("1"), lib.checkout("b", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), lib.available("1"))[3::2])(Library())
=> (2, 1, 0)
--- check test | No copies left
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "F")), lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), lib.add_member(Member("b", "Bo")), lib.checkout("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.checkout("b", "1", date(2026, 1, 1))))[-1])(Library())
--- check test | The same book twice is refused
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "F", 5)), lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), lib.checkout("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.checkout("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1))))[-1])(Library())
--- check test | At most three loans each
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_book(Book(str(i), f"B{i}", "A")) for i in range(4)], lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), [lib.checkout("a", str(i), date(2026, 1, 1)) for i in range(3)], raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.checkout("a", "3", date(2026, 1, 1))), len(lib.loans_of("a")))[3:])(Library()) == (True, 3)
--- check test | Unknown member or book
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "F")), lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.checkout("zz", "1", date(2026, 1, 1))), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.checkout("a", "9", date(2026, 1, 1))), lib.loans)[2:])(Library()) == (True, True, [])
--- check test | Returning frees the copy; returning twice is refused
(lambda lib: (lib.add_book(Book("1", "Dune", "F")), lib.add_member(Member("a", "Ada")), lib.checkout("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), lib.return_book("a", "1").isbn, lib.available("1"), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.return_book("a", "1")))[3:])(Library()) == ("1", 1, True)

+++ practice | A tool rental shop
--- task
A tool shop has one of each tool and rents them out for a week. Write:

- The constants `RENT_DAYS = 7` and `MAX_RENTALS = 2`, `class RentalError(Exception)`, and a dataclass `Rental(customer, item, due)`, where `due` is a `date`.
- `Shop(items)`, made with a list of the tool names the shop owns, and a list `rentals` that starts empty. Its methods:
  - `rented_by(customer)`: the names of the tools that customer has, sorted.
  - `rent(customer, item, today)`: checks, in this order, that the shop owns the tool, that it is not already rented out, and that the customer has fewer than `MAX_RENTALS` tools. Any failed check raises `RentalError`. Then it adds a `Rental` due `RENT_DAYS` after `today`, and returns it.
  - `give_back(customer, item)`: removes that rental and returns it, or raises `RentalError` if that customer does not have that tool.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta


class Shop:
    def __init__(self, items):
        self.items = items
        self.rentals = []

    def rent(self, customer, item, today):
        self.rentals.append((customer, item))
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta

RENT_DAYS = 7
MAX_RENTALS = 2


class RentalError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Rental:
    customer: str
    item: str
    due: date


class Shop:
    def __init__(self, items):
        self.items = set(items)
        self.rentals = []

    def rented_by(self, customer):
        return sorted(r.item for r in self.rentals if r.customer == customer)

    def rent(self, customer, item, today):
        if item not in self.items:
            raise RentalError(f"no such tool: {item}")
        if any(r.item == item for r in self.rentals):
            raise RentalError(f"{item} is already rented out")
        if len(self.rented_by(customer)) >= MAX_RENTALS:
            raise RentalError("rental limit reached")
        rental = Rental(customer, item, today + timedelta(days=RENT_DAYS))
        self.rentals.append(rental)
        return rental

    def give_back(self, customer, item):
        for i, rental in enumerate(self.rentals):
            if rental.customer == customer and rental.item == item:
                return self.rentals.pop(i)
        raise RentalError(f"{customer} does not have {item}")
--- hint
Whether a tool is free is not stored anywhere: it is free when no rental in the list has that item. Work it out with `any(...)` when you need it.
--- hint
Do every check before appending anything. The due date is `today + timedelta(days=RENT_DAYS)`. For `give_back`, loop with `enumerate` and `pop` the matching rental; raise after the loop if none matched.
--- check case | A rental is due a week later, even across a month
(lambda s: s.rent("ada", "drill", date(2026, 1, 28)))(Shop(["drill", "saw"]))
=> Rental("ada", "drill", date(2026, 2, 4))
--- check test | A tool that is out, or not owned, is refused
(lambda s: (s.rent("ada", "drill", date(2026, 1, 1)), raises(RentalError, lambda: s.rent("bo", "drill", date(2026, 1, 1))), raises(RentalError, lambda: s.rent("bo", "laser", date(2026, 1, 1))), len(s.rentals))[1:])(Shop(["drill", "saw"])) == (True, True, 1)
--- check test | At most two tools each
(lambda s: ([s.rent("ada", t, date(2026, 1, 1)) for t in ("saw", "drill")], raises(RentalError, lambda: s.rent("ada", "ladder", date(2026, 1, 1))), s.rented_by("ada"), s.rent("bo", "ladder", date(2026, 1, 1)).customer)[1:])(Shop(["drill", "saw", "ladder"])) == (True, ["drill", "saw"], "bo")
--- check test | Giving back frees the tool; giving back twice is refused
(lambda s: (s.rent("ada", "saw", date(2026, 1, 1)), s.give_back("ada", "saw").item, s.rented_by("ada"), raises(RentalError, lambda: s.give_back("ada", "saw")), s.rent("bo", "saw", date(2026, 1, 2)).due)[1:])(Shop(["saw"])) == ("saw", [], True, date(2026, 1, 9))

+++ practice | Renewing a loan
--- task
The starter has `Loan`, now with a `renewals` count (default 0), and a small `Library` whose `lend` records a loan without any checks. Add `renew(member_id, isbn, today)`, which gives a member more time:

- If that member has no loan of that book, raise `LibraryError`.
- A loan that is already overdue (`today` is after its due date) cannot be renewed: raise `LibraryError`. Renewing on the due date itself is fine.
- A loan can be renewed at most `MAX_RENEWALS` times: after that, raise `LibraryError`.
- Otherwise the loan's new due date is `LOAN_DAYS` after `today`, its `renewals` goes up by one, and `renew` returns the loan.

A refused renewal changes nothing.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta

LOAN_DAYS = 14
MAX_RENEWALS = 2


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date
    renewals: int = 0


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.loans = []

    def lend(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        loan = Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=LOAN_DAYS))
        self.loans.append(loan)
        return loan

    def renew(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        pass
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta

LOAN_DAYS = 14
MAX_RENEWALS = 2


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date
    renewals: int = 0


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.loans = []

    def lend(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        loan = Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=LOAN_DAYS))
        self.loans.append(loan)
        return loan

    def find_loan(self, member_id, isbn):
        for loan in self.loans:
            if loan.member_id == member_id and loan.isbn == isbn:
                return loan
        raise LibraryError(f"{member_id} has not borrowed {isbn}")

    def renew(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        loan = self.find_loan(member_id, isbn)
        if today > loan.due:
            raise LibraryError("an overdue loan cannot be renewed")
        if loan.renewals >= MAX_RENEWALS:
            raise LibraryError("renewal limit reached")
        loan.due = today + timedelta(days=LOAN_DAYS)
        loan.renewals += 1
        return loan
--- hint
Finding the loan is a job of its own: a helper that returns the matching loan, or raises when there is none, keeps `renew` short.
--- hint
Dates compare the way you would hope: `today > loan.due` is true when today is later. Check both rules before changing the loan's `due` and `renewals`.
--- check case | Renewing gives 14 days from today
(lambda lib: (lib.lend("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 1)), lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 10)))[-1])(Library())
=> Loan("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 24), 1)
--- check test | On the due date is fine; the day after is too late
(lambda lib: (lib.lend("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 1)), lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 15)).due, raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 3, 30))), lib.loans[0].renewals)[1:])(Library()) == (date(2026, 3, 29), True, 1)
--- check test | At most two renewals, and the third changes nothing
(lambda lib: (lib.lend("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 5)), lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 10)), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.renew("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 12))), lib.loans[0])[3:])(Library()) == (True, Loan("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 24), 2))
--- check test | No such loan
(lambda lib: (lib.lend("a", "1", date(2026, 1, 1)), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.renew("b", "1", date(2026, 1, 2))), raises(LibraryError, lambda: lib.renew("a", "2", date(2026, 1, 2))))[1:])(Library()) == (True, True)

+++ practice | Who has the most books out
--- task
The starter has `Member`, `Loan` and a small `Library` with `add_member` and `lend`. Add `loan_report()`, which returns one line per member, **every** member, even those with no loans:

- the member's name left-aligned in 12 characters, then their number of current loans right-aligned in 3;
- most loans first, and members with the same number by name;
- no members at all gives `["no members"]`.

Work the counts out from `self.loans`; do not store them.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.members = {}
        self.loans = []

    def add_member(self, member):
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def lend(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        self.loans.append(Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=14)))
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.members = {}
        self.loans = []

    def add_member(self, member):
        self.members[member.member_id] = member

    def lend(self, member_id, isbn, today):
        self.loans.append(Loan(member_id, isbn, today + timedelta(days=14)))

    def loan_report(self):
        if not self.members:
            return ["no members"]
        counts = {member_id: 0 for member_id in self.members}
        for loan in self.loans:
            counts[loan.member_id] += 1
        rows = sorted(((self.members[m].name, n) for m, n in counts.items()), key=lambda r: (-r[1], r[0]))
        return [f"{name:<12}{n:>3}" for name, n in rows]
--- hint
Start every member at 0 (a dict comprehension over `self.members` does it), then add 1 for each loan. Members with no loans are then already in the dict.
--- hint
Turn the counts into `(name, count)` pairs, look the name up by id, and sort on `(-count, name)`.
--- check case | Most loans first, members with none included
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_member(m) for m in (Member("a", "Ada"), Member("b", "Bo"), Member("c", "Cy"))], [lib.lend(m, i, date(2026, 1, 1)) for m, i in (("b", "1"), ("c", "2"), ("b", "3"))], lib.loan_report())[-1])(Library())
=> ["Bo            2", "Cy            1", "Ada           0"]
--- check case | Ties by name
(lambda lib: ([lib.add_member(m) for m in (Member("z", "Zoe"), Member("m", "Max"))], lib.loan_report())[-1])(Library())
=> ["Max           0", "Zoe           0"]
--- check case | No members
Library().loan_report()
=> ["no members"]

+++ practice | Borrow several books at once
--- task
The starter is the library from the lesson. Add `checkout_many(member_id, isbns, today)`, which lends a member several books in one go, **all or nothing**:

- It checks, in this order: the member exists; no ISBN appears twice in `isbns`; for each ISBN, the book exists, the member does not already have it, and a copy is available; and the member's current loans plus the new ones come to at most `MAX_LOANS`.
- Any failed check raises `LibraryError`, and **no** loan is added, not even for the books that were fine.
- Otherwise it adds one `Loan` per ISBN, each due `LOAN_DAYS` after `today`, and returns them as a list in the order given.
- An empty list of ISBNs returns `[]` and changes nothing (the member must still exist).
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace
from datetime import date, timedelta

LOAN_DAYS = 14
MAX_LOANS = 3


class LibraryError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str
    author: str
    copies: int = 1


@dataclass
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str


@dataclass
class Loan:
    member_id: str
    isbn: str
    due: date


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.members = {}
        self.loans = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        if book.copies < 1:
            raise LibraryError("a book needs at least one copy")
        if book.isbn in self.books:
            self.books[book.isbn].copies += book.copies
        else:
            self.books[book.isbn] = replace(book)
        return self.books[book.isbn]

    def add_member(self, member):
        if member.member_id in self.members:
