overdue_members([], date(2026, 1, 1))
=> []

+++ practice | The fines report
--- task
Write `fines_report(loans, today)`, a report for the front desk. `loans` is a list of `(member, due)` pairs, and a member can have several. The fine is 20 cents for every day each loan is late. The starter has `format_cents`.

- One line per member who owes something: the name left-aligned in 10 characters, then `format_cents(fine)` right-aligned in 8.
- The biggest fine first, and equal fines by name.
- Then a line of 18 `-` characters, then `TOTAL` laid out the same way with all the fines added up.
- If nobody owes anything, return `["no fines"]`.

For example, on 20 March, loans due on 15 March and 18 March for Ada are 5 + 2 = 7 days late, so Ada's line is `"ada           1.40"`.
--- starter
from datetime import date

FINE_PER_DAY = 20


def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


def fines_report(loans, today):
    return ["no fines"]
--- solution
from datetime import date

FINE_PER_DAY = 20


def format_cents(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100:,}.{cents % 100:02d}"


def days_late(due, today):
    return max(0, (today - due).days)


def fines_report(loans, today):
    fines = {}
    for member, due in loans:
        fine = days_late(due, today) * FINE_PER_DAY
        if fine:
            fines[member] = fines.get(member, 0) + fine
    if not fines:
        return ["no fines"]
    rows = sorted(fines.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
    lines = [f"{member:<10}{format_cents(fine):>8}" for member, fine in rows]
    lines.append("-" * 18)
    lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<10}{format_cents(sum(fines.values())):>8}")
    return lines
--- hint
Write the lesson's `days_late` helper for one due date first. Each loan's fine is its late days times 20; add each member's fines up in a dict, leaving out loans that are not late.
--- hint
Then it is the expense report again: sort the dict's items on `(-fine, name)`, lay out each row with `:<10` and `:>8`, and add the rule and the TOTAL line.
--- check case | Two members, worst first
fines_report([("ada", date(2026, 3, 15)), ("bo", date(2026, 3, 19)), ("ada", date(2026, 3, 18)), ("cy", date(2026, 3, 25))], date(2026, 3, 20))
=> ["ada           1.40", "bo            0.20", "------------------", "TOTAL         1.60"]
--- check case | Equal fines by name
fines_report([("zoe", date(2026, 1, 1)), ("max", date(2026, 1, 1))], date(2026, 1, 11))
=> ["max           2.00", "zoe           2.00", "------------------", "TOTAL         4.00"]
--- check case | Nobody owes anything
[fines_report([("ada", date(2026, 3, 20))], date(2026, 3, 20)), fines_report([], date(2026, 3, 20))]
=> [["no fines"], ["no fines"]]

+++ practice | A grace period and a cap
--- task
A library is kind to members who are a little late. Write `fee(due, returned)`, the fee in cents for a book due on `due` and returned on `returned` (both dates):

- Returned early, on time, or up to 2 days late: no fee, `0`.
- 3 or more days late: 25 cents for **every** late day, the first two included. So 3 days late is 75 cents.
- The fee is never more than 500 cents.
--- starter
from datetime import date


def fee(due, returned):
    return (returned - due).days * 25
--- solution
from datetime import date

GRACE_DAYS = 2
FEE_PER_DAY = 25
MAX_FEE = 500


def fee(due, returned):
    late = (returned - due).days
    if late <= GRACE_DAYS:
        return 0
    return min(late * FEE_PER_DAY, MAX_FEE)
--- hint
Work out how many days late the book is, then deal with the three cases in order: within the grace period (or early), charged, and capped.
--- hint
`min(fee, 500)` gives the smaller of the two, so it caps the fee in one step, the way `max(0, days)` stops the days going below zero. Name the numbers as constants.
--- check case | Early, on time, and inside the grace period
[fee(date(2026, 3, 15), date(2026, 3, d)) for d in (10, 15, 16, 17)]
=> [0, 0, 0, 0]
--- check case | Just past the grace period counts every day
[fee(date(2026, 3, 15), date(2026, 3, d)) for d in (18, 19, 25)]
=> [75, 100, 250]
--- check case | The cap, and across a month
[fee(date(2026, 3, 1), date(2026, 3, 21)), fee(date(2026, 3, 1), date(2026, 5, 1)), fee(date(2026, 1, 30), date(2026, 2, 3))]
=> [500, 500, 100]

+++ practice | Debug: reminders miss today
--- task
`reminders(loans, today, within=3)` lists the titles of the books due back from `today` to `within` days after it, both ends included, soonest first and then by title. Each loan is a `(title, due)` pair.

The reminders work for books due tomorrow and later. But a book due **today** never gets a reminder. Fix it.
--- starter
from datetime import date


def reminders(loans, today, within=3):
    soon = [(due, title) for title, due in loans if 0 < (due - today).days <= within]
    return [title for due, title in sorted(soon)]
--- solution
from datetime import date


def reminders(loans, today, within=3):
    soon = [(due, title) for title, due in loans if 0 <= (due - today).days <= within]
    return [title for due, title in sorted(soon)]
--- hint
What is `(due - today).days` for a book due today? Does that number pass the test?
--- hint
"Both ends included" means 0 (today) counts as well as `within`: the first comparison must be `<=`, not `<`.
--- check case | Due today gets a reminder
reminders([("Dune", date(2026, 3, 15)), ("Emma", date(2026, 3, 17))], date(2026, 3, 15))
=> ["Dune", "Emma"]
--- check case | Soonest first, then by title, both ends included
reminders([("Ulysses", date(2026, 3, 18)), ("Beloved", date(2026, 3, 15)), ("Arcadia", date(2026, 3, 18)), ("Late", date(2026, 3, 14)), ("Far", date(2026, 3, 19))], date(2026, 3, 15))
=> ["Beloved", "Arcadia", "Ulysses"]
--- check case | A window of 0 days is just today
reminders([("Dune", date(2026, 3, 15)), ("Emma", date(2026, 3, 16))], date(2026, 3, 15), within=0)
=> ["Dune"]

+++ practice | The longest silence
--- task
A ground station logs the dates it heard from a probe. Write `longest_gap(dates)`, which finds the longest silence: the most days between two contacts that come one after the other.

- The dates may be in any order, and a date may appear more than once.
- Return `(start, end, days)`: the two dates on either side of the gap, and the number of days between them.
- If two gaps are equally long, return the earlier one.
- With fewer than two different dates there is no gap: return `None`.

For example, contacts on 1 March, 4 March and 10 March give `(date(2026, 3, 4), date(2026, 3, 10), 6)`.
--- starter
from datetime import date


def longest_gap(dates):
    return (min(dates), max(dates), (max(dates) - min(dates)).days)
--- solution
from datetime import date


def longest_gap(dates):
    days = sorted(set(dates))
    best = None
    for earlier, later in zip(days, days[1:]):
        gap = (later - earlier).days
        if best is None or gap > best[2]:
            best = (earlier, later, gap)
    return best
--- hint
Sort the different dates (a set drops the repeats). Then the gaps are between neighbours in that sorted list.
--- hint
`zip(days, days[1:])` pairs each date with the next one. Keep the best `(start, end, days)` so far, and replace it only when a gap is strictly longer, so ties keep the earlier gap.
--- check case | Three contacts
longest_gap([date(2026, 3, 10), date(2026, 3, 1), date(2026, 3, 4)])
=> (date(2026, 3, 4), date(2026, 3, 10), 6)
--- check case | Repeats, and a tie keeps the earlier gap
longest_gap([date(2026, 1, 5), date(2026, 1, 1), date(2026, 1, 5), date(2026, 1, 9)])
=> (date(2026, 1, 1), date(2026, 1, 5), 4)
--- check case | Across a year
longest_gap([date(2025, 12, 20), date(2026, 1, 15), date(2026, 1, 16)])
=> (date(2025, 12, 20), date(2026, 1, 15), 26)
--- check test | No gap at all
longest_gap([]) is None and longest_gap([date(2026, 1, 1)]) is None and longest_gap([date(2026, 1, 1), date(2026, 1, 1)]) is None

=== pyp-13 | Capstone: a to-do manager with undo
--- teach
The library system is finished. For twelve lessons, every step was laid out for you, and the code in the editor was always your program from the lesson before. That changes now.

The last four lessons are **capstones**: projects that show you can build something on your own. For each one you get a [[specification|spec-reading]] and an empty file. There are no step-by-step instructions, and the checks test only what your program *does*, never how it is built inside. So the design is yours.

This first capstone is a to-do list with an **undo** button.

### How to approach a specification

Think of a builder handed a drawing of a finished house. The drawing says what the house must look like. It does not say which wall to build first. That part is the builder's job, and here it is yours. Five steps help.

**1. Read it twice, then write every behavior as a one-line example.** A **behavior** is one thing the program must do in one situation. Writing them down turns a paragraph of rules into a checklist you can tick off:

```
add("buy milk")                    returns 1
add("   ")                         raises ValueError
undo() with nothing to undo        returns False
```

**2. Choose the data model first.** The **data model** is what you store, and in what shape. Most of the difficulty of a program is [[decided right here|tables-first]]. For this to-do list, one good shape is a dict from each id to a small record, plus one counter holding the next id to hand out:

```python
items = {
    1: {"title": "pay rent", "priority": "high", "tags": {"home"}, "done": False},
    2: {"title": "email Sam", "priority": "normal", "tags": set(), "done": True},
}
next_id = 3
```

**3. Build a skeleton.** A **skeleton** is the class with every method written down but doing nothing yet: each body is `pass`. It already runs, so you can try it at once. Then make one behavior work at a time, and try each one as you go.

```python
class TodoList:
    def __init__(self):
        pass

    def add(self, title, priority="normal", tags=()):
        pass
```

**4. Hunt for [[edge cases|edge-case]].** An edge case is an unusual input at the very edge of what is allowed: an empty title, an id that does not exist, marking something done twice, undoing when there is nothing left to undo. Bugs like to live there.

**5. [[Refactor|refactor]] once it works.** Refactoring means changing how the code is written without changing what it does: clearer names, repeated code moved into one small helper.

### Sorting the pending list

`pending()` must list the most important items first, and the oldest first among items of the same priority. You met this in the expense tracker's report: sort with a **tuple key**, and Python compares the first parts, then uses the second parts to break ties. Priorities are words, so turn each word into a rank number first:

```python
rank = {"high": 0, "normal": 1, "low": 2}
sorted([(rank["low"], 1), (rank["high"], 2)])     # [(0, 2), (2, 1)]
```

Ids count up, so a smaller id means an older item. A key of (rank, id) gives exactly the order the spec asks for.

### What undo has to remember

Undo means "put things back the way they were before my last change". Press it again and it goes back one more step. So the program must remember a whole history, newest last, and each undo takes the newest step off the end.

A list used this way (add to the end, take from the end) is a [[stack|undo-stack]], which you met in the typing lesson of the expert course. `append` pushes a step on; `pop()` takes the last one back off.

### Two ways to build undo

There are two classic designs for what goes on that stack.

**Snapshots.** Picture photographing a whiteboard before every change. To undo, you wipe the board and copy the last photo back onto it. In code: before every change, push a copy of the whole state; `undo()` pops the last copy and makes it the state again. It is simple and hard to get wrong. It costs memory, but a to-do list is tiny, so that does not matter here.

**Commands.** Picture a notebook where you write down each change instead: "added item 3", "marked item 1 done". To undo, you read the last line and do the opposite. In code, each change is recorded as a [[small object that knows how to reverse itself|command-pattern]]: an `add` is undone by removing the item, a `remove` by putting the item back. It uses less memory, and it needs more code.

Either is a good answer. Choose one on purpose, before you start typing.

### A snapshot must be a deep copy

If you choose snapshots, the copy has to be **deep**. The advanced course's aliasing lesson showed why. `dict(items)` makes a new outer dict, but the records inside it are the *same* objects as before. Change a record, and the "saved" one changes with it:

```python
import copy

items = {1: {"title": "a", "done": False}}
saved = dict(items)               # shallow copy: shares the inner record
items[1]["done"] = True
saved[1]["done"]                  # True: the saved copy changed too!

items = {1: {"title": "a", "done": False}}
saved = copy.deepcopy(items)      # deep copy: copies the records too
items[1]["done"] = True
saved[1]["done"]                  # False: the snapshot is safe
```

`copy.deepcopy` copies [[all the way down|deep-vs-shallow]], so nothing is shared.

### What undo must not touch

Not everything goes back. The spec says [[ids are never reused|ids-forever]], even after undoing an `add`. So keep the id counter *outside* the snapshots. Undoing an `add` removes the item, but the counter keeps counting: add "a" (id 1), add "b" (id 2), undo, add "c", and "c" gets id 3.

And only changes that really happened can be undone. An operation that raised an error changed nothing, so it must leave nothing in the history.

**Watch out:** saving the snapshot *before* checking the input. If `done(99)` pushes a snapshot and only then raises `KeyError`, the history now holds a step that changed nothing. The next `undo()` quietly "undoes" that empty step and returns `True`, and the change the user really wanted to undo is still there. Check everything first. Save the snapshot last, right before you change anything.

::: context spec-reading Reading a spec like an engineer
A specification says *what* a program must do, never *how*. Engineers read one slowly, sentence by sentence, because every word can be a requirement. NASA's requirements documents use the word "shall" for things that must be true ("the software shall reject an empty title"), and each "shall" is later checked by a test. The checks in these capstones play that part: each one tests a sentence of the spec. When a sentence could mean two things, a real engineer asks the person who wrote it. Here, the examples in the checks can settle it.
:::

::: context tables-first Show me your tables
Fred Brooks, who led the team that built the operating system for IBM's System/360 computers in the 1960s, wrote in *The Mythical Man-Month* (1975): "Show me your flowchart and conceal your tables, and I shall continue to be mystified. Show me your tables, and I won't usually need your flowchart; it'll be obvious." His "tables" are what we now call the data model. Once the shape of the data is right, most methods become a few obvious lines. When the shape is wrong, every method has to fight it.
:::

::: context edge-case Where bugs hide
Picture the allowed inputs as a field with a fence around it. Most inputs sit in the middle of the field, and most code handles them fine. The trouble is near the fence: an empty list, the first id, the last item, zero, doing something twice. Testers call this **boundary testing**: for every limit, try the value just inside it, exactly on it, and just outside it. For this to-do list the fence posts include an empty history, an unknown id, and a title that is only spaces.
:::

::: context refactor Changing the shape, not the behavior
The word was made popular by Martin Fowler's 1999 book *Refactoring*. The rule is strict: after a refactoring, the program must do exactly what it did before, only written more clearly. That is why you refactor *after* the checks pass. Run them again after every small change, and if one fails, you know the last change broke something. Tests are what make refactoring safe; without them, every tidy-up is a gamble.
:::

::: context undo-stack The history is a stack
Each change pushes a snapshot onto the top of the history. Undo pops the top one, the newest, and makes it the state again. Last in, first out:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="90" y="110" width="150" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="90" y="80" width="150" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="90" y="50" width="150" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="165" y="130" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">before add "a": empty</text>
  <text x="165" y="100" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">before add "b": a</text>
  <text x="165" y="70" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">before done(1): a, b</text>
  <line x1="60" y1="20" x2="60" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="60,68 55,58 65,58" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="60" y="14" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">push</text>
  <line x1="270" y1="60" x2="270" y2="24" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="270,16 265,26 275,26" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="300" y="44" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">undo pops</text>
  <text x="180" y="162" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the newest snapshot is always on top</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context command-pattern The Command pattern
Recording each change as an object is a well-known design called the **Command pattern**. It was named in the 1994 book *Design Patterns* by Gamma, Helm, Johnson and Vlissides. Each command object has two methods: one that does the change and one that reverses it. Text editors use it for undo, and it also makes **redo** easy: keep the undone commands on a second stack and do them again. Snapshots can support redo too, with a second stack of snapshots.
:::

::: context deep-vs-shallow Shallow and deep, drawn
A shallow copy makes a new outer dict, but its values still point at the same records. A deep copy makes new records too:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="16" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">shallow</text>
  <rect x="20" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="50" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">items</text>
  <rect x="100" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="130" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">saved</text>
  <rect x="45" y="106" width="90" height="32" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="127" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">one record</text>
  <line x1="50" y1="56" x2="80" y2="104" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="56" x2="100" y2="104" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="16" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">deep</text>
  <rect x="200" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="230" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">items</text>
  <rect x="280" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="310" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">saved</text>
  <rect x="195" y="106" width="70" height="32" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="230" y="127" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">record</text>
  <rect x="275" y="106" width="70" height="32" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="310" y="127" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">copy</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="56" x2="230" y2="104" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="310" y1="56" x2="310" y2="104" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="180" y="162" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">shared record: change one, both change</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context ids-forever Why an id is never handed out twice
An id is a promise: "this number means this item". Other things may hold on to it: a reminder that says "item 2", a message, a line in a log. If item 2 is removed and a new item later gets id 2, all those old references now point at the wrong thing, and nothing warns you. So many systems never reuse an id, even when the item is gone. Many databases hand out ids from a counter that only ever goes up, for exactly this reason.
:::
--- task
Build a class `TodoList` to this specification. How you store things inside it is up to you.

- `add(title, priority="normal", tags=())` adds an item and returns its id: 1, 2, 3, … Ids are never reused, even after `remove` or `undo`. The title is stripped; an empty title raises `ValueError`. `priority` must be `"low"`, `"normal"` or `"high"`, otherwise `ValueError`.
- `done(item_id)` marks an item done; `remove(item_id)` deletes it. An unknown id raises `KeyError`.
- `pending(tag=None)` returns the titles of items not done, `high` first, then `normal`, then `low`, and oldest first within the same priority. With a tag, only items that have that tag.
- `search(text)` returns the ids (ascending) of all items, done or not, whose title contains `text`, ignoring case.
- `undo()` reverses the most recent `add`, `done` or `remove` that has not been undone yet, and returns `True`; with nothing left to undo, it returns `False`. Failed operations (those that raised) are not undoable.
- `summary()` returns `"<n> pending, <m> done"`.
--- starter
# Capstone: design and build TodoList to the specification in the task.
--- solution
import copy

PRIORITIES = {"high": 0, "normal": 1, "low": 2}


class TodoList:
    def __init__(self):
        self._items = {}
        self._next_id = 1
        self._history = []

    def _snapshot(self):
        self._history.append(copy.deepcopy(self._items))

    def _get(self, item_id):
        if item_id not in self._items:
            raise KeyError(item_id)
        return self._items[item_id]

    def add(self, title, priority="normal", tags=()):
        title = title.strip()
        if not title:
            raise ValueError("title must not be empty")
        if priority not in PRIORITIES:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown priority: {priority}")
        self._snapshot()
        item_id = self._next_id
        self._next_id += 1
        self._items[item_id] = {"title": title, "priority": priority, "tags": set(tags), "done": False}
        return item_id

    def done(self, item_id):
        self._get(item_id)
        self._snapshot()
        self._items[item_id]["done"] = True

    def remove(self, item_id):
        self._get(item_id)
        self._snapshot()
        del self._items[item_id]

    def undo(self):
        if not self._history:
            return False
        self._items = self._history.pop()
        return True

    def pending(self, tag=None):
        rows = [
            (PRIORITIES[item["priority"]], item_id, item["title"])
            for item_id, item in self._items.items()
            if not item["done"] and (tag is None or tag in item["tags"])
        ]
        return [title for _, _, title in sorted(rows)]

    def search(self, text):
        text = text.lower()
        return sorted(item_id for item_id, item in self._items.items() if text in item["title"].lower())

    def summary(self):
        done = sum(1 for item in self._items.values() if item["done"])
        return f"{len(self._items) - done} pending, {done} done"
--- hint
Decide your data model first. One option: a dict from id to a small record (title, priority, tags, done), plus a counter for the next id. Then write the skeleton and make `add` work before anything else.
--- hint
For undo, the simplest correct design is snapshots: push a `copy.deepcopy` of your items onto a history list before every successful change (after all the checks), and pop it back in `undo()`. Keep the id counter out of the snapshot so ids are never reused.
--- hint
For `pending`, sort by a tuple: a priority rank (`{"high": 0, "normal": 1, "low": 2}`), then the id. `summary` counts the done items and subtracts from the total.
--- check case | Ids count up from 1
(lambda t: [t.add("a"), t.add("b"), t.add("c")])(TodoList())
=> [1, 2, 3]
--- check case | pending orders by priority, then age
(lambda t: (t.add("water plants", "low"), t.add("pay rent", "high"), t.add("email Sam"), t.add("fix bike", "high"), t.pending())[-1])(TodoList())
=> ["pay rent", "fix bike", "email Sam", "water plants"]
--- check case | done items leave the pending list; titles are stripped
(lambda t: (t.add("  a  "), t.add("b"), t.done(1), t.pending(), t.summary())[-2:])(TodoList())
=> (["b"], "1 pending, 1 done")
--- check case | pending by tag
(lambda t: (t.add("a", tags=["home"]), t.add("b", tags=["work"]), t.add("c", "high", tags=("home", "urgent")), t.pending("home"))[-1])(TodoList())
=> ["c", "a"]
--- check test | Bad input raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: TodoList().add("   ")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: TodoList().add("x", "urgent"))
--- check test | Unknown ids raise KeyError
raises(KeyError, lambda: TodoList().done(1)) and raises(KeyError, lambda: TodoList().remove(7))
--- check case | search ignores case and includes done items
(lambda t: (t.add("Buy milk"), t.add("call mum"), t.add("MILK the cow"), t.done(3), t.search("milk"))[-1])(TodoList())
=> [1, 3]
--- check case | undo reverses done, remove and add, newest first
(lambda t: (t.add("a"), t.add("b"), t.done(1), t.remove(2), t.undo(), t.pending(), t.undo(), t.pending(), t.undo(), t.pending())[4:])(TodoList())
=> (True, ["b"], True, ["a", "b"], True, ["a"])
--- check test | Nothing to undo returns False
(lambda t: (t.undo(), t.add("x"), t.undo(), t.undo(), t.summary()))(TodoList()) == (False, 1, True, False, "0 pending, 0 done")
--- check test | Ids are never reused, even after undo
(lambda t: (t.add("a"), t.add("b"), t.undo(), t.add("c")))(TodoList())[-1] == 3
--- check test | A failed operation cannot be undone
(lambda t: (t.add("a"), raises(KeyError, lambda: t.done(5)), raises(ValueError, lambda: t.add("")), t.undo(), t.undo()))(TodoList())[3:] == (True, False)

+++ practice | A tiny text editor with undo
--- task
Build a class `Editor` that holds one piece of text and can undo changes. The design inside is up to you.

- `editor.text` is the current text. It starts as `""`.
- `write(s)` adds `s` to the end of the text. An empty `s` raises `ValueError`.
- `delete(n)` removes the last `n` characters (all of them, if there are fewer than `n`). An `n` below 1 raises `ValueError`. Deleting from an empty text changes nothing.
- `undo()` puts the text back the way it was before the most recent change that has not been undone yet, and returns `True`; with nothing left to undo it returns `False`.
- Only real changes can be undone: an operation that raised, or that changed nothing, leaves nothing to undo.
--- starter
class Editor:
    def __init__(self):
        self.text = ""

    def write(self, s):
        self.text += s

    def delete(self, n):
        self.text = self.text[:-n]

    def undo(self):
        return False
--- solution
class Editor:
    def __init__(self):
        self.text = ""
        self._history = []

    def _change(self, new_text):
        if new_text != self.text:
            self._history.append(self.text)
            self.text = new_text

    def write(self, s):
        if not s:
            raise ValueError("nothing to write")
        self._change(self.text + s)

    def delete(self, n):
        if n < 1:
            raise ValueError("delete at least one character")
        self._change(self.text[:max(0, len(self.text) - n)])

    def undo(self):
        if not self._history:
            return False
        self.text = self._history.pop()
        return True
--- hint
A string can never change in place, so a snapshot of the text is just the old string itself: no copy needed. Keep a list of old texts, newest last.
--- hint
One private helper that takes the new text, and saves the old one only if they differ, gives every change the same rule. `self.text[:len(self.text) - n]` goes wrong when `n` is bigger than the length, so cut at `max(0, len(self.text) - n)`.
--- check case | Write, delete and undo, newest first
(lambda e: (e.write("hello"), e.write(" world"), e.delete(3), e.text, e.undo(), e.text, e.undo(), e.text, e.undo(), e.text, e.undo())[3:])(Editor())
=> ("hello wo", True, "hello world", True, "hello", True, "", False)
--- check test | Deleting more than there is
(lambda e: (e.write("abc"), e.delete(10), e.text, e.undo(), e.text)[2:])(Editor()) == ("", True, "abc")
--- check test | Changes that did nothing, or raised, cannot be undone
(lambda e: (e.delete(2), raises(ValueError, lambda: e.write("")), raises(ValueError, lambda: e.delete(0)), e.undo(), e.write("x"), e.undo(), e.undo(), e.text))(Editor()) == (None, True, True, False, None, True, False, "")

+++ practice | Undo and redo on a canvas
--- task
Build a class `Canvas`: a drawing where every pixel is white until it is painted. Pixels are named by `(x, y)` and colours are strings.

- `color_at(x, y)` returns the pixel's colour, `"white"` if it was never painted.
- `paint(x, y, color)` paints one pixel. Painting a pixel the colour it already has changes nothing and cannot be undone.
- `undo()` reverses the most recent paint that has not been undone, and returns `True`, or `False` if there is nothing to undo.
- `redo()` puts back the most recent undone paint, and returns `True`, or `False` if there is nothing to redo.
- A new paint (that changes something) clears everything there was to redo.
--- starter
class Canvas:
    def __init__(self):
        self.pixels = {}

    def color_at(self, x, y):
        return self.pixels.get((x, y), "white")

    def paint(self, x, y, color):
        self.pixels[(x, y)] = color
--- solution
class Canvas:
    def __init__(self):
        self.pixels = {}
        self._undo = []
        self._redo = []

    def color_at(self, x, y):
        return self.pixels.get((x, y), "white")

    def paint(self, x, y, color):
        if self.color_at(x, y) == color:
            return
        self._undo.append(dict(self.pixels))
        self._redo.clear()
        self.pixels[(x, y)] = color

    def undo(self):
        if not self._undo:
            return False
        self._redo.append(self.pixels)
        self.pixels = self._undo.pop()
        return True

    def redo(self):
        if not self._redo:
            return False
        self._undo.append(self.pixels)
        self.pixels = self._redo.pop()
        return True
--- hint
Keep two stacks of snapshots: one to undo and one to redo. An undo moves the current state onto the redo stack and takes the newest snapshot off the undo stack; a redo does the opposite.
--- hint
The values in the dict are strings, which never change in place, so `dict(self.pixels)` is a safe snapshot here. A new paint pushes a snapshot and empties the redo stack with `.clear()`.
--- check case | Undo, then redo
(lambda c: (c.paint(0, 0, "red"), c.paint(1, 0, "blue"), c.undo(), c.color_at(1, 0), c.redo(), c.color_at(1, 0), c.redo())[2:])(Canvas())
=> (True, "white", True, "blue", False)
--- check case | A new paint clears the redo stack
(lambda c: (c.paint(0, 0, "red"), c.paint(0, 0, "green"), c.undo(), c.paint(5, 5, "black"), c.redo(), c.color_at(0, 0), c.undo(), c.color_at(5, 5), c.color_at(0, 0))[4:])(Canvas())
=> (False, "red", True, "white", "red")
--- check test | Nothing to undo or redo, and a paint that changes nothing
(lambda c: (c.undo(), c.redo(), c.paint(2, 2, "white"), c.undo(), c.paint(2, 2, "red"), c.paint(2, 2, "red"), c.undo(), c.undo(), c.color_at(2, 2)))(Canvas()) == (False, False, None, False, None, None, True, False, "white")

+++ practice | Undo by doing the opposite
--- task
Build a class `Stock` for a storeroom, with undo done the *command* way: instead of saving snapshots, record each change and undo it by doing the opposite.

- `add(item, n)` adds `n` of an item; `take(item, n)` removes `n`. `n` must be at least 1, and you cannot take more than there is: both raise `ValueError`, and a change that raised cannot be undone.
- `count(item)` returns how many there are (0 for an unknown item).
- `undo()` reverses the most recent change that has not been undone, and returns `True`, or `False` if there is nothing to undo.
- `report()` returns one line per item with more than zero, sorted by name: the name left-aligned in 10 characters and the count right-aligned in 4. With nothing in stock it returns `["empty"]`.
--- starter
class Stock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}

    def add(self, item, n):
        self.counts[item] = self.counts.get(item, 0) + n

    def take(self, item, n):
        self.counts[item] -= n
--- solution
class Stock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.counts = {}
        self._changes = []

    def _apply(self, item, change):
        self.counts[item] = self.counts.get(item, 0) + change

    def count(self, item):
        return self.counts.get(item, 0)

    def add(self, item, n):
        if n < 1:
            raise ValueError("add at least one")
        self._apply(item, n)
        self._changes.append((item, n))

    def take(self, item, n):
        if n < 1 or n > self.count(item):
            raise ValueError(f"cannot take {n} {item}")
        self._apply(item, -n)
        self._changes.append((item, -n))

    def undo(self):
        if not self._changes:
            return False
        item, change = self._changes.pop()
        self._apply(item, -change)
        return True

    def report(self):
        rows = [(item, n) for item, n in sorted(self.counts.items()) if n > 0]
        return [f"{item:<10}{n:>4}" for item, n in rows] or ["empty"]
--- hint
Every change is "add this many to this item", where taking is adding a negative number. Record each change as an `(item, change)` pair; undoing it applies `-change`.
--- hint
A private `_apply(item, change)` used by `add`, `take` and `undo` keeps the arithmetic in one place. Check first, then apply, then record, so a refused change leaves no trace.
--- check case | Changes and undo
(lambda s: (s.add("bolts", 10), s.take("bolts", 4), s.add("nuts", 3), s.undo(), s.count("nuts"), s.undo(), s.count("bolts"), s.report())[3:])(Stock())
=> (True, 0, True, 10, ["bolts       10"])
--- check test | Refused changes raise and cannot be undone
(lambda s: (s.add("gear", 2), raises(ValueError, lambda: s.take("gear", 3)), raises(ValueError, lambda: s.add("gear", 0)), raises(ValueError, lambda: s.take("cog", 1)), s.undo(), s.undo(), s.count("gear"))[1:])(Stock()) == (True, True, True, True, False, 0)
--- check case | The report skips empty items and sorts by name
(lambda s: (s.add("wire", 5), s.add("bolts", 12), s.add("fuse", 1), s.take("fuse", 1), s.report(), Stock().report())[-2:])(Stock())
=> (["bolts       12", "wire         5"], ["empty"])

+++ practice | A board with a short memory
--- task
Build a class `Board(rows, cols)`: a grid of cells, each holding one character, all `"."` at the start. Rows and columns are numbered from 0.

- `set(row, col, mark)` puts a one-character mark in a cell. A cell outside the board raises `IndexError`, and **negative numbers count as outside** (so `set(-1, 0, "x")` raises, even though Python lists allow `-1`). A mark that is not exactly one character raises `ValueError`.
- `cell(row, col)` returns a cell's mark, with the same `IndexError` rule.
- `lines()` returns the board as a list of strings, one per row: `["x..", "..."]`.
- `undo()` reverses the most recent successful `set` and returns `True`, or returns `False` if there is nothing to undo. The board only remembers the **last 3** sets: older ones cannot be undone any more.
- A `set` that raised cannot be undone.

A snapshot of a grid must copy the rows as well as the outer list.
--- starter
class Board:
    def __init__(self, rows, cols):
        self.grid = [["."] * cols] * rows
        self._history = []

    def set(self, row, col, mark):
        self._history.append(list(self.grid))
        self.grid[row][col] = mark

    def undo(self):
        if not self._history:
            return False
        self.grid = self._history.pop()
        return True

    def lines(self):
        return ["".join(row) for row in self.grid]
--- solution
import copy

MAX_UNDO = 3


class Board:
    def __init__(self, rows, cols):
        self.grid = [["." for _ in range(cols)] for _ in range(rows)]
        self._history = []

    def _check(self, row, col):
        if not (0 <= row < len(self.grid) and 0 <= col < len(self.grid[0])):
            raise IndexError(f"no cell at {row}, {col}")

    def cell(self, row, col):
        self._check(row, col)
        return self.grid[row][col]

    def set(self, row, col, mark):
        self._check(row, col)
        if len(mark) != 1:
            raise ValueError("a mark is exactly one character")
        self._history.append(copy.deepcopy(self.grid))
        if len(self._history) > MAX_UNDO:
            self._history.pop(0)
        self.grid[row][col] = mark

    def undo(self):
        if not self._history:
            return False
        self.grid = self._history.pop()
        return True

    def lines(self):
        return ["".join(row) for row in self.grid]
--- hint
The starter has two aliasing bugs. `[["."] * cols] * rows` makes every row the *same* list, and `list(self.grid)` copies only the outer list, so the saved rows are still the live ones. Build each row separately, and snapshot with `copy.deepcopy`.
--- hint
Check the position yourself with `0 <= row < number of rows` (and the same for columns) before touching the grid. After pushing a snapshot, if the history holds more than 3, drop the oldest with `pop(0)`.
--- check case | Set, undo, and the rows are separate
(lambda b: (b.set(0, 0, "x"), b.set(1, 2, "o"), b.lines(), b.undo(), b.lines())[2:])(Board(2, 3))
=> (["x..", "..o"], True, ["x..", "..."])
--- check test | Only the last three sets can be undone
(lambda b: ([b.set(0, c, "x") for c in range(5)], [b.undo() for _ in range(4)], b.lines())[1:])(Board(1, 5)) == ([True, True, True, False], ["xx..."])
--- check test | Outside the board, negative included, raises IndexError
(lambda b: all(raises(IndexError, lambda p=p: b.set(*p, "x")) for p in [(2, 0), (0, 3), (-1, 0), (0, -1)]) and raises(IndexError, lambda: b.cell(-1, -1)))(Board(2, 3))
--- check test | Failed sets are not recorded
(lambda b: (raises(ValueError, lambda: b.set(0, 0, "xy")), raises(IndexError, lambda: b.set(5, 5, "x")), b.undo(), b.cell(0, 0)))(Board(1, 1)) == (True, True, False, ".")

+++ practice | Debug: undo forgets what was done
--- task
`TaskList` keeps tasks as a dict from id to a small record, `{"title": ..., "done": ...}`, and saves a snapshot before every change so `undo()` can go back.

Undoing an `add` works. But undoing a `done` or a `rename` does nothing: the task stays done, or keeps its new title, even though `undo()` returns `True`. Fix it.
--- starter
class TaskList:
    def __init__(self):
        self.tasks = {}
        self.next_id = 1
        self.history = []

    def _snapshot(self):
        self.history.append(dict(self.tasks))

    def add(self, title):
        self._snapshot()
        task_id = self.next_id
        self.next_id += 1
        self.tasks[task_id] = {"title": title, "done": False}
        return task_id

    def done(self, task_id):
        if task_id not in self.tasks:
            raise KeyError(task_id)
        self._snapshot()
        self.tasks[task_id]["done"] = True

    def rename(self, task_id, title):
        if task_id not in self.tasks:
            raise KeyError(task_id)
        self._snapshot()
        self.tasks[task_id]["title"] = title

    def undo(self):
        if not self.history:
            return False
        self.tasks = self.history.pop()
        return True

    def pending(self):
        return [t["title"] for _, t in sorted(self.tasks.items()) if not t["done"]]
--- solution
import copy


class TaskList:
    def __init__(self):
        self.tasks = {}
        self.next_id = 1
        self.history = []

    def _snapshot(self):
        self.history.append(copy.deepcopy(self.tasks))

    def add(self, title):
        self._snapshot()
        task_id = self.next_id
        self.next_id += 1
        self.tasks[task_id] = {"title": title, "done": False}
        return task_id

    def done(self, task_id):
        if task_id not in self.tasks:
            raise KeyError(task_id)
        self._snapshot()
        self.tasks[task_id]["done"] = True

    def rename(self, task_id, title):
        if task_id not in self.tasks:
            raise KeyError(task_id)
        self._snapshot()
        self.tasks[task_id]["title"] = title

    def undo(self):
        if not self.history:
            return False
        self.tasks = self.history.pop()
        return True

    def pending(self):
        return [t["title"] for _, t in sorted(self.tasks.items()) if not t["done"]]
--- hint
`dict(self.tasks)` makes a new outer dict. Are the records inside it new too, or the same ones the live dict holds?
--- hint
The snapshot shares its records with the live dict, so changing a record changes the "saved" one as well. A snapshot must copy all the way down: `copy.deepcopy(self.tasks)`.
--- check case | Undoing done brings the task back
(lambda t: (t.add("a"), t.add("b"), t.done(1), t.pending(), t.undo(), t.pending())[3:])(TaskList())
=> (["b"], True, ["a", "b"])
--- check case | Undoing a rename brings the old title back
(lambda t: (t.add("buy milk"), t.rename(1, "buy oat milk"), t.undo(), t.pending())[-1])(TaskList())
=> ["buy milk"]
--- check test | Undoing an add still works, and nothing left gives False
(lambda t: (t.add("a"), t.undo(), t.pending(), t.undo()))(TaskList()) == (1, True, [], False)

+++ practice | Undo a whole batch at once
--- task
Build a class `Notes`: a list of text lines with undo, where several changes can be grouped so that one `undo()` reverses them all.

- `notes.lines` is the list of lines, empty at the start.
- `add(text)` appends a line, `edit(i, text)` replaces line `i`, `delete(i)` removes line `i`. Text is stripped, and empty text raises `ValueError`; a position outside the list (negative included) raises `IndexError`.
- `undo()` reverses the most recent change (or batch) and returns `True`, or returns `False` if there is nothing to undo. A change that raised cannot be undone.
- `with notes.batch():` groups every change made inside the `with` block into one undo step. If the block raises an exception, every change made in it is reversed straight away and the exception carries on to the caller; nothing is recorded. A batch that changed nothing records nothing.
- Calling `undo()` inside a batch, or starting a batch inside another one, raises `RuntimeError`.
--- starter
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Notes:
    def __init__(self):
        self.lines = []

    def add(self, text):
        self.lines.append(text)
--- solution
from contextlib import contextmanager


class Notes:
    def __init__(self):
        self.lines = []
        self._history = []
        self._in_batch = False

    def _save(self):
        if not self._in_batch:
            self._history.append(list(self.lines))

    def _text(self, text):
        text = text.strip()
        if not text:
            raise ValueError("a line needs some text")
        return text

    def _check(self, i):
        if not 0 <= i < len(self.lines):
            raise IndexError(f"no line {i}")

    def add(self, text):
        text = self._text(text)
        self._save()
        self.lines.append(text)

    def edit(self, i, text):
        self._check(i)
        text = self._text(text)
        self._save()
        self.lines[i] = text

    def delete(self, i):
        self._check(i)
        self._save()
        del self.lines[i]

    def undo(self):
        if self._in_batch:
            raise RuntimeError("cannot undo inside a batch")
        if not self._history:
            return False
        self.lines = self._history.pop()
        return True

    @contextmanager
    def batch(self):
        if self._in_batch:
            raise RuntimeError("batches cannot be nested")
        before = list(self.lines)
        self._in_batch = True
        try:
            yield self
        except Exception:
            self.lines = before
            raise
        finally:
            self._in_batch = False
        if self.lines != before:
            self._history.append(before)
--- hint
Snapshots are the easy design here: the lines are strings, so `list(self.lines)` is a safe copy. Inside a batch, single changes do not save snapshots; the batch saves one snapshot of how things were before it began.
--- hint
Write `batch` with `@contextmanager`: remember the lines, set a flag saying a batch is open, and `yield` inside `try`. On an exception, put the old lines back and `raise` again; in `finally`, clear the flag.
--- hint
After the `try` statement (which only runs when the block finished without an error), push the saved lines onto the history, but only if the lines really changed.
--- check case | One undo reverses the whole batch
(lambda n: (n.add("one"), exec("with n.batch():\n    n.add('two')\n    n.edit(0, ' ONE ')\n    n.delete(1)\n", {"n": n}), list(n.lines), n.undo(), list(n.lines), n.undo(), n.lines)[2:])(Notes())
=> (["ONE"], True, ["one"], True, [])
--- check test | An error inside a batch rolls it back and carries on
(lambda n: (n.add("keep"), raises(ValueError, lambda: exec("with n.batch():\n    n.add('temp')\n    n.edit(0, '   ')\n", {"n": n})), n.lines, n.undo(), n.lines, n.undo())[2:])(Notes()) == (["keep"], True, [], False)
--- check test | undo and nested batches are refused inside a batch
(lambda n: (raises(RuntimeError, lambda: exec("with n.batch():\n    n.undo()\n", {"n": n})), raises(RuntimeError, lambda: exec("with n.batch():\n    with n.batch():\n        pass\n", {"n": n})), n.add("x"), n.undo()))(Notes()) == (True, True, None, True)
--- check test | Empty batches and failed changes record nothing
(lambda n: (exec("with n.batch():\n    pass\n", {"n": n}), raises(IndexError, lambda: n.delete(-1)), raises(ValueError, lambda: n.add("")), n.undo()))(Notes()) == (None, True, True, False)

=== pyp-14 | Capstone: a calculator language with variables
--- teach
The to-do list stored things and changed them. This capstone goes back to the calculator you built at the end of the expert course, and grows it into a tiny programming language.

Remember the pieces. The tokenizer lesson cut text like `2 * (3 + 4)` into tokens. The parser lessons turned those tokens into a value by **recursive descent**: one method per grammar rule, each calling the next one down.

A calculator forgets everything after each answer. A language remembers. You will write an **[[interpreter|interpreter]]**: a program that reads lines one at a time, carries each one out, and keeps what it has learned for the next line. Here is a session with it (`>` marks what the user typed; the line under it is the reply):

```
> let rate = 1.5
rate = 1.5
> let hours = 8
hours = 8
> rate * hours
12
> max(rate, 2) ^ 2
4
> y + 1
error: unknown variable y
```

It has **variables** (names that hold values), **functions** like `max`, its own power operator `^`, and error messages instead of crashes. And it is designed by you, from the spec in the task.

### Same pipeline, new pieces

The expert course's plan still works: **tokenize, then parse and evaluate**, with one grammar rule for each level of precedence. Your parser can still work out each value as it goes, so parsing and evaluating happen together. [[Reuse the plan, not only the code|pipeline]]: each new feature slots into one stage of it.

### A new kind of token: names

A **name** is a word like `rate` or `total_2`. The spec's rule is the one Python uses for [[its own names|identifiers]]: a letter or an underscore first, then any number of letters, digits or underscores. So `total_2` is a name. `2total` is not: it is the number `2` followed by the name `total`, two tokens in a row, which is a syntax error.

As a regular expression, that rule is `[A-Za-z_]\w*`. Piece by piece: `[A-Za-z_]` is one letter or underscore; `\w` is one "word character", meaning a letter, a digit or an underscore; and `*` means zero or more of them.

```python
import re
re.match(r"[A-Za-z_]\w*", "total_2 * 2").group()     # 'total_2'
```

You can also grow the `while` loop from your tokenizer instead, the same way it collects the digits of a number.

### Tokens that say what kind they are

In the expert course a token was either a number or a string like `"+"`. Now a string might be an operator or a name, and the parser has to tell `"max"` from `"("` at a glance. A clean fix is to [[tag every token with its kind|tagged-tokens]]: make each token a pair of (kind, value).

```python
[("name", "rate"), ("op", "*"), ("num", 2)]      # the tokens of: rate * 2
```

The parser then asks "is the kind `"name"`?" instead of guessing from the value. The comma between function arguments is one more operator token: `,`.

### The grammar grows

Here is the grammar from the calculator lesson, with `**` replaced by `^`, a bigger `atom` and one new rule, `args`:

```
expr   := term   (("+" | "-") term)*
term   := unary  (("*" | "/") unary)*
unary  := "-" unary  |  power
power  := atom   ("^" unary)?
atom   := NUMBER  |  NAME "(" args ")"  |  NAME  |  "(" expr ")"
args   := expr  ("," expr)*
```

Only `atom` and `args` are new. In words:

- An atom that starts with a NAME is a **function call** if the next token is `(`, and a **variable** otherwise. Take the name, then peek at the next token to find out which.
- A call's `args` are one expression, then any number of "comma, expression". So `max(1, 7, 3)` has three arguments. `max()` has none, which this rule does not allow, so it is a syntax error.
- A variable is looked up in the interpreter's dict of variables. A name that is not in the dict is the error `unknown variable NAME`.

Everything above `atom` works as before. `^` groups from the right, so `2 ^ 3 ^ 2` is `2 ^ 9 = 512`. Unary minus is looser than `^`, so `-2 ^ 2` is `-(2 ^ 2) = -4`. And the right side of `^` goes through `unary`, so `2 ^ -1` is `0.5`.

### Calling a Python function from your language

Keep the functions in a dict from each name to the real Python function:

```python
import math
FUNCTIONS = {"sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max}
```

Your parser collects a call's arguments into a list. A `*` in front of a list, inside a call, [[spreads the list out|star-unpack]] into separate arguments:

```python
args = [1, 7, 3]
FUNCTIONS["max"](*args)      # the same as max(1, 7, 3): 7
```

`sqrt` and `abs` take exactly one argument, so check how many you got before calling them. A name that is not in the dict is an unknown function.

### let is a statement

An **expression** is something that has a value, like `rate * 2`. A **statement** is an instruction that does something, like "store this value under this name". `let x = ...` is a [[statement|statement-expression]], so it is not part of the expression grammar at all. Recognize it first, before any parsing, with a regular expression:

```python
LET = re.compile(r"^let\s+([A-Za-z_]\w*)\s*=(.*)$")
LET.match("let rate = 1.5").groups()      # ('rate', ' 1.5')
LET.match("let 5 = 3")                    # None: 5 is not a name
```

Read the pattern left to right: `^let` means the line starts with `let`; `\s+` is one or more spaces; group 1 is a name; `\s*=` is any spaces and then an equals sign; group 2, `(.*)`, is everything after that. Group 2 is an ordinary expression, so hand it to your expression parser. A line that does not match is an expression line.

Two more rules from the spec. `let` cannot assign to a function name, so `let sqrt = 4` is an error. And the order matters: **evaluate first, store second.** If the right side fails, you never reach the store, and the old value survives. That is what "a failed `let` leaves the variable unchanged" asks for.

### State that lasts between lines

The variables live in the `Interpreter` object, in a dict such as `self.variables`, so they are still there on the next call to `execute`. Each line gets a fresh parser, handed that same dict to read from.

### Errors are part of the design

An interactive calculator must answer every line, even nonsense. That takes two parts:

1. **Your own exception class**, such as `class CalcError(Exception)`. Raise it for every deliberate error, with the exact messages the spec asks for, like `unknown variable y` and `division by zero`. Because the class is yours, a real bug (say a `NameError` from a typo in your code) cannot be mistaken for one of these.
2. **One place that catches it**: `execute`. It wraps all the work in `try`, and turns the error into the reply `f"error: {err}"`. This is the [[error boundary|error-boundary]] from the expense tracker again.

Check for division by zero yourself, in `term`, before you divide. Then the message is exactly `division by zero`. (Python's own message is different for floats: `1.0 / 0` says `float division by zero`.)

Python's maths can also raise errors of its own, and `execute` must turn those into replies too:

```python
math.sqrt(-1)       # ValueError: math domain error
2.0 ** 10000        # OverflowError: too big for a float
0 ** -1             # ZeroDivisionError
```

### Showing the answer

In Python, `7 / 2` is `3.5` and `6 / 2` is `3.0`. The spec wants `"3"`, not `"3.0"`: a float with a [[whole-number value|whole-floats]] prints without `.0`. A float can tell you whether it is whole:

```python
(3.0).is_integer()     # True
(3.5).is_integer()     # False
str(int(3.0))          # '3'
```

Ints already print without `.0`, so only floats need the check. Write it once, as a small helper that every reply goes through.

**Watch out:** do not compute `^` with Python's `^`. In Python, `^` [[means something else entirely|caret-xor]]: `2 ^ 3` is `1`, not `8`. Your language spells power `^`, but your code must compute it with `**`.

Plan the grammar on paper before you write code. Getting the grammar right is most of the work.

::: context interpreter Interpreters you already use
An **interpreter** reads a program and carries it out directly, line by line or piece by piece. Python is one: when you type `python3` with no file, it shows a `>>>` prompt, reads a line, runs it, prints the result, and waits for the next. That cycle is called a **REPL**: read, evaluate, print, loop. Many home computers of the late 1970s and 1980s started up straight into a BASIC interpreter, and a whole generation learned to program by typing lines at its prompt. Your `execute` is the "evaluate" part of a REPL.
:::

::: context pipeline The pipeline, one line at a time
Every line goes through the same stages. `let` is spotted before parsing; the variables dict is what makes one line remember another:


:::

::: context identifiers The rules for names
Python's rule for a name is the one this language uses: start with a letter or underscore, then letters, digits or underscores. Names are **case-sensitive**, so `rate`, `Rate` and `RATE` are three different names. Most languages add one more rule: some words are **reserved** and cannot be names. In Python you cannot call a variable `if` or `for`. In this language, `let` plays that part, and so do the function names, which the spec forbids as `let` targets.
:::

::: context tagged-tokens Every token carries a label
Tagging tokens is how real tokenizers work. Python's own `tokenize` module labels each piece of `rate * 2` with a type: `NAME` for `rate`, `OP` for `*`, `NUMBER` for `2`. The label lets the parser decide what to do from the kind alone, and the value is there when it needs the details. It also means `("op", "(")` can never be confused with a name, whatever characters a name might one day be allowed to contain.
:::

::: context star-unpack The star in a call
You met `*args` in a function *definition*, where it gathers extra arguments into a tuple. In a function *call*, the star does the opposite: it spreads a list or tuple out into separate arguments. So `max(*[1, 7, 3])` is exactly `max(1, 7, 3)`. That is how your language can pass any number of arguments to `min` and `max` without knowing in advance how many there will be. A double star, `**`, does the same for a dict of keyword arguments.
:::

::: context statement-expression Statements and expressions
In Python, `x = 3` is a statement: it does something and has no value, so `y = (x = 3)` is a syntax error. Python 3.8 added the "walrus" operator, `:=`, which is an assignment that is also an expression. The keyword `let` comes from BASIC, where assignments were written `LET X = 5`. Starting the line with a keyword makes a statement easy to spot before any parsing happens, which is exactly why your interpreter can check for it first.
:::

::: context error-boundary One place turns errors into replies
Deep inside the parser, `atom` finds an unknown variable and raises. It does not know who is typing or how to show a message, and it should not. The error travels up through `power`, `unary`, `term` and `expr` untouched, until `execute` catches it and turns it into a reply. One catch, at the top, instead of error handling scattered through every method. The expense tracker's run loop was built the same way.
:::

::: context whole-floats Why 3.0 and 3 look different
Python keeps `int` and `float` as different types, even when they hold the same value: `6 / 2` is the float `3.0`, while `2 + 1` is the int `3`. A float stores every whole number exactly up to 9,007,199,254,740,992, which is 2 to the power 53, so `is_integer()` gives a reliable answer for the numbers a calculator meets. Showing `3` for both keeps the replies tidy, the way a pocket calculator shows `3`, not `3.0`.
:::

::: context caret-xor What ^ really does in Python
In Python, `^` is **exclusive or** (XOR), which works on the bits of whole numbers: each bit of the answer is 1 when the two bits differ. 2 is 010 in binary and 3 is 011, so `2 ^ 3` is 001, which is 1.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="60" y="30" font-size="13" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">2</text>
  <text x="60" y="60" font-size="13" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <text x="60" y="100" font-size="13" text-anchor="end" fill="#1d6fd1">2 ^ 3</text>
  <text x="120" y="30" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <text x="150" y="30" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="30" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <text x="120" y="60" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <text x="150" y="60" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="60" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <line x1="100" y1="74" x2="200" y2="74" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="120" y="100" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">0</text>
  <text x="150" y="100" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">0</text>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">1</text>
  <text x="225" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">= 1, not 8</text>
  <text x="225" y="60" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">1 where bits differ</text>
</svg>
```

C, Java and JavaScript use `^` the same way. Spreadsheets and many calculators use `^` for power, which is why your language does.
:::
--- task
Build a class `Interpreter` with a method `execute(line)` that returns a string. The design inside is up to you.

- `let NAME = EXPR` evaluates `EXPR`, stores it in variable `NAME`, and returns `"NAME = VALUE"`. Otherwise, the line is an expression and the reply is its value.
- Expressions: numbers (`3`, `2.5`), variables, `+ - * /` with the usual precedence (left to right), `^` for power (right-associative, binding tighter than unary minus: `-2 ^ 2` is `-4`, `2 ^ -1` is `0.5`), unary minus, brackets, and the functions `sqrt(x)`, `abs(x)`, `min(a, b, ...)` and `max(a, b, ...)`.
- Values print as Python prints them, except that a float with a whole value prints without `.0`: `7 / 2` → `"3.5"`, `6 / 2` → `"3"`.
- Errors never raise: they return a string starting with `"error: "`. Exactly `"error: unknown variable NAME"` for an undefined variable and `"error: division by zero"` for dividing by zero. A failed `let` leaves the variable unchanged. Names are case-sensitive, and `let` cannot assign to a function name.
--- starter
# Capstone: design and build Interpreter to the specification in the task.
--- solution
import math
import re

TOKEN = re.compile(r"\s*(?:(\d+\.\d*|\.\d+|\d+)|([A-Za-z_]\w*)|(\S))")
FUNCTIONS = {"sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max}


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    pos = 0
    text = text.rstrip()
    while pos < len(text):
        m = TOKEN.match(text, pos)
        number, name, op = m.groups()
        if number:
            tokens.append(("num", float(number) if "." in number else int(number)))
        elif name:
            tokens.append(("name", name))
        elif op in "+-*/^(),":
            tokens.append(("op", op))
        else:
            raise CalcError(f"unexpected character {op!r}")
        pos = m.end()
    return tokens


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens, variables):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0
        self.variables = variables

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else (None, None)

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expect(self, op):
        if self.take() != ("op", op):
            raise CalcError(f"expected {op}")

    def parse(self):
        value = self.expr()
        if self.pos != len(self.tokens):
            raise CalcError("syntax error")
        return value

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in (("op", "+"), ("op", "-")):
            op = self.take()[1]
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in (("op", "*"), ("op", "/")):
            op = self.take()[1]
            right = self.unary()
            if op == "/":
                if right == 0:
                    raise CalcError("division by zero")
                value = value / right
            else:
                value = value * right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == ("op", "-"):
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == ("op", "^"):
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def atom(self):
        kind, value = self.take()
        if kind == "num":
            return value
        if kind == "name":
            if self.peek() == ("op", "("):
                return self.call(value)
            if value not in self.variables:
                raise CalcError(f"unknown variable {value}")
            return self.variables[value]
        if (kind, value) == ("op", "("):
            result = self.expr()
            self.expect(")")
            return result
        raise CalcError("syntax error")

    def call(self, name):
        if name not in FUNCTIONS:
            raise CalcError(f"unknown function {name}")
        self.expect("(")
        args = [self.expr()]
        while self.peek() == ("op", ","):
            self.take()
            args.append(self.expr())
        self.expect(")")
        if name in ("sqrt", "abs") and len(args) != 1:
            raise CalcError(f"{name} takes one argument")
        if name == "sqrt" and args[0] < 0:
            raise CalcError("square root of a negative number")
        return FUNCTIONS[name](*args)


def show(value):
    if isinstance(value, float) and value.is_integer():
        return str(int(value))
    return str(value)


class Interpreter:
    LET = re.compile(r"^let\s+([A-Za-z_]\w*)\s*=(.*)$")

    def __init__(self):
        self.variables = {}

    def evaluate(self, text):
        tokens = tokenize(text)
        if not tokens:
            raise CalcError("empty expression")
        return Parser(tokens, self.variables).parse()

    def execute(self, line):
        line = line.strip()
        try:
            m = self.LET.match(line)
            if m:
                name = m.group(1)
                if name in FUNCTIONS or name == "let":
                    raise CalcError(f"cannot assign to {name}")
                value = self.evaluate(m.group(2))
                self.variables[name] = value
                return f"{name} = {show(value)}"
            return show(self.evaluate(line))
        except CalcError as err:
            return f"error: {err}"
        except (OverflowError, ValueError, TypeError, ZeroDivisionError) as err:
            return f"error: {err}"
--- hint
Start from the tokenizer and parser of the expert course. Make each token a (kind, value) pair, add a `name` kind (the regular expression `[A-Za-z_]\w*` finds one) and a `,` operator.
--- hint
Grammar, loosest first: expr (`+ -`), term (`* /`), unary (`-`), power (`atom ^ unary`), atom (number, name, call, or bracketed expr). In `execute`, check the line against `^let\s+([A-Za-z_]\w*)\s*=(.*)$` before parsing; evaluate group 2, and only then store it.
--- hint
Define your own exception class, raise it for every deliberate error (with the exact messages from the specification), and catch it once in `execute` to return `f"error: {err}"`. Catch `ValueError`, `OverflowError` and `ZeroDivisionError` there too, and pass every value through one helper that turns a whole float like `3.0` into `"3"`.
--- check case | Arithmetic with precedence
(lambda i: [i.execute(l) for l in ["1 + 2 * 3", "(1 + 2) * 3", "10 - 4 - 3", "7 / 2", "6 / 2", "2.5 * 2"]])(Interpreter())
=> ["7", "9", "3", "3.5", "3", "5"]
--- check case | Power and unary minus
(lambda i: [i.execute(l) for l in ["2 ^ 3 ^ 2", "-2 ^ 2", "(-2) ^ 2", "2 ^ -1", "--4"]])(Interpreter())
=> ["512", "-4", "4", "0.5", "4"]
--- check case | Variables persist between lines
(lambda i: [i.execute(l) for l in ["let rate = 1.5", "let hours = 8", "rate * hours", "let hours = hours + 2", "rate * hours"]])(Interpreter())
=> ["rate = 1.5", "hours = 8", "12", "hours = 10", "15"]
--- check case | Functions
(lambda i: [i.execute(l) for l in ["sqrt(16)", "abs(-3.5)", "max(1, 7, 3)", "min(4, -2 * 3)", "max(sqrt(9), 2) + 1"]])(Interpreter())
=> ["4", "3.5", "7", "-6", "4"]
--- check test | Unknown variables and division by zero
(lambda i: i.execute("y + 1") == "error: unknown variable y" and i.execute("1 / (2 - 2)") == "error: division by zero")(Interpreter())
--- check test | Syntax errors reply with an error, never raise
(lambda i: all(i.execute(l).startswith("error: ") for l in ["", "1 +", "(1", "1)", "2 3", "* 4", "let = 3", "let 5 = 3", "max()", "sqrt(1, 2)", "3 $ 4", "let sqrt = 4"]))(Interpreter())
--- check test | A failed let does not change the variable
(lambda i: (i.execute("let x = 5"), i.execute("let x = 1 / 0"), i.execute("x"))[1:])(Interpreter()) == ("error: division by zero", "5")
--- check test | Names are case-sensitive and can contain digits and underscores
(lambda i: (i.execute("let total_2 = 3"), i.execute("total_2 * 2"), i.execute("Total_2"))[1:])(Interpreter()) == ("6", "error: unknown variable Total_2")
--- check test | Zero to a negative power replies with an error, never raises
Interpreter().execute("0 ^ -1").startswith("error: ")

+++ practice | Tokens that say what they are
--- task
Write `class CalcError(Exception)` and `tokenize(text)`, which cuts a line of the calculator language into tokens, each a `(kind, value)` pair:

- **Numbers**: digits with at most one `.`, such as `3`, `2.5`, `.5` or `5.`: `("num", 3)`. The value is an `int` when there is no dot and a `float` when there is. A number with two dots, like `1.2.3`, raises `CalcError`.
- **Names**: a letter or `_`, then any letters, digits or `_`: `("name", "rate")`.
- **Operators**: each of `+ - * / ^ ( ) , =` is `("op", "+")` and so on.
- Spaces are skipped. Any other character raises `CalcError(f"unexpected character {ch!r}")`.

For example, `tokenize("rate * 2")` is `[("name", "rate"), ("op", "*"), ("num", 2)]`, and `tokenize("2total")` is `[("num", 2), ("name", "total")]`.
--- starter
class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def tokenize(text):
    return [("op", word) for word in text.split()]
--- solution
class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


OPERATORS = "+-*/^(),="


def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise CalcError(f"bad number {number!r}")
            tokens.append(("num", float(number) if "." in number else int(number)))
        elif ch.isalpha() or ch == "_":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isalnum() or text[i] == "_"):
                i += 1
            tokens.append(("name", text[start:i]))
        elif ch in OPERATORS:
            tokens.append(("op", ch))
            i += 1
        else:
            raise CalcError(f"unexpected character {ch!r}")
    return tokens
--- hint
Use the tokenizer shape from the expert course: an index `i`, a `while` loop, and one branch per kind of token. A number and a name each remember where they started and move `i` along while the next character still belongs.
--- hint
Test for a number before a name, so `2total` gives the number first. A name may go on with digits, so its inner loop tests `text[i].isalnum() or text[i] == "_"`. Tag each token as you append it.
--- check case | Names, operators and numbers
tokenize("rate * 2")
=> [("name", "rate"), ("op", "*"), ("num", 2)]
--- check case | A let line with a call
tokenize("let x_1 = max(2.5, .5)")
=> [("name", "let"), ("name", "x_1"), ("op", "="), ("name", "max"), ("op", "("), ("num", 2.5), ("op", ","), ("num", 0.5), ("op", ")")]
--- check test | A number then a name, ints and floats, and nothing at all
tokenize("2total") == [("num", 2), ("name", "total")] and type(tokenize("7")[0][1]) is int and type(tokenize("5.")[0][1]) is float and tokenize("   ") == []
--- check case | A bad character, with its message
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    tokenize('3 $ 4')\nexcept CalcError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "unexpected character '$'"
--- check test | Two dots is not a number
raises(CalcError, lambda: tokenize("1.2.3"))

+++ practice | Reverse Polish notation
--- task
Some calculators put the operator *after* its two numbers: `3 4 +` means 3 + 4, and `3 4 + 2 *` means (3 + 4) × 2. This is **reverse Polish notation**, and it needs no brackets. You work it out with a stack: a number or variable is pushed; an operator pops two values (the second one popped is the left side), and pushes the result.

Write `class CalcError(Exception)` and `rpn(text, variables)`, where the words of `text` are separated by spaces:

- A number is digits with at most one `.` (an `int` without a dot, a `float` with one). A name (a letter or `_`, then letters, digits or `_`) is looked up in the dict `variables`; a missing one raises `CalcError(f"unknown variable {name}")`.
- The operators are `+`, `-`, `*` and `/`. Dividing by zero raises `CalcError("division by zero")`.
- An operator without two values to use, or more or fewer than one value left at the end, raises `CalcError("bad expression")`. Any other word raises `CalcError`.
- Return the one value left.
--- starter
class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def rpn(text, variables):
    return eval(text)
--- solution
import re


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


NAME = re.compile(r"^[A-Za-z_]\w*$")
NUMBER = re.compile(r"^(\d+\.?\d*|\.\d+)$")


def apply(op, left, right):
    if op == "+":
        return left + right
    if op == "-":
        return left - right
    if op == "*":
        return left * right
    if right == 0:
        raise CalcError("division by zero")
    return left / right


def rpn(text, variables):
    stack = []
    for word in text.split():
        if word in ("+", "-", "*", "/"):
            if len(stack) < 2:
                raise CalcError("bad expression")
            right = stack.pop()
            left = stack.pop()
            stack.append(apply(word, left, right))
        elif NUMBER.match(word):
            stack.append(float(word) if "." in word else int(word))
        elif NAME.match(word):
            if word not in variables:
                raise CalcError(f"unknown variable {word}")
            stack.append(variables[word])
        else:
            raise CalcError(f"bad word {word}")
    if len(stack) != 1:
        raise CalcError("bad expression")
    return stack[0]
--- hint
A list is the stack: `append` pushes, `pop()` takes the last value. For `10 4 -`, the first `pop()` gives 4 (the right side) and the second gives 10 (the left side).
--- hint
Decide what each word is: an operator, a number, a name, or something bad. Two patterns with `^` and `$`, such as `^[A-Za-z_]\w*$` for a name, check a whole word. At the end, exactly one value must be left on the stack.
--- check case | Numbers and operators
[rpn("3 4 +", {}), rpn("3 4 + 2 *", {}), rpn("10 4 -", {}), rpn("10 4 /", {}), rpn("2.5 2 *", {})]
=> [7, 14, 6, 2.5, 5.0]
--- check case | Variables
rpn("rate hours * bonus +", {"rate": 1.5, "hours": 8, "bonus": 3})
=> 15.0
--- check case | Every error message
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    rpn(t, {'x': 1})\nexcept CalcError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), t=t)) for t in ("1 0 /", "1 +", "1 2", "", "z 1 +", "x -")]
=> ["division by zero", "bad expression", "bad expression", "bad expression", "unknown variable z", "bad expression"]
--- check test | Other words are refused
raises(CalcError, lambda: rpn("3 4 %", {})) and raises(CalcError, lambda: rpn("1.2.3", {})) and rpn("x x x - -", {"x": 1}) == 1

+++ practice | A calculator session with memory
--- task
The starter has `CalcError` and `evaluate(text, variables)`, a finished evaluator for `+` and `-` on numbers and variable names; it raises `CalcError` for anything it cannot work out. Write `run(lines)`, a session loop that returns the output lines:

- Blank lines and lines starting with `#` (after stripping) produce nothing.
- `let NAME = EXPR` (a name is a letter or `_`, then letters, digits or `_`) evaluates the expression, stores it, and produces `"NAME = VALUE"`.
- `del NAME` removes a variable and produces `"deleted NAME"`; a name that is not set produces `"error: unknown variable NAME"`.
- `vars` produces `"a = 1, b = 2.5"`, sorted by name, or `"no variables"`.
- Any other line is an expression, and produces its value.
- A `CalcError` produces `"error: <message>"`, and a failed `let` leaves the variable as it was.
- Values are shown as Python shows them, except that a float with a whole value has no `.0`: `3.0` shows as `"3"`.
--- starter
import re


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def value_of(word, variables):
    if word.replace(".", "", 1).isdigit():
        return float(word) if "." in word else int(word)
    if word in variables:
        return variables[word]
    raise CalcError(f"unknown variable {word}")


def evaluate(text, variables):
    """Adds and subtracts numbers and variables, like "a + 2.5 - b"."""
    words = text.replace("+", " + ").replace("-", " - ").split()
    if not words or len(words) % 2 == 0:
        raise CalcError("syntax error")
    total = value_of(words[0], variables)
    for op, word in zip(words[1::2], words[2::2]):
        if op not in ("+", "-"):
            raise CalcError("syntax error")
        value = value_of(word, variables)
        total = total + value if op == "+" else total - value
    return total


def run(lines):
    return [str(evaluate(line, {})) for line in lines]
--- solution
import re


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def value_of(word, variables):
    if word.replace(".", "", 1).isdigit():
        return float(word) if "." in word else int(word)
    if word in variables:
        return variables[word]
    raise CalcError(f"unknown variable {word}")


def evaluate(text, variables):
    """Adds and subtracts numbers and variables, like "a + 2.5 - b"."""
    words = text.replace("+", " + ").replace("-", " - ").split()
    if not words or len(words) % 2 == 0:
        raise CalcError("syntax error")
    total = value_of(words[0], variables)
    for op, word in zip(words[1::2], words[2::2]):
        if op not in ("+", "-"):
            raise CalcError("syntax error")
        value = value_of(word, variables)
        total = total + value if op == "+" else total - value
    return total


LET = re.compile(r"^let\s+([A-Za-z_]\w*)\s*=(.*)$")


def show(value):
    if isinstance(value, float) and value.is_integer():
        return str(int(value))
    return str(value)


def execute(line, variables):
    let = LET.match(line)
    words = line.split()
    if let:
        name = let.group(1)
        value = evaluate(let.group(2), variables)
        variables[name] = value
        return f"{name} = {show(value)}"
    if len(words) == 2 and words[0] == "del":
        if words[1] not in variables:
            raise CalcError(f"unknown variable {words[1]}")
        del variables[words[1]]
        return f"deleted {words[1]}"
    if words == ["vars"]:
        shown = [f"{name} = {show(value)}" for name, value in sorted(variables.items())]
        return ", ".join(shown) or "no variables"
    return show(evaluate(line, variables))


def run(lines):
    variables = {}
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            out.append(execute(line, variables))
        except CalcError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
    return out
--- hint
Recognise the statements before treating a line as an expression: `let` with the lesson's pattern, `del` as exactly two words, `vars` as exactly one. Everything else goes to `evaluate`.
--- hint
Evaluate first, store second: then a `let` whose expression raises never reaches the line that stores. One helper that turns a whole float into an int's text, used for every value you show, keeps the output rule in one place.
--- hint
A helper `execute(line, variables)` that returns one output line, or raises `CalcError`, keeps `run` to the usual loop: strip, skip, `try`, append, `except CalcError`.
--- check case | A session with memory
run(["let a = 1.5", "let b = a + 1.5", "b - 1", "vars", "# a comment", "", "a + b"])
=> ["a = 1.5", "b = 3", "2", "a = 1.5, b = 3", "4.5"]
--- check case | del, and errors along the way
