run(["let x = 5", "let x = y + 1", "x", "del x", "x", "del x", "vars", "1 +"])
=> ["x = 5", "error: unknown variable y", "5", "deleted x", "error: unknown variable x", "error: unknown variable x", "no variables", "error: syntax error"]
--- check case | Whole floats print without .0
run(["let half = 0.5", "half + half", "2.5 - 0.5", "7"])
=> ["half = 0.5", "1", "2", "7"]

+++ practice | Showing numbers to people
--- task
Write `show(value)`, the one place a calculator turns an answer into text. `value` is an `int` or a `float`.

- An `int` is shown as Python shows it: `7` → `"7"`, `-3` → `"-3"`.
- A float is first rounded to 10 decimal places, so the tiny errors of floating point disappear: `0.1 + 0.2` shows as `"0.3"`, not `"0.30000000000000004"`.
- Then, a float with a whole value is shown without `.0`: `6 / 2` → `"3"`, `1e16` → `"10000000000000000"`.
- Minus zero, which floating point can produce (`-0.0`), shows as `"0"`.
- Infinity and "not a number" are shown as Python shows them: `"inf"`, `"-inf"` and `"nan"`.
- Any other float is shown as Python shows it: `2.5` → `"2.5"`.
--- starter
def show(value):
    return str(value)
--- solution
def show(value):
    if isinstance(value, float):
        value = round(value, 10)
        if value.is_integer():
            return str(int(value))
    return str(value)
--- hint
Only floats need work. `round(value, 10)` rounds to 10 decimal places, and after that `value.is_integer()` tells you whether it is a whole number.
--- hint
`int(-0.0)` is `0`, so converting a whole float to an `int` fixes minus zero for free. Infinity and not-a-number are never "whole" (`is_integer()` is `False` for them), so they fall through to `str`.
--- check case | Ints and ordinary floats
[show(7), show(-3), show(2.5), show(-0.25)]
=> ["7", "-3", "2.5", "-0.25"]
--- check case | Floating-point noise disappears
[show(0.1 + 0.2), show(1.1 * 3), show(0.3 - 0.1)]
=> ["0.3", "3.3", "0.2"]
--- check case | Whole floats, big ones, and minus zero
[show(6 / 2), show(-3.0), show(1e16), show(-0.0), show(0.1 * 3 * 10)]
=> ["3", "-3", "10000000000000000", "0", "3"]
--- check case | Infinity and not a number
[show(float("inf")), show(float("-inf")), show(float("nan"))]
=> ["inf", "-inf", "nan"]

+++ practice | Debug: power in the wrong direction
--- task
`calculate(text)` works out `+ - * / ^` with brackets and unary minus, by recursive descent. `^` is power, and it should work like the capstone's: it groups from the right, so `2 ^ 3 ^ 2` is `2 ^ 9 = 512`, and the power can be negative, so `2 ^ -1` is `0.5`. Unary minus is looser than `^`: `-2 ^ 2` is `-4`.

The bug: `2 ^ 3 ^ 2` gives `64`, and `2 ^ -1` raises `CalcError: syntax error`. Fix the method that handles `^`.
--- starter
import re


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def tokenize(text):
    tokens = re.findall(r"\d+\.?\d*|\S", text)
    return [(float(t) if "." in t else int(t)) if t[0].isdigit() else t for t in tokens]


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def parse(self):
        value = self.expr()
        if self.pos != len(self.tokens):
            raise CalcError("syntax error")
        return value

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            if op == "/" and right == 0:
                raise CalcError("division by zero")
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        value = self.atom()
        while self.peek() == "^":
            self.take()
            value = value ** self.atom()
        return value

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise CalcError("expected )")
            return value
        raise CalcError("syntax error")


def calculate(text):
    return Parser(tokenize(text)).parse()
--- solution
import re


class CalcError(Exception):
    pass


def tokenize(text):
    tokens = re.findall(r"\d+\.?\d*|\S", text)
    return [(float(t) if "." in t else int(t)) if t[0].isdigit() else t for t in tokens]


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def parse(self):
        value = self.expr()
        if self.pos != len(self.tokens):
            raise CalcError("syntax error")
        return value

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            if op == "/" and right == 0:
                raise CalcError("division by zero")
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "^":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise CalcError("expected )")
            return value
        raise CalcError("syntax error")


def calculate(text):
    return Parser(tokenize(text)).parse()
--- hint
A `while` loop that keeps combining the value so far with the next atom groups from the *left*: `(2 ^ 3) ^ 2`. And its right side is only ever an atom, which can never start with a minus sign.
--- hint
Follow the capstone's grammar rule: `power := atom ("^" unary)?`. Take one atom; if a `^` follows, take it and let the right side be a whole `unary`, which calls `power` again. That recursion is what makes it group from the right.
--- check case | ^ groups from the right
[calculate("2 ^ 3 ^ 2"), calculate("(2 ^ 3) ^ 2")]
=> [512, 64]
--- check case | A negative power, and unary minus is looser
[calculate("2 ^ -1"), calculate("-2 ^ 2"), calculate("(-2) ^ 2"), calculate("2 ^ --2")]
=> [0.5, -4, 4, 4]
--- check case | The rest still works
[calculate("1 + 2 * 3"), calculate("2 * 3 ^ 2"), calculate("10 - 4 - 3")]
=> [7, 18, 3]

+++ practice | A rule language
--- task
A spacecraft's alarm rules are written as conditions, like `temp > 30 and not door`. Write `class RuleError(Exception)` and `check(rule, facts)`, which works out a rule and returns `True` or `False`. `facts` is a dict from names to values (numbers or booleans).

**Tokens.** Numbers (digits with at most one `.`), names (a letter or `_`, then letters, digits or `_`), the comparisons `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `==`, `!=`, and the brackets `(` and `)`. The words `and`, `or`, `not`, `true` and `false` are keywords, not names. Spaces are skipped.

**Grammar**, loosest first:

- `or` joins two conditions; it is looser than `and`, so `a or b and c` means `a or (b and c)`.
- `and` joins two conditions; it is looser than `not`.
- `not` in front of a condition turns it around. It applies to a whole comparison: `not x == 1` means `not (x == 1)`.
- A comparison is a value, optionally followed by one comparison and another value. Comparisons do not chain: `1 < 2 < 3` is a syntax error.
- A value is a number, a name (looked up in `facts`), `true`, `false`, or a whole rule in brackets.

**Errors.** Raise `RuleError`:

- `"unknown name <name>"` for a name missing from `facts`;
- `"not a condition"` when `and`, `or`, `not`, or the whole rule, get something that is not `True` or `False` (like the number in `temp and door`);
- `f"unexpected character {ch!r}"` for a character that is none of the above;
- `"syntax error"` for anything else wrong: an empty rule, a missing bracket, a token where it does not belong, or tokens left over.
--- starter
class RuleError(Exception):
    pass


def check(rule, facts):
    return eval(rule, {}, facts)
--- solution
class RuleError(Exception):
    pass


KEYWORDS = {"and", "or", "not", "true", "false"}
COMPARE = {
    "<": lambda a, b: a < b,
    "<=": lambda a, b: a <= b,
    ">": lambda a, b: a > b,
    ">=": lambda a, b: a >= b,
    "==": lambda a, b: a == b,
    "!=": lambda a, b: a != b,
}


def tokenize(rule):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(rule):
        ch = rule[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit():
            start = i
            while i < len(rule) and (rule[i].isdigit() or rule[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            text = rule[start:i]
            if text.count(".") > 1:
                raise RuleError(f"bad number {text}")
            tokens.append(("num", float(text) if "." in text else int(text)))
        elif ch.isalpha() or ch == "_":
            start = i
            while i < len(rule) and (rule[i].isalnum() or rule[i] == "_"):
                i += 1
            word = rule[start:i]
            tokens.append(("word" if word in KEYWORDS else "name", word))
        elif rule[i:i + 2] in COMPARE:
            tokens.append(("op", rule[i:i + 2]))
            i += 2
        elif ch in "<>()":
            tokens.append(("op", ch))
            i += 1
        else:
            raise RuleError(f"unexpected character {ch!r}")
    return tokens


def condition(value):
    if not isinstance(value, bool):
        raise RuleError("not a condition")
    return value


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens, facts):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0
        self.facts = facts

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else (None, None)

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def parse(self):
        value = self.either()
        if self.pos != len(self.tokens):
            raise RuleError("syntax error")
        return condition(value)

    def either(self):
        value = self.both()
        while self.peek() == ("word", "or"):
            self.take()
            left, right = condition(value), condition(self.both())
            value = left or right
        return value

    def both(self):
        value = self.negation()
        while self.peek() == ("word", "and"):
            self.take()
            left, right = condition(value), condition(self.negation())
            value = left and right
        return value

    def negation(self):
        if self.peek() == ("word", "not"):
            self.take()
            return not condition(self.negation())
        return self.comparison()

    def comparison(self):
        left = self.value()
        kind, op = self.peek()
        if kind == "op" and op in COMPARE:
            self.take()
            return COMPARE[op](left, self.value())
        return left

    def value(self):
        kind, token = self.take()
        if kind == "num":
            return token
        if kind == "name":
            if token not in self.facts:
                raise RuleError(f"unknown name {token}")
            return self.facts[token]
        if (kind, token) in (("word", "true"), ("word", "false")):
            return token == "true"
        if (kind, token) == ("op", "("):
            inside = self.either()
            if self.take() != ("op", ")"):
                raise RuleError("syntax error")
            return inside
        raise RuleError("syntax error")


def check(rule, facts):
    return Parser(tokenize(rule), facts).parse()
--- hint
Use the capstone's plan: a tokenizer that tags each token with its kind, then one parser method per level of the grammar, loosest first: `or`, `and`, `not`, comparison, value. Each method calls the next one down.
--- hint
Try the two-character comparisons before the one-character ones, as the tokenizer lesson did for `**`. Keep a dict from each comparison to a small function, so the parser does `COMPARE[op](left, right)`.
--- hint
A helper that raises `"not a condition"` unless its argument is `True` or `False` does all the type checks: call it on both sides of `and` and `or`, on what `not` gets, and on the final answer.
--- check test | The example rule
check("temp > 30 and not door", {"temp": 35, "door": False}) and not check("temp > 30 and not door", {"temp": 35, "door": True})
--- check case | and binds tighter than or; brackets change it
[check("a or b and c", {"a": True, "b": False, "c": False}), check("(a or b) and c", {"a": True, "b": False, "c": False}), check("not x == 1", {"x": 2}), check("not not true", {})]
=> [True, False, True, True]
--- check case | All six comparisons, and floats
[check(f"3 {op} 3", {}) for op in ("<", "<=", ">", ">=", "==", "!=")] + [check("pressure <= 1.5", {"pressure": 1.2})]
=> [False, True, False, True, True, False, True]
--- check case | Every kind of error
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    check(r, {'temp': 35, 'door': False})\nexcept RuleError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), r=r)) for r in ("fuel < 3", "temp", "temp and door", "not 5", "temp $ 3", "", "temp >", "(door", "door)", "1 < 2 < 3", "and door", "true false")]
=> ["unknown name fuel", "not a condition", "not a condition", "not a condition", "unexpected character '$'", "syntax error", "syntax error", "syntax error", "syntax error", "syntax error", "syntax error", "syntax error"]

=== pyp-15 | Capstone: a bank ledger with validation
--- teach
Your interpreter had to answer every line, even nonsense, without crashing. This capstone asks for something stricter. Money software is unforgiving: a lost cent is a bug report, and a transfer that happens only halfway is a disaster. The theme is **correctness under bad input**: the program stays right, whatever it is given.

You will build a small bank: accounts, deposits, withdrawals, transfers, and a statement for each account.

### Check once, at the door

Picture airport security. Every bag is checked once, at the entrance. After that, everyone inside the terminal is trusted, and nobody checks bags again at the café.

Programs can work the same way. The **boundary** is the place where values arrive from outside: from a user, a file, or another program. **Validate at the boundary**: check each value the moment it arrives, and turn it into a trusted form that the rest of the code can rely on. Then the inside of the program never has to [[worry about it again|boundary-trust]].

Here, amounts arrive as strings like `"12.34"`. At the boundary you check the format strictly and convert it to whole cents, an `int`, once. After that, no other code ever sees a string or a float.

### Strict means strict

The spec allows digits, then optionally a `.` with one or two decimals, with spaces around them allowed. So `"12"`, `"12.3"` and `" 12.34 "` are fine. Each of these must be refused with `ValueError`:

- `"12.345"`: three decimals.
- `"-5"`, `"abc"`, `"5."`, `""` and `" "`: not digits in the right shape.
- `"1e3"`: Python's `float("1e3")` reads it as `1000.0`, but it is not an amount in this format.
- `12.5`, which is not a string at all. Accepting floats would [[invite the rounding errors|float-money]] that storing cents is meant to avoid.

The expense tracker checked amounts piece by piece with `partition` and `isdigit()`. A regular expression can say the whole format in one line. It needs one new piece: `{1,2}` means "one or two times".

```python
import re
AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")
```

In words: `\d+` is one or more digits; `\.` is a real dot (a bare `.` would mean "any character"); `\d{1,2}` is one or two digits; and the `( )?` around the dot part makes that whole part optional.

### match or fullmatch

Here is a trap. `match` only checks that the pattern fits at the **start** of the string. It is happy if more text follows. `fullmatch` checks that the pattern fits the **whole** string:

```python
bool(AMOUNT.fullmatch("12.34"))     # True
bool(AMOUNT.fullmatch("12.345"))    # False: the 5 is left over
bool(AMOUNT.match("12.345"))        # True: "12.34" fits at the start
```

For validation you want [[fullmatch|match-vs-fullmatch]].

One step comes before the pattern: check that the value is a string at all, with `isinstance(amount, str)`. Otherwise `amount.strip()` on the float `12.5` crashes with `AttributeError`, not the `ValueError` the spec asks for.

Once the format is right, convert to cents the way the expense tracker did, with `partition(".")` and `ljust(2, "0")`.

### Showing cents as money

Balances are shown with two decimals and no thousands separator: `123450` cents is `"1234.50"`. That is the expense tracker's formatting without the `,`:

```python
cents = 123450
f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"      # '1234.50'
```

### Account numbers

Account numbers are `"ACC-0001"`, `"ACC-0002"`, and so on. Keep a counter of accounts opened, and pad it with the format `:04d`, which means "a whole number, at least four digits wide, [[padded with zeros|zero-padding]] on the left":

```python
f"ACC-{7:04d}"       # 'ACC-0007'
f"ACC-{123:04d}"     # 'ACC-0123'
```

### All or nothing

A transfer is two changes: take money from one account, give it to another. If the first happens and the second fails, the money has vanished.

An operation that happens completely or not at all is called **[[atomic|atomic]]**. The rule that makes an operation atomic: **check everything that can fail first, then make all the changes**, with nothing between the changes that can raise. For a transfer, that means: look up both accounts, check they are different, validate the amount, and check the balance. Only then change the two balances.

Here is the wrong order:

```python
src["balance"] -= cents               # the money leaves the source...
dst = self._account(target)           # ...then this raises KeyError
```

If `target` does not exist, the second line raises, and the money has left the source with nowhere to land. A failed transfer, whether to a missing account or for more than the balance, must leave *both* accounts, and both statements, exactly as they were.

### An audit trail

Real ledgers never overwrite a balance and forget. They record every movement. Here each account keeps a **statement**: a list of lines, one per movement, each ending with the balance after it:

```
open 10.00 -> 10.00
deposit 5.50 -> 15.50
withdraw 2.00 -> 13.50
```

Anyone can check the arithmetic line by line: 10.00 plus 5.50 is 15.50, minus 2.00 is 13.50. A record like this is called an [[audit trail|audit-trail]]. Add a line only after an operation has succeeded, so failed operations leave no trace.

### Different errors for different problems

The spec uses three kinds of error, because they mean three different things to the code that calls yours:

- `ValueError`: bad input, like `"12.345"`. A form would show the user a message and let them try again.
- `KeyError`: an account number that does not exist. That is probably a bug somewhere in the calling code.
- `InsufficientFunds`: a withdrawal or transfer bigger than the balance. This is a [[normal business outcome|expected-errors]], not a mistake.

`InsufficientFunds` is your own exception class, like `LibraryError` in the library system: `class InsufficientFunds(Exception):` with `pass` as its body.

### Design questions to settle first

Before you type, decide: How will you store an account (its name, its balance in cents, its statement lines)? How will you make account numbers? Where does validation live, so that it is written once and used by `open`, `deposit`, `withdraw` and `transfer`?

**Watch out:** writing the amount check in four places, slightly differently each time. Sooner or later one of them lets `"12.345"` through. Write one function that validates a string and converts it to cents, and one that also insists on more than zero. Deposits, withdrawals and transfers use the second; `open` uses only the first, because an account may start at zero.

::: context boundary-trust A spacecraft lost at a boundary
In 1999, NASA's Mars Climate Orbiter was lost as it arrived at Mars. Software on the ground, built by one team, reported thruster impulse in pound-force seconds. The navigation software, built by another team, read those numbers as newton-seconds, which are about 4.45 times smaller. Nothing checked the numbers where they crossed from one system to the other, so the trajectory was wrong, and the orbiter came in far too low. The boundary between two programs is exactly where a value must be checked, and turned into one agreed form.
:::

::: context float-money The float version drifts
One of this lesson's checks deposits 0.10 a thousand times, then withdraws 0.07 a thousand times. In whole cents that is exact: 10,000 minus 7,000 is 3,000 cents, which is 30.00. With floats, the tiny error in each step piles up:

```python
b = 0.0
for _ in range(1000): b += 0.10
for _ in range(1000): b -= 0.07
b     # 30.00000000000196
```

Neither 0.10 nor 0.07 can be stored exactly in binary, as the floating-point lesson showed. A bank that did this across millions of accounts would be out by real money.
:::

::: context match-vs-fullmatch How much of the string has to match
`match` is satisfied once the pattern fits somewhere starting at position 0. `fullmatch` needs it to fit from the first character to the last:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="16" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="110" y="36">1</text><text x="140" y="36">2</text><text x="170" y="36">.</text>
    <text x="200" y="36">3</text><text x="230" y="36">4</text><text x="260" y="36">5</text>
  </g>
  <text x="20" y="72" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">match</text>
  <rect x="96" y="58" width="148" height="20" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="300" y="72" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">True</text>
  <text x="20" y="108" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">fullmatch</text>
  <rect x="96" y="94" width="178" height="20" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="300" y="108" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">False</text>
  <text x="260" y="52" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">left over</text>
</svg>
```

Adding `$` to the end of a pattern is not quite enough: in Python `$` also matches just before a final newline, so `re.match(r"\d+$", "12\n")` succeeds. `fullmatch` has no such gap.
:::

::: context zero-padding Four digits is a minimum
The `0` in `:04d` says "pad with zeros", and the `4` is a minimum width, not a limit. So `f"{12345:04d}"` is `'12345'`: the 12,345th account still gets its number, just one digit longer. Zero-padding makes numbers the same length, so they line up in lists and sort correctly as text: `"ACC-0010"` comes after `"ACC-0009"`, while `"ACC-10"` would sort before `"ACC-9"`.
:::

::: context atomic All or nothing
The word comes from the Greek *atomos*, "uncuttable": an atomic operation cannot be split into a half that happened and a half that did not.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="24" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">change, then check</text>
  <rect x="20" y="34" width="90" height="34" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="65" y="56" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ada 7.00</text>
  <rect x="150" y="34" width="90" height="34" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="5 4"/>
  <text x="195" y="56" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">no account</text>
  <text x="300" y="56" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">3.00 lost</text>
  <text x="20" y="98" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">check, then change</text>
  <rect x="20" y="108" width="90" height="34" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="65" y="130" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ada 10.00</text>
  <rect x="150" y="108" width="90" height="34" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="5 4"/>
  <text x="195" y="130" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">no account</text>
  <text x="300" y="130" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">nothing moved</text>
</svg>
```

Databases give you this as a **transaction**: a group of changes that is either saved as a whole or rolled back as a whole. Atomicity is the A in ACID, the four promises a reliable database makes.
:::

::: context audit-trail Why ledgers keep every line
Bookkeeping has worked this way for centuries. Luca Pacioli described double-entry bookkeeping in a mathematics book printed in 1494: every movement of money is written down twice, once where it left and once where it arrived, so the books can always be checked. Your transfer does the same with its `transfer-out` and `transfer-in` lines. Spacecraft keep audit trails too: every command sent and every event on board goes into a log, so that engineers can later work out exactly what happened and when.
:::

::: context expected-errors Errors that are not mistakes
Not every exception means something went wrong in the code. "Not enough money" is a normal answer a bank gives every day, just as "that book is out on loan" was in the library system. A separate exception class lets the calling code pick exactly that case, `except InsufficientFunds:`, and show a friendly message, while a `KeyError` from a real bug still stops the program loudly, where a test will catch it. Catching every exception with one broad `except` would hide the bugs along with the business outcomes.
:::
--- task
Build `class InsufficientFunds(Exception)` and `class Bank`, to this specification. The design inside is up to you.

- Amounts are strings of digits with an optional `.` and one or two decimals (`"12"`, `"12.3"`, `"12.34"`), surrounding spaces allowed. Anything else (including non-strings) raises `ValueError`. Balances are always shown as strings with two decimals and no thousands separator, like `"1234.50"`.
- `open(name, initial="0.00")` returns a new account number: `"ACC-0001"`, `"ACC-0002"`, … An empty name raises `ValueError`. The initial amount may be zero.
- `deposit(number, amount)` and `withdraw(number, amount)`: the amount must be more than zero (`ValueError`); withdrawing more than the balance raises `InsufficientFunds`.
- `transfer(source, target, amount)`: moves money between two different accounts (the same account twice is a `ValueError`); insufficient funds raises `InsufficientFunds`. A failed transfer changes nothing.
- An unknown account number raises `KeyError` everywhere.
- `balance(number)` returns the balance string. `total()` returns the sum of all balances.
- `statement(number)` returns a list of lines, one per movement: `"open 10.00 -> 10.00"`, `"deposit 5.50 -> 15.50"`, `"withdraw 2.00 -> 13.50"`, `"transfer-out 3.00 to ACC-0002 -> 10.50"`, `"transfer-in 3.00 from ACC-0001 -> 3.00"`. Each ends with the balance after that movement. Failed operations add no line.
--- starter
# Capstone: design and build InsufficientFunds and Bank to the specification in the task.
--- solution
import re


class InsufficientFunds(Exception):
    pass


AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    """"12.34" -> 1234 cents. Only strings with at most two decimals."""
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))


def fmt(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Bank:
    def __init__(self):
        self._accounts = {}
        self._opened = 0

    def _account(self, number):
        if number not in self._accounts:
            raise KeyError(number)
        return self._accounts[number]

    def _positive(self, amount):
        cents = parse_amount(amount)
        if cents <= 0:
            raise ValueError("amount must be more than zero")
        return cents

    def _record(self, number, kind, cents, detail=""):
        account = self._accounts[number]
        account["history"].append(f"{kind} {fmt(cents)}{detail} -> {fmt(account['balance'])}")

    def open(self, name, initial="0.00"):
        if not isinstance(name, str) or not name.strip():
            raise ValueError("an account needs a name")
        cents = parse_amount(initial)
        self._opened += 1
        number = f"ACC-{self._opened:04d}"
        self._accounts[number] = {"name": name.strip(), "balance": cents, "history": []}
        self._record(number, "open", cents)
        return number

    def deposit(self, number, amount):
        account = self._account(number)
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        account["balance"] += cents
        self._record(number, "deposit", cents)

    def withdraw(self, number, amount):
        account = self._account(number)
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > account["balance"]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(f"{number} has only {fmt(account['balance'])}")
        account["balance"] -= cents
        self._record(number, "withdraw", cents)

    def transfer(self, source, target, amount):
        src = self._account(source)
        self._account(target)
        if source == target:
            raise ValueError("cannot transfer to the same account")
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > src["balance"]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(f"{source} has only {fmt(src['balance'])}")
        src["balance"] -= cents
        self._accounts[target]["balance"] += cents
        self._record(source, "transfer-out", cents, f" to {target}")
        self._record(target, "transfer-in", cents, f" from {source}")

    def balance(self, number):
        return fmt(self._account(number)["balance"])

    def statement(self, number):
        return list(self._account(number)["history"])

    def total(self):
        return fmt(sum(a["balance"] for a in self._accounts.values()))
--- hint
Validate at the boundary: write one function that checks an amount (`isinstance(text, str)` first, then `re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")` with `fullmatch` on the stripped text) and converts it to integer cents. Use it everywhere, and write one function that formats cents for display.
--- hint
Store each account as a record with its name, balance in cents and a list of statement lines. Count opened accounts to make numbers with `f"ACC-{n:04d}"`. Add a statement line only after an operation has succeeded.
--- hint
In `transfer`, look up both accounts, check they differ, validate the amount and check the balance before you change either balance. Only then subtract, add, and record both lines.
--- check case | Open, deposit, withdraw
(lambda b: (lambda a: (b.deposit(a, "5.50"), b.withdraw(a, " 2 "), b.balance(a), b.statement(a)))(b.open("Ada", "10.00")))(Bank())[2:]
=> ("13.50", ["open 10.00 -> 10.00", "deposit 5.50 -> 15.50", "withdraw 2.00 -> 13.50"])
--- check test | Account numbers count up
(lambda b: [b.open("A"), b.open("B"), b.open("C")])(Bank()) == ["ACC-0001", "ACC-0002", "ACC-0003"]
--- check case | A transfer moves money and records both sides
(lambda b: (b.open("Ada", "20"), b.open("Bo"), b.transfer("ACC-0001", "ACC-0002", "3"), b.balance("ACC-0001"), b.balance("ACC-0002"), b.statement("ACC-0001")[-1], b.statement("ACC-0002")[-1], b.total())[3:])(Bank())
=> ("17.00", "3.00", "transfer-out 3.00 to ACC-0002 -> 17.00", "transfer-in 3.00 from ACC-0001 -> 3.00", "20.00")
--- check test | Invalid amounts raise ValueError
(lambda b: (lambda a: all(raises(ValueError, lambda x=x: b.deposit(a, x)) for x in ["12.345", "-5", "abc", "", "1e3", "0", "0.00", "5.", 12.5, None]))(b.open("Ada")))(Bank())
--- check test | Overdrawing raises InsufficientFunds and changes nothing
(lambda b: (lambda a: (raises(InsufficientFunds, lambda: b.withdraw(a, "10.01")), b.balance(a), len(b.statement(a))))(b.open("Ada", "10")))(Bank()) == (True, "10.00", 1)
--- check test | A failed transfer changes nothing at all
(lambda b: (b.open("Ada", "10"), b.open("Bo", "1"), raises(InsufficientFunds, lambda: b.transfer("ACC-0001", "ACC-0002", "50")), raises(KeyError, lambda: b.transfer("ACC-0001", "ACC-0099", "1")), raises(ValueError, lambda: b.transfer("ACC-0001", "ACC-0001", "1")), b.balance("ACC-0001"), b.balance("ACC-0002"), len(b.statement("ACC-0001")), len(b.statement("ACC-0002")))[2:])(Bank()) == (True, True, True, "10.00", "1.00", 1, 1)
--- check test | Unknown accounts raise KeyError
raises(KeyError, lambda: Bank().balance("ACC-0001")) and raises(KeyError, lambda: Bank().deposit("ACC-0001", "1"))
--- check test | An account needs a name
raises(ValueError, lambda: Bank().open("   "))
--- check test | Exact to the cent over many operations
(lambda b: (lambda a: ([b.deposit(a, "0.10") for _ in range(1000)], [b.withdraw(a, "0.07") for _ in range(1000)], b.balance(a))[-1])(b.open("Ada")))(Bank()) == "30.00"
--- check test | Big balances print with no separator
(lambda b: b.balance(b.open("Rich", "1234567.8")))(Bank()) == "1234567.80"

+++ practice | Strict percentages
--- task
An interest-rate form sends percentages as text, like `"12.5%"`. Write `parse_percent(text)`, which validates one at the boundary and returns it as a whole number of hundredths of a percent (an `int`):

- `"12.5%"` → `1250`, `"7.25%"` → `725`, `"0%"` → `0`, `" 100% "` → `10000`.
- The format is digits, then optionally a `.` and one or two digits, then `%` straight after. Spaces around the whole thing are allowed.
- The value may be at most 100%.
- Anything else raises `ValueError`: `"12.345%"`, `"12"`, `"%"`, `"-1%"`, `"12.5 %"`, `"1e2%"`, `"100.01%"`, and anything that is not a string, such as `12.5` or `None`.
--- starter
def parse_percent(text):
    return int(float(text.strip("% ")) * 100)
--- solution
import re

PERCENT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?%")


def parse_percent(text):
    """"12.5%" -> 1250 hundredths of a percent."""
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not PERCENT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid percentage: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip()[:-1].partition(".")
    value = int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
    if value > 10000:
        raise ValueError(f"more than 100%: {text!r}")
    return value
--- hint
This is the lesson's amount check with a `%` on the end: check the type first, then `fullmatch` a pattern on the stripped text, so that nothing extra can sneak in before or after.
--- hint
After the pattern passes, cut off the `%` with `[:-1]` and convert exactly like cents. Check the 100% limit on the whole number, `10000`, not on a float.
--- check case | Valid percentages
[parse_percent(t) for t in ("12.5%", "7.25%", "0%", " 100% ", "0.01%")]
=> [1250, 725, 0, 10000, 1]
--- check test | Wrong formats raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: parse_percent(t)) for t in ("12.345%", "12", "%", "-1%", "12.5 %", "1e2%", "", "5.%", "12.5%%"))
--- check test | Over 100%, and values that are not strings
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: parse_percent(t)) for t in ("100.01%", "101%", 12.5, None, 50))

+++ practice | Account numbers, both ways
--- task
A bank's account numbers are `"ACC-"` and exactly four digits, from `"ACC-0001"` to `"ACC-9999"`. Write two functions:

- `account_number(n)` returns the account number for a whole number `n` (an `int`) from 1 to 9999: `7` → `"ACC-0007"`. Anything else raises `ValueError`, including `0`, `10000`, `"7"`, `7.0` and `True`.
- `account_index(text)` does the reverse: `"ACC-0042"` → `42`. The text must be exactly `ACC-`, in capitals, and four digits, with nothing before or after, and not `"ACC-0000"`. Anything else raises `ValueError`, including values that are not strings.
--- starter
def account_number(n):
    return "ACC-" + str(n)


def account_index(text):
    return int(text[4:])
--- solution
import re

ACCOUNT = re.compile(r"ACC-(\d{4})")


def account_number(n):
    if type(n) is not int or not 1 <= n <= 9999:
        raise ValueError(f"no account number for {n!r}")
    return f"ACC-{n:04d}"


def account_index(text):
    m = ACCOUNT.fullmatch(text) if isinstance(text, str) else None
    if not m or int(m.group(1)) == 0:
        raise ValueError(f"invalid account number: {text!r}")
    return int(m.group(1))
--- hint
`:04d` pads a number with zeros to four digits. `type(n) is not int` refuses floats, strings and booleans in one test, since `True` is a kind of `int` for `isinstance`.
--- hint
`\d{4}` is exactly four digits. Put them in a group so `m.group(1)` hands them back after `fullmatch`, and refuse `0000` afterwards.
--- check case | Numbers to account numbers
[account_number(n) for n in (7, 42, 123, 9999, 1)]
=> ["ACC-0007", "ACC-0042", "ACC-0123", "ACC-9999", "ACC-0001"]
--- check case | Account numbers to numbers
[account_index(t) for t in ("ACC-0042", "ACC-9999", "ACC-0001")]
=> [42, 9999, 1]
--- check test | Bad numbers raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda n=n: account_number(n)) for n in (0, 10000, -1, "7", 7.0, True))
--- check test | Bad account numbers raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: account_index(t)) for t in ("ACC-42", "acc-0042", "ACC-00042", "ACC-0000", " ACC-0001", "ACC0001", "ACC-12a4", 42, None))

+++ practice | Running a batch of bank commands
--- task
The starter has a finished `Bank` from the capstone (with `InsufficientFunds`). Write `run(bank, lines)`, which carries out a file of commands on it and returns the output lines:

- `deposit <account> <amount>`, `withdraw <account> <amount>` and `transfer <from> <to> <amount>` produce `"ok"`.
- `balance <account>` produces `"ACC-0001: 12.50"`.
- Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped. Command words are case-insensitive.
- A line with an unknown command, or the wrong number of words, produces `"error: bad command"`.
- A `ValueError` produces `"error: <its message>"`, a `KeyError` produces `"error: unknown account"`, and `InsufficientFunds` produces `"error: insufficient funds"`. The run always carries on with the next line.
--- starter
import re


class InsufficientFunds(Exception):
    pass


AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))


def fmt(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Bank:
    def __init__(self):
        self._balances = {}

    def open(self, initial="0.00"):
        number = f"ACC-{len(self._balances) + 1:04d}"
        self._balances[number] = parse_amount(initial)
        return number

    def _positive(self, amount):
        cents = parse_amount(amount)
        if cents <= 0:
            raise ValueError("amount must be more than zero")
        return cents

    def deposit(self, number, amount):
        self._balances[number]  # raises KeyError for an unknown account
        self._balances[number] += self._positive(amount)

    def withdraw(self, number, amount):
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > self._balances[number]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(number)
        self._balances[number] -= cents

    def transfer(self, source, target, amount):
        self._balances[source]  # each raises KeyError for an unknown account
        self._balances[target]
        if source == target:
            raise ValueError("cannot transfer to the same account")
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > self._balances[source]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(source)
        self._balances[source] -= cents
        self._balances[target] += cents

    def balance(self, number):
        return fmt(self._balances[number])


def run(bank, lines):
    return []
--- solution
import re


class InsufficientFunds(Exception):
    pass


AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))


def fmt(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Bank:
    def __init__(self):
        self._balances = {}

    def open(self, initial="0.00"):
        number = f"ACC-{len(self._balances) + 1:04d}"
        self._balances[number] = parse_amount(initial)
        return number

    def _positive(self, amount):
        cents = parse_amount(amount)
        if cents <= 0:
            raise ValueError("amount must be more than zero")
        return cents

    def deposit(self, number, amount):
        self._balances[number]  # raises KeyError for an unknown account
        self._balances[number] += self._positive(amount)

    def withdraw(self, number, amount):
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > self._balances[number]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(number)
        self._balances[number] -= cents

    def transfer(self, source, target, amount):
        self._balances[source]  # each raises KeyError for an unknown account
        self._balances[target]
        if source == target:
            raise ValueError("cannot transfer to the same account")
        cents = self._positive(amount)
        if cents > self._balances[source]:
            raise InsufficientFunds(source)
        self._balances[source] -= cents
        self._balances[target] += cents

    def balance(self, number):
        return fmt(self._balances[number])


WORDS = {"deposit": 3, "withdraw": 3, "transfer": 4, "balance": 2}


def execute(bank, words):
    cmd = words[0].lower()
    if WORDS.get(cmd) != len(words):
        return "error: bad command"
    if cmd == "balance":
        return f"{words[1]}: {bank.balance(words[1])}"
    if cmd == "deposit":
        bank.deposit(words[1], words[2])
    elif cmd == "withdraw":
        bank.withdraw(words[1], words[2])
    else:
        bank.transfer(words[1], words[2], words[3])
    return "ok"


def run(bank, lines):
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            out.append(execute(bank, line.split()))
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
        except KeyError:
            out.append("error: unknown account")
        except InsufficientFunds:
            out.append("error: insufficient funds")
    return out
--- hint
A dict from each command to the number of words its line must have checks "unknown command" and "wrong number of words" in one step: `WORDS.get(cmd) != len(words)`.
--- hint
One `try` can have several `except` clauses, one per kind of error, each producing its own line. The bank raises all three kinds; `run` only translates them into words.
--- check case | A day of banking
(lambda b: (b.open("20"), b.open(), run(b, ["deposit ACC-0001 5.50", "transfer ACC-0001 ACC-0002 3", "WITHDRAW ACC-0002 1.25", "balance ACC-0001", "balance ACC-0002"]))[-1])(Bank())
=> ["ok", "ok", "ok", "ACC-0001: 22.50", "ACC-0002: 1.75"]
--- check case | Each kind of error, and the run carries on
(lambda b: (b.open("10"), run(b, ["withdraw ACC-0001 10.01", "deposit ACC-0009 1", "deposit ACC-0001 12.345", "deposit ACC-0001 0", "transfer ACC-0001 ACC-0001 1", "balance ACC-0001"]))[-1])(Bank())
=> ["error: insufficient funds", "error: unknown account", "error: invalid amount: '12.345'", "error: amount must be more than zero", "error: cannot transfer to the same account", "ACC-0001: 10.00"]
--- check case | Bad commands, blank lines and comments
(lambda b: (b.open(), run(b, ["", "# start", "balance", "deposit ACC-0001", "steal ACC-0001 5", "balance ACC-0001 now"]))[-1])(Bank())
=> ["error: bad command", "error: bad command", "error: bad command", "error: bad command"]

+++ practice | Splitting a bill fairly
--- task
Write `split_bill(total, people)`, which splits a bill between people, to the cent. `total` is an amount string in the capstone's format (digits, optionally a `.` and one or two digits, spaces around allowed), and `people` is a whole number (`int`) of at least 1.

- Return a list of `people` amounts, each formatted with two decimals and no thousands separator.
- The shares must add up to exactly the total. When the cents do not divide evenly, the leftover cents go one each to the first people in the list: `split_bill("10.00", 3)` is `["3.34", "3.33", "3.33"]`.
- A bad amount, or a `people` that is not an `int` of at least 1, raises `ValueError`.
--- starter
def split_bill(total, people):
    share = float(total) / people
    return [f"{share:.2f}"] * people
--- solution
import re

AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))


def fmt(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"


def split_bill(total, people):
    if type(people) is not int or people < 1:
        raise ValueError("at least one person must pay")
    share, left_over = divmod(parse_amount(total), people)
    return [fmt(share + 1 if i < left_over else share) for i in range(people)]
--- hint
Work in whole cents. `divmod(cents, people)` gives the share every person pays and the number of cents left over, which is always fewer than the number of people.
--- hint
The first `left_over` people pay one cent more. A comprehension over `range(people)` with `share + 1 if i < left_over else share` builds the list, formatted like the capstone's balances.
--- check case | Leftover cents go to the first people
[split_bill("10.00", 3), split_bill("0.01", 3), split_bill("100", 1)]
=> [["3.34", "3.33", "3.33"], ["0.01", "0.00", "0.00"], ["100.00"]]
--- check case | Nothing to pay, and an even split
[split_bill("0", 2), split_bill(" 12.3 ", 3)]
=> [["0.00", "0.00"], ["4.10", "4.10", "4.10"]]
--- check test | The shares always add up exactly
all(sum(int(s.replace(".", "")) for s in split_bill(t, n)) == int(t.replace(".", "")) for t, n in [("99.99", 7), ("1000.00", 3), ("0.05", 9), ("12345.67", 11)])
--- check test | Bad input raises ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda a=a: split_bill(*a)) for a in [("12.345", 2), ("-5", 2), ("10", 0), ("10", -1), ("10", 2.0), (10, 2)])

+++ practice | Debug: three decimals slip through
--- task
`parse_amount(text)` should turn an amount string into cents: digits, optionally a `.` and one or two digits, with spaces around allowed. `"12.34"` is `1234` and `" 7 "` is `700`. Anything else must raise `ValueError`.

But `parse_amount("12.345")` returns `1545` instead of raising, and a few other bad strings get through too. Fix the check.
--- starter
import re

AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.match(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
--- solution
import re

AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")


def parse_amount(text):
    if not isinstance(text, str) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(text.strip()):
        raise ValueError(f"invalid amount: {text!r}")
    whole, _, frac = text.strip().partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))
--- hint
The pattern itself is right. What does `match` check about the characters *after* the part that fits?
--- hint
`match` is happy when the pattern fits at the start, however much text follows: `"12.34"` fits at the start of `"12.345"`. `fullmatch` insists the pattern covers the whole string.
--- check test | Three decimals are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_amount("12.345"))
--- check test | Other strings with extra text are refused
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: parse_amount(t)) for t in ("12.3456", "5.", "7 8", "1.2.3", "12.34.56"))
--- check case | Good amounts still work
[parse_amount(t) for t in ("12.34", " 7 ", "0.5", "100")]
=> [1234, 700, 50, 10000]

+++ practice | Checking an audit trail
--- task
A statement from the bank capstone is a list of lines, each ending with the balance after it:

```
open 10.00 -> 10.00
deposit 5.50 -> 15.50
withdraw 2.00 -> 13.50
transfer-out 3.00 to ACC-0002 -> 10.50
transfer-in 1.25 from ACC-0003 -> 11.75
```

Write `audit(lines)`, which checks a statement and returns the position (from 0) of the first line that is wrong, or `None` if every line is right. An empty statement is right.

- Each line has exactly one of the five shapes above, with single spaces. Amounts and balances are digits, a `.` and exactly two digits. Account numbers are `ACC-` and four digits.
- The first line, and only the first, is `open`. Its amount (which may be zero) is the balance after it.
- `deposit` and `transfer-in` add their amount to the previous balance; `withdraw` and `transfer-out` subtract it. Their amount must be more than zero.
- A line is wrong if its shape is wrong, if its balance is not what the arithmetic gives, or if that balance would be below zero.
--- starter
def audit(lines):
    for i, line in enumerate(lines):
        if "->" not in line:
            return i
    return None
--- solution
import re

AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+\.\d\d")
ACCOUNT = re.compile(r"ACC-\d{4}")
SIGN = {"open": 0, "deposit": 1, "withdraw": -1, "transfer-out": -1, "transfer-in": 1}
LINK = {"transfer-out": "to", "transfer-in": "from"}


def cents(text):
    whole, _, frac = text.partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac)


def read_line(line):
    """(kind, amount, balance) in cents, or None if the line has the wrong shape."""
    words = line.split(" ")
    kind = words[0]
    if kind in LINK:
        if len(words) != 6 or words[2] != LINK[kind] or not ACCOUNT.fullmatch(words[3]):
            return None
        words = words[:2] + words[4:]
    if kind not in SIGN or len(words) != 4 or words[2] != "->":
        return None
    if not AMOUNT.fullmatch(words[1]) or not AMOUNT.fullmatch(words[3]):
        return None
    return kind, cents(words[1]), cents(words[3])


def audit(lines):
    balance = 0
    for i, line in enumerate(lines):
        parsed = read_line(line)
        if parsed is None:
            return i
        kind, amount, after = parsed
        if (kind == "open") != (i == 0):
            return i
        if kind != "open" and amount == 0:
            return i
        expected = amount if kind == "open" else balance + SIGN[kind] * amount
        if expected < 0 or after != expected:
            return i
        balance = after
    return None
--- hint
Validate at the boundary: first turn each line into a trusted `(kind, amount, balance)` in whole cents, or `None` when its shape is wrong. Then the checking loop only does arithmetic.
--- hint
Split on single spaces. The two transfer shapes have two extra words (`to ACC-0002` or `from ACC-0003`); check those and remove them, and then every shape is four words: kind, amount, `->`, balance.
--- hint
A dict from each kind to `+1` or `-1` turns the arithmetic into one line: `balance + SIGN[kind] * amount`. The rule "open first, and only first" is broken exactly when `(kind == "open") != (i == 0)`.
--- check test | A correct statement, and an empty one
audit(["open 10.00 -> 10.00", "deposit 5.50 -> 15.50", "withdraw 2.00 -> 13.50", "transfer-out 3.00 to ACC-0002 -> 10.50", "transfer-in 1.25 from ACC-0003 -> 11.75"]) is None and audit([]) is None and audit(["open 0.00 -> 0.00"]) is None
--- check case | Wrong arithmetic, and a balance below zero
[audit(["open 10.00 -> 10.00", "deposit 5.50 -> 15.50", "withdraw 2.00 -> 13.00"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "withdraw 2.00 -> -1.00"]), audit(["open 5.00 -> 6.00"])]
=> [2, 1, 0]
--- check case | Wrong shapes
[audit(["open 10.00 -> 10.00", "deposit 5.5 -> 15.50"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "transfer-out 1.00 from ACC-0002 -> 0.00"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "transfer-in 1.00 from ACC-2 -> 2.00"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "refund 1.00 -> 2.00"]), audit(["open 1.00  -> 1.00"])]
=> [1, 1, 1, 1, 0]
--- check case | open must come first, and only first; amounts above zero
[audit(["deposit 1.00 -> 1.00"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "open 1.00 -> 1.00"]), audit(["open 1.00 -> 1.00", "deposit 0.00 -> 1.00"])]
=> [0, 1, 1]

=== pyp-16 | Capstone: a text adventure state machine
--- teach
Your bank refused bad input and kept every account exactly right. This is the last capstone, and the last lesson of the whole Python ladder. It ends with a game, and the game is built on one of the most useful ideas in software.

A **text adventure** is a game played entirely in words. The game describes a room; you type a command like `go north` or `take key`; the game replies. Here is the start of a game in the world from the starter (`>` marks what the player typed):

```
> look
A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp.
> go north
Shelves of old books. Exits: south. You see: key, map.
> take key
Taken: key.
> inventory
You carry: key.
```

### A state machine

Think of a vending machine. At any moment it is in some **state**: waiting, holding 50 cents, handing out a drink. Each thing you do (put in a coin, press a button) moves it to a new state, and it shows you a reply. A program shaped like that is a **[[state machine|state-machine]]**: it is always in some state, each input moves it to a new state, and the reply describes what happened.

A text adventure is one. Its state is which room you are in, what you carry, what lies in each room, and whether the game is over. Each command changes the state and gets a reply. The same shape runs vending machines, network connections, the checkout on a shopping site, and the characters in video games.

### Data and logic, kept apart

The rooms, exits and items are **data**: the `WORLD` dict in the starter. You could edit it, or load it from a JSON file the way the library system did, without touching any code. The rules, how `go`, `take` and `drop` work, are **logic**: your code.

Keep them apart, and your code works for [[any world with the same shape|data-driven]], not only the example. The checks try a different world too, so no room or item name from `WORLD` should appear anywhere in your class.

Here is one room from the starter:

```python
"kitchen": {
    "description": "It smells of bread.",
    "exits": {"west": "hall", "down": "cellar"},
    "items": [],
    "locked": {"down": "key"},
},
```

`exits` maps each direction to the room it leads to. `locked` is optional: it maps a direction to the item you must carry to go that way. Here, going `down` from the kitchen needs the `key`.

### Name the state before you code

List exactly what changes during play:

- the current room;
- the **inventory**, the items you carry;
- the items in each room, because taking and dropping move them;
- whether the game is over.

Everything else stays fixed: descriptions, exits, and which exit needs which item. Keep the changing state in the `Game` object, for example in `self.room`, `self.inventory` and `self.over`. The spec says `game.room` must be the name of the current room.

### Do not change the world you were given

Picture two games made from the same `WORLD` dict. The first player takes the lamp. If `take` removes `"lamp"` from the list inside the *shared* dict, the second game starts with the lamp already gone. That is [[the aliasing bug|aliasing-again]] from the advanced course, once more.

So make your own deep copy of the world when a game starts, and change only your copy:

```python
import copy
self.world = copy.deepcopy(world)
```

### Reading a command: parse, dispatch, handle

Three small steps, like the command dispatcher in the expert course's plugin lesson.

**1. Parse.** Lowercase the command, so `TAKE KEY` works too, and split it into words. The first word is the **verb**; the rest is what it acts on.

```python
"Go  North".lower().split()     # ['go', 'north']
"".split()                      # []: nothing typed at all
```

**2. Dispatch.** Look at the verb and pick the method that handles it. An `if` chain works, and so does [[a dict from verb to method|dispatch-table]].

**3. Handle.** Write one method per verb. Each one checks its own **preconditions** (the things that must be true before the action can happen: is there such an exit? is it locked? is the item here?) and returns the reply.

Some commands do not fit any verb. A verb that needs a word but has none (`go` on its own), a verb that takes no word but got one (`look around`), an empty line and any unknown verb all get `"I don't understand."`.

### Locked exits

Most rooms have no `"locked"` key at all, so `room["locked"]` would raise `KeyError`. `.get` with a default handles the missing key: `room.get("locked", {})` gives an empty dict when there is none. A second `.get` then finds the item a direction needs, or `None`:

```python
room = {"exits": {"up": "rim"}, "items": []}
room.get("locked", {}).get("up")       # None: nothing locks this way
```

So `go` checks, in order: does the exit exist? If it is locked, is its item in your inventory? Only then move, and reply with the new room's `look` text.

### The end state

Taking the goal item wins the game. From then on, every command gets the same reply, `"The game is over."`. So check "is the game over?" *first* in `do`, before any other logic runs. A check at the very top of a method that returns early is called a [[guard clause|guard-clause]].

### Exact output

The checker is a program, and it compares your replies [[character for character|exact-output]]: every full stop, every comma, every list in the right order. Read the spec closely for which lists are sorted:

- the exits in `look`: sorted;
- the items in a room: in the room's own order, with a dropped item added at the end;
- the inventory: sorted.

A room with no exits says `Exits: none.`. One tidy way to get that: `", ".join` of an empty list is `""`, and an empty string counts as false, so `or` moves on to its right side:

```python
", ".join(sorted({"north": "library", "east": "kitchen"})) or "none"    # 'east, north'
", ".join(sorted({})) or "none"                                          # 'none'
```

**Watch out:** putting the "game over" check anywhere but the top of `do`. Then, after the win, `look` still describes the cellar and `take` still works. The spec wants every command after the win to reply `"The game is over."`, so the guard goes first.

When this passes, you have climbed the whole Python ladder: from printing one line to a game engine that runs any world you give it. [[Where you go from here|where-next]] is up to you.

::: context state-machine The game as a state machine
Every command is an arrow from one state to the next. Most arrows lead back to "playing"; one leads to "over", and there is no arrow out of "over":

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 165" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="40" y="60" width="100" height="44" rx="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="87" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">playing</text>
  <rect x="240" y="60" width="100" height="44" rx="22" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="290" y="87" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">over</text>
  <line x1="140" y1="82" x2="232" y2="82" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="240,82 230,77 230,87" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="190" y="74" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">take goal</text>
  <path d="M70,60 C60,20 120,20 110,56" fill="none" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="110,60 106,50 115,52" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="16" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">go, take, drop, look</text>
  <path d="M270,104 C260,144 320,144 310,108" fill="none" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="310,104 306,114 315,112" fill="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="290" y="158" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">any command</text>
</svg>
```

The expert course met the same idea in a generator that remembered what it had been sent. Flight software is full of state machines: a launch sequence moves through states such as countdown, liftoff and staging, and each state accepts only certain commands.
:::

::: context data-driven The world is data
The first famous text adventure, *Colossal Cave Adventure*, was written by Will Crowther around 1976 and greatly expanded by Don Woods in 1977. Soon after, a group at MIT wrote *Zork*. When its authors founded Infocom to sell it, they wrote each game as a story file run by one small program, the "Z-machine", so the same games could be played on many different home computers. Separating the engine from the world is what lets one engine run any number of worlds, and it is why the checks can give your `Game` a world it has never seen.
:::

::: context aliasing-again Two games, one list
Without a copy, both games hold the very same room lists, so one player's `take` empties the other player's room. With a deep copy, each game changes only its own lists, and `WORLD` itself stays as it was:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 140" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="16" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">no copy</text>
  <rect x="20" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="50" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">game 1</text>
  <rect x="100" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="130" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">game 2</text>
  <rect x="40" y="96" width="100" height="30" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="116" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">hall items: []</text>
  <line x1="50" y1="56" x2="80" y2="94" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="56" x2="100" y2="94" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="270" y="16" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">deep copy</text>
  <rect x="200" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="230" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">game 1</text>
  <rect x="280" y="26" width="60" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="310" y="46" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">game 2</text>
  <rect x="195" y="96" width="70" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="230" y="116" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">[]</text>
  <rect x="275" y="96" width="70" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="310" y="116" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">["lamp"]</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="56" x2="230" y2="94" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="310" y1="56" x2="310" y2="94" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context dispatch-table A table of handlers
Methods are values, so a dict can hold them: `{"go": self.go, "take": self.take, "drop": self.drop}`. Dispatching is then one lookup, `handlers.get(verb)`, and `None` means an unknown verb. Adding a new verb means adding one method and one entry, without touching the dispatcher. Spacecraft handle commands from the ground the same way: each command has an id, a table maps ids to handlers, and an unknown id is rejected instead of guessed at.
:::

::: context guard-clause Guard clauses
A **guard clause** is an `if` at the top of a function that handles a special case and returns at once. Everything after it can then assume the special case is gone: the rest of `do` never has to ask "but what if the game is over?" again. Guards keep code flat, instead of nesting the whole method inside an `if not self.over:` block, and they put the most important rule where a reader sees it first.
:::

::: context exact-output When a program is the reader
People forgive a missing full stop; programs do not. Whenever output is read by another program, the format is part of the contract. Spacecraft send their measurements to the ground as **telemetry** in fixed formats, and ground software decodes every field by its exact position. If one field moves, every field after it is read wrong. The checks here compare whole strings, so `"Taken: key"` without its full stop fails just as surely as a wrong answer.
:::

::: context where-next After the Python ladder
Python is everywhere in aerospace work on the ground: analyzing test data, planning trajectories, running tests, and building the tools that talk to spacecraft. The software on board is usually written in C or C++, because it must run in tight memory with exactly predictable timing. NASA JPL's F Prime flight software framework, for example, is written in C++, and much of the tooling around it is Python. The C++ courses here start from the ideas you now know: variables, loops, functions, classes, and the habit of designing before you type.
:::
--- task
The starter defines `WORLD`, an example world. Build a class `Game(world)` that works for any world of that shape: `start`, `goal`, and `rooms`, each room with `description`, `exits`, `items` and optionally `locked`, which maps a direction to the item needed to go that way.

`game.room` is the current room's name. `game.do(command)` returns the reply (commands are case-insensitive):

- `look`: `"<description> Exits: <exits, sorted, comma-separated>."`, followed by `" You see: <items in the room's order>."` if the room has items. With no exits: `Exits: none.`
- `go <direction>`: moves and returns the new room's `look` text. No such exit: `"You can't go that way."`. A locked exit without its item in your inventory: `"The way <direction> is locked."`.
- `take <item>`: `"Taken: <item>."`, or `"There is no <item> here."`. Taking the goal item replies `"Taken: <item>. You win!"` and ends the game.
- `drop <item>`: puts it in the current room (at the end of its item list): `"Dropped: <item>."`, or `"You don't have <item>."`.
- `inventory`: `"You carry: <items, sorted>."` or `"You carry nothing."`.
- Anything else, including a verb missing its word: `"I don't understand."`. After the game is over, every command replies `"The game is over."`.

Creating or playing a game must not change the world dict it was given.
--- starter
WORLD = {
    "start": "hall",
    "goal": "treasure",
    "rooms": {
        "hall": {
            "description": "A dusty hall.",
            "exits": {"north": "library", "east": "kitchen"},
            "items": ["lamp"],
        },
        "library": {
            "description": "Shelves of old books.",
            "exits": {"south": "hall"},
            "items": ["key", "map"],
        },
        "kitchen": {
            "description": "It smells of bread.",
            "exits": {"west": "hall", "down": "cellar"},
            "items": [],
            "locked": {"down": "key"},
        },
        "cellar": {
            "description": "Treasure glitters in the dark.",
            "exits": {"up": "kitchen"},
            "items": ["treasure"],
        },
    },
}

# Capstone: design and build Game to the specification in the task.
--- solution
import copy

WORLD = {
    "start": "hall",
    "goal": "treasure",
    "rooms": {
        "hall": {
            "description": "A dusty hall.",
            "exits": {"north": "library", "east": "kitchen"},
            "items": ["lamp"],
        },
        "library": {
            "description": "Shelves of old books.",
            "exits": {"south": "hall"},
            "items": ["key", "map"],
        },
        "kitchen": {
            "description": "It smells of bread.",
            "exits": {"west": "hall", "down": "cellar"},
            "items": [],
            "locked": {"down": "key"},
        },
        "cellar": {
            "description": "Treasure glitters in the dark.",
            "exits": {"up": "kitchen"},
            "items": ["treasure"],
        },
    },
}


class Game:
    def __init__(self, world):
        self.world = copy.deepcopy(world)
        self.rooms = self.world["rooms"]
        self.room = self.world["start"]
        self.inventory = []
        self.over = False

    def look(self):
        room = self.rooms[self.room]
        exits = ", ".join(sorted(room["exits"])) or "none"
        text = f"{room['description']} Exits: {exits}."
        if room["items"]:
            text += f" You see: {', '.join(room['items'])}."
        return text

    def go(self, direction):
        room = self.rooms[self.room]
        if direction not in room["exits"]:
            return "You can't go that way."
        key = room.get("locked", {}).get(direction)
        if key and key not in self.inventory:
            return f"The way {direction} is locked."
        self.room = room["exits"][direction]
        return self.look()

    def take(self, item):
        items = self.rooms[self.room]["items"]
        if item not in items:
            return f"There is no {item} here."
        items.remove(item)
        self.inventory.append(item)
        if item == self.world.get("goal"):
            self.over = True
            return f"Taken: {item}. You win!"
        return f"Taken: {item}."

    def drop(self, item):
        if item not in self.inventory:
            return f"You don't have {item}."
        self.inventory.remove(item)
        self.rooms[self.room]["items"].append(item)
        return f"Dropped: {item}."

    def do(self, command):
        if self.over:
            return "The game is over."
        words = command.lower().split()
        if not words:
            return "I don't understand."
        verb, rest = words[0], " ".join(words[1:])
        if verb == "look" and not rest:
            return self.look()
        if verb == "inventory" and not rest:
            if not self.inventory:
                return "You carry nothing."
            return f"You carry: {', '.join(sorted(self.inventory))}."
        if verb == "go" and rest:
            return self.go(rest)
        if verb == "take" and rest:
            return self.take(rest)
        if verb == "drop" and rest:
            return self.drop(rest)
        return "I don't understand."
--- hint
State that changes: the current room, the inventory, each room's items, and whether the game is over. Deep-copy the world in `__init__` with `copy.deepcopy`, so the items can change safely.
--- hint
In `do`, check "game over" first. Then `words = command.lower().split()`; the verb is `words[0]` and the rest is `" ".join(words[1:])`. Call one method per verb, and reply `"I don't understand."` when a verb is missing its word or has one it should not.
--- hint
For `go`, check the exit exists, then look in the room's `locked` dict (use `.get("locked", {})`, since most rooms have none) for a required item that is not in your inventory. For `look`, `", ".join(sorted(exits)) or "none"` gives the exits part.
--- check case | Looking around the start
Game(WORLD).do("look")
=> "A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp."
--- check case | Moving, taking and the inventory
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["go north", "take key", "take sword", "inventory", "go west", "go south", "look"]])(Game(WORLD))
=> ["Shelves of old books. Exits: south. You see: key, map.", "Taken: key.", "There is no sword here.", "You carry: key.", "You can't go that way.", "A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp.", "A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp."]
--- check case | A locked way needs its key
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["go east", "go down", "go west", "go north", "take key", "go south", "go east", "go down"]] + [g.room])(Game(WORLD))
=> ["It smells of bread. Exits: down, west.", "The way down is locked.", "A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp.", "Shelves of old books. Exits: south. You see: key, map.", "Taken: key.", "A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp.", "It smells of bread. Exits: down, west.", "Treasure glitters in the dark. Exits: up. You see: treasure.", "cellar"]
--- check case | Winning ends the game
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["go north", "TAKE KEY", "go south", "go east", "go down", "take treasure", "look"]][-3:] + [g.room])(Game(WORLD))
=> ["Treasure glitters in the dark. Exits: up. You see: treasure.", "Taken: treasure. You win!", "The game is over.", "cellar"]
--- check case | Dropping puts the item in the current room
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["take lamp", "drop map", "go north", "drop lamp", "look", "inventory"]])(Game(WORLD))
=> ["Taken: lamp.", "You don't have map.", "Shelves of old books. Exits: south. You see: key, map.", "Dropped: lamp.", "Shelves of old books. Exits: south. You see: key, map, lamp.", "You carry nothing."]
--- check test | Unknown commands
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["dance", "", "go", "take", "look around"]] == ["I don't understand."] * 5)(Game(WORLD))
--- check test | Games do not change the world, or each other
(lambda w: (lambda g1: (g1.do("take lamp"), Game(w).do("look"), w["rooms"]["hall"]["items"])[1:])(Game(w)))(WORLD) == ("A dusty hall. Exits: east, north. You see: lamp.", ["lamp"])
--- check case | Works for a different world
(lambda g: [g.do(c) for c in ["look", "go up", "take coin", "take star", "inventory", "go up"]])(Game({"start": "pit", "goal": "star", "rooms": {"pit": {"description": "A deep pit.", "exits": {"up": "rim"}, "items": ["coin", "rope"], "locked": {"up": "rope"}}, "rim": {"description": "The rim.", "exits": {}, "items": ["star"]}}}))
=> ["A deep pit. Exits: up. You see: coin, rope.", "The way up is locked.", "Taken: coin.", "There is no star here.", "You carry: coin.", "The way up is locked."]
--- check case | A room with no exits
(lambda g: [g.do("take rope"), g.do("go up")])(Game({"start": "pit", "goal": "star", "rooms": {"pit": {"description": "A deep pit.", "exits": {"up": "rim"}, "items": ["rope"], "locked": {"up": "rope"}}, "rim": {"description": "The rim.", "exits": {}, "items": ["star"]}}}))
=> ["Taken: rope.", "The rim. Exits: none. You see: star."]

+++ practice | A vending machine
--- task
A vending machine is a state machine: what a button does depends on how much money is in it. Build `class Vending(prices)`, where `prices` is a dict from each item to its price in cents. Every method returns the machine's reply. Amounts in replies have two decimals, like `0.70`.

- `insert(coin)`: the machine takes coins of 5, 10, 20, 50, 100 and 200 cents, adds them to the credit and replies `"Credit: 0.70."`. Any other coin is refused: `"Rejected: 3."`.
- `choose(item)`: an item not on sale replies `"Unknown item."`. Not enough credit replies `"Insert 0.30 more."`. Otherwise the item comes out, the change is given back, the credit goes back to 0, and the reply is `"Enjoy your tea. Change: 0.20."`.
- `refund()`: gives the credit back, `"Refunded 0.70."`, or `"Nothing to refund."` if there is none.

The machine must work for any price list, and must not change the dict it was given.
--- starter
class Vending:
    def __init__(self, prices):
        self.prices = prices

    def insert(self, coin):
        return f"Credit: {coin}."
--- solution
COINS = {5, 10, 20, 50, 100, 200}


def fmt(cents):
    return f"{cents // 100}.{cents % 100:02d}"


class Vending:
    def __init__(self, prices):
        self.prices = dict(prices)
        self.credit = 0

    def insert(self, coin):
        if coin not in COINS:
            return f"Rejected: {coin}."
        self.credit += coin
        return f"Credit: {fmt(self.credit)}."

    def choose(self, item):
        if item not in self.prices:
            return "Unknown item."
        price = self.prices[item]
        if self.credit < price:
            return f"Insert {fmt(price - self.credit)} more."
        change = self.credit - price
        self.credit = 0
        return f"Enjoy your {item}. Change: {fmt(change)}."

    def refund(self):
        if not self.credit:
            return "Nothing to refund."
        amount = self.credit
        self.credit = 0
        return f"Refunded {fmt(amount)}."
--- hint
The only state that changes is the credit, a whole number of cents. The price list is data: keep your own copy of it and only read from it.
--- hint
Each method checks its preconditions first and returns the matching reply early; only the successful path changes the credit. A small helper that formats cents as `0.70` keeps every reply consistent.
--- check case | Buying a tea with change
(lambda v: [v.insert(50), v.insert(20), v.choose("tea"), v.choose("coffee"), v.insert(100), v.choose("coffee")])(Vending({"tea": 50, "coffee": 130}))
=> ["Credit: 0.50.", "Credit: 0.70.", "Enjoy your tea. Change: 0.20.", "Insert 1.30 more.", "Credit: 1.00.", "Insert 0.30 more."]
--- check case | Refused coins, unknown items and refunds
(lambda v: [v.insert(3), v.refund(), v.insert(200), v.choose("soup"), v.refund(), v.refund()])(Vending({"tea": 50}))
=> ["Rejected: 3.", "Nothing to refund.", "Credit: 2.00.", "Unknown item.", "Refunded 2.00.", "Nothing to refund."]
--- check case | Exact money gives no change
(lambda v: [v.insert(100), v.insert(5), v.choose("water")])(Vending({"water": 105}))
=> ["Credit: 1.00.", "Credit: 1.05.", "Enjoy your water. Change: 0.00."]
--- check test | The price list given is not changed
(lambda p: (Vending(p).choose("tea"), p)[1] == {"tea": 50})({"tea": 50})

+++ practice | A state machine from a table
--- task
Build a general state machine whose rules are data. `Machine(transitions, start)` takes a dict from each state to a dict of the events it accepts and the state each one leads to:

```python
DOOR = {
    "closed": {"open": "opened", "lock": "locked"},
    "opened": {"close": "closed"},
    "locked": {"unlock": "closed"},
}
```

- `machine.state` is the current state; it starts at `start`. A `start` that is not a key of `transitions` raises `ValueError`.
- `can(event)` says whether the current state accepts the event.
- `fire(event)` moves to the next state and returns it. An event the current state does not accept raises `ValueError(f"cannot {event} when {state}")`, and nothing changes.
- `machine.history` is the list of every state visited, starting with `start`.
- A state may appear only as a destination, with no key of its own: it accepts no events.
- The machine keeps its own copy of the table, so changing the dict afterwards does not change the machine.
--- starter
class Machine:
    def __init__(self, transitions, start):
