        self.transitions = transitions
        self.state = start

    def fire(self, event):
        self.state = self.transitions[self.state][event]
        return self.state
--- solution
import copy


class Machine:
    def __init__(self, transitions, start):
        if start not in transitions:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown start state: {start}")
        self.transitions = copy.deepcopy(transitions)
        self.state = start
        self.history = [start]

    def can(self, event):
        return event in self.transitions.get(self.state, {})

    def fire(self, event):
        if not self.can(event):
            raise ValueError(f"cannot {event} when {self.state}")
        self.state = self.transitions[self.state][event]
        self.history.append(self.state)
        return self.state
--- hint
All the logic is two lookups in the table: the current state's dict of events, then the event's next state. `self.transitions.get(self.state, {})` gives an empty dict for a state with no key of its own.
--- hint
The table is a dict of dicts, so keep a deep copy (`copy.deepcopy`), not the caller's object. Check `can(event)` first, and change `state` and `history` only after it passes.
--- check case | Opening, closing and locking a door
(lambda m: [m.fire("open"), m.fire("close"), m.fire("lock"), m.can("open"), m.fire("unlock"), m.history])(Machine({"closed": {"open": "opened", "lock": "locked"}, "opened": {"close": "closed"}, "locked": {"unlock": "closed"}}, "closed"))
=> ["opened", "closed", "locked", False, "closed", ["closed", "opened", "closed", "locked", "closed"]]
--- check case | A refused event, with its message, changes nothing
(lambda m: [(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    m.fire('open')\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), m=m)), m.state, m.history])(Machine({"locked": {"unlock": "closed"}}, "locked"))
=> ["cannot open when locked", "locked", ["locked"]]
--- check test | A state with no key of its own accepts nothing
(lambda m: (m.fire("break"), m.can("fix"), raises(ValueError, lambda: m.fire("fix"))))(Machine({"ok": {"break": "broken"}}, "ok")) == ("broken", False, True)
--- check test | A bad start, and the table is copied
raises(ValueError, lambda: Machine({"a": {}}, "b")) and (lambda t: (lambda m: (t["a"].update({"go": "b"}), m.can("go"))[1])(Machine(t, "a")))({"a": {}}) is False

+++ practice | The shortest way there
--- task
Write `route(world, start, goal, inventory=())` for a world shaped like the capstone's: `world["rooms"]` maps each room to a dict with `exits` (direction to room) and optionally `locked` (direction to the item needed). It returns the shortest list of directions from `start` to `goal`, or `None` if the goal cannot be reached.

- A locked exit can only be used if its item is in `inventory`.
- `start == goal` gives `[]`.
- When several routes are equally short, find the route by breadth-first search: explore rooms in the order they were first reached, try each room's exits in alphabetical order of direction, and keep the first way each room was reached.
--- starter
def route(world, start, goal, inventory=()):
    return list(world["rooms"][start]["exits"])
--- solution
from collections import deque


def route(world, start, goal, inventory=()):
    rooms = world["rooms"]
    came_from = {start: None}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        room = queue.popleft()
        if room == goal:
            break
        info = rooms[room]
        for direction in sorted(info["exits"]):
            needed = info.get("locked", {}).get(direction)
            if needed and needed not in inventory:
                continue
            next_room = info["exits"][direction]
            if next_room not in came_from:
                came_from[next_room] = (room, direction)
                queue.append(next_room)
    if goal not in came_from:
        return None
    path = []
    room = goal
    while came_from[room] is not None:
        room, direction = came_from[room]
        path.append(direction)
    return path[::-1]
--- hint
This is the breadth-first search from the advanced course, on rooms instead of grid cells: a queue of rooms to explore, and a dict recording how each room was first reached (from which room, by which direction).
--- hint
Skip a locked exit when its item is not in the inventory, using the capstone's `.get("locked", {}).get(direction)`. To rebuild the route, walk back from the goal through the dict, collecting directions, then reverse the list.
--- check case | Through a locked door with the key
route({"rooms": {"hall": {"exits": {"north": "library", "east": "kitchen"}}, "library": {"exits": {"south": "hall"}}, "kitchen": {"exits": {"west": "hall", "down": "cellar"}, "locked": {"down": "key"}}, "cellar": {"exits": {"up": "kitchen"}}}}, "library", "cellar", ["key"])
=> ["south", "east", "down"]
--- check test | Without the key there is no way, and here to here is no steps
(lambda w: route(w, "hall", "cellar") is None and route(w, "hall", "hall") == [] and route(w, "cellar", "hall") == ["up", "west"])({"rooms": {"hall": {"exits": {"east": "kitchen"}}, "kitchen": {"exits": {"west": "hall", "down": "cellar"}, "locked": {"down": "key"}}, "cellar": {"exits": {"up": "kitchen"}}}})
--- check case | Equal routes: exits in alphabetical order
route({"rooms": {"a": {"exits": {"south": "c", "east": "b"}}, "b": {"exits": {"south": "d"}}, "c": {"exits": {"east": "d"}}, "d": {"exits": {}}}}, "a", "d")
=> ["east", "south"]
--- check case | The shortest, not the first found
route({"rooms": {"a": {"exits": {"east": "b", "west": "z"}}, "b": {"exits": {"east": "c"}}, "c": {"exits": {"east": "z"}}, "z": {"exits": {}}}}, "a", "z")
=> ["west"]

+++ practice | Commands the way players type them
--- task
Players type in many ways. Write `parse(command)`, which turns a command into a `(verb, noun)` pair for the game, or `None` if it makes no sense:

- Case and extra spaces do not matter. The words `the`, `a` and `an` are dropped after the verb: `"take the gold coin"` → `("take", "gold coin")`.
- Directions are `north`, `south`, `east`, `west`, `up` and `down`, with the short forms `n`, `s`, `e`, `w`, `u` and `d`. A direction on its own means go: `"n"` → `("go", "north")`. After `go`, short forms work too: `"go d"` → `("go", "down")`.
- `take` can also be typed `get` or `pick up`. `take`, `drop` and `go` need a noun; with none (after dropping `the`, `a` and `an`) the command is `None`.
- `look` (or `l`) and `inventory` (or `i` or `inv`) take no noun: `("look", None)`. With a noun, as in `"look around"`, the command is `None`.
- An empty command, or any other verb, is `None`.
--- starter
def parse(command):
    words = command.split()
    return (words[0], " ".join(words[1:]))
--- solution
DIRECTIONS = {"n": "north", "s": "south", "e": "east", "w": "west", "u": "up", "d": "down"}
VERBS = {"get": "take", "l": "look", "i": "inventory", "inv": "inventory"}
ARTICLES = {"the", "a", "an"}


def parse(command):
    words = command.lower().split()
    if not words:
        return None
    if len(words) == 1 and (words[0] in DIRECTIONS or words[0] in DIRECTIONS.values()):
        return ("go", DIRECTIONS.get(words[0], words[0]))
    if words[:2] == ["pick", "up"]:
        verb, rest = "take", words[2:]
    else:
        verb, rest = VERBS.get(words[0], words[0]), words[1:]
    noun = " ".join(w for w in rest if w not in ARTICLES)
    if verb in ("look", "inventory"):
        return None if noun else (verb, None)
    if verb in ("go", "take", "drop") and noun:
        return (verb, DIRECTIONS.get(noun, noun) if verb == "go" else noun)
    return None
--- hint
Keep the spellings as data: a dict from short direction to full direction, a dict from other spellings of a verb to the real verb, and a set of the words to drop. Then the code only looks things up.
--- hint
Handle the two special shapes first: a lone direction, and the two-word verb `pick up`. After that the first word is the verb (translated through your dict) and the rest, minus `the`, `a` and `an`, is the noun.
--- check case | Directions, short and long
[parse("n"), parse("NORTH"), parse("go d"), parse("  go   west ")]
=> [("go", "north"), ("go", "north"), ("go", "down"), ("go", "west")]
--- check case | take, get and pick up, with articles dropped
[parse("take the gold coin"), parse("get a lamp"), parse("Pick Up an apple"), parse("drop the  map")]
=> [("take", "gold coin"), ("take", "lamp"), ("take", "apple"), ("drop", "map")]
--- check case | Verbs with no noun
[parse("look"), parse("L"), parse("i"), parse("inv"), parse("inventory")]
=> [("look", None), ("look", None), ("inventory", None), ("inventory", None), ("inventory", None)]
--- check case | Commands that make no sense
[parse(""), parse("   "), parse("take"), parse("take the"), parse("go"), parse("look around"), parse("dance wildly"), parse("pick up")]
=> [None, None, None, None, None, None, None, None]

+++ practice | Debug: the lamp disappears from every game
--- task
`Game(world)` runs a tiny adventure: `look` describes the room and `take <item>` picks something up. Several games can be made from the same `WORLD` dict, one per player.

The bug: after one player takes the lamp, the lamp is gone from `WORLD` itself, so the next game made from it starts with no lamp. Fix it so that playing a game never changes the world it was given.
--- starter
WORLD = {
    "start": "hall",
    "rooms": {
        "hall": {"description": "A dusty hall.", "items": ["lamp", "map"]},
    },
}


class Game:
    def __init__(self, world):
        self.world = world
        self.room = world["start"]
        self.inventory = []

    def look(self):
        room = self.world["rooms"][self.room]
        text = room["description"]
        if room["items"]:
            text += f" You see: {', '.join(room['items'])}."
        return text

    def take(self, item):
        items = self.world["rooms"][self.room]["items"]
        if item not in items:
            return f"There is no {item} here."
        items.remove(item)
        self.inventory.append(item)
        return f"Taken: {item}."
--- solution
import copy

WORLD = {
    "start": "hall",
    "rooms": {
        "hall": {"description": "A dusty hall.", "items": ["lamp", "map"]},
    },
}


class Game:
    def __init__(self, world):
        self.world = copy.deepcopy(world)
        self.room = world["start"]
        self.inventory = []

    def look(self):
        room = self.world["rooms"][self.room]
        text = room["description"]
        if room["items"]:
            text += f" You see: {', '.join(room['items'])}."
        return text

    def take(self, item):
        items = self.world["rooms"][self.room]["items"]
        if item not in items:
            return f"There is no {item} here."
        items.remove(item)
        self.inventory.append(item)
        return f"Taken: {item}."
--- hint
`self.world = world` does not copy anything: the game and `WORLD` are two names for one dict. What does `items.remove(item)` change, then?
--- hint
The room's item lists live deep inside the world dict, so a shallow copy would still share them. Make the game's own deep copy with `copy.deepcopy(world)` when it starts.
--- check test | WORLD is not changed by playing
(lambda g: (g.take("lamp"), WORLD["rooms"]["hall"]["items"])[1] == ["lamp", "map"])(Game(WORLD))
--- check case | A second game still has the lamp
(lambda g1: (g1.take("lamp"), g1.look(), Game(WORLD).look())[1:])(Game(WORLD))
=> ("A dusty hall. You see: map.", "A dusty hall. You see: lamp, map.")
--- check case | Taking still works inside one game
(lambda g: [g.take("map"), g.take("map"), g.inventory])(Game(WORLD))
=> ["Taken: map.", "There is no map here.", ["map"]]

+++ practice | Dark rooms
--- task
Build `Game(world)` for worlds with dark rooms. A world has `start`, `light` (the name of the item that lights dark rooms) and `rooms`; each room has `description`, `exits` and `items`, and may have `"dark": True`. `game.room` is the current room's name, and `game.do(command)` returns the reply (commands are case-insensitive):

- `look`: `"<description> Exits: <exits, sorted, comma-separated>."`, then `" You see: <items in the room's order>."` if there are items. With no exits: `Exits: none.`
- `go <direction>`: moves and replies with the new room's `look` text, or `"You can't go that way."`.
- `take <item>`: `"Taken: <item>."`, or `"There is no <item> here."`.
- `inventory`: `"You carry: <items, sorted>."` or `"You carry nothing."`.
- **In a dark room, unless you carry the light**, `look` and `take` both reply `"It is too dark to see."`, and so does `go` when it moves you into such a room. `go` and `inventory` still work in the dark.
- Anything else: `"I don't understand."`. Playing must not change the world dict.
--- starter
import copy


class Game:
    def __init__(self, world):
        self.world = world
        self.room = world["start"]
--- solution
import copy


class Game:
    def __init__(self, world):
        self.world = copy.deepcopy(world)
        self.rooms = self.world["rooms"]
        self.room = self.world["start"]
        self.inventory = []

    def can_see(self):
        return not self.rooms[self.room].get("dark") or self.world["light"] in self.inventory

    def look(self):
        if not self.can_see():
            return "It is too dark to see."
        room = self.rooms[self.room]
        text = f"{room['description']} Exits: {', '.join(sorted(room['exits'])) or 'none'}."
        if room["items"]:
            text += f" You see: {', '.join(room['items'])}."
        return text

    def go(self, direction):
        exits = self.rooms[self.room]["exits"]
        if direction not in exits:
            return "You can't go that way."
        self.room = exits[direction]
        return self.look()

    def take(self, item):
        if not self.can_see():
            return "It is too dark to see."
        items = self.rooms[self.room]["items"]
        if item not in items:
            return f"There is no {item} here."
        items.remove(item)
        self.inventory.append(item)
        return f"Taken: {item}."

    def do(self, command):
        words = command.lower().split()
        if not words:
            return "I don't understand."
        verb, rest = words[0], " ".join(words[1:])
        if verb == "look" and not rest:
            return self.look()
        if verb == "inventory" and not rest:
            if not self.inventory:
                return "You carry nothing."
            return f"You carry: {', '.join(sorted(self.inventory))}."
        if verb == "go" and rest:
            return self.go(rest)
        if verb == "take" and rest:
            return self.take(rest)
        return "I don't understand."
--- hint
One question decides the dark rule everywhere: "can the player see in this room?" Write it once, as a small method, and ask it at the top of `look` and `take`.
--- hint
Because `go` replies with the new room's `look` text, moving into a dark room without the light already says "It is too dark to see." once `look` checks the dark rule.
--- check case | Into the dark without the lamp, then with it

=== pyp-gate | Python projects: mastery gate
--- teach
The gate covers the whole projects course: parsing commands and keeping money in cents, a model whose answers are worked out from its data, reports sorted with tuple keys, the run loop and its error boundary, regular expressions, block converters with buffers and state, the index loop, domain models with ids and copies, rules checked before anything changes, dates, JSON that is validated on the way in, undo, recursive descent, validation at the boundary, and state machines. Every problem is new, and most of them join several lessons into one small program, so plan the data and the layers before you type. There are no hints. The questions after the problems check that you understand why the code works, not only that it runs. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hardest, from memory, without opening the lesson.

+++ problem | A training log
--- task
Write a program that logs training time, in layers like the expense tracker. `run(lines)` returns the list of output lines.

Commands (command words and activities are case-insensitive; activities are stored lowercased):

- `train <time> <activity> [note]`: a time is hours, a colon and exactly two digits of minutes, from `00` to `59`, like `1:30` or `0:45`; the hours are one or more digits. It must be more than zero. Produces `"added 1:30 of swim"`.
- `total` produces `"total: 3:05"`; `total <activity>` produces `"total swim: 2:20"`.
- `report` produces one line per activity: the activity left-aligned in 12 characters and its time right-aligned in 8, most time first and equal times by name; then a line of 20 `-`; then `TOTAL` laid out the same way. With nothing added, it produces `"no entries"`.

Times are always shown as hours, a colon and two digits of minutes. Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped. Every problem produces `"error: <message>"` and the run carries on, with these messages:

- a bad or zero time: `"bad time: <text>"`
- `train` with fewer than two arguments: `"usage: train <h:mm> <activity> [note]"`
- `total` with more than one argument: `"usage: total [activity]"`; `report` with any: `"usage: report"`
- any other command: `"unknown command: <word>"`, with the word as typed.
--- starter
def run(lines):
    return []
--- solution
def to_minutes(text):
    hours, colon, minutes = text.partition(":")
    valid = hours.isdigit() and colon and len(minutes) == 2 and minutes.isdigit()
    if not valid or int(minutes) >= 60 or int(hours) * 60 + int(minutes) == 0:
        raise ValueError(f"bad time: {text}")
    return int(hours) * 60 + int(minutes)


def hm(minutes):
    return f"{minutes // 60}:{minutes % 60:02d}"


def parse(line):
    words = line.split()
    cmd, args = words[0].lower(), words[1:]
    if cmd == "train":
        if len(args) < 2:
            raise ValueError("usage: train <h:mm> <activity> [note]")
        return {"cmd": "train", "minutes": to_minutes(args[0]), "activity": args[1].lower()}
    if cmd == "total":
        if len(args) > 1:
            raise ValueError("usage: total [activity]")
        return {"cmd": "total", "activity": args[0].lower() if args else None}
    if cmd == "report":
        if args:
            raise ValueError("usage: report")
        return {"cmd": "report"}
    raise ValueError(f"unknown command: {words[0]}")


class TrainingLog:
    def __init__(self):
        self.entries = []

    def add(self, minutes, activity):
        self.entries.append((activity, minutes))

    def total(self, activity=None):
        return sum(m for a, m in self.entries if activity is None or a == activity)

    def report(self):
        if not self.entries:
            return ["no entries"]
        totals = {}
        for activity, minutes in self.entries:
            totals[activity] = totals.get(activity, 0) + minutes
        rows = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
        lines = [f"{activity:<12}{hm(minutes):>8}" for activity, minutes in rows]
        lines.append("-" * 20)
        lines.append(f"{'TOTAL':<12}{hm(self.total()):>8}")
        return lines


def run(lines):
    log = TrainingLog()
    out = []
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        try:
            command = parse(line)
        except ValueError as err:
            out.append(f"error: {err}")
            continue
        if command["cmd"] == "train":
            log.add(command["minutes"], command["activity"])
            out.append(f"added {hm(command['minutes'])} of {command['activity']}")
        elif command["cmd"] == "total":
            activity = command["activity"]
            label = "total" if activity is None else f"total {activity}"
            out.append(f"{label}: {hm(log.total(activity))}")
        else:
            out.extend(log.report())
    return out
--- check case | Logging and totals
run(["train 1:30 Swim lake laps", "train 0:45 run", "# rest day", "", "TRAIN 0:50 SWIM", "total", "total swim", "total run", "total yoga"])
=> ["added 1:30 of swim", "added 0:45 of run", "added 0:50 of swim", "total: 3:05", "total swim: 2:20", "total run: 0:45", "total yoga: 0:00"]
--- check case | The report, most time first and ties by name
run(["train 2:00 rowing", "train 2:00 climbing", "train 10:05 cycling", "report"])[3:]
=> ["cycling        10:05", "climbing        2:00", "rowing          2:00", "--------------------", "TOTAL          14:05"]
--- check case | Every error, and the run carries on
run(["train 1:5 swim", "train 1:60 swim", "train 0:00 swim", "train :30 swim", "train 2:00", "total a b", "report now", "Fly 3", "report"])
=> ["error: bad time: 1:5", "error: bad time: 1:60", "error: bad time: 0:00", "error: bad time: :30", "error: usage: train <h:mm> <activity> [note]", "error: usage: total [activity]", "error: usage: report", "error: unknown command: Fly", "no entries"]

+++ problem | A wiki markup converter
--- task
A wiki uses its own markup. Write `wiki(text)`, which turns one line of it into HTML:

- First, the text is escaped: `&`, `<`, `>` and `"` become `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;` and `&quot;` (the `&` first).
- `{{text}}` is code: `<code>text</code>`. What is inside is escaped but never formatted. Two code parts on one line stay separate.
- `'''text'''` (three apostrophes each side) is `<strong>text</strong>`, and `''text''` (two each side) is `<em>text</em>`. The characters just inside must not be spaces, and two of the same on one line stay separate.
- `[url text]` is a link: the URL is everything up to the first space (no spaces in it), the text is the rest up to `]`. It becomes `<a href="url">text</a>`. The text may be formatted too.
- A single apostrophe, as in `don't`, is just an apostrophe.
--- starter
def wiki(text):
    return text.replace("'''", "<strong>").replace("''", "<em>")
--- solution
import re

CODE = re.compile(r"(\{\{.+?\}\})")
LINK = re.compile(r"\[(\S+) ([^\]]+)\]")
STRONG = re.compile(r"'''(\S|\S.*?\S)'''")
EM = re.compile(r"''(\S|\S.*?\S)''")


def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def format_text(text):
    text = LINK.sub(r'<a href="\1">\2</a>', escape(text))
    text = STRONG.sub(r"<strong>\1</strong>", text)
    return EM.sub(r"<em>\1</em>", text)


def wiki(text):
    parts = CODE.split(text)
    out = []
    for i, part in enumerate(parts):
        if i % 2 == 1:
            out.append(f"<code>{escape(part[2:-2])}</code>")
        else:
            out.append(format_text(part))
    return "".join(out)
--- check case | Strong and em
wiki("'''bold''' and ''soft'' text, '''a''' '''b'''")
=> "<strong>bold</strong> and <em>soft</em> text, <strong>a</strong> <strong>b</strong>"
--- check case | Code is escaped, never formatted
wiki("run {{a < '''b'''}} now & {{c}}{{d}}")
=> "run <code>a &lt; '''b'''</code> now &amp; <code>c</code><code>d</code>"
--- check case | Links, with formatted text
wiki("see [https://example.com/?a=1&b=2 the '''docs'''] or [x.html here]")
=> 'see <a href="https://example.com/?a=1&amp;b=2">the <strong>docs</strong></a> or <a href="x.html">here</a>'
--- check case | Apostrophes and spaces that are not markup
wiki("don't '' spaced '' say \"hi\"")
=> "don't '' spaced '' say &quot;hi&quot;"
--- check test | Nothing at all
wiki("") == ""

+++ problem | Tables and paragraphs
--- task
Write `convert(md)`, which turns a document of tables and paragraphs into HTML, one entry per line (joined with `"\n"`):

- A **table row** is a line that, after stripping, starts and ends with `|` and is at least 2 characters long. Its cells are the text between the bars, each stripped: `| Ada | 36 |` has the cells `Ada` and `36`.
- Rows that come one after another form one table: `<table>`, then one line per row, then `</table>`. The first row of a table is the header, `<tr><th>Ada</th><th>36</th></tr>`; the others use `<td>`.
- A row whose cells are all made of one or more `-` (like `|---|---|`) is a separator and produces nothing.
- Other non-blank lines that come one after another form one paragraph, `<p>...</p>`, their stripped texts joined with single spaces.
- A blank line ends a block. A table row also ends a paragraph, and any other line ends a table.
- All text is escaped (`&`, `<`, `>`, `"`, the `&` first).
--- starter
def convert(md):
    return "\n".join(f"<p>{line}</p>" for line in md.splitlines() if line.strip())
--- solution
def escape(text):
    return text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace('"', "&quot;")


def cells(line):
    return [cell.strip() for cell in line[1:-1].split("|")]


def is_separator(row):
    return all(cell and set(cell) == {"-"} for cell in row)


def convert(md):
    html = []
    paragraph = []
    table = []

    def flush():
        if paragraph:
            html.append(f"<p>{escape(' '.join(paragraph))}</p>")
            paragraph.clear()
        if table:
            html.append("<table>")
            for i, row in enumerate(table):
                tag = "th" if i == 0 else "td"
                html.append("<tr>" + "".join(f"<{tag}>{escape(cell)}</{tag}>" for cell in row) + "</tr>")
            html.append("</table>")
            table.clear()

    for raw in md.splitlines():
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line:
            flush()
        elif len(line) >= 2 and line.startswith("|") and line.endswith("|"):
            if paragraph:
                flush()
            row = cells(line)
            if not is_separator(row):
                table.append(row)
        else:
            if table:
                flush()
            paragraph.append(line)
    flush()
    return "\n".join(html)
--- check case | A table with a header and a separator
convert("| name | age |\n|---|---|\n| Ada | 36 |\n| Bo | 7 |")
=> "<table>\n<tr><th>name</th><th>age</th></tr>\n<tr><td>Ada</td><td>36</td></tr>\n<tr><td>Bo</td><td>7</td></tr>\n</table>"
--- check case | Paragraphs before and after a table
convert("Crew:\n  |a|b|  \nafter this\nline")
=> "<p>Crew:</p>\n<table>\n<tr><th>a</th><th>b</th></tr>\n</table>\n<p>after this line</p>"
--- check case | Escaping and an empty cell
convert("| x < y | |\n| & | \"q\" |")
=> "<table>\n<tr><th>x &lt; y</th><th></th></tr>\n<tr><td>&amp;</td><td>&quot;q&quot;</td></tr>\n</table>"
--- check case | A blank line makes two tables
convert("|a|\n\n|b|\n| - |")
=> "<table>\n<tr><th>a</th></tr>\n</table>\n<table>\n<tr><th>b</th></tr>\n</table>"
--- check test | Nothing at all, and a lone bar is text
convert("") == "" and convert("|") == "<p>|</p>"

+++ problem | Hotel room bookings
--- task
Write the model for a small hotel. Guests stay for whole nights: a stay that checks in on 1 March for 3 nights uses the nights of 1, 2 and 3 March and checks out on 4 March, when the room is free for the next guest.

- `class BookingError(Exception)` and `MAX_NIGHTS = 14`.
- A dataclass `Booking(guest, room, check_in, nights)`, where `check_in` is a `date`, with a method `check_out()` that returns the check-out date.
- `Hotel(rooms)`, made with a list of room names, and a list `bookings` that starts empty. Its methods:
  - `book(guest, room, check_in, nights)` checks, in this order, that the room exists, that `nights` is from 1 to `MAX_NIGHTS`, and that no other booking of that room shares a night with this one. Any failed check raises `BookingError` and stores nothing. Otherwise it stores the booking and returns it.
  - `cancel(guest, room, check_in)` removes that booking and returns it, or raises `BookingError`.
  - `free_rooms(day)` returns the rooms nobody is staying in on the night of `day`, sorted.
  - `guests_on(day)` returns `(room, guest)` pairs for everyone staying on the night of `day`, sorted.
--- starter
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta


class Hotel:
    def __init__(self, rooms):
        self.rooms = rooms
        self.bookings = []
--- solution
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date, timedelta

MAX_NIGHTS = 14


class BookingError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Booking:
    guest: str
    room: str
    check_in: date
    nights: int

    def check_out(self):
        return self.check_in + timedelta(days=self.nights)


class Hotel:
    def __init__(self, rooms):
        self.rooms = set(rooms)
        self.bookings = []

    def book(self, guest, room, check_in, nights):
        if room not in self.rooms:
            raise BookingError(f"no such room: {room}")
        if not 1 <= nights <= MAX_NIGHTS:
            raise BookingError(f"a stay is 1 to {MAX_NIGHTS} nights")
        new = Booking(guest, room, check_in, nights)
        for b in self.bookings:
            if b.room == room and b.check_in < new.check_out() and new.check_in < b.check_out():
                raise BookingError(f"room {room} is taken")
        self.bookings.append(new)
        return new

    def cancel(self, guest, room, check_in):
        for i, b in enumerate(self.bookings):
            if (b.guest, b.room, b.check_in) == (guest, room, check_in):
                return self.bookings.pop(i)
        raise BookingError("no such booking")

    def staying(self, day):
        return [b for b in self.bookings if b.check_in <= day < b.check_out()]

    def free_rooms(self, day):
        return sorted(self.rooms - {b.room for b in self.staying(day)})

    def guests_on(self, day):
        return sorted((b.room, b.guest) for b in self.staying(day))
--- check test | A booking and its check-out date, across a month
(lambda h: (lambda b: b == Booking("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 30), 3) and b.check_out() == date(2026, 4, 2) and h.bookings == [b])(h.book("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 30), 3)))(Hotel(["101", "102"]))
--- check test | Back to back is fine; any shared night is refused
(lambda h: (h.book("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 10), 3), h.book("bo", "101", date(2026, 3, 13), 2).guest, h.book("cy", "101", date(2026, 3, 7), 3).guest, [raises(BookingError, lambda d=d, n=n: h.book("zed", "101", d, n)) for d, n in ((date(2026, 3, 11), 1), (date(2026, 3, 9), 2), (date(2026, 3, 5), 14), (date(2026, 3, 14), 1))], h.book("zed", "102", date(2026, 3, 11), 1).room, len(h.bookings))[1:])(Hotel(["101", "102"])) == ("bo", "cy", [True, True, True, True], "102", 4)
--- check test | Bad rooms and lengths store nothing
(lambda h: ([raises(BookingError, lambda a=a: h.book(*a)) for a in (("ada", "999", date(2026, 1, 1), 2), ("ada", "101", date(2026, 1, 1), 0), ("ada", "101", date(2026, 1, 1), 15))], len(h.bookings), h.book("ada", "101", date(2026, 1, 1), 14).nights))(Hotel(["101"])) == ([True, True, True], 0, 14)
--- check case | Who is staying, and which rooms are free
(lambda h: (h.book("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 1), 3), h.book("bo", "103", date(2026, 3, 3), 1), [h.free_rooms(date(2026, 3, d)) for d in (1, 3, 4)], h.guests_on(date(2026, 3, 3)), h.guests_on(date(2026, 3, 4)))[2:])(Hotel(["103", "101", "102"]))
=> ([["102", "103"], ["102"], ["101", "102", "103"]], [("101", "ada"), ("103", "bo")], [])
--- check test | Cancelling frees the room
(lambda h: (h.book("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 1), 3), h.cancel("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 1)).guest, raises(BookingError, lambda: h.cancel("ada", "101", date(2026, 3, 1))), h.book("bo", "101", date(2026, 3, 2), 1).guest)[1:])(Hotel(["101"])) == ("ada", True, "bo")

+++ problem | A pantry that saves itself
--- task
Write a pantry that tracks batches of food with best-before dates, and saves itself as JSON.

- `class StoreError(Exception)` and a dataclass `Batch(sku, qty, best_before)`, where `best_before` is a `date`.
- `Pantry()` with a list `batches` that starts empty, and these methods:
  - `add(batch)` stores a **copy** of the batch. A `qty` that is not an `int` of at least 1 raises `StoreError`. The same SKU can have many batches.
  - `to_json()` returns `json.dumps({"batches": [...]}, indent=2, sort_keys=True)`, each batch as `{"sku": ..., "qty": ..., "best_before": "YYYY-MM-DD"}`, in the order they were added.
  - The class method `Pantry.from_json(text)` builds a new pantry, adding each batch with `add`. Broken JSON, missing keys, a bad date or a bad quantity raise `StoreError` with a message starting `"corrupt save file: "`.
  - `expired(today)` returns `(sku, total qty)` pairs for batches whose best-before date is **before** `today`, added up per SKU and sorted by SKU. Food is still fine on its best-before date.
  - `use(sku, qty, today)` takes `qty` items of a SKU from batches that have not expired, the soonest best-before date first (batches with the same date in the order they were added). Batches that reach 0 are removed. If the unexpired batches do not hold enough, or `qty` is below 1, it raises `StoreError` and changes nothing.
--- starter
import json
from dataclasses import asdict, dataclass, replace
from datetime import date


class Pantry:
    def __init__(self):
        self.batches = []
--- solution
import json
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace
from datetime import date


class StoreError(Exception):
    pass


@dataclass
class Batch:
    sku: str
    qty: int
    best_before: date


class Pantry:
    def __init__(self):
        self.batches = []

    def add(self, batch):
        if type(batch.qty) is not int or batch.qty < 1:
            raise StoreError("a batch needs at least one item")
        self.batches.append(replace(batch))

    def to_json(self):
        data = [{"sku": b.sku, "qty": b.qty, "best_before": b.best_before.isoformat()} for b in self.batches]
        return json.dumps({"batches": data}, indent=2, sort_keys=True)

    @classmethod
    def from_json(cls, text):
        try:
            pantry = cls()
            for item in json.loads(text)["batches"]:
                pantry.add(Batch(item["sku"], item["qty"], date.fromisoformat(item["best_before"])))
        except (ValueError, KeyError, TypeError, StoreError) as err:
            raise StoreError(f"corrupt save file: {err}") from err
        return pantry

    def expired(self, today):
        totals = {}
        for b in self.batches:
            if b.best_before < today:
                totals[b.sku] = totals.get(b.sku, 0) + b.qty
        return sorted(totals.items())

    def use(self, sku, qty, today):
        usable = sorted((b for b in self.batches if b.sku == sku and b.best_before >= today), key=lambda b: b.best_before)
        if qty < 1 or sum(b.qty for b in usable) < qty:
            raise StoreError(f"not enough {sku}")
        for b in usable:
            taken = min(b.qty, qty)
            b.qty -= taken
            qty -= taken
        self.batches = [b for b in self.batches if b.qty > 0]
--- check case | to_json writes the agreed format
(lambda p: (p.add(Batch("rice", 2, date(2026, 11, 1))), p.to_json())[-1])(Pantry())
=> '{\n  "batches": [\n    {\n      "best_before": "2026-11-01",\n      "qty": 2,\n      "sku": "rice"\n    }\n  ]\n}'
--- check test | A round trip gives real batches back, and add keeps a copy
(lambda p, b: (p.add(b), p.add(Batch("oats", 1, date(2027, 1, 5))), setattr(b, "qty", 99), (lambda new: new.batches == [Batch("rice", 2, date(2026, 11, 1)), Batch("oats", 1, date(2027, 1, 5))] and type(new.batches[0].best_before) is date)(Pantry.from_json(p.to_json())))[-1])(Pantry(), Batch("rice", 2, date(2026, 11, 1)))

+++ problem | A spreadsheet with undo and redo
--- task
Build a class `Sheet`: a spreadsheet of whole numbers with undo and redo. The design inside is up to you.

- Cells are named by one capital letter and a row number from 1 to 99, with no leading zero: `A1`, `C12`, `Z99`. Any other name (such as `a1`, `A0`, `A01`, `A100` or `AA1`), or a name that is not a string, raises `ValueError`.
- `set(cell, text)` stores a whole number given as text: optional `-`, then digits, with spaces around allowed (`"42"`, `" -7 "`). Anything else raises `ValueError`. Setting a cell to 0 empties it.
- `get(cell)` returns the cell's number, `0` if it is empty. `clear(cell)` empties it.
- `total(column)` returns the sum of all the cells in a column, such as `"A"`.
- `undo()` reverses the most recent change that has not been undone and returns `True`, or `False` if there is none. `redo()` puts back the most recent undone change and returns `True`, or `False`.
- A change that raised, or that left every cell as it was (like clearing an empty cell), cannot be undone. A new change clears everything there was to redo.
--- starter
class Sheet:
    def __init__(self):
        self.cells = {}

    def set(self, cell, text):
        self.cells[cell] = int(text)

    def get(self, cell):
        return self.cells[cell]
--- solution
import re

CELL = re.compile(r"[A-Z][1-9]\d?")
NUMBER = re.compile(r"-?\d+")


class Sheet:
    def __init__(self):
        self.cells = {}
        self._undo = []
        self._redo = []

    def _check(self, cell):
        if not isinstance(cell, str) or not CELL.fullmatch(cell):
            raise ValueError(f"bad cell: {cell!r}")

    def _change(self, cells):
        if cells != self.cells:
            self._undo.append(self.cells)
            self._redo.clear()
            self.cells = cells

    def set(self, cell, text):
        self._check(cell)
        if not isinstance(text, str) or not NUMBER.fullmatch(text.strip()):
            raise ValueError(f"bad number: {text!r}")
        cells = dict(self.cells)
        cells[cell] = int(text)
        if cells[cell] == 0:
            del cells[cell]
        self._change(cells)

    def get(self, cell):
        self._check(cell)
        return self.cells.get(cell, 0)

    def clear(self, cell):
        self._check(cell)
        cells = dict(self.cells)
        cells.pop(cell, None)
        self._change(cells)

    def total(self, column):
        return sum(value for cell, value in self.cells.items() if cell[0] == column)

    def undo(self):
        if not self._undo:
            return False
        self._redo.append(self.cells)
        self.cells = self._undo.pop()
        return True

    def redo(self):
        if not self._redo:
            return False
        self._undo.append(self.cells)
        self.cells = self._redo.pop()
        return True
--- check case | Cells, totals and empty cells
(lambda s: (s.set("A1", "10"), s.set("A2", " -3 "), s.set("A12", "5"), s.set("B1", "7"), s.get("A1"), s.get("C9"), s.total("A"), s.total("B"), s.total("Z"))[4:])(Sheet())
=> (10, 0, 12, 7, 0)
--- check case | Undo and redo, and a new change clears redo
(lambda s: (s.set("A1", "1"), s.set("A1", "2"), s.clear("A1"), s.undo(), s.get("A1"), s.undo(), s.get("A1"), s.redo(), s.get("A1"), s.set("B2", "9"), s.redo(), s.undo(), s.get("B2"))[3:])(Sheet())
=> (True, 2, True, 1, True, 2, None, False, True, 0)
--- check test | Bad cells and bad numbers raise ValueError and cannot be undone
(lambda s: all(raises(ValueError, lambda c=c: s.set(c, "1")) for c in ["a1", "A0", "A01", "A100", "AA1", "1A", "", 5]) and all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: s.set("A1", t)) for t in ["1.5", "abc", "", "--3", "- 3", 5]) and s.undo() is False)(Sheet())
--- check test | Changes that change nothing cannot be undone
(lambda s: (s.clear("A1"), s.set("A1", "0"), s.undo(), s.set("B1", "4"), s.set("B1", "4"), s.undo(), s.undo(), s.get("B1")))(Sheet()) == (None, None, False, None, None, True, False, 0)

+++ problem | A parser for nested lists
--- task
Write `parse_list(text)`, which reads a list of whole numbers written like Python, where items can themselves be lists, and returns it as real Python lists: `parse_list("[1, [2, 3], []]")` is `[1, [2, 3], []]`.

- A value is a whole number (optional `-` straight before the digits, like `-12`) or a list.
- A list is `[`, then either nothing or values separated by commas, then `]`. A comma must be followed by a value, so `[1,]` is wrong.
- Spaces between the pieces are allowed anywhere.
- The whole text must be exactly one list, with nothing after it.
- Anything else raises `ValueError`: `""`, `"1"`, `"[1, 2"`, `"[1,, 2]"`, `"[1 2]"`, `"[,]"`, `"[1,]"`, `"[]]"`, `"[a]"`, `"[- 5]"`.
--- starter
def parse_list(text):
    return eval(text)
--- solution
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch in "[],":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or (ch == "-" and i + 1 < len(text) and text[i + 1].isdigit()):
            start = i
            i += 1
            while i < len(text) and text[i].isdigit():
                i += 1
            tokens.append(int(text[start:i]))
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r}")
    return tokens


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def value(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "[":
            return self.rest_of_list()
        if isinstance(token, int):
            return token
        raise ValueError("expected a value")

    def rest_of_list(self):
        items = []
        if self.peek() == "]":
            self.take()
            return items
        items.append(self.value())
        while self.peek() == ",":
            self.take()
            items.append(self.value())
        if self.take() != "]":
            raise ValueError("expected ]")
        return items


def parse_list(text):
    tokens = tokenize(text)
    if not tokens or tokens[0] != "[":
        raise ValueError("expected a list")
    parser = Parser(tokens)
    result = parser.value()
    if parser.pos != len(tokens):
        raise ValueError("text after the list")
    return result
--- check case | Nested lists
parse_list("[1, [2, [3, []]], -4]")
=> [1, [2, [3, []]], -4]
--- check case | Spaces anywhere, and empty lists
[parse_list("  [ ]  "), parse_list("[[],[ [ ] ]]"), parse_list("[ 10 ,-20 ]")]
=> [[], [[], [[]]], [10, -20]]
--- check test | Deep nesting
parse_list("[" * 50 + "7" + "]" * 50) == eval("[" * 50 + "7" + "]" * 50)
--- check test | Everything that is not exactly one list raises ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: parse_list(t)) for t in ["", "1", "[1, 2", "[1,, 2]", "[1 2]", "[,]", "[1,]", "[]]", "[a]", "[- 5]", "[[]", "][", "[1] [2]", "[1.5]"])

+++ problem | Transfers, all or nothing
--- task
Write `apply(balances, lines)`, which applies a batch of transfers between accounts, all or nothing.

- `balances` is a dict from each account name to its balance as an amount string (digits, optionally a `.` and one or two digits). It is never changed.
- Each line is exactly `<from> -> <to> <amount>`, with single spaces; names are letters, digits or `_`. Blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped.
- The transfers happen in order, so money received on one line can be sent on a later one.
- Return a **new** dict with every account's final balance as a string with two decimals and no thousands separator.
- On the first problem, raise `ValueError(f"line {n}: <reason>")`, where `n` counts every line from 1 (blank and comment lines included), and the reason is one of: `bad line`, `unknown account <name>` (checking the sender first), `same account`, `invalid amount <text>` (a bad format, or zero), `insufficient funds in <name>`. Then nothing is applied at all.

For example, `apply({"ada": "10.00", "bo": "0"}, ["ada -> bo 2.50", "bo -> ada 1"])` is `{"ada": "8.50", "bo": "1.50"}`.
--- starter
def apply(balances, lines):
    for line in lines:
        source, _, target, amount = line.split()
        balances[source] = str(float(balances[source]) - float(amount))
        balances[target] = str(float(balances[target]) + float(amount))
    return balances
--- solution
import re

AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")
LINE = re.compile(r"(\w+) -> (\w+) (\S+)")


def cents(text):
    whole, _, frac = text.partition(".")
    return int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0"))


def fmt(amount):
    return f"{amount // 100}.{amount % 100:02d}"


def apply(balances, lines):
    money = {name: cents(amount) for name, amount in balances.items()}
    for number, raw in enumerate(lines, start=1):
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#"):
            continue
        m = LINE.fullmatch(line)
        if not m:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: bad line")
        source, target, amount = m.group(1), m.group(2), m.group(3)
        for name in (source, target):
            if name not in money:
                raise ValueError(f"line {number}: unknown account {name}")
        if source == target:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: same account")
        if not AMOUNT.fullmatch(amount) or cents(amount) == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: invalid amount {amount}")
        if cents(amount) > money[source]:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: insufficient funds in {source}")
        money[source] -= cents(amount)
        money[target] += cents(amount)
    return {name: fmt(amount) for name, amount in money.items()}
--- check case | Two transfers
apply({"ada": "10.00", "bo": "0"}, ["ada -> bo 2.50", "bo -> ada 1"])
=> {"ada": "8.50", "bo": "1.50"}
--- check case | Money received can be sent later; comments and blanks
apply({"a": "0", "b": "5", "c": "1234.5"}, ["# payday", "b -> a 5", "", "a -> c 4.99"])
=> {"a": "0.01", "b": "0.00", "c": "1239.49"}
--- check case | Every reason, with its line number
[(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    apply({'a': '5', 'b': '1'}, ls)\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g.get("msg"))[1])(dict(globals(), ls=ls)) for ls in (["a->b 1"], ["", "a -> z 1"], ["x -> z 1"], ["a -> a 1"], ["a -> b 1.234"], ["# c", "a -> b 0"], ["a -> b 3", "a -> b 2.01"], ["a  -> b 1"])]
=> ["line 1: bad line", "line 2: unknown account z", "line 1: unknown account x", "line 1: same account", "line 1: invalid amount 1.234", "line 2: invalid amount 0", "line 2: insufficient funds in a", "line 1: bad line"]
--- check test | The input is never changed, even when a later line fails
(lambda b: (raises(ValueError, lambda: apply(b, ["a -> b 1", "b -> a 99"])), apply(b, []), b))({"a": "5", "b": "1"}) == (True, {"a": "5.00", "b": "1.00"}, {"a": "5", "b": "1"})

+++ problem | An elevator
--- task
Build a class `Elevator(floors)`, a state machine for a lift in a building whose floors are numbered from 0 to `floors - 1`. It starts at floor 0 with no requests. A `floors` below 2 raises `ValueError`.

- `request(floor)` asks the lift to stop at a floor. A floor that is not an `int` in range raises `ValueError`. Asking twice for the same floor is the same as asking once.
- `step()` moves time on by one tick and returns what happened:
  1. If the current floor was requested, the doors open there and the request is done: `"doors open at 3"`. The lift does not move this tick.
  2. Otherwise, with no requests left, it waits: `"idle at 3"`, and it has no direction any more.
  3. Otherwise it moves one floor, `"up to 4"` or `"down to 2"`. It keeps going the way it was going while there are requests further that way. If there are none that way (or it had no direction), it heads for the nearest requested floor; if two are equally near, it goes up.
--- starter
class Elevator:
    def __init__(self, floors):
        self.floor = 0
        self.stops = []

    def request(self, floor):
        self.stops.append(floor)

    def step(self):
        if not self.stops:
            return f"idle at {self.floor}"
        self.floor = self.stops.pop(0)
        return f"doors open at {self.floor}"
--- solution
class Elevator:
    def __init__(self, floors):
        if type(floors) is not int or floors < 2:
            raise ValueError("a building needs at least two floors")
        self.floors = floors
        self.floor = 0
        self.direction = None
        self.stops = set()

    def request(self, floor):
        if type(floor) is not int or not 0 <= floor < self.floors:
            raise ValueError(f"no floor {floor!r}")
        self.stops.add(floor)

    def step(self):
        if self.floor in self.stops:
            self.stops.remove(self.floor)
            return f"doors open at {self.floor}"
        if not self.stops:
            self.direction = None
            return f"idle at {self.floor}"
        above = any(s > self.floor for s in self.stops)
        below = any(s < self.floor for s in self.stops)
        if not ((self.direction == "up" and above) or (self.direction == "down" and below)):
            nearest = min(self.stops, key=lambda s: (abs(s - self.floor), -s))
            self.direction = "up" if nearest > self.floor else "down"
        self.floor += 1 if self.direction == "up" else -1
        return f"{self.direction} to {self.floor}"
--- check case | One request
(lambda e: (e.request(3), [e.step() for _ in range(6)])[1])(Elevator(10))
=> ["up to 1", "up to 2", "up to 3", "doors open at 3", "idle at 3", "idle at 3"]
--- check case | It finishes going up before coming down
(lambda e: (e.request(4), e.step(), e.step(), e.request(1), e.request(6), [e.step() for _ in range(13)])[-1])(Elevator(10))
=> ["up to 3", "up to 4", "doors open at 4", "up to 5", "up to 6", "doors open at 6", "down to 5", "down to 4", "down to 3", "down to 2", "down to 1", "doors open at 1", "idle at 1"]
--- check case | From idle, the nearest floor wins, and a tie goes up
(lambda e: (e.request(5), [e.step() for _ in range(7)], e.request(3), e.request(7), e.step(), e.request(2), e.step(), e.step(), e.step())[4:])(Elevator(10))
=> ("up to 6", None, "up to 7", "doors open at 7", "down to 6")
--- check case | From idle, a nearer floor below wins
(lambda e: (e.request(5), [e.step() for _ in range(7)], e.request(4), e.request(9), e.step(), e.step(), e.step())[4:])(Elevator(10))
=> ("down to 4", "doors open at 4", "up to 5")
--- check test | Bad floors, and a request for the floor it is on
raises(ValueError, lambda: Elevator(1)) and (lambda e: all(raises(ValueError, lambda f=f: e.request(f)) for f in (4, -1, "2", 1.0)) and (e.step(), e.request(0), e.step(), e.step()) == ("idle at 0", None, "doors open at 0", "idle at 0"))(Elevator(4))

+++ problem | A web server log report
--- task
Write a program that reads a web server's log from standard input and prints a summary. A log line looks like this, with single spaces:

```
2026-09-29 12:00:01 GET /index.html 200 5120
```

That is: a date (`YYYY-MM-DD`), a time (`HH:MM:SS`), the method (`GET` or `POST`), the path (a `/` and then any characters that are not spaces), a status of exactly three digits, and the size in bytes (digits).

Write `report(lines)`, which returns these output lines, and an entry point under `if __name__ == "__main__":` that prints the report of all of standard input, one line at a time. (The Input box holds a log.)

```
requests: 7
errors: 2
bytes: 21,504
top paths:
  /index.html 3
  /api/items 2
  /about 1
malformed: 1
```

- `requests` counts the valid lines, and `errors` those with a status of 400 or more.
- `bytes` is the total size, with a thousands separator.
- `top paths` lists at most the 3 most requested paths, each indented by two spaces, most requests first and ties in alphabetical order.
- `malformed` counts the non-blank lines that are not valid log lines. Blank lines are ignored. (Lines are stripped first.)
--- starter
import sys


def report(lines):
    return [f"requests: {len(lines)}"]
--- solution
import re
import sys

LOG_LINE = re.compile(r"\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2} \d{2}:\d{2}:\d{2} (GET|POST) (/\S*) (\d{3}) (\d+)")


def report(lines):
    requests = errors = size = malformed = 0
    paths = {}
    for raw in lines:
        line = raw.strip()
        if not line:
            continue
        m = LOG_LINE.fullmatch(line)
        if not m:
            malformed += 1
            continue
        requests += 1
        path, status, nbytes = m.group(2), int(m.group(3)), int(m.group(4))
        if status >= 400:
            errors += 1
        size += nbytes
        paths[path] = paths.get(path, 0) + 1
    out = [f"requests: {requests}", f"errors: {errors}", f"bytes: {size:,}", "top paths:"]
    top = sorted(paths.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))[:3]
    out.extend(f"  {path} {count}" for path, count in top)
    out.append(f"malformed: {malformed}")
    return out


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for output_line in report(sys.stdin.read().splitlines()):
        print(output_line)
--- stdin

2026-09-29 12:00:02 GET /api/items 200 2048
2026-09-29 12:00:05 POST /api/items 500 128

2026-09-29 12:01:00 GET /index.html 304 0
2026-09-29 12:01:30 GET /about 200 4096
this line is broken
2026-09-29 12:02:00 GET /zebra 404 2
2026-09-29 12:03:00 GET /index.html 200 10110
--- check output | The report of the whole log, from standard input
requests: 7
errors: 2
bytes: 21,504
top paths:
  /index.html 3
  /api/items 2
  /about 1
malformed: 1
--- check case | Ties in alphabetical order, and at most three paths
report(["2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET /b 200 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET /a 200 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 POST /d 201 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET /c 200 1"])[3:]
=> ["top paths:", "  /a 1", "  /b 1", "  /c 1", "malformed: 0"]
--- check case | Malformed lines: wrong method, status or spacing
report(["2026-01-01 00:00:00 PUT /a 200 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET /a 20 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET  /a 200 1", "2026-01-01 00:00:00 GET a 200 1", "", "   "])
=> ["requests: 0", "errors: 0", "bytes: 0", "top paths:", "malformed: 4"]

+++ question | Half a dollar
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
whole, dot, frac = "3.5".partition(".")
print(int(whole) * 100 + int(frac.ljust(2, "0")))
```
--- answer
350
--- why
`partition` gives `'3'`, `'.'` and `'5'`. `ljust(2, "0")` pads the fraction on the right to `'50'`, because 5 tenths is 50 cents. So the result is 3 × 100 + 50 = 350 cents.

+++ question | Why cents
--- ask
Why does the course store money as a whole number of cents instead of a float like `12.5`?
--- choice
Floats cannot hold numbers bigger than a few million.
--- choice correct
A float is only the nearest value it can store, so sums of prices pick up tiny errors; an `int` of cents is always exact.
--- choice
Ints use less memory than floats, and a ledger can grow large.
--- choice
Python cannot format a float with two decimals.
--- why
`0.1 + 0.2` is not exactly `0.3` in floating point, and adding thousands of prices lets the errors pile up until a total is off by a cent. Whole cents are exact at any size. The display format with two decimals is added only when the number is shown.

+++ question | A tuple key
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
totals = {"bus": 5, "art": 5, "rent": 90}
rows = sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: (-kv[1], kv[0]))
print(" ".join(name for name, _ in rows))
```
--- answer
rent art bus
--- why
The key is minus the total, then the name. `-90` is the smallest key, so `rent` comes first. `bus` and `art` tie on `-5`, so their names break the tie in alphabetical order: `art`, then `bus`.

+++ question | The missing continue
--- ask
A run loop catches the parser's error but forgets the `continue`:

```python
for line in lines:
    try:
        command = parse_command(line)
    except ValueError as err:
        out.append(f"error: {err}")
    if command["cmd"] == "add":
        ledger.add(command["amount"], command["category"], command["note"])
```

What happens when the input is `["add 5 food", "add x food"]`?
--- choice
The second line is reported as an error and skipped, because the `except` handled it.
--- choice correct
The second line is reported as an error, and then the first line's command runs again, adding 5 to food a second time.
--- choice
The program crashes with `NameError` on the second line, because `command` was never set.
--- choice
Python refuses to run the loop, because an `except` must end with `continue`.
--- why
After the `except` block, the loop body carries on. `command` still holds the dict from the line before, so the old command runs again. (On the very first line there would be no `command` yet, and it would crash with `NameError`.) The `continue` sends a bad line straight to the next one.

+++ question | Greedy stars
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
import re
print(re.sub(r"\*(.+)\*", r"<\1>", "*a* and *b*"))
```
--- answer
<a* and *b>
--- why
`.+` is greedy: it takes as much as it can, so the match runs from the first star to the *last* one, and group 1 is `a* and *b`. With the lazy `.+?` it would stop at the first closing star and give `<a> and <b>`.

+++ question | Emptying the buffer
--- ask
Inside `convert`, the nested `flush()` empties the paragraph buffer with `paragraph.clear()`. Why not `paragraph = []`?
--- choice
`clear()` is faster than making a new list.
--- choice correct
Assigning to `paragraph` inside `flush` makes a new local variable there, so the list that `convert` keeps using would never be emptied (and reading it first raises an error).
--- choice
`paragraph = []` would empty every list in the program, not just this one.
--- choice
Lists made with `[]` cannot be joined with `" ".join`.
--- why
An assignment anywhere in a function makes that name local to the function, for the whole function. `clear()` changes the shared list object itself, which the nested function can see through the closure, so no `nonlocal` is needed. Assigning would need `nonlocal paragraph`.

+++ question | Keys in JSON
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
import json
print(json.loads(json.dumps({1: [True, None]})))
```
--- answer
{'1': [True, None]}
--- why
JSON object keys are always strings, so `json.dumps` writes the key `1` as `"1"`, and it comes back as the string `'1'`. `True` and `None` are written as `true` and `null` and come back as `True` and `None`. This is why a save format has to be designed, and checked on the way back in.

+++ question | A copy or the same box
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace

@dataclass
class Box:
    n: int

a = Box(1)
store = {"x": a}
b = replace(a)
store["x"].n += 1
b.n += 10
print(a.n, b.n)
```
--- answer
2 11
--- why
`store["x"]` is the same object as `a`, so adding 1 through the dict changes `a` to 2. `replace(a)` made a new `Box` with a copy of the fields, so `b` is separate: 1 + 10 = 11, and `a` is not touched by it.

+++ question | The cost of a derived count
--- ask
The library works out `available(isbn)` from the loans each time: `copies - sum(1 for loan in self.loans if loan.isbn == isbn)`. With B books and L loans, what does it cost to work out the available copies of **every** book, one call per book?
--- choice
O(B + L)
--- choice correct
O(B × L)
--- choice
O(L log L)
--- choice
O(B), because each call looks up one book in a dict.
--- why
Each call looks the book up in a dict (fast), but then walks the whole list of loans: O(L). Doing that once per book is B × L. For a small library that is fine, and it can never disagree with the loans. If it ever got slow, one pass that counts loans per ISBN into a dict would bring it down to O(B + L).

+++ question | match or fullmatch
--- ask
An amount check uses `AMOUNT = re.compile(r"\d+(\.\d{1,2})?")`. Why must it call `AMOUNT.fullmatch(text)` rather than `AMOUNT.match(text)`?
--- choice
`match` ignores the `?` in the pattern, so the decimals would not be checked.
--- choice
`fullmatch` strips the spaces around the text first, and `match` does not.
--- choice correct
`match` only checks that the pattern fits at the start, so `"12.345"` passes because `"12.34"` fits; `fullmatch` needs the whole text to fit.
--- choice
`match` returns a string and `fullmatch` returns a match object, which is what `if` needs.
--- why
Both return a match object or `None`. The difference is how much of the text must fit: `match` is happy with any text after the part that fits, which is exactly what validation must not allow. Neither one strips spaces.

+++ question | A shallow snapshot
--- ask
A to-do list saves `dict(self.items)` before each change, where each item is a small dict like `{"title": "a", "done": False}`. What goes wrong?
--- choice
Nothing: `dict(...)` makes a full copy of everything inside it.
--- choice
Undoing an `add` fails, because the new id is still in the snapshot.
--- choice correct
The snapshot shares the item dicts with the live list, so after `done(1)` the saved copy says done too, and undo cannot bring it back.
--- choice
Python raises `TypeError`, because a dict of dicts cannot be copied with `dict()`.
--- why
`dict(...)` is a shallow copy: a new outer dict whose values are the very same inner dicts. Changing an inner dict changes it in both. Adding or removing whole items still undoes correctly, which hides the bug; changing a field inside an item does not. `copy.deepcopy` copies all the way down.

+++ question | The order inside a transfer
--- ask
Which version of `transfer` can lose money when the target account does not exist?

A:
```python
src = self._account(source)
dst = self._account(target)
cents = self._positive(amount)
if cents > src["balance"]:
    raise InsufficientFunds(source)
src["balance"] -= cents
dst["balance"] += cents
```

B:
```python
src = self._account(source)
cents = self._positive(amount)
if cents > src["balance"]:
    raise InsufficientFunds(source)
src["balance"] -= cents
dst = self._account(target)
dst["balance"] += cents
```
--- choice
A, because it looks up the target before checking the amount.
--- choice correct
B, because it takes the money from the source before the target lookup can raise `KeyError`.
--- choice
Neither: an exception undoes every change made before it.
--- choice
Both, because `+=` on a dict value is not atomic.
--- why
An exception does not undo anything: every line that ran before it stays done. In B, the money leaves the source and then the lookup raises, so it vanishes. A checks everything that can fail first and only then makes the two changes, with nothing between them that can raise: the transfer is atomic.
